# PREFACE

---

The history of the manuscript being published now and the principles I followed in editing it are fully discussed in the Introduction.

I only wish to express my gratitude here to Mr. Thomas Raleigh. When I first began this work, he was a Reader in English Law at Oxford and a Delegate of the University Press; he is now the Registrar to the Judicial Committee of the Privy Council. In addition to reading through the text and commenting on passages he thought were flawed or required explanation, he has since answered, with tireless patience, the inquiries I sent to him regarding legal points. Many of these points must have seemed trivial to anyone except an editor striving to be conscientious. It must be understood, however, that since he has not had the chance to see how I used the information he provided, he is no more responsible for anything that actually appears in the notes than Mr. Serjeant Hawkins or any other legal authority I consulted.

EDWIN CANNAN.

OXFORD,
*August 1896.*

<summary>In this preface, editor Edwin Cannan thanks Mr. Thomas Raleigh for his extensive legal advice and assistance in reviewing the manuscript. Cannan also notes that he alone is responsible for the final content of the editorial notes.</summary>
<keywords>Edwin Cannan, Thomas Raleigh, Oxford University Press, Judicial Committee of the Privy Council, legal history, manuscript editing, Serjeant Hawkins</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the Editor's Introduction, specifically Chapter I, which traces the history of the reports of Adam Smith's lectures and addresses the long-standing belief that his Glasgow lectures were lost to history.</meta>

# EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION

---

## CHAPTER I. HISTORY OF THE REPORT.

‘Of Mr. Smith’s lectures while a professor at Glasgow, no part has been preserved, except what he himself published in the *Theory of Moral Sentiments* and in the *Wealth of Nations.*’ This statement was made by Dugald Stewart in the ‘Account of the Life and Writings of Adam Smith,’ which he read before the Royal Society of Edinburgh early in 1793. He allowed it to be printed in the *Transactions* of the society in 1794 <note>Vol. iii. pt. i. p. 61.</note>, and to be reprinted both in 1795 <note>*Essays on Philosophical Subjects* by the late Adam Smith, LL.D., to which is prefixed an *Account of the Life and Writings of the Author* by Dugald Stewart, F.R.E.S. p. xv.</note> and in 1811 <note>*Biographical Memoirs of Adam Smith, LL.D., of William Robertson, D.D., and of Thomas Reid, D.D.*, read before the Royal Society of Edinburgh. Now collected into one volume, with some additional notes, p. 12.</note> without alteration. For a little more than a century it has remained unquestioned, and, so far as Adam Smith’s own lecture notes are concerned, it is undoubtedly correct.

When setting out for London in April 1773, Adam Smith wrote a letter to Hume, whom he had made his literary executor, giving instructions regarding the disposal of his papers in case of his death. Except for those which he carried with him—that is to say, the manuscript of the *Wealth of Nations*—there were none, he said, worth publication, unless perhaps the fragment on the history of astronomical systems, to be found in a certain desk, might be printed as a portion ‘of an intended juvenile work.’ ‘All the other loose papers which you will find in that desk,’ the letter continues, ‘or within the glass folding doors of a bureau which stands in my bedroom, together with

<summary>This introduction examines the historical claim that Adam Smith's Glasgow lectures were lost, citing Dugald Stewart's accounts and Smith's own instructions to David Hume regarding his unpublished papers.</summary>
<keywords>Adam Smith, Dugald Stewart, David Hume, University of Glasgow, Theory of Moral Sentiments, Wealth of Nations, History of Astronomy, Literary Executor, Royal Society of Edinburgh</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of Adam Smith's manuscript disposal, focusing on his final instructions before his death to destroy his lecture volumes.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<page-num>xii</page-num>
<header>EDITOR'S INTRODUCTION</header>
<columns>2</columns>

"...about eighteen thin folio paper books which you will also find within the same glass folding doors, I wish to be destroyed without any examination." Fourteen years later, when again planning a visit to London, Adam Smith "instructed his friends to whom he had entrusted the disposal of his manuscripts, that in the event of his death, they should destroy all the volumes of his lectures, doing what they liked with the rest of his manuscripts." In July 1790, ten days or two weeks before he died, "he spoke to his friends again on the same subject. They urged him to set his mind at ease, as he could depend on them fulfilling his wish. He was then satisfied. But some days later, finding his anxiety not entirely gone, he begged one of them to destroy the volumes immediately. This was done accordingly, and his mind was so much relieved that he was able to receive his friends in the evening with his usual composure." He was unable, however, to sit up with them as usual, and went to bed before supper, saying goodbye with the words, "I believe we must adjourn this meeting to some other place" <note>Dugald Stewart, in *Transactions of the Royal Society of Edinburgh*, vol. iii. pt. i. p. 131; in Adam Smith’s *Essays*, p. lxxxviii; in *Biographical Memoirs*, p. 109, note.</note>.

Dr. James Hutton, the narrator of this story, was one of the two friends to whom Adam Smith had entrusted the disposal of his manuscripts, Dr. Joseph Black being the other <note>See *Essays*, pp. iii. iv., and Adam Smith’s will in Bonar, *Catalogue of Adam Smith’s Library*, pp. xvi, xvii.</note>.

<column-break/>

From his careful use of the phrase "one of them," and the impersonal "this was done," most readers would conclude that Hutton himself was the one who destroyed the manuscripts; however, Mackenzie, who was present at the supper, is reported to have told Samuel Rogers that Black performed the deed <note>P. W. Clayden, *Early Life of Samuel Rogers*, p. 167.</note>. No one who has tried to burn several hundred pages of folio manuscript will be surprised that Adam Smith, in his weak state, would have shrunk from trying the task with his own hands, even if he was sitting up and had a fire on that July morning. What is suggested, however, by the wording of the account, when combined with the letter to Hume already quoted, is that Smith was in bed in the morning when his friend called on him, and that the "thin folio paper books" were still, as they had been seventeen years before at Kirkcaldy, "within the glass..."

<summary>This page details Adam Smith's insistent final request that his lecture manuscripts be destroyed shortly before his death, a task carried out by his friends Dr. James Hutton or Dr. Joseph Black. The account highlights Smith's anxiety regarding these unpublished works and the eventual relief he felt once they were burned.</summary>
<keywords>Adam Smith, James Hutton, Joseph Black, manuscripts, destruction of papers, Kirkcaldy, Dugald Stewart, Samuel Rogers, Mackenzie, folio books</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Adam Smith's final instructions regarding his manuscripts, specifically examining the circumstances of their destruction and transitioning into John Millar’s account of Smith's university lectures.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<page-num>xiii</page-num>
<header>EDITOR'S INTRODUCTION</header>

folding doors of a bureau in his bedroom, and thus in his sight, but, while he was so ill, before his revival in the evening, altogether out of his reach. Nothing could be more natural in these circumstances than that he should ask his visitor to take the manuscripts out of the bureau and destroy them at once, whether before his eyes in the bedroom or elsewhere.

The manuscripts having thus perished, three generations have been obliged to content themselves with the account of the lectures which Dugald Stewart obtained from John Millar, who seems to have heard all or most of the lectures himself <note>See his *Historical View of the English Government*, p. 528, and Rae, *Life of Adam Smith*, pp. 43, 53.</note>:

> "During the professorship of Logic, to which Mr. Smith was appointed upon his first introduction into this University, he soon saw the necessity of departing widely from the plan that had been followed by his predecessors, and of directing the attention of his pupils to studies of a more interesting and useful nature than the logic and metaphysics of the schools. Accordingly, after exhibiting a general view of the powers of the mind, and explaining as much of the ancient logic as was required to satisfy curiosity regarding an artificial method of reasoning which had once occupied the universal attention of scholars, he dedicated all the rest of his time to the delivery of a system of rhetoric and *belles lettres*. . . .
> 
> "About a year after his appointment to the professorship of Logic, Mr. Smith was elected to the chair of Moral Philosophy. His course of lectures on this subject was divided into four parts. The first contained Natural Theology, in which he considered the proofs of the existence and attributes of God, and those principles of the human mind upon which religion is founded. The second included Ethics strictly so called, and consisted chiefly of the doctrines which he afterwards published in his 'Theory of Moral Sentiments.' In the third part he treated at more length that branch of morality which relates to *justice*, and which, being subject to precise and accurate rules, is for that reason capable of a full and particular explanation.
> 
> "Upon this subject he followed the plan that seems to be suggested by Montesquieu; endeavoring to trace the gradual progress of jurisprudence, both public and private, from the most primitive to the most refined ages, and to point out the effects of those arts which contribute to subsistence and to the accumulation of property, in producing corresponding improvements or alterations in law and government. This important branch of his labors he also intended to give to the public; but this intention, which is mentioned in the conclusion of the 'Theory of Moral Sentiments,' he did not live to fulfill.

---

<summary>This page describes the destruction of Adam Smith's unpublished manuscripts and provides John Millar's detailed account of the curriculum Smith taught at Glasgow University. It outlines Smith's transition from teaching traditional logic to focusing on rhetoric, ethics, and the evolution of jurisprudence.</summary>
<keywords>Adam Smith, John Millar, University of Glasgow, Logic, Moral Philosophy, Natural Theology, Ethics, Jurisprudence, Montesquieu, Theory of Moral Sentiments, Belles Lettres</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues John Millar’s account of Adam Smith's university lectures, specifically identifying how the final portion of his Moral Philosophy course served as the foundation for his most famous published work.</meta>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

<margin>1 *Transactions of the Royal Society of Edinburgh*, vol. ii. pt. i. pp. 61-63; *Essays*, pp. xvi-xviii; *Biographical Memoirs*, pp. 12-15.</margin>
“In the last part of his lectures, he examined those political regulations which are founded not upon the principle of *justice*, but that of *expediency*, and which are designed to increase the wealth, the power, and the prosperity of a state. From this perspective, he considered the political institutions relating to commerce, finances, and ecclesiastical and military establishments. What he presented on these subjects contained the substance of the work he later published under the title of ‘An Inquiry into the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations.’” 1.

From a purely biographical point of view, it would undoubtedly be extremely interesting to have before us the text or a full report of Adam Smith’s lectures on rhetoric, belles-lettres, and natural theology. But these are not of historical importance. However excellent any of them may have been, they did not have the opportunity to exercise a very wide influence in their own time; and it is, of course, pointless to expect that anything first printed a century and a half after it was written will ever have much influence on human thought or action. Each generation needs to be addressed from a specific perspective, and arguments which would have been convincing in 1763 will fall completely flat in 1896. There are indeed some classics which have been lost or have suffered a total eclipse for a time and yet seem to have exercised an influence after their reappearance, but it will always be found upon examination that the influence is really that of their commentators and critics, or in some cases even of their translators.

Very little interest attaches to the second part of Adam Smith’s Moral Philosophy course—his lectures on “Ethics strictly so called”—for either the historian or the biographer. There is no reason to doubt Millar’s statement that it consisted chiefly of the doctrines contained in the *Theory of Moral Sentiments*. Since that work was published in 1759, while Smith still held his professorship and only seven years after his appointment, it is hardly possible that the publication of the lectures could add anything of much value to the history of either the lecturer or his subject.

But the third and fourth parts of the Moral Philosophy course occupy an entirely different position. The influence of the

<summary>The editor argues that while Smith's lectures on rhetoric and ethics are of minor historical interest because they were either unpublished or superseded by his own books, the lectures on political expediency are significant as the precursor to the Wealth of Nations.</summary>
<keywords>Adam Smith, John Millar, Wealth of Nations, Theory of Moral Sentiments, Moral Philosophy, Ethics, political regulations, expediency, historical influence</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction, moving from the potential value of lost lectures to the specific discovery and physical description of a newly found manuscript containing Adam Smith's lectures on jurisprudence.</meta>

<page-num>xv</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

The influence of the *Wealth of Nations* in politics has been so great that every researcher into the history of political science must have regretted that he had no access to the third part, in which Adam Smith ‘endeavored to trace the gradual progress of jurisprudence, both public and private, from the most primitive to the most refined ages, and to point out the effects of those arts which contribute to subsistence and to the accumulation of property, in producing corresponding improvements or alterations in law and government.’ The fourth part of the course resembles the second in being said to have served as the foundation for a published book. But that book—the *Wealth of Nations*—was of incomparably greater importance than the *Moral Sentiments*, and it was not published until more than twelve years after Smith had ceased lecturing. Of this period, a portion is known to have been spent in communication with the French *Économistes*, and nearly all the rest in research. There has consequently been good reason to believe that the lectures, if they could be obtained, would show exactly how certain economic ideas, which were eventually received into public favor, grew up in the mind of the man who did most to recommend them to the world.

No one could have been more sensible of the historical value of the last two parts of the lectures than I, but I cannot claim any credit for having discovered the manuscript which is now published. On April 21, 1895, Mr. Charles C. Maconochie, Advocate, whom I then met for the first time, happened to be present when, in the course of conversation with the literary editor of the *Oxford Magazine*, I had occasion to make some remark about Adam Smith. Mr. Maconochie thereupon immediately said that he possessed a manuscript report of Adam Smith’s lectures on jurisprudence, which he regarded as being of considerable interest.

This manuscript, which is copied in the present volume, forms an octavo book 9 inches high, 7½ inches broad and 1⅛ inches thick. It has a substantial calf binding, the sides of which, however, have completely parted company from the back—apparently, as often happens in the case of calf-bound books a century old, from age rather than from use. On the back there is some gilt cross-hatching and the word JURIS PRUDENCE (thus divided between two lines) in gilt letters on a red label. There are in all 192 leaves. Two of these are fly-leaves of dissimilar paper and have their matching pages pasted on the insides

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>EDITOR'S INTRODUCTION</header>

of the cover, front and back. The rest consists entirely of paper of a consistent character, watermarked ‘L. V. Gerrevink.’

The manuscript is written on both sides of the paper within a rectangular space formed by four previously ruled red ink lines, which leave a margin of about three-quarters of an inch. In addition to the flyleaves, there are three blank leaves at the end and two at the beginning.

There is no conclusive evidence to show whether the writing was first executed on separate sheets that were later bound, or in a blank notebook that was later rebound, or in the book as it currently appears.

No characteristic of the spelling, handwriting, or paper provides any reason to suspect that the manuscript is of a later date than the one it bears on its title page, namely, 1766. Mr. Falconer Madan of the Bodleian Library, before seeing that date, estimated the handwriting to be as early as the second quarter of the eighteenth century. Paper watermarked ‘L. V. Gerrevink’ was in use fifteen years earlier, as shown by the fact that there is a letter in the Glasgow University Library from Dr. Pearce, Bishop of Bangor, to Professor Rosse, written on such paper and dated June 20, 1751.

Inside the front cover, written large with a very thick pen, is the inscription ‘J. A. Maconochie 1811’ near the top; in the middle, the same signature, without the date, is written small with a very fine pen over the remains of a bookplate. Unfortunately, the bookplate has been so ruthlessly cut away with a knife that identification would only be possible if another copy were discovered. There is also Mr. C. C. Maconochie’s signature with the date 1876. On the inside of the first blank leaf, ‘1/2’ is marked in the top left-hand corner in ink as faded as that of the manuscript.

A. Maconochie, have been in the legal profession. Among others, I took the manuscript in question, and it has been in my possession since that date.

->Believe me,<-
->Yours very truly,<-
->**CHARLES C. MACONOCHIE.**<-

That the manuscript is a fair copy and not the original notes taken at the lectures is shown, first, by the fact that the date on the title page is ‘1766,’ whereas Adam Smith resigned his professorship in January 1764; secondly, by its clean and well-written appearance and the almost total absence of abbreviations, combined with the fact that the report is often obviously verbatim; and, thirdly, by the circumstance that some of the mistakes are clearly caused by misreading and not by mishearing.

That the fair copy was not made by the person who took the original notes is shown by the fact that although the original note-taker must have been capable and intelligent, the transcrip-

<meta>This page continues the editor’s analysis of the manuscript, transitioning from its physical discovery to an evaluation of the copyist's accuracy and methods.</meta>

<header>EDITOR'S INTRODUCTION</header>
<page-type>preface</page-type>

The transcription is evidently the work of a person who often did not understand what he was writing. For example, in a place where the context obviously requires "one," he writes "me,"<note>Below, p. 181, note 4.</note> simply because the initial letter of "one," written narrow or "blind," resembles the first part of the initial letter of "me," carelessly written with a loop. In other places, he substitutes "shop" for "ship"<note>P. 12, note 1.</note> and "corn" for "coin,"<note>P. 200.</note> regardless of the sense. He habitually makes nonsense of the argument by dividing sentences and paragraphs at the wrong place. Moreover, his somewhat elaborate and characterless handwriting suggests a professional copyist of mature years rather than a young man who has just completed his academic course.

It does not seem possible to give a decided answer to the question of whether the copyist copied directly from the original notes or from a fair copy made by the original note-taker. It is evident throughout the manuscript that he takes pains to make his pages correspond with the pages from which he was copying. He constantly spreads out or compresses his handwriting as he approaches the end of a page, and when unsuccessful in filling the page exactly, he does not scruple to leave the last line partially blank. For example, the last two lines on p. 134 and the first on p. 135 of the manuscript are written thus:

-> 'a better chance for its being abolished, Because <-
-> One Single Person is Lawgiver <-
-> And the Law will not extend to him nor diminish——' <-

and the last two lines on p. 223 and the first on p. 224 appear as follows:

-> 'progress of Opulence both in Ancient and <-
-> Modern Times, <-
-> Which Causes shall be shown either to Affect——' <-

The amounts contained on a page are very unequal. Page 104, for instance, contains twenty-six lines of manuscript which occupy twenty-five of print<note>P. 75, lines 8 to 32.</note>, while page 106 contains only twenty lines of manuscript, equal to nineteen lines of print<note>P. 76, line 19, 'The reason,' to p. 77, line 5, 'her husband.'</note>, two of which, owing to the chances of paragraphing, are more

<meta>This page continues the editor’s investigation into the manuscript's origins, focusing on whether it was copied from original notes and establishing a date for the lectures based on internal evidence.</meta>

more nearly empty than any others in the manuscript. Such great inequality makes it appear probable that the pagination of the original notes was followed, and this would scarcely have been the case unless an index existed for the original notes. Now, it seems improbable that a student who was likely to make a fair copy of his notes would have made the index before instead of after making that fair copy; therefore, we might infer that the copyist copied directly from the original notes. But, on the other hand, it seems improbable that any rough notes—which would almost necessarily be full of abbreviations—could have been clear enough for a not-very-intelligent copyist to reproduce without many more obvious blunders than are found in the manuscript.

The original notes were probably destroyed after the fair copy was made, and if the manuscript was copied from them directly, it may have always been unique. However, in any case, it is quite possible, and even probable, that there were at one time several copies in existence. "In those days, manuscript copies of a popular professor’s lectures, transcribed from his students’ notebooks, were often kept for sale in the booksellers’ shops. Blair’s lectures on rhetoric, for example, were for years in general circulation in this intermediate state"<note>Rae, *Life of Adam Smith*, p. 64. See Blair’s preface to *Lectures on Rhetoric and Belles Lettres*, 1783.</note>. There can, however, hardly have been many copies, or Adam Smith himself and his literary executors would have become aware of the fact. The description of the burning of the manuscripts before Adam Smith’s death makes it certain that none of the three parties concerned suspected such a thing.

Adam Smith lectured at Glasgow as Professor of Moral Philosophy from 1752 to the end of December 1763, and perhaps for a few days at the beginning of January 1764<note>Rae, *Life of Adam Smith*, pp. 46, 169.</note>. Internal evidence enables us to attribute the report of the lectures to the end of this period. Frequent references to the Seven Years’ War as "the late" or "the last" war<note>Pp. 27, 32, 268, cf. p. 271.</note> indicate a date certainly no earlier than the beginning of the academic session of 1762–63, when negotiations were proceeding, and almost certainly no earlier than the signing of the Treaty of Fontainebleau on November 3, 1762. If this indication of date is rejected on the ground that it would be natural after the

It is therefore probable that the actual lectures from which the notes were taken were delivered either during the part of the academic session of 1763–64 which preceded Adam Smith's departure, or in the session of 1762–63; it is almost certain they were not delivered before 1761–62, and absolutely certain they were not delivered before 1760–61.

In this edition, the punctuation of the manuscript has been entirely ignored, the spelling has been modernized, and section headings have been added. To have followed the punctuation of the manuscript would have been simply ridiculous and would have made the work almost unreadable. If the spelling had been merely archaic, it would, of course, have been right to keep it; but in fact, it is not so much archaic as outrageously erratic and inconsistent, even when judged by the loose standards prevailing in the middle of the eighteenth century⁴. Attempting to spell as Adam Smith himself would have spelled in 1763 was an ideal that soon proved impossible to achieve in practice with enough success to make the laborious task worth the effort. Without the addition of new headings and divisions, the work would have consisted of tediously long blocks, and the reader would have found it difficult to navigate because of the abrupt changes of subject not indicated by any external marks. So far as possible, the new headings have been adapted from

<meta>This page continues the editor’s explanation of the editorial methodology, specifically regarding the source of headings, the handling of clerical errors in the transcript, and the nature of the historical and explanatory notes provided in this edition.</meta>

<page-num>xxi</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

words used in the text and modeled on the headings in the *Moral Sentiments* and the *Wealth of Nations*. The added headings are distinguished from those which occur in the manuscript by being enclosed in square brackets.

No attempt has been made to amend the report itself, much less the lectures, but mere clerical errors of the copyist have been corrected wherever there appeared to be no reasonable doubt as to the correct reading. In every such case, however trivial, the reading of the manuscript is recorded; words left out or altered are printed in the notes, and words added are enclosed in square brackets <note>The manuscript, of course, has no headlines at the top of the pages. It is always legible, except that 'those' is usually indistinguishable from 'these.'</note>.

The notes are purely explanatory and historical. They are intended to help the reader understand the text, judge the accuracy of the report, and compare it with the authorities available to Adam Smith and with the subsequent development of his thought in the *Wealth of Nations*. The most conscientious effort has been made to resist the temptation—to which commentators on the *Wealth of Nations* have generally succumbed—of using the text as a mere clothesline on which to hang editorial opinions on economic theory.

To estimate in every case the degree of probability that Adam Smith used a particular work would have occupied too much space. Consequently, as a rule, the passages in earlier authors which he may possibly have used, and those which he almost certainly did use, are simply quoted or referred to without comment.

Except in a few cases where practical difficulties stood in the way, the references to earlier authors have been made to the edition of each work that Adam Smith was most likely to have used in 1763. The volume and page references to the *Wealth of Nations* (abbreviated to "*W. of N.*") refer to Thorold Rogers' edition published by the Oxford University Press (2nd edition, 1880).

### CHAPTER II. VALUE OF THE REPORT.

Doubts may well be felt as to whether it is right to publish a report of lectures which has been made by a university student. A lecturer generally finds that his apparently most

<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction, transitioning from a discussion on editorial methodology to a defense of publishing Adam Smith’s lecture notes despite his original request to have his manuscripts destroyed.</meta>

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

incorruptible ideas have significantly deteriorated after passing through the minds and notebooks of his pupils. However, after all, the doctrines of more than one of the greatest teachers of antiquity have reached us in no other way than through the records left by disciples who had listened to their oral instruction. If we were to reject everything that has been transmitted to us in this manner, we would be left with some very significant gaps in both philosophy and religion. In this case, we know that the disciple was both loyal and intelligent. We have exceptional means for judging the accuracy of his work, and we find that it withstands the strictest tests in a way that might be envied by a modern reporter with the advantage of shorthand. It is unnecessary to give examples here. A reader who takes the trouble to look up a few of the hundred references to the *Wealth of Nations*, and the four hundred other references provided in the notes, can easily satisfy themselves on this point.

Granting that the report is satisfactory in itself, a further objection to its publication may be made: that it is an act of disrespect toward Adam Smith’s memory. It is a circumvention of his last wishes, and if Black and Hutton had not honestly complied with those wishes, we would be inclined to condemn their actions, even if we could not claim to regret them. Adam Smith himself, however, would not have judged harshly the disregard of wishes more than a century old. He did not even trust his good friends Black and Hutton to fulfill their solemn promise to destroy his manuscripts immediately after his death; and thirty years earlier, he had taught the Glasgow students that "respect for the dead can only exist while their memory is fresh in the minds of men: a power to dispose of estates forever is clearly absurd." <note>Below, p. 124.</note>

Moreover, it is probable that if he had been acquainted with the criticisms that were to be leveled against his work, he would have withdrawn all objection to the publication of his lectures.

Du Pont de Nemours said, in his haste, of the *Wealth of Nations*, "everything that is true in this respectable but tedious work in two thick quarto volumes is to be found in Turgot’s *Reflections on the Formation and Distribution of Wealth*; everything added by Adam Smith is inaccurate, not

to say incorrect.'¹ At a later period, he repented of this outburst and confessed to a certain lack of knowledge of the English language, which had prevented him from appreciating Smith’s work as he should have. But until quite recent times, if not to the present day, authoritative writers have often expressed the belief that the *Wealth of Nations* owes much to Turgot’s *Réflexions*. Du Pont’s learned and able biographer, as recently as 1888, went so far as to speak of 'the care with which' Adam Smith 'omits to quote' the principal works of the physiocrats and 'especially that of Turgot.'²

Indeed, there was never much foundation for the specific accusation that Adam Smith does not acknowledge his debts to Turgot. He certainly does not acknowledge any obligations; but did he have any to acknowledge? Turgot’s book, although written in 1766, was only published six years before the *Wealth of Nations*, and even then only in the periodical *Éphémérides du Citoyen*³. Since this was not in the Advocates’ Library at Edinburgh in 1776⁴, and is not among the collections of Adam Smith’s books that Dr. James Bonar has catalogued⁵, we are not justified in assuming that Adam Smith had even seen the work. The internal evidence is extremely weak. To rely on general similarities of doctrine in such a case is childish. Such similarities are constantly found in the writings of contemporary authors who could not possibly have known each other’s works. The coincidence is explained simply by the fact that in literature, as in everything else, the same causes produce the same effects. There is surely nothing surprising in the fact that two men who have read the same books and observed the same events should occasionally use the same arguments and arrive at the same conclusions. Something much more definite is needed, and

no serious attempt has ever been made to support this claim by pointing out specific passages in the *Wealth of Nations* which appear to owe anything to the *Réflexions*<note>Professor Thorold Rogers, indeed, after stating in the preface to his edition of the *Wealth of Nations* that ‘in the First Book, particularly, passages will be found which are almost transcripts from Turgot’s divisions and arguments’ (p. xxiii), quotes Turgot seven times in the notes to Book I. In one case (p. 14) the text bears a remote resemblance to the passage quoted from Turgot, but an infinitely closer resemblance to passages in earlier English writers quoted below on p. 162, note *r*. In the other six cases, there is not the slightest resemblance between the text and the passage quoted.</note>.

Myths of this kind, however, die hard, and if the lectures had remained unknown, the statement that Adam Smith made much use of the *Réflexions* would probably have been repeated from textbook to textbook for at least another half-century. But as it now appears that the resemblance between the *Réflexions* and the lectures is just as close as that between the *Réflexions* and the *Wealth of Nations*, and as the *Réflexions* were not even written until after Adam Smith had stopped lecturing and had already seen and conversed with Turgot, it may be assumed that the enthusiasts of plagiarism will now seek to show that instead of Smith stealing from Turgot, the truth was that Turgot stole from Smith.

But the report of the lectures does much more regarding the *Wealth of Nations* than merely dispose finally of the Turgot myth. It enables us to follow the gradual construction of the work almost from its very foundation, and to distinguish positively between what the author's original genius created out of British materials on the one hand and French materials on the other.

In the work of professors, as in many other things, a kind of atavism is often observable. A professor has rarely been a student under his immediate predecessor in the chair. While he has been obtaining experience in a less prestigious post, or has been away acquiring the honor which it is proverbially difficult for a prophet to obtain in his own country, his master has died or retired and been succeeded by a man of an intermediate generation, and probably of intermediate views, whom he very likely regards with that slight dash of contempt which men are apt to feel for those who are older than themselves, but yet not old enough to

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction, transitioning from the discussion of Turgot’s influence to the significant role played by Francis Hutcheson in shaping Adam Smith’s intellectual development while at Glasgow University.</meta>

<page-num>xxv</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

obtain from them the respect universally and fortunately accorded to the surviving lights of a past age and an "old school," whose virtues have become uncommon, and whose weaknesses and eccentricities, instead of annoying or disgusting, afford kindly amusement. We should do well, therefore, to look in Adam Smith’s work for important traces of the influence of Francis Hutcheson, who was Professor of Moral Philosophy at Glasgow from 1729 to 1746, even if Hutcheson had been only an undistinguished member of the series of professors, instead of a teacher of unusual ability and originality, to whom Adam Smith acknowledged obligations, and of whom he used warm words of praise ¹.

In 1745, Hutcheson published in Latin a small volume entitled *Philosophiae moralis institutio compendiaria libris III. ethices et iurisprudentiae naturalis elementa continens.* Of this, he authorized a translation, published in 1747 as *A Short Introduction to Moral Philosophy in three books, containing the Elements of Ethics and the Law of Nature.* From it, we may gather with sufficient accuracy what Smith was taught as a boy in the classroom at Glasgow before he left, at the age of barely seventeen, for his long stay at Oxford.

The address "to the students in Universities," which forms the preface to the work, opens thus:

> "The celebrated division of philosophy among the ancients was into the *rational* or *logical*, the *natural*, and the *moral*. Their moral philosophy contained these parts: *ethics* taken more strictly, teaching the nature of virtue and regulating the internal dispositions; and the knowledge of the *law of nature*. This latter contained, 1. the doctrine of *private rights*, or the laws obtaining in natural liberty; 2. *Economics*, or the laws and rights of the several members of a family; and 3. *Politics*, showing the various plans of civil government, and the rights of states with respect to each other."

The three Books are accordingly headed: "The Elements of Ethics," "Elements of the Law of Nature" (in the Latin "*Iurisprudentia privata*") and the "Principles of Economics and Politics." The part of Smith’s course which eventually grew into the *Theory of Moral Sentiments* obviously corresponds with Book I; "Private Law," the third division of his "Justice," corresponds with Book II; while "Domestic Law" and "Public

---

<note>¹ Rae, *Life of Adam Smith*, pp. 13, 14, 411.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the intellectual influence of Francis Hutcheson on Adam Smith, highlighting how Smith’s university education at Glasgow mirrored Hutcheson’s structured approach to moral philosophy, ethics, and economics.</summary>
<keywords>Adam Smith, Francis Hutcheson, University of Glasgow, Moral Philosophy, Theory of Moral Sentiments, Economics, Ethics, Jurisprudence, Oxford</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Francis Hutcheson’s *Philosophiae moralis institutio compendiaria* and Adam Smith’s later lectures, focusing on how Hutcheson’s structure influenced Smith’s organization of jurisprudence and the eventual development of the *Wealth of Nations*.</meta>

<page-num>xxvi</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

"Jurisprudence," the first two divisions of his "Justice," correspond with Book III. The method of treatment is very different, as Adam Smith goes into legal details in a way quite foreign to Hutcheson, but the main subjects treated are, roughly speaking, the same. The Law of Nations is divided in Hutcheson's work between chapter 15 of Book II, "Rights arising from Damage done and the Laws of War," and the last two chapters of Book III, regarding the "Laws of War" and "Of Treaties, and Ambassadors, and the entire dissolution of States." Neither Smith’s "Revenue" nor his "Arms" correspond to anything in Hutcheson, and nearly the same may be said of his "Police." Hutcheson has, however, a short chapter in Book II (ch. 12), "Concerning the Values or Prices of Goods," in which the causes of high and low prices and the characteristics of good money are discussed.

Probably it is in this chapter that the seed of the *Wealth of Nations* is to be found. In writing the chapter, Hutcheson simply followed Pufendorf, and he does not make its connection with the adjoining chapters, "Of Oaths and Vows" and "Of the Several Sorts of Contracts," very distinct or obvious. Thus, Adam Smith may well have thought, when he began his lectures, that it would be an improvement in logical arrangement to transfer the whole to a new heading, "Police," since the regulation of prices and the creation of money by the state both came under the heading of "Police," as the word was understood in his time. As he lectured year after year, however, he would be led from this in two ways toward the consideration of the question of what constitutes opulence or wealth. He would perceive both that regulations which interfere with natural prices diminish plenty or opulence, and that mere additions to a nation’s stock of money do not increase its opulence—as at least some of the more extreme mercantilists really believed, and as all of them to some extent silently or explicitly assumed. Observing the overwhelming importance of this question, he was not the man to be deterred by concerns about the symmetry of his general organizational scheme from putting it in the primary position and allowing it to introduce various subjects that cannot possibly be regarded as part of "police."

In some such way as this, the second and only significant portion of "Police" assumed its present form, in which it consists

<summary>The editor discusses how Adam Smith's "Justice" and "Police" sections relate to Francis Hutcheson's earlier work, suggesting that Smith's focus on wealth grew out of a small chapter on prices by Hutcheson. This evolution led Smith to prioritize the study of opulence and natural prices over traditional state-regulated "police" functions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between Adam Smith’s earlier lectures and the final structure of the *Wealth of Nations*, specifically detailing how the lecture sections on "Police," "Revenue," and "Arms" were reorganized into the various books and chapters of his major work.</meta>

<page-num>xxvii</page-num>
<header>EDITOR'S INTRODUCTION</header>

of, first, a discussion of the material wants of mankind and of the great cause, the <term>division of labor</term>, which allows them to be better satisfied in a civilized than in an uncivilized nation (§§ 1–6); next, the traditional inquiry regarding prices and money (§§ 7, 8) with a large appendix explaining various evil consequences of the notion that money alone constitutes <term>opulence</term> (§§ 9–13) and corollaries regarding interest (§ 14) and exchange (§ 15); thirdly, a dissertation on the causes why <term>opulence</term> does not increase as fast as might be expected (§ 16); and, lastly, a description of the influence of commerce (which, because of the effects of the <term>division of labor</term>, is the great cause of <term>opulence</term>) on manners (§ 17). Even the Third Part of the whole scheme, "Of Revenue," is brought in as one of the causes of the slow progress of <term>opulence</term>. The portion of "<term>Jurisprudence</term>" dealing with "<term>Police</term>" thus became, with the exception of a scrap about security and a bare mention of sanitation, an "*Inquiry into the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations*."

If the Contents of the *Wealth of Nations* and those of the lectures on "<term>Police</term>," "Revenue," and "Arms" are compared, a close correspondence between them is observable. The first three chapters of the first book of the *Wealth of Nations*, on the <term>division of labor</term>, correspond with §§ 3–6 of "<term>Cheapness or Plenty</term>" in the lectures; chapter 4, on money, corresponds with § 8, and chapters 5, 6, and 7, on prices, correspond with § 7; Book II, chapter 4, on stock lent at interest, corresponds with § 14; Book III, on the different progress of <term>opulence</term> in different nations, has practically the same subject as § 16; the first eight chapters of Book IV, on the <term>mercantile system</term>, treat of the same matter as §§ 9–12; Book V, on revenue, corresponds with Part III of the lectures, and also absorbs much of Part IV, "Of Arms."

Looking at the question first from the side of the lectures, we see that this leaves §§ 1, 2, 13, 15 and 17 of "<term>Cheapness or Plenty</term>" unaccounted for. It is not easy to explain why the first two sections were omitted from the *Wealth of Nations*, and the fact will be regretted by those who ask for a theory of consumption as a preliminary to the other parts of <term>political economy</term>. The explanation of the omission of § 13 is given by <term>Adam Smith</term> himself. It was simply that the <term>Mississippi scheme</term> had been "explained so fully, so clearly, and with so much order and distinctness by Mr. <term>Du Verney</term>" that

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Adam Smith’s earlier lectures and the final structure of the *Wealth of Nations*, highlighting which sections were omitted, moved, or newly developed after his time in France.</meta>

<page-num>xxviii</page-num>
<header>EDITOR'S INTRODUCTION</header>

it was unnecessary to give any account of it<note>*W. of N.* book ii. ch. ii. vol. i. p. 318.</note>. A mere summary of Duverney's description, however well suited for an academic lecture, could not properly appear in a great book. Exchange (§ 15) was doubtless omitted as too elementary, and § 17, on the influence of commerce on manners, finds no special place, because most of it was absorbed in Book V, chapter i, article ii: "Of the Expense of the Institutions for the Education of Youth."

Turning now to the consideration of the question from the side of the *Wealth of Nations*, we are at once struck by the fact that not only chapter ix of Book IV, on the system of the <term>Économistes</term> or <term>physiocrats</term>, but also chapter viii of Book I, on wages, chapter ix, on profits, chapter x, on differences of wages and profits, and chapter xi, on rent, are as yet unaccounted for. Further examination shows that the main ideas and many of the illustrations of chapter viii, of chapter ix, and still more of chapter x, are contained in the section of the lectures which deal with prices, but that there is no trace whatever in the lectures of the scheme of distribution which the *Wealth of Nations* sets forth. The main body of Book II, "Of the Nature, Accumulation and Employment of <term>Stock</term>," is also entirely unaccounted for. There is nothing at all about capital in the lectures, and <term>stock</term> is not given an important place, while there is no mention whatever of that distinction between <term>productive labour</term> and <term>unproductive labour</term> which is fundamental in the *Wealth of Nations*, and to which a large portion of Book II is devoted.

When Adam Smith went to France he found "a few men of great learning and ingenuity" whose leader had constructed an elaborate table containing an arithmetical example of "three sorts of expenses, their source, their advances, their distribution, their effects, their reproduction, their relation to each other, to population, to agriculture, to manufactures, to commerce, and to the general riches of a nation." This table was regarded by the sect with extraordinary veneration, and doubtless every possible effort was made to explain it to Adam Smith. Its three sorts of expenses are productive expenses, expenses of revenue and sterile expenses, but of these three the middle one, expenses of revenue, is almost immediately divided between the other two. Productive expenses are annual advances in

<summary>The editor explains how major components of the Wealth of Nations, such as theories on distribution, capital, and productive labor, were absent from Adam Smith's early lectures. These concepts are linked to Smith’s later exposure to the French Physiocrats and their "arithmetical" economic tables.</summary>
<keywords>Adam Smith, Wealth of Nations, Physiocrats, Économistes, distribution, capital, productive labour, unproductive labour, economic history, Duverney</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how Adam Smith’s exposure to the French physiocrats, particularly François Quesnay, influenced the structure and theoretical framework of the *Wealth of Nations* compared to his earlier lectures.</meta>

<page-num>xxix</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

agriculture, and sterile expenses are annual advances in other industries. The *Reproduit totale* (total product), estimated at the bottom of the table, is entirely the result of productive expenses and operations, and not at all of the sterile ones. It is distributed among three classes: the productive class, the sterile class, and the proprietors of land.

To us today, the table, with its tangle of zigzag lines, appears almost like a childish toy, and its recent republication by the British Economic Association<note>*Tableau Oeconomique*, by François Quesnay, 1894.</note> excited very little interest. Nevertheless, because it attempts to give a comprehensive view of the total results of a year's industry, it marks an enormous advance in economic theory; we can easily imagine that an acute mind like Adam Smith’s would immediately grasp its importance. He was not prepared to accept it exactly as it stood, but he adopted the author's point of view. Accordingly, we find in the *Wealth of Nations* something that is absent from his lectures: namely, a definite conception of labor set in motion by a particular kind of expenditure and producing a total annual output that is "distributed" into several large categories. The specific kind of expenditure that sets productive labor in motion is identified as the investment of capital stock. It is assumed that all labor set in motion by this investment of capital produces sellable objects, and it is argued that all such labor—and no other—is properly called productive. This new doctrine forms the main body of Book II, "Of the Nature, Accumulation, and Employment of Stock" in the *Wealth of Nations*.

If the theory were fully accepted, it would seem that Book II should logically have been placed first. According to the "Introduction and Plan," the average produce per person "must in every nation be regulated by two different circumstances: first, by the skill, dexterity, and judgment with which its labor is generally applied; and second, by the proportion between the number of those who are employed in useful labor and those who are not so employed." These two circumstances are clearly in the wrong order. We should consider what proportion of the population is employed in useful labor before we consider how skillfully and dexterously they work.

<summary>The editor explains how François Quesnay's "Tableau Oeconomique" influenced Adam Smith to incorporate theories of capital stock and productive labor into the *Wealth of Nations*. He also notes a logical inconsistency in Smith's organizational structure regarding the importance of labor proportions versus labor skill.</summary>

<keywords>Adam Smith, Wealth of Nations, François Quesnay, Tableau Oeconomique, productive labor, sterile class, capital stock, economic distribution, British Economic Association</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how Adam Smith’s exposure to the French physiocrats influenced the structure of the *Wealth of Nations*. It specifically discusses why the dissertation on capital appears in Book II and the theory of distribution is integrated into the chapter on prices.</meta>

<page-num>xxx</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

‘The number of useful and productive laborers,’ we are assured, ‘is everywhere in proportion to the quantity of capital stock which is employed in setting them to work, and to the particular way in which it is so employed.’ If this is so, an economic treatise surely should begin with a dissertation on capital. But Adam Smith had already, in his lectures, begun his treatment of the subject with his dissertation on the productive powers of labor. He had briefly treated stock in storage not as something essential before labor can be set in motion, but merely as something required ‘after the eras of hunting and fishing,’ or ‘when manufactures were introduced’ and ‘a great deal of time’ was required<note>1 Below, p. 181.</note>. It would have been astonishing if he had been willing to move his own excellent discussion on the division of labor to the second place. Consequently, it is no surprise that capital is treated only in the second Book, despite the adoption of the *Tableau's* view regarding its function in governing the amount of productive labor<note>2 In the Introduction to Book II, one can observe an attempt to combine the old and the new view.</note>.

It has always been obvious that despite the mention of the problem of distribution in the title of Book I of the *Wealth of Nations*<note>3 ‘On the Causes of Improvement in the Productive Powers of Labor, and the Order according to which its Product is naturally distributed
<column-break/>
among the different Ranks of the People.’</note>, ‘Adam Smith’s theory of distribution, instead of being made one of the main subjects of the Book, is inserted in the middle of the chapter on prices as a mere appendage or corollary of his doctrine of prices’<note>4 Cannan, *History of the Theories of Production and Distribution in English Political Economy from 1776 to 1848*, 1893, p. 186.</note>. To explain the discrepancy, it was possible to hypothesize that ‘in all probability the Book existed in a fairly complete form before Adam Smith became acquainted with the Physiocratic doctrine,’ and that when that event took place ‘he may very well have thought that his theory of prices and his observations on wages, profit, and rent made a very good theory of what the Physiocrats called “distribution,” and thus was led to attach the present title of the Book and to insert the passage about the whole produce being divided and distributed as wages, profit, and rent’<note>5 *Ibid.* p. 188.</note>. This hypothesis

<meta>This page continues the analysis of how Adam Smith’s lectures in Glasgow formed the basis for the *Wealth of Nations*, specifically noting that his core economic theories existed before his time in France, while his distribution theory was a later addition influenced by the physiocrats.</meta>

<page-num>xxxi</page-num>
<header>EDITOR’S INTRODUCTION</header>

is now shown to be substantially correct. The dissertations on the division of labor, money, prices, and the causes of the differences in wages in different employments clearly existed in nearly their present form before Adam Smith went to France; the distribution scheme, on the other hand, was entirely absent. It is clear that Smith acquired the idea of the necessity of a distribution scheme from the physiocrats, and that he attached his own scheme (which was very different from theirs) onto his already existing theory of prices¹.

Besides thus clarifying the composition of the *Wealth of Nations*, the lectures serve to settle the undoubtedly less important but still interesting question of the nature of Adam Smith’s proposed work on Justice, or that portion of jurisprudence not dealt with in the *Wealth of Nations*.

Millar, in the account of the Glasgow lectures quoted above, says that Smith intended to give to the public the substance of the third part of his course—the lectures on Justice—and that he mentioned this intention at the conclusion of the *Theory of Moral Sentiments*. Turning to the passage referred to, which is the same in the sixth edition as in the first, we find Adam Smith first condemning casuistry and then declaring that the two useful parts of moral philosophy are ethics and jurisprudence. “Every system of positive law,” he says, “may be regarded as a more or less imperfect attempt toward a system of natural jurisprudence, or toward an enumeration of the particular rules of justice.” But, owing to various difficulties which he lists, the attempt is never perfectly successful. “The reasoning of lawyers regarding the various imperfections and improvements of the laws of different countries” might have been expected to “have led them to aim at establishing a system of what might properly be called natural jurisprudence, or a theory of the general principles which ought to run through and be the foundation of the laws of all nations.” However, “it was very late in the world before any such general system was thought of, or before the philosophy of law was treated by itself and

<meta>This page continues the editor’s exploration of how Adam Smith’s lectures in Glasgow provided the framework for his major published works and his unfinished project on the history of jurisprudence.</meta>

<page-num>xxxii</page-num>

without regard to the particular institutions of any one nation.’

> "Grotius," Adam Smith concludes, "seems to have been the first who attempted to give the world anything like a system of those principles which should permeate and be the foundation of the laws of all nations; and his treatise on the laws of war and peace, despite all its imperfections, is perhaps today the most complete work that has yet been produced on this subject. In another discourse, I shall endeavor to provide an account of the general principles of law and government and of the various transformations they have undergone across the different ages and periods of society—not only regarding justice, but also regarding <term>police</term>, revenue, and arms, and whatever else is the object of law. I shall not, therefore, at present enter into any further detail concerning the history of jurisprudence."

In the Preface to the sixth edition of the *Moral Sentiments*, published in 1790, after quoting from this passage the promise of "another discourse," Adam Smith says—

> "In the *Inquiry concerning the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations*, I have partially fulfilled this promise; at least so far as it concerns <term>police</term>, revenue, and arms. What remains—the theory of jurisprudence, which I have long planned—I have until now been prevented from completing by the same responsibilities which had, until now, kept me from revising the present work."

It has always seemed somewhat strange that Adam Smith regarded the publication of the *Wealth of Nations* as a partial fulfillment of a promise to provide an account of the general principles of law and government and of the various transformations they have undergone in different ages and periods of society regarding <term>police</term>, revenue, and arms—even when we remember the broad meaning the word "<term>police</term>" carried at that time. Nor has it been entirely clear how the *Wealth of Nations* fit into the "history of jurisprudence."

The report clarifies every difficulty. The lectures included in it are clearly the third and fourth parts of the moral philosophy course described by Millar, and they are also the draft of the "account of the general principles of law and government" or "history of jurisprudence" envisioned as a future work by Adam Smith when he wrote the last page of the *Moral Sentiments* in 1759. Part I, "Of Justice," along with perhaps the fifth part, titled "Of the Laws of Nations," is the third

These lectures bridge the gap between his earlier work on ethics and his later economic theories, specifically regarding public policy and government.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the editor’s exploration of how Adam Smith’s lectures in Glasgow provided the framework for his major published works and his unfinished project on the history of jurisprudence.</meta>

Parts II, III, and IV, "Of Police, Revenue, and Arms," are the fourth part in Millar's description of the course; serving as the first draft of the *Wealth of Nations*, they led Adam Smith to say that he had fulfilled his promise regarding police, revenue, and arms, though no one unfamiliar with the lectures would have described the *Wealth of Nations* as a treatise on those three subjects in that order.

It does not seem likely that Adam Smith ever made much progress with the projected work on Justice. Mackenzie, if Rogers reports him correctly, seems to have believed that the manuscripts which were burned by Black and Hutton consisted of this book in a nearly completed condition. Before he arrived that evening, he says, Adam Smith, with the assistance of Dr. Black, "had burned sixteen volumes in manuscript on jurisprudence—the sum of one course of his lectures at Glasgow, just as the *Wealth of Nations* was of another; but these had not received his final corrections, and from what he had seen he had formed a low opinion of posthumous publications in general." Little importance, however, need be attached to this, since, according to Rogers, Mackenzie also described Adam Smith, an only child, as "an affectionate brother," and stated that he died "a few hours after" the supper, though he lived as a matter of fact for six days <note>1. P. W. Clayden, *Early Life of Samuel Rogers*, p. 167.</note>. After the publication of the *Wealth of Nations* he must have had far greater distractions than before, and his official duties at the Board of Customs <note>2. He describes himself in 1787 as a</note> must have occupied a portion of his time. In November 1785, after mentioning a new edition of the *Moral Sentiments*, he wrote: "I likewise have two other great works in progress; the one is a sort of Philosophical History of all the different branches of Literature, of Philosophy, Poetry, and Eloquence; the other is a sort of theory and History of Law and Government." He had, it thus appears, failed to concentrate his energies on one work, and he could only say of the two that "the materials of both are in a great measure collected, and

We can well believe that he did indeed "struggle violently" against the "indolence of old age," which he felt was "coming fast upon" him <note>1. The reader now has the opportunity to judge for himself what those parts were.

# JURISPRUDENCE

---

## INTRODUCTION

<note>§ 1. *Of Works on Natural Jurisprudence.*</note>

JURISPRUDENCE is that science which inquires into the general principles that should be the foundation of the laws of all nations. Grotius seems to have been the first who attempted to give the world anything like a regular system of natural jurisprudence, and his treatise *On the Laws of War and Peace*, despite all its imperfections, is perhaps today the most complete work on this subject ¹. It is a type of casuistical book for sovereigns and states, determining in what cases war may justly be made and how far it may be carried on. As states have no common sovereign and are, with respect to one another, in a state of nature, war is their only method of redressing injuries. He determines war to be lawful in every case where the state receives an injury that would be redressed by an equitable civil magistrate ². This naturally led him to inquire into the constitution of states and the principles of civil laws; into the rights of sovereigns and subjects; into the nature of crimes, contracts, property, and whatever else was the object of law, so that the first two books of

---

<note>1. *Moral Sentiments*, at the end.</note>
<note>2. Book ii. chapter i. § 2.</note>

<summary>This page defines jurisprudence as the science of the principles underlying all national laws and identifies Hugo Grotius's work as the first systematic treatment of natural jurisprudence. It explains that Grotius used the laws of war and peace to explore civil laws, sovereignty, and the rights of subjects.</summary>

<keywords>Jurisprudence, Natural Jurisprudence, Hugo Grotius, On the Laws of War and Peace, Sovereignty, State of Nature, Civil Law, Casuistry</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of jurisprudence, moving from the works of Grotius to the controversial theories of Thomas Hobbes, the subsequent reactions from theologians, and the contributions of Pufendorf and Baron de Cocceji.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>JURISPRUDENCE</header>

his treatise, which is on this subject, is a complete system of jurisprudence.

The next notable writer after Grotius was Mr. Hobbes. He had conceived an utter abhorrence for the clergy, and the bigotry of his times gave him cause to think that the subjection of the consciences of men to ecclesiastical authority was the cause of the dissensions and civil wars that occurred in England during the times of Charles I and Cromwell. In opposition to them, he endeavored to establish a system of morals by which the consciences of men might be subjected to the civil power, and which represented the will of the magistrate as the only proper rule of conduct. Before the establishment of civil society, mankind, according to him, was in a state of war; and in order to avoid the evils of a natural state, men entered into a contract to obey one common sovereign who would determine all disputes. According to him, obedience to his will constituted civil government, without which there could be no virtue, and consequently, it was also the foundation and essence of virtue.

The theologians felt obliged to oppose this harmful doctrine concerning virtue and attacked it by endeavoring to show that a state of nature was not a state of war, but that society might exist, though not in such a harmonious manner, without civil institutions. They endeavored to show that man in this state has certain inherent rights, such as a right to his body, to the fruits of his labor, and the fulfillment of contracts. With this goal, Puffendorf wrote his large treatise. The sole intention of the first part of it is to refute Hobbes, though in reality it serves no purpose to discuss the laws that would exist in a state of nature, or by what means the succession to property was carried out, as no such state exists.

The next person who wrote on this subject was the Baron de Cocceji, a Prussian. There are five folio volumes of his

<keywords>Thomas Hobbes, Samuel von Pufendorf, Baron de Cocceji, State of Nature, State of War, Civil Government, Natural Rights, Jurisprudence</keywords>

published works, many of which are very clever and distinct, especially those that deal with laws. In the last volume, he provides an account of some German systems¹.

Aside from these, there are no noteworthy systems on this subject.

### [§ 2. On the Division of the Subject.]

<margin>(</margin>Jurisprudence is the theory of the general principles of law and government.

The four great objects of law are justice, police, revenue, and arms.

<margin>(</margin>The object of justice is security from injury, and it is the foundation of civil government.

The objects of police are the affordability of commodities, public security, and cleanliness—if the latter two were not too minor for a lecture of this type. Under this heading, we will consider the opulence of a state.

It is also necessary that the magistrate who devotes his time and labor to the business of the state should be compensated for it. For this purpose, and to cover the expenses of government, some fund must be raised. This is the origin of revenue. The subject for consideration under this heading will be the proper methods of levying revenue,

In general, whatever revenue can be raised most unnoticeably from the people should be preferred; and in the following sections, it is intended to show how far the laws of Britain and of other European nations are designed for this purpose.

Since the best police cannot provide security unless the government can defend itself from foreign injuries and attacks, the fourth area established by law serves this purpose. Under this heading, we will examine the different types of arms with their advantages and disadvantages, the organization of standing armies, militias, etc.

After these, the laws of nations will be considered, which include the claims that one independent society may have against another, the privileges of aliens, and the legitimate grounds for declaring war.

# PART I: OF JUSTICE

---

## [INTRODUCTION]

The purpose of justice is to provide security from injury. A man may be injured in several respects:
First, as a man.
Secondly, as a member of a family.
<note>Small ink mark in the left margin.</note> Thirdly, as a member of a state.
As a man, he may be injured in his body, reputation, or estate.
As a member of a family, he may be injured as a father, as a son, as a husband or wife, as a master or servant, or as a guardian or pupil. For the last two are to be considered in a family relation until such time as the pupil can take care of himself.
As a member of a state, a magistrate may be injured by disobedience, or a subject by oppression, etc.
A man may be injured:
First, in his body by wounding, maiming, murdering, or by infringing his liberty.
Secondly, in his reputation, either by falsely representing him as a proper object of resentment or punishment—such as by calling him a thief or robber—or by depreciating his real worth and endeavoring to degrade him below the level of his profession. A physician's character is injured when

<vocab>justice, injury, family, state, reputation, estate, magistrate, subject, liberty, physician</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the primary goal of justice as the prevention of injury and categorizes the different types of harm a person can suffer as an individual, a family member, or a citizen. It specifically begins to detail injuries to the person, including physical harm, loss of liberty, and defamation of character.</summary>
<keywords>justice, injury, domestic relations, civil law, reputation, physical injury, estate, guardian and pupil, magistrate, defamation</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of injuries to a person's reputation before transitioning into a detailed classification of rights regarding estate and property, specifically distinguishing between natural and acquired rights, as well as real and personal rights.</meta>

# PART I: JUSTICE

we endeavor to persuade the world he kills his patients instead of curing them, for by such a report he loses his business. We do not, however, injure a man when we do not give him all the praise that is due to his merit. We do not injure Sir Isaac Newton or Mr. Pope when we say that Sir Isaac was no better a philosopher than Descartes, or that Mr. Pope was no better a poet than the ordinary ones of his own time. By these expressions we do not bestow on them all the praise that they deserve, yet we do them no injury, for we do not cast them below the ordinary rank of men in their own professions. These rights which a man has to the preservation of his body and reputation from injury are called natural, or as the civil lawyers express them, *iura hominum naturalia* <note>natural rights of men</note>.

Thirdly, a man may be injured in his estate. His rights to his estate are called acquired or *iura adventitia* <note>acquired rights</note>, and are of two kinds: real and personal.

A real right is that whose object is a physical thing and which can be claimed *a quocumque possessore* <note>from any possessor</note>. Such are all possessions, houses, and furniture.

Personal rights are such as can be claimed by a lawsuit from a particular person, but not *a quocumque possessore*. Such are all debts and contracts, the payment or performance of which can be demanded only from one person. If I buy a horse and have it delivered to me, though the former owner sells it to another, I can claim it *a quocumque possessore*; but if it was not delivered to me, I can only sue the seller.

Real rights are of four kinds: property, servitudes, pledges, and exclusive privileges.

Property is our possessions of every kind, which if in any way lost or taken from us by theft or violence, may be reclaimed *a quocumque possessore*.

Servitudes are burdens upon the property of another. Thus, I may have a liberty of passing through a field belonging to another which lies between me and the high-

<summary>This page defines natural rights regarding body and reputation and introduces acquired rights, specifically distinguishing between real rights (claims against any possessor of a thing) and personal rights (claims against a specific individual). It further categorizes real rights into property, servitudes, pledges, and privileges, beginning an explanation of property and servitudes.</summary>
<keywords>natural rights, acquired rights, iura hominum naturalia, iura adventitia, real rights, personal rights, property, servitudes, legal claims, Isaac Newton, Alexander Pope</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of "real rights" (iura in re) under the heading of estate or property, moving from the basic definition of property to specific categories like servitudes, pledges, and exclusive privileges, before transitioning to "personal rights."</meta>

INTRODUCTION

way, or if my neighbor has plenty of water in his fields and I have none in mine for my cattle, I may have a right to drive them to his. Such burdens on the property of another are called servitudes. These rights were originally personal, but the trouble and expense of numerous lawsuits in order to get possession of them—when the adjacent property which was burdened with them passed through many hands—induced legislators to make them real and claimable *a quocumque possessore* <note>from any possessor</note>. Afterward, the property was transferred with these servitudes upon it.

Pledges, which include all pawns and mortgages, are securities for something else to which we have a right. The laws of most civilized nations have considered them as real rights and give a liberty to claim them as such.

Exclusive privileges are those such as that of a bookseller to sell a book for a certain number of years, and to prevent any other person from doing so during that period. These rights are for the most part creatures of the civil law, though some few of them are natural; as in a state of hunters even before the origin of civil government, if a man has started a hare and pursued her for some time, he has an exclusive privilege to hunt her, by which he can prevent any other to come in upon her with a fresh pack of hounds.

An heir also has an exclusive privilege of preventing any person from taking possession of the inheritance left to him while he is deliberating whether or not it will be for his interest to take possession of it and pay off the debts with which it is burdened <note>The reference is to Roman or Scotch law, not English.</note>.

Personal rights are of three kinds, as they arise from contract, quasi contract, or delinquency.

The foundation of contract is the reasonable expectation which the person who promises raises in the person to whom he binds himself, the satisfaction of which may be extorted by force.

<summary>This page defines various real rights—servitudes, pledges, and exclusive privileges—explaining how they attach to property regardless of the owner. It also introduces personal rights, categorizing them into contracts, quasi contracts, and delinquencies based on the foundation of reasonable expectation.</summary>

<keywords>servitudes, real rights, personal rights, a quocumque possessore, pledges, mortgages, exclusive privileges, civil law, contract, quasi contract, delinquency, property law, inheritance</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of personal rights, specifically defining quasi-contracts and delinquency, before exploring the interdependent relationship between the state of property and the form of civil government.</meta>

# PART I : JUSTICE

Page 8

A quasi-contract is the right which one has to compensation for necessary trouble and expense regarding another man’s affairs. If a person finds a watch on the highway, he has a claim to a reward and to the reimbursement of his expenses in finding the owner. If a man lends^1 me a sum of money, he has a right not only to the sum, but to interest also.

Delinquency is based upon damage done to any person, whether through malice or culpable negligence. A person has a right to claim these only from a specific person.

The objects of these seven rights make up the whole of a man’s estate.

The origin of natural rights is quite evident. No one doubts that a person has a right to have his body free from injury and his liberty free from infringement unless there is a proper cause. But acquired rights, such as property, require more explanation. Property and civil government depend very much on one another. The preservation of property and the inequality of possession first formed government, and the state of property must always vary with the form of government. The civil lawyers begin by considering government and then treat property and other rights. Others^2 who have written on this subject begin with the latter and then consider family and civil government. There are several advantages peculiar to each of these methods, though that of the civil law seems on the whole preferable.

# [DIVISION I.] OF PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE

## [§ 1. *Of the Original Principles of Government.*]

There are two principles that induce men to enter into a civil society, which we shall call the principles of authority and utility. At the head of every small society or association of men, we find a person of superior abilities. In a warlike society, he is a man of superior strength, and in a refined one, he is a man of superior mental capacity. Age and a long possession of power also tend to strengthen authority. In our imagination, age is naturally connected with wisdom and experience, and a continuation in power bestows a kind of right to exercise it. But superior wealth, even more than any of these qualities, contributes to conferring authority. This does not result from any dependence that the poor have upon the rich—for, in general, the poor are independent and support themselves by their labor—yet, even though they expect no benefit from them, they have a strong inclination to pay them respect. This principle is fully explained in *The Theory of Moral Sentiments*¹, where it is shown that it arises from our sympathy with our superiors being greater than our sympathy with our equals or inferiors: we admire their

---

<note>¹ *The Theory of Moral Sentiments* by Adam Smith, Professor of Moral Philosophy in the University of Glasgow, was published early in 1759 (see John Rae, *Life of Adam Smith*, 1895, pp. 141–</note>

<column-break/>

<note>146) in one octavo volume of 551 pages. This edition contains much less content than the sixth edition (1790), which would occupy considerably more than 800 similar pages.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the two main reasons people form civil societies: authority and utility. It explains that authority is derived from superior ability, age, long-term possession of power, and—most significantly—wealth, due to a natural human sympathy for those in superior positions.</summary>
