happy situation, enter into it with pleasure, and endeavor to promote it <note>1. *Moral Sentiments*, 1759, part i. section 4. chapter ii. ‘Of the origin of ambition, and of the distinction of ranks.’</note>.

Among the great, since superior physical and mental abilities are not so easily judged by others, it is more convenient, as well as more common, to give preference to wealth. It is evident that an old family—that is, one which has been long distinguished by its wealth—has more authority than any other. An upstart is always disagreeable; we envy his superiority over us and think ourselves as well entitled to wealth as he is <note>2. ‘An upstart, though of the greatest merit, is generally disagreeable, and a sentiment of envy commonly prevents us from heartily sympathizing with his <column-break/> joy.’ *Moral Sentiments*, 1759, p. 86. ‘Upstart greatness is everywhere less respected than ancient greatness.’ *Wealth of Nations*, book v. chapter i. part 2, volume ii. p. 296.</note>. If I am told that a man’s grandfather was very poor and dependent on my family, I will greatly resent seeing his grandson in a position above me, and will not be very inclined to submit to his authority. Superior age, superior physical and mental abilities, ancient family, and superior wealth seem to be the four things that give one man authority over another <note>3. These four things reappear in *Wealth of Nations*, book v. chapter i. part 2, volume ii. pp. 294–6, and are treated more fully there.</note>.

The second principle which induces men to obey the civil magistrate is utility. Everyone is aware of the necessity of this principle to preserve justice and peace in society. Through civil institutions, the poorest may obtain redress for injuries from the wealthiest and most powerful; and though there may be some irregularities in particular cases, as there undoubtedly are, we still submit to them to avoid greater evils. It is the sense of public utility, more than private utility, which influences men toward obedience. It may sometimes be in my interest to disobey and to wish the government overturned, but I am aware that other men have a different opinion than I do and would not assist me

<summary>This page identifies four sources of personal authority—age, ability, lineage, and wealth—and introduces utility as the second reason for obeying government. It explains that people prioritize public utility and the preservation of order over their own private interests to avoid the greater evils of social collapse.</summary>

# PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE
11

...in the enterprise. I therefore submit to its decision for the good of the whole.

If government has been long-standing in a country, and if it is supported by proper revenues and is at the same time in the hands of a man of great abilities, authority is then in perfection.

In all governments both of these principles occur to some degree, but in a monarchy the principle of authority prevails, and in a democracy that of utility. In Britain, which has a mixed government, the factions formed some time ago under the names of Whig and Tory were influenced by these principles. The former submitted to government because of its utility and the advantages which they derived from it, while the latter claimed that it was a divine institution, and to offend against it was as criminal as for a child to rebel against its parent. Men in general follow these principles according to their natural dispositions. In a man of a bold, daring, and active nature, the principle of utility is predominant, and a peaceable, easy disposition is usually pleased with a quiet submission to superiority <note>1. Hume, 'Of the Parties of Great Britain,' at the beginning, *Essays*, 1741, pp. 119, 120.</note>.

It has been a common doctrine in this country that a contract is the foundation of allegiance to the civil magistrate <note>2. Adam Smith's teacher, Hutcheson, taught this doctrine. See his *Introduction to Moral Philosophy*, 1747, p. 285, and his posthumous *System of Moral Philosophy*, <column-break/> 1755, vol. ii. pp. 225 and following.</note>. But that this is not the case will appear from the following reasons.

In the first place, the doctrine of an original contract is unique to Great Britain; yet government exists where it was never even considered, which is the case even for the majority of people in this country <note>3. 'These connections are always conceived to be equally independent of our consent, in Persia and China; in France and Spain; and even in Holland and England wherever the doctrines mentioned above have not been care- <column-break/> </note>. <meta>This page continues the critique of the "original contract" theory, arguing that common people do not base their allegiance to government on any such agreement, and that residence in a country does not constitute valid tacit consent.</meta>

# PART I: JUSTICE
12

If you ask a porter or day-labourer why he obeys the civil magistrate, he will tell you that it is right to do so, that he sees others do it, that he would be punished if he refused to do it, or perhaps that it is a sin against God not to do it. But you will never hear him mention a contract as the foundation of his obedience.

Secondly, when certain powers of government were at first entrusted to certain persons upon certain conditions, it is true that the obedience of those who entrusted it might be founded on a contract, but their posterity have nothing to do with it; they are not conscious of it, and therefore cannot be bound by it. It may indeed be said that by remaining in the country you tacitly consent to the contract and are bound by it. But how can you avoid staying in it? You were not consulted on whether you should be born in it or not. And how can you get out of it? Most people know no other language nor country, are poor, and are obliged to stay not far from the place where they were born to labour for a subsistence. They cannot, therefore, be said to give any consent to a contract, though they may have the strongest sense of obedience. To say that by staying in a country a man agrees to a contract of obedience to government is just the same as carrying a man onto a ship and, after he is at a distance from land, telling him that by being on the ship¹ he has contracted to obey the master². The foundation of a duty cannot be a principle with which mankind is entirely unacquainted. They must have some idea, however confused, of the principle upon which they act.

<meta>This page continues the argument against the "original contract" theory of government, noting that states treat subjects, aliens, and legal oaths in ways that contradict the existence of such a contract.</meta>

# PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE

But again, assuming an original contract exists, by leaving the state, you expressly declare that you will no longer remain its subject and are freed from the obligation you owed it. Yet every state claims its own subjects and punishes them for such practices ¹, which would be the highest injustice if their residence in the country implied consent to a previous agreement. Again, if there were such a thing as an original contract, aliens who enter a country, preferring it to others, give the most express consent to it. Yet a state always suspects aliens of retaining a prejudice in favor of their home country, and they are never as much relied upon as free-born subjects ². English law is so much influenced by this principle that no alien can hold a position in the government, even if he should be naturalized by an act of parliament ³. Besides, if such a contract were assumed, why should the state require an oath of allegiance whenever a man enters any office? For if they assumed a previous contract, what reason would there be for renewing it? A breach of allegiance or high treason is a much greater crime, and more severely punished in all nations, than a breach of contract, which only requires fulfillment. They must, therefore, be on a different footing; the lesser contract can by no means involve the greater one. Contract, therefore, is not the principle of obedience to civil government; rather, it is the principles of authority and utility explained previously.

### [§ 2. *On the Nature of Government and its Progress in the First Ages of Society.*]

We shall now endeavor to explain the nature of government, its different forms, what circumstances gave rise to it, and by what means it is maintained.

The forms of government, however various, may be appropriately reduced to these three: monarchical, aristocratic, and democratic. These may be blended in a great number of ways, and we usually name the government according to whichever form prevails.

Monarchical government is where the supreme power and authority is vested in one person who can do as he pleases—making peace and war, imposing taxes, and the like.

Aristocratic government is where a certain class of people in the state, consisting either of the wealthiest or of specific families, have the power to choose magistrates who are to manage the state.

Democratic government is where the management of affairs belongs to the whole body of the people together.

These last two forms may be called republican, and thus the division of government is into monarchical and republican.

To acquire a proper understanding of government, it is necessary to consider its first form and observe how the other forms arose from it.

<image-desc>A handwritten ink bracket in the left margin marks the beginning of the following paragraph.</image-desc>

In a nation of hunters, there is strictly no government at all¹. The society consists of a few independent families

those who live in the same village and speak the same language, and have agreed among themselves to stay together for their mutual safety, but they have no authority over one another. The whole society interests itself in any offense; if possible they settle the matter between the parties; if not, they banish from their society, kill, or deliver up to the anger of the injured person the one who has committed the crime ¹.

But this is no regular government, for though there may be some among them who are much respected and have great influence in their decisions, yet a leader can never do anything without the consent of the whole.

Thus among hunters there is no regular government; they live according to the laws of nature.

The appropriation of herds and flocks, which introduced an inequality of wealth, was what first gave rise to regular government ². Until there is property, there can be no government, the very purpose of which is to secure wealth and to defend the rich from the poor ³. <insert>]</insert> <insert>[</insert> In this age of shepherds, if one man possessed 500 oxen and another had none at all, unless there were some government to secure them to him, he would not be allowed to possess them. <header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

<note>¹ *The Wealth of Nations*, Book V, Chapter I, Part 2, Vol. II, pp. 294–295.</note>
...came to have great influence over the rest; and accordingly, we find in the Old Testament that Abraham, Lot, and the other patriarchs were like little petty princes. It is to <insert>[be]</insert> observed that this inequality of wealth in a nation of shepherds caused greater influence than in any period after that. Even at present, a man may spend a large estate and yet acquire no dependents. Arts and manufactures are increased by it, but it may make very few people dependent. In a nation of shepherds, it is quite otherwise. They have no possible means of spending their property—having no domestic luxury—except by giving it as gifts to the poor; and by this means, they attain such influence over them as to make them, in a sense, their slaves¹.

We come now to explain how one man came to have more authority than the rest, and how chieftains were introduced. A nation consists of many families who have met together and agreed to live with one another. At their public meetings, there will always be one person of superior influence to the rest, who will in large measure direct and govern their resolutions; this is the extent of the authority of a chieftain in a barbarous country. As the chieftain is the leader of the nation, his son naturally becomes the chief of the young people, and upon the death of his father, he succeeds to his authority. Thus, chieftainship becomes hereditary. This power of chieftainship comes to be increased by a variety of circumstances during the progress of society. The number of gifts he receives increases his wealth and, consequently, his authority; for among barbarous nations, nobody approaches the chieftain or makes any application for his favor without bringing something in his hand. In a civilized nation, the person who gives a gift is superior to the person who receives it, but in a barbarous nation, the case is exactly the opposite.

We shall now consider the different powers which naturally belong to government, how they are distributed, and what their progress is during the early periods of society.

The powers of government are three: namely, the legislative, which makes laws for the public good; the judicial, or that which obliges private individuals to obey these laws and punishes those who disobey; and the executive, or as some call it, the federal power, to which belongs the making of war and peace¹.

All these powers, in the original form of government, belonged to the whole body of the people. It was indeed a long time before legislative power was introduced, as it is the highest exercise of government to make laws and lay down rules that bind not only ourselves, but also our descendants and those who never gave any consent to their creation. As for the judicial power: when two persons quarreled with each other, the whole society naturally intervened, and when they could not resolve the matter, they expelled them from the society. During this early age, crimes were few², and it was a long time before the punishment was made equal to the crime.

Cowardice and treason were the first crimes punished; for cowardice among hunters is considered treason because, when they went out in small numbers, if an enemy attacked them and some of their party deserted them, the rest might suffer for it. Therefore, those who deserted were punished for treason.

The priest generally inflicted the punishment, as if by the command of the gods—so weak was government at that time. The power of making peace and war likewise belonged to the people, and all the heads of families were consulted about it.

Though the judicial power which concerns individuals

<columns>2</columns>

...was long precarious; society first intervened as friends and then as arbitrators, but the executive power very soon came to be exercised absolutely. When any private quarrel happens concerning the property of this cow or of that ox, society is not immediately concerned, but it is deeply interested in making peace and war. In the age of shepherds, this power is absolutely exercised. In Great Britain, we can observe vestiges of the precariousness of judicial power, but none regarding executive power. When a criminal was brought to trial, he was asked how he would choose his case to be decided—whether by combat, the ordeal trial, or the laws of his country. Society only required him not to disturb them during the decision. In England, the question still remains, although the answer is no longer a matter of choice¹. It was very common in more primitive ages to demand a trial by dipping their hands in boiling water; by this means, almost everyone was found innocent, though now hardly anyone would escape by this method. When people were constantly exposed to the weather, boiling water could have little effect on them; however, now that we are quite covered, it must have the opposite effect². This choice of trial shows the weakness of judicial laws. We find that judicial combat continued in England as late as the days of Queen Elizabeth³. It has since worn out gradually and imperceptibly, without so much as a law or a rule of court being made against it. In the periods of hunters and fishers, and in that of shepherds, as was previously observed, crimes are few; minor crimes passed without any notice. In those ages, no controversies arose from the interpretation of wills, settlements, or contracts, which make our lawsuits so numerous,

for these were unknown among them. When these occurred and complex trades began to be practiced, controversies became more frequent; however, as men were generally employed in one branch of trade or another, they could not spare the time to attend to them without great detriment to themselves. All cases would have to be left undecided, which would cause every kind of inconvenience, or they had to find some other method more suitable to the various members of society. The natural method they would settle on would be to choose some of their number to whom all cases should be referred. The chieftain, who before this was distinguished by his superior influence, would maintain his usual precedence when this occurred, and would naturally be one of those chosen for this purpose. A certain number would be chosen to sit with him, and in the early stages of society, this number was always large¹. They would be afraid to trust important matters to a few people, and accordingly, we find that in Athens there were 500 judges at the same time². By this means, the chieftain would further increase his authority, and the government would appear to some degree monarchical. But this is only in appearance, for the final decision still rests with the whole body of the people, and the government is truly democratic.

The power of making peace and war, as previously observed, was at first held by the whole body of the people. But as society advanced and towns were fortified, military storehouses prepared, funds collected, and generals and officers appointed, the whole body of the people could not attend to deliberations of this kind. This province would either fall to the court of justice, or

<note>² *Wealth of Nations, book 5, ch. 1, part 3, art. 2, vol. 2, pp. 361, 362.*</note>

It explains how the rise of fortified towns and professional military management shifted governance away from direct public deliberation toward specialized institutions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of government functions, specifically the emergence of senatorial power for administrative tasks and the social and military characteristics of early societies like hunters and shepherds.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>PART I: JUSTICE</header>

...another group of people would be appointed for this purpose, though it would naturally fall to the court of justice at first. This is properly called the senatorial power, which in Rome managed the public revenue, public buildings, and similar matters. Later in Rome, however, the court of justice and the senatorial court became quite distinct. The same can be said of the Areopagite court in Athens.

We will now make some observations on nations in the first two stages of society: specifically, hunters and shepherds.

In a nation of hunters and fishers, few people can live together; in a short time, any significant number of people would destroy all the game in the country and, consequently, would lack the means of subsistence. At most, twenty or thirty families can live together, and these constitute a village. Since they live together for mutual defense and to assist one another, their villages are not far apart. When a dispute arises between people from different villages, it is decided by a general assembly of both villages. Just as each individual village has its own leader, there is also one person who serves as the leader of the entire nation. The nation consists of an alliance of the different villages, and the chieftains have great influence over their decisions, especially among shepherds. In no era is an ancient family lineage more respected than in this one. The principle of authority is very powerful, and they have a very keen sense of the usefulness of maintaining law and government.

The difference in the conduct of these nations during peace and war is worth observing.

The exploits of hunters, though brave and gallant, are never very significant. Because few of them can march together, their numbers seldom exceed 200 men, and even these cannot be supported for more than fourteen days. Therefore, there is very little danger from a nation of hunters; our colonies are very afraid of them without any just cause.

<vocab>senatorial power, Rome, Areopagite court, Athens, hunters, shepherds, fishers, subsistence, village, chieftains, law, government</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the separation of administrative "senatorial power" from judicial functions and describes the social structure of hunter-gatherer societies, which are limited to small villages due to food constraints and present little military threat.</summary>
<keywords>senatorial power, Ancient Rome, Areopagite court, hunter-gatherer societies, social evolution, tribal leadership, military capacity, subsistence</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of early societies, contrasting the military potential of hunters and shepherds and explaining how nomadic life facilitates mass migrations.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<columns>2</columns>
<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE</header>

They may indeed cause them some trouble by their raids and excursions, but they can never be very formidable¹. On the other hand, a much larger number of shepherds can live together. There may be a thousand families in the same village. The Arabs and Tartars, who have always been shepherds, have on many occasions caused the most terrible havoc. A Tartar chief is extremely formidable, and when one of them defeats another, the most terrible and violent revolutions always occur. They take their entire flocks and herds into the field along with them, and whoever is defeated loses both his people and his wealth. The victorious nation follows its flocks and pursues its conquest, and if it enters a cultivated country with such a large number of men, it is quite irresistible. It was in this manner that Mahomet ravaged all of Asia².

There is a very great difference between barbarous nations and those that are somewhat civilized. Where the land is not divided, and the people live in huts which they carry about with them, they can have no attachment to the soil, as all their property consists of livestock which they can easily carry about with them. For this reason, barbarous nations are always prepared to leave their country. Thus, we find such migrations among the Helvetii, Teutones, and Cimbrians. The Huns, who lived for a long time on the north side of the Chinese wall, drove out the Astrogoths on the other side of the Palus Maeotis; they in turn drove out the Wisigoths, etc.

### [§ 3. *How Republican Governments were introduced.*]

Having considered the original principles of government, and its progress in the first ages of society, having found

<meta>This page follows the discussion of shepherd societies, continuing the analysis of how the geography of regions like Tartary and Arabia prevents social "improvement," and transitions into why republican governments emerged in the distinct geography of Greece and Europe.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>PART I: JUSTICE</header>

...it generally to be democratic, we now come to consider how republican governments were introduced.

It is observed in general that the geography of a country, and the degree of improvement it is capable of—not only in the farming of the land but in other branches of trade—is favorable to the introduction of a republican government. There is little probability that any such government will ever be introduced into Tartary or Arabia, because the nature of those countries is such that they cannot be improved. Most of these areas consist of hills and deserts that cannot be cultivated and are only suitable for grazing. Besides, they are generally dry and lack any significant rivers¹. The opposite is the case in those countries where republican governments have been established, and particularly in Greece. Two-thirds of Attica is surrounded by sea, and the other side is bordered by a ridge of high mountains. Through this, they have communication with their neighboring countries by sea and, at the same time, are secured from the raids of their neighbors. Most European countries share many of these same advantages. They are divided by rivers and arms of the sea and are naturally suited for the cultivation of the soil and other arts. We shall now see how favorable this is to the adoption of a republican government. We may suppose the progress of government in Attica during the infancy of society to have been much the same as in Tartary and the other countries we have mentioned; we actually find that at the time of the Trojan War it was in much the same situation, for then there was little or no cultivation of the ground, and cattle were the main part of their property. All property disputes in Homer concern cattle². Here, as in every other country during the same period, the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the transition from chieftainship to republican government, specifically focusing on the role of trade and fortified towns in Greece.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

The influence of the chieftain over his own vassals was very significant. When the division of land began to take place and its cultivation became generally practiced, a people inhabiting such a country would naturally sell the surplus of their products to their neighbors, and this would act as a spur to their industry. But at the same time, it would be a temptation to their neighbors to raid them. They must, therefore, find some method to secure themselves from danger and to preserve what had previously cost them so much trouble to obtain. It would be easier to fortify a town in a convenient location than to fortify the borders of the entire country, and accordingly, this was the method they chose. They built fortified towns in the most convenient places, and whenever they were invaded, they took shelter in them with their flocks and movable goods; here, they cultivated the arts and sciences. Consistent with this, we find that Theseus fortified Athens and required the people of Attica to bring all their goods into it¹, which not only increased his power over them but also the authority of that state above others. When people agreed to live in towns in this manner, the chieftains of the various clans would soon lose their authority, and the government would become republican. This occurred because their income was small and could not make them so prominent and distinguished above others as to keep the people dependent on them. The citizens gradually increased in wealth and, coming nearer to the level of the chieftain, became² jealous of his authority. Accordingly, we find that Theseus himself was turned out. After this, nine regents were established who were at first to have authority for life, but were afterwards continued only for ten years³. Thus Athens, and in

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of government in Greek states, moving from chieftainships to republics, and explores the role of economic changes and slavery in shaping ancient and modern aristocracies.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>
<columns>2</columns>

In the same way, all the Greek states moved from a chieftainship to something like a monarchy, and from there to an aristocracy. In general, as was previously observed, the revenue becomes insufficient to support the authority of many chieftains; instead, a few individuals, acquiring superior wealth, form an aristocracy.

<margin>L</margin>
It should be noted that there is a considerable difference between ancient and modern aristocracies. In the modern republics of Venice, Milan, etc., the state government is entirely in the hands of the hereditary nobility, who possess all three powers of government. In both modern and ancient aristocracies, the people chose those in authority, but the difference is this: in modern times, only the nobility could be elected. The institution of slavery is the cause of this difference. When free men had all their work done by slaves, they had the ability to attend public deliberations; but when the land came to be cultivated by free men, the lower classes did not have the ability to attend, and, acting in their own interest, they would endeavor to avoid it. Consistent with this, we find that in Venice the populace desired to be free of it. Similarly, the towns in Holland voluntarily gave it up to the town council, which was, as a result, vested with all the power.

Nothing like this happened in the republics of Greece and Rome. In the early ages of these states, although the populace held all the power, they were called aristocracies because they always chose their magistrates from among the nobility. Indeed, they were not hindered by any specific law to do otherwise, but it was customary

<column-break/>

The phrase "continued them in their government only for ten years" occurs in that work, volume 1, page 13.

# PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE

to do so, because the lower classes were supported by the wealth of the rich and thus became dependent on them, giving their vote to the person whose generosity they shared. The nobility might disagree among themselves about elections, but they would never suggest electing commoners. Thus, the influence of the nobility functioned as the law, rather than any explicit prohibition.

In Athens, Solon decreed that none of the lower of the four classes into which the people were divided should be elected; however, later, magistrates were elected from all classes, and the government became democratic<note>Potter, *Archaeologia Graeca*, vol. i. pp. 14, 16.</note>.

In Rome, it took a long time before the eligibility for election was extended to the entire body of the people. After the <term>decemvirs</term> were appointed, the power of the people began to encroach more and more upon the nobles, and even more so when they succeeded in electing <term>military tribunes</term>. The cause of this was the improvement of arts and manufacturing. When a man becomes able to spend on personal luxury what previously supported a hundred dependents, his power and influence naturally decrease. Furthermore, the elite usually had every trade practiced by their own slaves; therefore, the tailors and shoemakers, no longer being dependent on them, would not give them their votes. The popular leaders then tried to have laws passed so that they could be allowed to be elected as magistrates. It was a long time before even the majority of the <term>plebeians</term> would agree to this, because they found it unpleasant to have their equals raised so far above them<note>Livy, *History*, book iv. chapter 25.</note>. Over time, however, they passed a law stating that there should be an equal number of <term>patricians</term> and <term>plebeians</term> in authority—namely, a <term>consul</term> chosen from each group.

### [§ 4. *How Liberty was lost.*]

We have shown how republics arose and how they became democratic; next, we will show how this liberty was lost and how monarchy, or something similar, was introduced.
<margin>v</margin>
Considering these states in the situation described above—possessing their towns and a small territory in the surrounding countryside—they must either confine themselves within their original boundaries or expand their territory through conquest. They must be what could appropriately be called either a defensive republic or a conquering one. The Greek states are a good example of the former, while Rome and Carthage represent the latter. We will show how each of these lost their liberty, starting with how the defensive states lost theirs.

When a country reaches a certain level of refinement, it becomes less suited for war. When the arts reach a certain degree of improvement, the population increases, yet the number of fighting men decreases. In a society of shepherds, the entire nation can go to war; even when it becomes more refined and the division of labor occurs, and everyone owns a small farm, they can still send out a large number of soldiers. In such an age, their campaigns are always conducted in summer; from planting time until harvest, their young men have nothing to do but serve in the military. All the work at home can be performed by the old men and women, and even they have sometimes defeated the enemy in the absence of their soldiers. In a state where the arts are practiced, and which consists mainly of <term>manufacturers</term><note>This word is used (as always by Adam Smith) in its older, more literal sense, rather than its modern sense of "people who employ others to make things using machinery."</note>, such large numbers cannot be sent out,

<summary>This page discusses the transition of republics into monarchies, distinguishing between defensive states like Greece and conquering states like Rome, and notes how economic refinement reduces a nation's military readiness.</summary>
<keywords>Adam Smith, republics, liberty, monarchy, defensive republic, conquering republic, refinement, division of labor, manufacturers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that as a society develops "arts" (industry and specialized trades), it becomes less capable of fielding a large citizen militia because workers cannot be spared from their specific tasks without halting production.</meta>

because if a weaver or tailor is called away, nothing is done in his absence. Scarcely one in a hundred can be spared from Britain and Holland¹ <note>Wealth of Nations, book 5, chapter 1, part 1, volume 2, pages 276-279.</note>. Out of a hundred inhabitants, fifty are women, and of the fifty men, twenty-five are unfit for war. In the last war, Britain could not spare even that many, as almost anyone might be convinced if he reflects on whether he missed one out of twenty-five among his acquaintances. Based on this principle, Athens, though a small state, could once send out 30,000 fighting men, which made a very significant impact; but after the improvement of arts, they could not send out more than 10,000, which was quite insignificant. Britain—despite the civilization and refinement at which it has arrived—can still send out a very formidable army because of the size of its territories² <note>Specifically, 'compared with those of the Greek states.'</note>, but a small state necessarily declines. However, there is one advantage to having slavery in a small republic, which seems to be its only advantage: it slows their decline. In Rome and Athens, the trades were carried out by slaves, and the Lacedaemonians went so far as to forbid any freeman from being trained in <term>mechanic employments</term><note>Manual trades or industrial labor.</note>, because they imagined that such work harmed the body. Accordingly, we find that at the battle of Chaeronea, when the Athenians had reached a high degree of refinement, they were still able to send out great numbers of men specifically because all trades were carried out by slaves. We may observe that in the Italian republics, where slavery did not exist, they soon lost their liberty. When a state has become wealthy as a consequence of the improvement of arts, it must be considered a great hardship to go out to war, whereas among our ancestors, it was thought to be no inconvenience to take the field. A knight (*eques*) was nothing more than a horseman, and a foot-soldier was a gentleman. They were accustomed to hardships at home, and therefore a campaign

But when wealth and luxury increased, the rich would not take the field except for the most urgent reasons; therefore, it became necessary to employ mercenaries and the lowest classes of people to serve in war. Such people could never be trusted in war unless they were organized into a standing army and subjected to rigid discipline, because they had little personal interest in the outcome. Therefore, without such treatment, they could not be expected to be very determined in their efforts. Gentlemen may carry on a war without much discipline, but a mob can never do so. Since the citizens in Greece thought it beneath them to bear arms and entrusted the republic to mercenaries, their military strength declined. Consequently, this provided a way for the government to fall. Another cause of their decline was the improvement of the art of war, which made everything uncertain. In early times, it was very difficult to capture a city, as it could only be done through a long blockade. The siege of Troy lasted ten years, and Athens was once able to withstand a siege by both land and sea for two years¹. In modern times, the besiegers have an advantage over the besieged, and a skilled engineer can force almost any town to surrender in six weeks. But it was not always this way. Philip of Macedon made great improvements in this art, which ultimately led to the dissolution of all the Greek governments and their subjection to foreign powers. Rome held out much longer than Greece because the number of its citizens was increasing daily. In Rome, any person could be made a citizen, as this provided little direct benefit. But in Athens, the right of citizenship was granted to very few, because being a citizen was itself like owning a small estate. However, after wealth and luxury increased, Rome itself shared the fate of other republics, although this happened in a different way. Until the time of Marius, the higher class of free men went out

That army, which previously had consisted of gentlemen, was now composed of runaway slaves and the lowest classes of people. With such an army, Marius conquered the provinces and kept them in awe. He had the authority to assign all offices and posts within this army. Every man among them owed his advancement to him and was, consequently, dependent upon him.

Whenever such a general was insulted, he would naturally turn to his army for support, and the soldiers would easily be persuaded to side with their general against their own nation. This was the very tactic that Marius adopted. Through the influence of Sylla <note>Lucius Cornelius Sulla</note>, he was banished from Rome in his absence, and a price was set upon his head. Marius turned to his army, who were determined to follow him at all costs. He marched to Rome while Sylla was away on an expedition against Mithridates, took control of the government, and defeated Sylla’s faction. Marius died shortly after, and Sylla, having conquered Mithridates, returned to Rome. In turn, he defeated the Marian faction, transformed the government into a monarchy, and made himself perpetual Dictator, though he later had the generosity and greatness of spirit to resign the position. About thirty or forty years later, the same thing occurred between Caesar and Pompey. Caesar, like Sylla, had himself made perpetual Dictator, but he lacked enough public spirit to resign. His veteran troops, who were settled in Italy and remained mindful of the favors he had granted them, gathered around his adopted son Octavius after his death and invested him with supreme authority. A very similar thing happened in our own country regarding Oliver Cromwell. When Parliament became suspicious of this man and disbanded the army, he appealed to the soldiers in a manner that was indeed more sanctimonious than that of the Roman generals; he had the Parliament dismissed and a new

Thus, we have seen how small republics, whether aggressive or defensive, eventually reached a dissolution due to improvements in the mechanical arts, commerce, and the arts of war.

### [§ 5. *Of Military Monarchy*.]

We are next to consider which form of government followed the republican one.

When small republics were conquered by another state, a monarchy—or whatever other form of government pleased the conquerors—was established, though they generally followed the model of their own country. The Athenians always established democracy, and the Spartans aristocracy. The Romans, indeed, more prudently divided their conquests into provinces, which were governed quite absolutely by individuals appointed by the Senate for that purpose. The case is somewhat different when a state is conquered by its own subjects. Both the nature of the action and the means by which it is performed require a military monarchy, or a monarchy supported by military force, because it is as necessary to keep the subjects in awe as it is to conquer them. This was the form of government established in Rome during the time of the emperors. These emperors took all executive power into their own hands; they made peace and war as they saw fit, and even named the magistrates, either directly themselves or through a Senate of their own appointment. They did not, however, alter any institutions of the civil law; matters of right and wrong were decided as they had been previously. Cromwell did the same in our own country; he kept the state in awe with an insignificant army, but he allowed the

---
¹ These facts are dealt with more briefly in the *Wealth of Nations*, book 5, chapter 1, part 1, volume 2, page 290.

<summary>This page explains how the decline of small republics led to the rise of military monarchies, specifically examining how Rome and Oliver Cromwell used military force to maintain absolute executive power while leaving civil law largely intact.</summary>
<keywords>Military Monarchy, Republics, Roman Empire, Athens, Sparta, Senate, Civil Law, Oliver Cromwell, Wealth of Nations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of military monarchies, specifically comparing the administration of justice under historical figures like Oliver Cromwell and the Roman emperors to the military governments of Asia.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE</header>

allowed a judge to determine right and wrong as formerly. Indeed, he made such improvements in the civil law by abolishing wardships, etc., that the first thing the Parliament of Charles II did was to confirm many of Cromwell’s laws.

The Roman authors tell us that justice was never better administered than under the worst of the emperors, Domitian and Nero¹. It is in the interest of all new administrators to make few alterations in what the general public is concerned with and has long been accustomed to. People will more easily accept anything else when they are indulged in this. It was particularly in the interest of the emperors to maintain the ancient system of laws, and accordingly we find that all consuls who misbehaved in their respective provinces were severely punished. This was not the case under the republic; the most scandalous crimes were committed by governors, as we learn from Cicero’s Orations. A military government allows for the strictest administration of justice. Indeed, no one can have a fair trial where the Emperor is directly concerned; then he will do as he pleases. But where he is in no way interested, it is in his interest to adhere to the ancient laws.

It should be observed that there was a very great difference between the military government established at Rome and those established in Asia. At Rome, the conquerors and conquered were the same people. The conquerors themselves were aware of the good effects of these laws and were so far from being willing to abolish them that they made improvements upon them. It is not so with the Asian governments, though they are purely military. Turkey, Persia, and other countries were conquered by Tartars, Arabs, and other barbarous nations who had no regular system of laws and were entirely ignorant of their good effects. They established in all public offices their own people, who were entirely ignorant

of all their duties. A Turkish bashaw or other inferior officer is the decisive judge of everything and is as absolute in his own jurisdiction as the Signior. Life and fortune are completely precarious when they depend in this way on the whim of the lowest magistrate. A more miserable and oppressive government cannot be imagined.

### [§ 6. *How Military Monarchy was Dissolved.*]

We have considered how the dissolution of small states was brought about, what form of government succeeded them, by what means an imperial government was introduced into conquering republics, and what kind of administration this was. We now come to show how this military monarchy came to share that fated dissolution that awaits every state and constitution whatsoever.

In the time of the imperial governments at Rome, they had arrived at a considerable degree of improvement in both arts and commerce. In proportion as people become acquainted with these and their consequence, domestic luxury, they become less fond of going out to war; and besides, the government finds that it would hurt its revenue [to call out] those employed in manufacturing. If barbarous nations are in the neighborhood, they can employ them as soldiers at a lower cost, and at the same time not hurt their own industry. Aware of these things, the Romans recruited their armies in Germany, Britain, and the northern barbarous countries which bordered on the Roman Empire. They had the liberty of recruiting in these countries in the same manner that the Dutch did in Scotland before the beginning of the last war <note>1</note>. After they had gone

on for some time in this practice, they would find for several reasons that it would be much easier to make a bargain with the chieftains of these barbarous nations whom they employed, and give them a certain amount of money to lead a number of men on this or that expedition.

Supposing then an institution of this kind, the barbarous chieftain, at the head of his men, possessed the whole military authority of the people for whom he fought. Whenever the government offended him in the least, he could turn his weapons against those who employed him and make himself master of their country. We find that all the western provinces were taken over in much this way. After they had, through their practice, given such invitations for the inroad of barbarians, we find that most of the Roman provinces were infested by them. In this country [Britain], the Romans built a wall and kept garrisons to secure their province from the pillagers of the north. The garrisons which secured this station were called away for the defense of Gaul, which at that time was also infested. The historians tell us that the Britons then received permission to shake off the Roman yoke, but it could be no advantage to the Romans to give any country in Britain its liberty, and it was no favor to the country to have no protection from Rome, which the province in reality wanted to continue. The Romans undoubtedly meant that the Britons should take the trouble of defending themselves, as the Romans were, for some time, to be otherwise employed. The Britons, however, did not like the proposal, but resolved to invite over a body of Saxons for their relief. Accordingly, Hengist and Hursa came over with a considerable army which was frequently reinforced; they entirely drove out the Romans¹, and

<column-break/>

commissions in the Scotch Brigade, in the service of the States General of the United Provinces, to take the oaths of allegiance and abjuration.'

Finding themselves masters of the whole country, they took possession of it and founded the Saxon Heptarchy. In this manner, the Western Empire of Europe fell, and military monarchy came to ruin. In the last hundred and fifty years of the Roman Empire, we find this custom of recruiting from barbarian nations being carried on, and many of their chieftains had greatly elevated themselves. Patricius Ælias¹ under Honorius, and many others, acquired great power. In the same way, all the Asian governments were dissolved. Their soldiers were hired from Tartary; while arts and manufactures were developed, the people earned more from their trades than by going to war. The East India trade, which Italy and some other nations carried on by way of the Red Sea, had made them very wealthy. Every nation, as well as Rome, was willing to make a bargain with neighboring barbarian princes to defend them, and this proved to be the ruin of those governments.

Having now considered all the ancient forms of government for which we have any clear accounts, we will next show what form succeeded the fall of the Roman Empire and provide an account of the origin of the modern governments of Europe.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the transition from the fall of the Roman Empire to the establishment of modern European government structures, focusing specifically on the nature of post-Roman land division and the rise of the allodial system.</meta>

<sig>D 2</sig>

not entirely unlike the Tartar constitution mentioned earlier, although the Germans and others who took possession of the western countries after the fall of the Roman Empire had better concepts of property and were slightly more accustomed to the division of lands. The king and the other chieftains, after they had become conquerors of the country, would naturally take possession of a great part of it for their own purposes. They would distribute it among their vassals and dependents as they saw fit, and would leave only a very insignificant share to the original inhabitants. However, they did not wipe them out entirely, but still showed them some small consideration. Among the Franks who took possession of Gaul, a person who killed a Frank paid only five times the fine that was required for killing one of the former inhabitants<note>For a Frank 200 sous, for a Roman serf 45; Montesquieu, *The Spirit of the Laws*, book 28, chapter 3; book 30, chapter 25.</note>. As these nations were almost lawless and under no authority, raids were continually committed throughout the country, and all types of trade ceased.

As a result of this, the allodial government arose, which introduced an inequality of wealth. All these chieftains held their lands allodially, without any burden of tax, wardship, and so on. One of these great lords might possess almost an entire county; however, because he was unable to benefit from so much of it himself, he found it necessary to divide it among vassals, who either paid a certain yearly payment, accompanied him in war, or performed some service of this nature. Through this means, his income became so great that—since there was then no domestic luxury—he could only consume it by maintaining a large number of retainers in his household. These were another type of dependents who increased his authority and ensured domestic peace, for they kept the tenants in awe and were themselves kept in awe by the tenants. So great was the authority of these lords that if anyone claimed a debt from

This period was marked by lawlessness and a significant legal distinction between the conquerors and the original inhabitants.</summary>

any of their vassals, the king had no power to send
a messenger into the lord’s territory to force payment ¹.
He could only apply to the lord and request that he do
justice. They also held the final authority ² in judging all
types of property under their own jurisdiction, the power
of life and death, the coining of money, and the power
of making bylaws and regulations in their own territories.
But besides this power of government, which was largely shared
between the king and the great lords, the balance
would not have been properly maintained if there had been no other power.
However, in addition to the allodial lords, there were many
free people who were allowed to deliberate on justice
within their own spheres. Every county was divided into
hundreds and subdivided into tens. Each of these had
their respective court, namely: the decennary court and the hundred
court. Above these was placed the Wittenagemot, or the as-
sembly of the whole people ³. Appeals were brought from
the ten to the hundred, and from there to the county court.
An appeal could be brought to the king’s court if
the inferior court denied justice by refusing to hear
a case, or if it was drawn out by unreasonable delays ⁴.
Appeals were also sometimes carried to the Wittenagemot,
which was composed of the king, allodial lords, aldermen
or earls, bishops, abbots, etc. This was the first form of
government in Western Europe after the fall of
the Roman Empire.

one another; in order to ensure the attendance of their tenants, they gave them leases for the lands they possessed from year to year, which later, for the same reasons, came to be held for life¹.

When they were about to engage in any very dangerous enterprise, so that, in case of the worst outcome, the families of the vassals who accompanied them would not be left destitute, and so that they might be even more encouraged to follow them, they extended this right to the lives of the son and grandson. As it was thought cruel to evict an old possessor, the right eventually became hereditary and was called <term>feuda</term>². The feudal tenant was bound to certain duties, but service in war was the chief requirement; if the heir was not able to perform it, he was obliged to appoint someone in his place. It was in this manner that <term>wardships</term> were introduced³. When a female heir succeeded, the feudal baron had a right to marry her to whomever he pleased, because it was thought reasonable that he should have a vassal of his own choosing⁴. The <term>prima seizin</term> was another benefit for the lord. When the father died, the son had no right to the estate until he publicly declared his willingness to accept it; and for this reason, the lord sometimes held the estate in his own

hand, and enjoyed its profits for some time. The heir paid a sum to recover it, which was called **relief** <note>Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, pp. 49-59.</note>. There was still another benefit belonging to the lord, called **escheat**; that is, after the estate became hereditary, if there was no heir of the family to succeed to it, the land returned to the lord. The same thing happened if the heir failed to perform the services for which he held the tenure <note>Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, pp. 66-67.</note>. There were, besides these, some small sums due to the superior lord for ransoming him when he was taken prisoner, for the knighting of his eldest son <note>The original text says 'on knighting him,' but as the third footnote indicates, this should read 'for redeeming him when taken prisoner, or on the knighting of his eldest son.'</note>, and for the marriage of his daughter, and on other similar occasions <note>Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, p. 61.</note>.

The same causes that led allodial lords to give away their lands to their vassals on leases—which afterwards became hereditary—led the king to give away the greater part of his lands to be held feudally; and what a tenant possessed as a <term>feu</term> was much the same as real property. They were indeed subject to the above-mentioned payments, but they possessed their lands for themselves and their descendants. Feudal property may in some respects be inferior to allodial, but the difference is so insignificant that allodial lordships soon came to be held feudally. Around the tenth century, all estates came to be held feudally, and the allodial lords, so that they might enjoy the king’s protection, exchanged their rights for a feudal tenure <note>Hume, *History of England (Caesar to Henry VII)*, vol. i. p. 400.</note>.

It is to be observed that those historians who give an account of the origin of feudal laws as resulting from the usurpation of the nobility are quite mistaken <note>*Wealth of Nations*, book iii. chapter iv. volume i. p. 414.</note>. They say that the nobility wanted the lands which they held at the king's pleasure to be hereditary, so that it might not be in his power to evict them, and that the feudal law was introduced

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transition from allodial to feudal systems, moving from the personal motivations of lords to the broader political implications for royal power and the structure of government.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>

on account of the reduction of the king's power. But it was actually the opposite; it was on account of the increase of his power, and it required great influence on the part of the king to make the lords hold their lands feudally. The best proof of this is that William the Conqueror changed all the allodial lordships in England into feudal tenures, and Malcolm Canmore$^1$ <note>Canmore.</note> did the same in Scotland$^2$ <note>Lord Kames, *Essays upon British Antiquities*, 1747, pp. 11–17; Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, p. 25.</note>.

The introduction of the feudal system throughout Europe removed everything resembling popular government. The popular courts were all abolished. Neither decennary, hundred, nor county courts were allowed. All public affairs were managed by the king and the great feudal lords. No commoners—none but hereditary lords—had a right to sit in parliament. Those great lords who held land directly from the king were considered his companions, *pares convivii comites* <note>Peers of the feast, or companions.</note>. They provided advice regarding public affairs, and nothing of importance could be done without them. The consent of the majority had to be obtained before any law could be passed, and it was necessary to have them called together. The barons or inferior lords followed the same method in their own jurisdictions, and those who held$^3$ <note>The manuscript reads "them" between "held" and "of them."</note> land from them were called *pares curiae baronis* <note>Peers of the baron's court.</note>. It was also necessary that they should be consulted, as they too were armed men. The baron could neither go to war nor make a law without the consent of the majority. Nothing could be done in the kingdom without almost universal consent, and thus they fell into a kind of aristocracy with the king at the head of it.

Besides these ranks of men we have noted, there were two others which, during that period, were held in the utmost contempt$^4$ <note>Hume, *History of England* (*Caesar to Henry VII*), vol. i. p. 404.</note>. The first was that of the <term>villains</term> (*villani*) who plowed the ground and were

*adscripti glebae* <note>Bound to the soil.</note>. The second order was the inhabitants of boroughs, who were in much the same state of villainage as the former, or only slightly better. As the boroughs were heavily under the influence of the lord who provided them protection, it was in the king’s interest to weaken this influence as much as possible and to favor their liberty. Henry II carried this so far that if a slave escaped to a borough and lived there peacefully for a year and a day, he became free <note>This is attributed to Henry II, probably only because it is mentioned in Glanvill, *De legibus Angliae*, book v. chapter 5. He granted them many other privileges, but what secured them most was the power of forming themselves into corporations upon paying a certain sum to the king. They held land from him *in capite* <note>Directly from the crown.</note> <note>Madox, *Firma burgi*, chapter i. § 8, pp. 21-23.</note>, and at first every man paid his share to the king <note>*Wealth of Nations* book iii. chapter iii. volume i. p. 400.</note>; but afterwards the borough paid the sum and levied it as it saw fit. By this means, as the number of inhabitants increased, the burden became lighter, and the boroughs became wealthy and very significant. In the reign of King John, a law was made that if a lord married his ward to a burgher, he only forfeited his wardship <note>‘King John’ appears to be a mistake for ‘Henry III,’ the reference apparently being to the Statute of Merton, 20 Henry III, chapter 6, ‘As touching lords who marry those that they have in ward to villains or others, such as burgesses, where they are disparaged . . . the lord shall lose the wardship.’</note>.

In Henry III’s time, it was enacted that the smaller barons, who could not afford to attend parliament, should send a representative. These representatives were considered lords and sat in the same house as them². In the same manner, boroughs came to have representatives in parliament because they themselves had become opulent and powerful, and the king found it in his interest to give them some weight so as to lessen the authority of the peers³. It became necessary to have their consent, as well as that of the barons, before any law was passed. These representatives of the boroughs sat in a house by themselves; and the smaller barons—being far below the level of the great lords with whom they sat, and not much superior to the commoners—soon joined them⁴. The king’s revenues were then, on many occasions, insufficient for his demands. They consisted chiefly of: first, the royal demesnes; second, knights’ services; third, feudal emoluments such as wardships; fourth, fines, amercements <note>Financial penalties imposed by a court.</note>, compositions for crimes, etc.; and fifth, all waif⁵ goods, *res nullius* <note>Property belonging to no one.</note>, etc. These were the principal sources of the king’s revenue. But these were by no means sufficient to supply the increasing expenses of government. The two bodies of commoners, when joined, made a very considerable figure, and the greater part of the subsidies came from them. The king excused the smaller barons from constant attendance and called...

### § 10. *How the Government of England Became Absolute.*

Having shown how the House of Commons became significant, we shall next show how the nobility’s power decreased and the government became arbitrary.

In all the courts of Europe, the power of the nobility declined due to common causes: the improvements in arts and commerce. When a man could spend his fortune on personal luxury, he was forced to dismiss his retainers. Through their ancient, simple hospitality, they could more easily maintain one thousand retainers than they can now lodge one lord for a single night. Richard, Earl of Warwick, who was called the "Kingmaker," supported forty thousand people every day in addition to his tenants². But when luxury became common, he was unable to continue this³. Thus, the power of the nobility was diminished, and that happened even before the House of Commons had established its authority; as a result, the king became absolute. Under the House of Tudor, the government was quite arbitrary: the nobility were ruined, and the boroughs lost their power.

It might be expected that the sovereign should also have lost his authority through the improvement of arts and commerce, but a little attention will convince us that the case must have been quite the opposite. A man possessing forty thousand pounds a year, while no one else—

---

¹ Hume, *History of England (Caesar to Henry VII)*, volume 2, page 88.
² "Forty thousand" is likely a mistake for the thirty thousand mentioned in the *Wealth of Nations*, book 3, chapter 4, volume 1, page 411. See Hume, *History of England (Caesar to Henry VII)*, volume 2, pages 361, 362.
³ Andrew Fletcher, *Political Works*, 1737, pages 11-16; Hume, *History of England under the House of Tudor*, 1759, volume 1, page 63.

<summary>This page describes how the rise of commerce and luxury led the nobility to dismiss their retainers, thereby weakening their power and allowing the Tudor monarchs to establish absolute, arbitrary rule. It also notes the transition from feudal obligations to the creation of peers by royal writ.</summary>
<keywords>House of Commons, House of Tudor, nobility, absolute government, arbitrary power, commerce, retainers, Richard Earl of Warwick, Kingmaker, David Hume, Andrew Fletcher</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of how the rise of luxury and commerce shifted the balance of power from the nobility to the crown, resulting in an absolute monarchy in England, and begins to explain the subsequent restoration of liberty.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE</header>

can spend more than a hundred, cannot be affected by the increase of luxury. This is exactly the case of the king. He possesses a million, while none of his subjects can spend more than thirty or forty thousand pounds; therefore, he can spend it in no other way except by maintaining a great number of people. Luxury must, therefore, reduce the authority of the nobility, whose estates are small compared to the king’s; and as his remains unaffected, his power must become absolute. Although this was the case in most European nations, in Germany it was quite different. The monarchy there was elective and, consequently, could never have as much authority. The country is much larger than any other in Europe, and at the dissolution of the feudal government, the nobility—who already possessed considerable fortunes—received more in proportion than the rest. Thus, their estates rose so much higher than those immediately below them that it was impossible for them to spend their wealth on luxury; therefore, they were able to keep a considerable number of retainers. Thus, in Germany, the power of the nobility was preserved, while in England it was utterly destroyed and the king rendered absolute.

### § 11. *How Liberty was Restored*

We have now shown how the government of England became absolute; we shall next consider how liberty was restored and what security the British have for its possession.

The act of Henry VII allowing the nobility to sell their estates had already placed them entirely on a level with the commons. Elizabeth, who always cultivated

It also introduces the shift toward restoring liberty, starting with Henry VII's legislation that allowed the nobility to sell their land, placing them on a level with the commoners.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the English monarchy's power was curtailed and how liberty was restored through the financial dependency of the Crown on Parliament and the unique geographical security of the British Isles.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

popularity, was continually unwilling to impose taxes on her subjects. In order to supply her needs, she sold the royal estates, as she knew that none of her offspring would succeed her¹. Her successors, therefore, standing in need of frequent supplies, were obliged to apply to Parliament. The Commons had now become very influential, as they represented the whole body of the people; and as they knew the king could not do without them, they never granted him anything without in some degree limiting his privileges. At one time they obtained freedom of speech; at another, they had it enacted that their agreement should be necessary for every law. The king, because of his urgent needs, was forced to grant whatever they asked, and thus the authority of Parliament established itself. (A particular advantage which Britain enjoyed after the accession of James I was that, as the British dominions were bounded on every side by the sea, there was no need for a standing army, and consequently the king had no power by which he could intimidate either the people or Parliament.) The 1,200,000² pounds a year which was granted to the king at that time³ might have secured his independence, had not the poor financial management of Charles II rendered him as impoverished as any of his predecessors. His successor was still more dependent, and was forced to leave the throne and the kingdom altogether. This brought in a new family which, as the royal estates were entirely sold off, depended wholly upon taxes and was obliged to court the people for them. (Ever since, the king’s revenue, though much greater than it was then, depends so much on the agreement of Parliament that it never can endanger the liberty of the nation.

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the British government’s financial structure and how the king’s dependency on Parliament for funding ensures the preservation of liberty.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE</header>

The revenues at present consist chiefly of three branches, namely: first, the civil list, which is entirely spent on the maintenance of the royal family and can give the king no influence nor harm the liberty of the subject; secondly, the annual land and malt taxes, which depend entirely on the parliament; thirdly, the funds mortgaged for paying off the public debts, such as the taxes on salt, beer, malt<note>In addition to the annual malt tax mentioned under the second head, there was a perpetual malt tax.</note>, etc., collected by the officers of customs and excise. The king can by no means touch these: they are paid to the Court of Exchequer, which is generally managed by people of influence and integrity who hold their offices for life and are quite independent of the king. Even they can pay nothing except to those appointed by parliament and must receive a formal discharge from the public creditor. The surplus of the mortgages<note>That is, the surplus remaining after the interest on the debt has been paid out of the proceeds of the mortgaged taxes. See Thomas Mortimer, *Every Man His Own Broker*, fifth edition, 1762, pp. 205-207.</note> goes into what is called the sinking fund for paying the public debt, which secures the government in the present family; because if a revolution were to happen, the public creditors, who are men of influence, would lose both their principal and interest. Thus, the nation is quite secure in the management of the public revenue, and in this manner a rational system of liberty has been introduced into Britain. The parliament consists of about 200 peers and 500 commoners. The Commons in a great measure manage all public affairs, as no money bill can originate except in that House. Here is a happy mixture of all the different forms of government, properly restrained, and a perfect security for liberty and property.

There are still some other safeguards for liberty. The judges appointed for the administration of justice are fixed for life and are quite independent of the king.

The king’s ministers are liable to impeachment by the House of Commons for maladministration, and the king cannot pardon them <note>This should read, ‘cannot stay the proceedings by pardoning them.’</note>. The Habeas Corpus Act—by which the king's arbitrary measures to detain a person in prison as long as he pleases are restrained, and by which any judge who refuses to bring a prisoner to trial, if requested, within forty <note>Twenty days is the outside limit. The same mistake occurs again below, Div. iii. § 11.</note> days is rendered incapable of holding any office—is another security for the liberty of the subject. The method of election, and the placing of the power of judging all elections into the hands of the Commons, are also securities for liberty. All these established customs make it impossible for the king to attempt any absolute rule.

Besides all these, the establishment of the courts of justice is another security for liberty. We shall therefore consider the origin of these courts, their history, and their present state.

<column-break/>

### [§ 12. *Of the English Courts of Justice.*]

In England, and indeed in all Europe, after the feudal law was introduced, the kingdom was governed and justice was administered in the same manner as by a baron in his jurisdiction. Just as a steward managed all affairs in the county belonging to the lord, so the Grand Justiciary had the management of all affairs in the kingdom. He appointed sheriffs and other lower officers. He was himself a great lord, and, by the authority of his office, in every country except England he became as powerful as the king. But Edward I saw the danger and had it prevented. All kinds of law, criminal or civil, were determined by the justiciary or king’s court which always accompanied the king: those delays and adjournments in civil suits to which this court

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the evolution of the English judicial system, specifically the transition from the singular authority of the Grand Justiciary to the establishment of distinct courts under Edward I.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE</header>

The inconvenience that always attending the king must have involved gave cause for separating common lawsuits from the king’s court and establishing a Court of Common Pleas for them at Westminster. Criminal cases have always been decided more quickly <note>It was the rule to finish every criminal trial on the day on which it began; Lecky, *History of England*, vol. vi. 1887, p. 252.</note>. One would indeed think that when a person’s life is at stake, the debate should be longer than in any other case; however, resentment is roused in these cases, which leads to a rush to punish. It is of no importance to the spectator how a minor financial matter is decided, but it is by no means the same in criminal cases. When the common pleas were removed, the criminal and financial powers remained connected, and the power and authority of the Grand Justiciary were little diminished. Later, Edward I divided the business of the Justiciary into three different courts, namely:—

->The Court of King’s Bench.<-
->The Court of Exchequer.<-
->The Court of Common Pleas.<-

In the last, all civil lawsuits were tried. In the first, all criminal ones were tried, and it also heard appeals from the Court of Common Pleas. It was called the King’s Bench because the king then frequently sat on it, though this cannot be done now, as it is improper for the king to judge breaches of the king’s peace. The Court of Exchequer judged all matters between the king and his subjects, including debts owed by either party to the other and anything regarding the revenue. The Court of Chancery was originally not a court at all. The Chancellor was no more than a keeper of briefs or writs according to which justice was administered. The circumstances that led to the keeping of these briefs will now be considered.

Edward I abolished the power of the Grand Justiciary <note>Hume, *History of England (Caesar to Henry VII)*, vol. ii. p. 122.</note>. He employed lower-status persons to be judges, generally members of the clergy—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historical development of the English legal system, focusing on the early role of the Chancellor as a keeper of writs and the strict procedural limitations placed upon judges to prevent corruption and arbitrary rulings.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>PART I: JUSTICE</header>

men. As decisions depended on such people, their jurisdictions were exercised very precariously. Accordingly, we find that in both criminal and civil cases they intervened with hesitation—in the former as mediators and in the latter as arbitrators—and they were unwilling to grant justice in cases where they had no precedent from the court of justiciary. For this reason, all the briefs by which the court of justiciary made determinations were preserved. Keeping these seems to have been the original duty of the chancellor. If a person had a lawsuit, he went to the clerk of the court of chancery, who examined the briefs; if he found one that covered your case, justice was administered accordingly. But if one could not be found, you could not obtain justice. Thus, we find that the chancellor was not originally a judge. In Scotland, the office corresponding to the English chancellor is located within the court of session. In England, a brief was sent from the chancellor to the sheriff, by which the person was required to appear before the king’s judges. 

Judges at that time, due to the irregularity and inaccuracy of their proceedings, caused the king great suspicion. On this account, many severe sentences were issued against them; £10,000 was once levied from the judges because of corruption <note>Hume, *History of England (Caesar to Henry VII)*, vol. ii. p. 68; but the amount should be 100,000 marks.</note>. They were therefore strictly restricted to the chancery briefs and were always bound by their records in such a way that they could not be amended in the slightest—not even a misspelled word could be corrected. This level of precision still remains in some cases where it has not been removed by the statutes of amendment; a mere spelling error, even if obvious, has in many cases rendered the whole proceeding void. The judges were therefore restricted to the precise words of the brief or, if there was a statute, to the exact words of it. This was the origin and jurisdiction of the court of chancery.

<summary>This page explains that the English Chancellor was originally a keeper of legal briefs rather than a judge, and describes how judges were strictly bound to the literal text of these documents to prevent corruption and errors.</summary>
