<meta>This page continues the historical overview of the English legal system, specifically moving from the procedural rigidity of early courts and the role of the Chancellor to the subsequent competition for jurisdiction among the various high courts.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE 49</header>
<sig>E</sig>

During the improvement of the law of England, there arose rivalries among the several courts. We shall therefore show how each of them began to extend its power and encroach upon the privileges of another, and how the court of chancery increased its influence. The court of king’s bench, which judged criminal cases and every breach of the king’s peace, was the first that assumed—immediately and prior to an appeal—the right to judge in civil cases and to encroach on the jurisdiction of the court of common pleas. They did this by what is called a writ of error; that is, they alleged the person to be guilty of a trespass. For example, when a man owed £10 and did not come to pay it at the appointed time, an order went out from the king’s bench to examine and find him, assuming that he intended to hide himself, and they punished him for this trespass <note>There is some confusion or omission, as the process described is that followed under a ‘bill of <column-break/> Middlesex,’ not a ‘writ of error.’</note>. At present, an action on a contract can come immediately before the king’s bench. In this manner, this court extended its power, and, being supreme over all, none could encroach upon it.

The court of exchequer brought in civil cases to be tried immediately by them in the following manner: suppose a man owed a sum of money to the king, which it is the business of the court to manage, and the man cannot pay unless his own debtors first pay him; the court took it upon themselves to sue this other man by what is called the *quo minus*—that is, by that which he is rendered less able to pay the king. As the debts of the king were many, and as the profits of the judges arose from sentence money (which was more or less according to the business of the court), they eagerly grasped at this extension of their power. All the courts endeavored, by the speediness of their determinations and the accuracy of their proceedings, to encourage prosecutors to come before them <note>*Wealth of Nations* bk. v. ch. i. pt. 2, vol. ii. pp. 302, 303.</note>.

This competition was driven by the supreme status of certain courts and the fact that judges' incomes were tied to the volume of cases they adjudicated.</summary>

The manner in which the chancellor came to attain his equitable jurisdiction will be addressed next. Following the development of arts and commerce, which led to many lawsuits unheard of before, people suffered a great deal from the law's imperfections. Edward III <note>This should be ‘Edward I.’</note> found that there were many injuries for which no writ nor court statute provided a remedy. Therefore, the parliament allowed that if a person applied to the clerk of chancery and found there was no writ that could provide a remedy, the clerk should look for writs of a similar nature and, from them, compose a new one through which the complainant might obtain redress <note>Statute of Westminster II, 13 Edward I, chapter 24; Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, p. 316.</note>. In this manner, the chancery prescribed rules for the other courts. However, because they designated the writs and the manner of proceeding, this essentially resolved the matter; there was no need to go to any other court, and the chancery took these affairs into its own hands. No appeal could be brought from the courts of king’s bench or common pleas to the chancery, but people applied to it for what the common law could not redress. In this way, the chancellor obtained the power to judge all cases of equity and is consulted in most civil cases, the most important of which is, first, the specific performance of contracts. Under common law, if a person was bound by contract to deliver a piece of land and later refused to do so, he was only required to pay damages, rather than being forced to fulfill the contract specifically. The chancery, which was now regarded as a court of conscience, ordered its specific performance. Secondly, the chancery provided redress for all deceits <note>The reporter’s or copyist’s mistake for some other word, possibly ‘deceits.’</note> and frauds in trust when the common law could not. Since leaving lands to the church deprived the king of the profits arising from

The clergy ordered that they should be left to certain persons who would dispose of them for the benefit of the church, and if they did not fulfill it, then, as it was a fraud in trust, the chancellor allowed the bishop to ensure it was done. In a similar way, when people, in the state of affairs at that time, were obliged to alienate their estates to persons who had no concern in them, the chancellor caused them to be restored. Wills, legacies, and matters of this sort also fell under the equitable decision of the chancellor.

It will be proper, while we are treating of courts, to inquire into the origin of juries. In the beginning of the <term>allodial</term> government, when the various courts had arrived at a very small degree of improvement, and before they had the experience to examine matters thoroughly, whenever any person was brought before them in a legal action depending on his oath, he was obliged to bring twelve <term>compurgators</term> to swear that his oath was true. There are remnants of this at present in actions of debt, where, if the person can bring in a certain number of individuals to swear that his oath is true, he wins the suit. It is to be observed that the imperfection of this way of trial was one of the great causes that gave rise to <term>judicial combat</term>. A nobleman, or indeed any man of spirit, who was defrauded of his right by a set of perjured individuals, would rather choose to combat it in the field and appeal to the judgment of God than leave his cause to them. Henry II first established that the <term>sheriff</term> and a certain number of persons who had the opportunity to be best acquainted with the crime should have the whole affair laid before them, and that the person should be judged by their verdict. The law of England, always the friend of liberty, deserves praise in no instance more than in the careful provision of impartial juries. Those who are chosen must be from near the place where the crime was committed so that they may have an opportunity to be familiar with the facts. A great part of the jury may

He can set aside thirty of their number, and he can challenge them either *per capita*, that is, any single juryman, or any number of them, if he suspects the sheriff of partiality. There may be many minor reasons for suspecting partiality, and the court is the judge of their relevance. Nothing can provide greater security for life, liberty, and property than this institution; the judges are men of integrity, completely independent, holding their offices for life, yet they are bound by the law. The jurymen are your neighbors who are to judge a fact upon which your life depends. They too can be set aside for several reasons.

The laws of England regarding juries are only defective in one respect, in which they differ from the laws of Scotland. In England, the entire jury must be unanimous, which makes the role of a juryman a very unpleasant service. A case may appear clearer to you than it does to me, and it may actually be different from how it appears to either of us; yet, agreement is necessary, and consequently, there is a necessity that one of us should swear contrary to our conscience. In criminal cases, there is little danger, as people are generally disposed to favor innocence and to preserve life. But in civil cases, people are not as troubled; they are not as inclined to show favor, and many of them are extremely uncertain. People of fashion are not fond of involving themselves in a jury accompanied by such inconveniences, and therefore only people of lower social standing attend the judge. A prominent man would not choose to be called and summoned so often, and perhaps treated in a manner that no gentleman would find acceptable. In this instance, the law providing for security has gone too far. In this country, where unanimity is not required, the service is not so unpleasant. Even if a person differs from the majority, he may stand by his opinion and is not forced to comply, and the

Besides the courts that have been mentioned, several others were established by the <term>king’s patent</term>. <term>Henry VIII</term> established three: the <term>Court of High Commission</term>, which presided over <term>ecclesiastics</term>; the <term>Court of Star Chamber</term>, which deals with anything short of a death sentence; and the <term>Court of Wardship</term>, which oversaw the king’s interest in these revenues. This last court was abolished by <term>Charles II</term>, who accepted a fixed sum in exchange for the whole. It is now understood that the king cannot establish a court without the consent of <term>Parliament</term>. In no other European country is the law so precise as in England, because it has not existed for as long. The <term>Parliament of Paris</term> was only established around the time of <term>Henry VIII</term> of England. The British <term>Parliament</term> consists of a great number of men, and these are of great dignity <note>This and the preceding sentence appear hopelessly corrupt.</note>. All new courts refuse to follow the rules that were previously established. All new courts are a great evil because their power is not precisely defined at first; therefore, their decisions are bound to be loose and inaccurate.

Thus, we have considered the origin of government:
1. Among a nation of <term>savages</term>;
2. Among a nation of <term>shepherds</term>;
3. The government of small clans with <term>chieftains</term>; the manner in which <term>aristocracies</term> arose; the fall of small <term>republics</term>, whether conquering or defensive; and, finally, the different forms of government that arose in Europe after the dissolution of <term>arbitrary government</term>.

### [§ 13. *Of the small Republics in Europe.*]

We shall next consider the origin of the small <term>republics</term> in Europe and examine the rights of the sovereign and the subject.

# PART I : JUSTICE

First, concerning the origin of these republics. In some countries, provinces that were far from the seat of government occasionally became independent, as was the case in a large part of Germany and France during the time of Charlemagne. Hugh Capet, who was the <term>chief justiciary</term> <note>Gilbert, *Treatise on the Court of Exchequer*, 1758, p. 8.</note>, took control of the government but assumed only the title of King of France. By inciting disturbances in Germany, the Pope for a long time prevented the Emperor Otho from taking possession of Italy. But even when Otho did gain possession of it, he could not retain it because of the distance. Every small town formed itself into a republic, led by a council of its own choosing. Some towns in Germany that were well-fortified, such as Hamburg, assumed the same privileges and still retain <note>The original manuscript reads ‘retains’.</note> them to some extent. The Italian towns are governed by a hereditary nobility, even though the ancient republics were perfectly democratic. In Venice, the people voluntarily surrendered the government, as they also did in Holland, because they could not endure the trouble and burden it caused them. The Dutch and Swiss republics are organized into a *respublica foederata* <note>a federated republic</note>, and their strength depends on this arrangement.

We shall make some observations on the manner of voting in these republics. When there are 100 votes and three candidates, it is possible for the person who is most disliked to be elected. If *A*, *B*, and *C* are candidates, there may be 34 votes for *A*, 33 for *B*, and 33 for *C*. Thus, although there are 66 votes against *A*, he wins. This is even more problematic when a criminal is brought before such an assembly; for instance, 34 might think him guilty of murder, 33 of <term>manslaughter</term>, and 33 of <term>chance-medley</term>, yet he must still be punished for murder <note>Hutcheson, *System of Moral*

*Philosophy*, vol. ii. p. 241; Grotius, *De iure belli et pacis*, book ii. chapter v. § 19; Pufendorf, *De iure naturae et gentium*, book vii. chapter ii. § 18.</note>. To prevent this, in some of these republics they always bring the question

...to a simple state. Is he guilty of murder or not? If there are three candidates, they hold a preliminary vote by which they exclude one of the candidates. In their senates, the president never has a deliberative vote <note>a regular vote as a member</note>, but only a casting vote, because they will not allow any member to have two votes. When there is a tie, nothing can be done; therefore, the business is not rejected but referred to another meeting.

### [§ 14. *Of the Rights of Sovereigns.*]

We shall now consider what duty is owed to the sovereign and what the proper punishment for disobedience is. Every attempt to overturn this power is considered the greatest crime in every nation and is called high treason. It should be observed that there is a great difference between treason in monarchies and treason in republics. In the former, it is an attack on the king’s person; in the latter, it is an attack on the liberties of the people. From this, we may see how the principle of assassination came to be established in republics and not in monarchies. It is in the interest of monarchies that the person in authority be defended, regardless of his title or conduct, and that no person be allowed to investigate them. The laws of monarchy are, therefore, unfavorable to the assassination of tyrants. In a republic, the definition of a tyrant is quite clear: he is one who deprives the people of their liberty, levies armies and taxes, and puts citizens to death at his pleasure. This man cannot be brought to a court of justice; therefore, assassination is considered just and equitable. Current republican governments in Europe, indeed, do not encourage this principle because monarchies now set the fashion, and other governments copy their example. According to our current views, the assassination of Oliver Cromwell is most disgraceful, but it would have...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the distinctions between monarchical and republican systems, specifically moving from the justification of assassinating tyrants to a formal classification of the crime of treason.</meta>

appeared differently when the republics of Greece and Rome established the custom.

Having thus noted this difference between monarchical and republican governments, we shall next consider the crimes considered treason. There are three kinds of treason or attacks upon the essence of government. First, *perduellio*, or an attempt to overthrow the established government by force or rebellion. Secondly, *proditio*, or joining the enemy, surrendering forts, hostages, etc., to him, or refusing to surrender garrisons, etc., to the government when they demand them. This is called high treason. Thirdly, *laesa maiestas*, or an insult to the authority of the magistrate, which is not as heinous a crime as the two former. These were the kinds of treason among the Romans. Under the emperors, these were merged, and a breach of even the smallest kind—even in so trifling a manner as throwing a stone at the emperor’s statue—was punished with death <gloss>1</gloss>. Under Honorius, a conspiracy against any of the emperor's ministers was high treason <gloss>2</gloss>.

The crimes considered treason by English law <gloss>3</gloss> are the following. First, killing the king, wishing for his death <gloss>4</gloss>, or providing weapons against him; every attempt of this kind is punished by death. The Gunpowder Plot was never carried out, yet the conspirators were put to death. Had they intended only the death of some other person, they would not have been executed. Secondly, violating the king’s wife or eldest daughter <gloss>5</gloss>, because these are

<column-break/>

to throw a stone at the emperor’s statue on purpose.
<gloss>2</gloss> *Codex*. book 9, title 8. 5.
<gloss>3</gloss> I.e. by 25 Edward III, statute 5, chapter 2.
<gloss>4</gloss> Plotting or imagining the death of the king or queen, or of their eldest son.
<gloss>5</gloss> The eldest daughter while unmarried, or the wife of the king’s eldest son.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of treason laws, focusing on the historical shift from the rule of Cromwell to the Stewart Restoration and the subsequent Hanoverian succession, concluding with the harmonization of English and Scottish treason laws.</meta>

During the civil war and the usurpation of Cromwell, it became a question of how far it is lawful to resist the power of government. The court party believed the king to be absolute, and the popular doctrine was that the king is only a steward and may be removed at the pleasure of the people. After the Restoration, the court party prevailed, and the opposing party became disliked. At the Revolution, the Stewart family was set aside for excellent reasons, and the succession was established in the current family. Because of this, the court party was removed from power and began to influence the leanings of the people. It was therefore enacted that whoever should speak against the current succession should be guilty of treason ^2. This is now entirely unnecessary, because the government is now so well established that there is no reason to take notice of those who write or speak against it.

In Scotland, the laws regarding treason were very confused. Turning the people against the king, or the king against the people, was categorized as high treason. However, by the Act of Union, these laws were made the same as those of England ^3. These are the laws of Britain with respect to treason, and they subject the person who breaks them to the most severe penalties. He is partially hanged, and then his entrails are removed; he forfeits his estate, his wife’s dower ^4, etc.,

Besides these, there are other offenses against the crown
which do not subject one to the penalties of high treason, but rather
to those of felony¹. First, the making of coin below the
standard² and the exportation of coin³. From the notion
that wealth consists in money, Parliament resolved that
everyone might have <term>bullion</term> coined without any expense
for <term>mintage</term>⁴. Thus, coined money was never worth less than
the value of <term>bullion</term>, and therefore there was a temptation

practice a felony⁵. Secondly, any attempt to increase the amount of coin,
such as by the <term>philosopher’s stone</term>, was made a felony⁶. Thirdly,
destroying the king’s armor is also a felony⁷. Fourthly,
any attempt against the king’s officers is also a felony⁸, and
in general, whatever is a felony against another person is
a felony against the king. If his pocket were picked, it
would be a felony against him, just as it is against any private
gentleman, but the former offenses are committed against
him in his capacity as king. There are some other minor offenses which

<meta>This page continues the explanation of legal offenses against the crown, specifically transitioning from felonies to the minor offense of praemunire and the definition of misprision of treason.</meta>

<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

England was entirely under the power of the Pope. His legate brought over bulls and collected contributions as he pleased, and long before the Reformation it was necessary to defend the king’s liberty against the Pope. The king sometimes appointed one person to a <term>benefice</term>, and the Pope appointed another, and the Pope’s candidate was often preferred. A law was therefore made forbidding any bull to be brought from Rome, or any appeal to be carried there, and subjecting every person who refused to ordain the king’s candidate to the penalties of *praemunire regem*—that is, to fortify the king against the Pope<note>1</note>; the penalty was the forfeiture of goods and outlawry. After Henry VIII was declared head of the Church by the Pope, it was made a *praemunire* to attack the king’s prerogative regarding church matters<note>2</note>.

Besides these, there are other offenses called misprisions of treason, which are either positive or negative. Positive<note>3</note> misprision of treason is the failure to reveal an attempt against the king’s person, his eldest daughter, or the

<note>1</note> Regarding the etymology of this word (*Praemunire*), Sir Thomas Smith asserts "that it is so-called from *praemuniting*, and fortifying and strengthening the Crown by the former statutes against the usurpation of foreign and unnatural power." This opinion may find some basis in the statute of the 25th year of Edward III, Stat. 6, chapter 1; or it may come from the verb *praemonere*, which means "to forewarn"—as it were a forewarning to anyone, lest he fall by such an attempt into a *praemunire*. In this case, it is a barbaric translation of *praemonere*, a corruption taken from the unrefined interpreters of civil and canon law, who indeed often use the effect (*praemunire*) for the efficient cause

Having considered the offenses of the subject against the sovereign, we shall next discuss the crimes which the sovereign may commit against the subject. But first, it is proper to consider who are subjects of a state.

### [§ 15. *Of Citizenship.*]

The laws of different countries vary much with regard to those to whom the right of citizenship belongs. In most of the Swiss republics, nothing grants the right of citizenship except being born to a citizen. In Rome, a family might be *peregrina* for four or five generations. At Athens, no man was a citizen unless both his father and mother were Athenians. It should be observed that the Athenians were particularly cautious in granting the right of citizenship because it entitled them to very significant privileges. Even kings were denied that honor; all they did when they wanted to bestow a favor on a neighboring king was to exempt him from taxes on imports. They did this for Amyntas, the father of Philip, King of Macedon. As foreigners paid higher duties than natives, it was no small privilege to have these removed. After the defeat of the Persians, their forces amounted to 25,000 men; their country was well cultivated, and many cities in Asia paid them tribute. In consequence of this, the people were entitled to attend the courts of justice, to have their children educated at the public expense, and to receive certain distributions of money, along with many other benefits. If the number of citizens increased, these privileges would not be as valuable, and therefore they were very protective of them. Just as anyone who enters a parish in England must provide a bond not to become a burden to it<note>1 Either "sufficient security" (13 & 14 Charles II, chapter 12) or a certificate from the parish where he 
<column-break/>
was last legally settled (8 & 9 William III, chapter 30), *Wealth of Nations*, book 1, chapter 10, volume 1, pages 146, 147.</note>, so [in] all little

<summary>The text transitions from discussing offenses against the state to the requirements and privileges of citizenship in historical republics like Athens, Rome, and Switzerland. It notes that the Athenians restricted citizenship to preserve the value of the significant public benefits and tax exemptions granted to citizens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the criteria for citizenship, moving from the specific requirements of ancient Athens to the general practices of large and small states, and the legal status of foreigners.</meta>

<columns>2</columns>
<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE</header>

In republics where the number of freemen is small and election is in the hands of a few, citizenship is of great importance; but in a large city such as Rome, it was a very small compliment, and accordingly, they granted citizenship to whole provinces at once. In Britain, one born within the kingdom is under the protection of the laws, can purchase land, and, if of the established religion, can be elected to any office. In large states^1, the place of birth makes a citizen, and in small ones, being born of parents who are citizens. In like manner, the incapacity to be a citizen is different in different countries. By the old laws of Rome, and of every barbarous nation, the goods of every person who came within their territories were confiscated, and he himself became a slave to the first person who happened upon him. By a law of Pomponius, if he came from a nation at peace with Rome, he was treated as the law prescribed^2. In barbarous countries, they have only one word to signify both a stranger and an enemy. At Rome, every stranger was *hostis*^3, as they considered all nations as their enemies and the person who came from them as a spy. The *Litchfield* man-of-war was shipwrecked in the Emperor of Morocco’s dominions, and because we had no league with him, the whole crew were made slaves. Our sovereign so far complied with

When they<note>That is, the nations mentioned a few lines earlier.</note> discovered the advantage of exporting their own goods and importing those of others, they would naturally allow those who traded with them to be in a state of safety, both with respect to their person and goods, and would allow them a legal action if injured in either. This is the state of foreigners in most of the countries of Europe at present. In Britain, a foreigner cannot purchase or inherit land property, nor maintain a real legal action. He cannot make a will because it is the greatest extension of property rights and is founded on respect and affection for the deceased, which a foreigner can have few opportunities to deserve. By a specific statute, a foreign merchant, but not a tradesman, may have a lease on a house. This is the state of foreigners in most countries.

Since the king is the heir to aliens, he may transfer his own right, but he cannot take away the right of the person who is legally entitled to succeed. An alien who is a denizen may inherit an estate bequeathed to him, but to be fully capable of inheriting in every respect, an act of naturalization is necessary. By this act, he gains a right to all the privileges of a freeborn subject. When King William came to the throne, naturalized aliens were made peers. Since many Dutch families came over with him, it was natural to suppose that he would favor them with every privilege. The English, offended by this partiality, passed an act declaring that there should be no future act of Parliament by which they would be allowed such benefits <note>The act, which has already been somewhat obscurely referred to on page 13 above, is 1 George I, chapter 4. it provides that “no person shall hereafter be naturalized unless, in the bill presented for that purpose, there is a clause or specific words inserted to declare that such person shall not thereby be enabled to be part of the privy council, or a member of either house of parliament, or to take any office or place of trust, either civil or military, or to have any grant of lands, tenements, or hereditaments from the crown to himself or to any other person in trust for him; and that no bill of naturalization shall hereafter be received</note>. none among them ^1. In Rome, it was the right of citizens only to make a will.

It should be observed with respect to aliens that they are *aliens amis* (alien friends) or *aliens ennemie* (alien enemies) ^2. If a group of the latter should make war upon the king or injure him, they cannot be prosecuted for high treason because he is not their lawful sovereign, and they owe no allegiance to him. If the laws of nations do not protect them, they must be dealt with by martial law. Aliens, however, who live in the country are protected by the laws, and as they thus owe allegiance to the king, they may be prosecuted for treason and punished accordingly. Whatever makes a freeborn subject guilty of treason makes an *alien ami* guilty of it. An *alien ennemie*—that is, one who comes from a country at war with us—is also guilty of treason if he gives information to his natural sovereign.

### [§ 16. *Of the Rights of Subjects*.]

Having thus considered who are properly the subjects of a state, we come now to treat the crimes of the sovereign against the subject, or the limitations of his power.

On this branch of public law, it is impossible to speak with any degree of precision. The duties of one subject to another are sufficiently established by the laws of every country and the courts of justice, but there are no judges to determine when sovereigns do wrong. To suppose a sovereign is subject to judgment implies another sovereign. In England, it can be exactly as-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limits of sovereign power, tracing how the powers of government evolved from precarious beginnings into absolute authorities, with a focus on the judicial system's development.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>PUBLIC JURISPRUDENCE</header>

determined when the king encroaches on the privileges of the people, or they on those of the king, but no one can say how far the supreme power of king and parliament may extend. Likewise, where the absolute power of sovereignty is held by a single person, no one can accurately say what he may not do. God is the only judge of sovereigns, and we cannot say how he will decide. All decisions on this subject have been made by the prevailing party, and never impartially by a court of justice; therefore, they can shed no light on the matter. Our best understanding of it will arise from considering the various powers of government and their progression.

In the beginning of society, all the powers of government are exercised tentatively. The majority may declare war, but cannot force the minority to join, although this power was the first to be exercised absolutely. The judicial power was exercised tentatively for much longer than the federative power. In every country, judges once only intervened as mediators, and sometimes the defendant had the choice to refer his case to the judge or to God—by combat, boiling water, etc.—and indeed, if the judge's sentence did not please the defendant, he might challenge the judge to fight him in the court <sup>1</sup>. In time, however, it became absolute. Legislative power was absolute whenever it was introduced, but it did not exist in the beginnings of society; it arose from the growth of judicial power. When judicial power became absolute, the very sight of a judge was terrifying, as life, liberty, and property depended on him. Tacitus tells us that Quintilius Varus, having conquered part of Germany, wanted to civilize the people by establishing courts of justice, but this so irritated them that they massacred him and his entire army <sup>2</sup>. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the evolution of judicial power and the limits of sovereignty, examining how legislative power arose to restrain the judiciary and explaining the principles of utility and authority in government.</meta>

<columns>2</columns>

people a judge is the most terrible sight in the world. When property ownership was expanded, it therefore became necessary to restrain their arbitrary decisions by establishing strict rules which they must follow. Thus, the legislative power was introduced as a restraint upon the judicial. In Britain, the king has absolute executive and judicial power. However, the House of Commons may impeach his ministers, and the judges, whom he appoints, are thereafter independent of him. The legislative power is absolute in the king and parliament. There are, however, certain abuses which undoubtedly make resistance lawful in some cases, regardless of the principle on which government is founded.

Suppose that government is founded on a contract, and that these powers are entrusted to persons who grossly abuse them; it is evident that resistance is lawful because the original contract has now been broken. But we showed before that government was founded on the principles of utility and authority. We also showed that the principle of authority is more prevalent in a monarchy, and that of utility in a democracy, due to the citizens' frequent attendance at public meetings and courts of justice. In a government like the latter, since the principle of authority is effectively forbidden, popular leaders are prevented from acquiring too much power because they are not allowed to remain in office long enough to gain great influence; yet, respect is still paid to certain offices, regardless of who holds them. In Britain, both principles exist. Whatever the principle of allegiance may be, a right of resistance must undoubtedly be lawful, because no authority is completely unlimited. Absurdity of conduct may deprive an assembly of its influence just as it would a private person, and imprudent conduct will take away all sense of authority. The folly and cruelty

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the right of resistance against government authority, examining how absolute power and unfair taxation can justify such actions, and critiques the theory that sovereigns cannot alienate their dominions.</meta>

...of the Roman emperors make the impartial reader side with the conspiracies formed against them.

It should be observed that the right of resistance is more frequently exercised in absolute monarchies than in any other, because one man is more likely to fall into unwise measures than a group. In Turkey, eight or ten years seldom pass without a change of government. The same degree of maltreatment will justify resistance to a senate or a body of men. It must be admitted that resistance is in some cases lawful, but it is extremely difficult to say what an absolute sovereign may or may not do, and there are different opinions concerning it. Mr. Locke says that when a sovereign raises taxes against the will of the people, resistance is lawful¹, but there is no country besides England where the people have any vote in the matter. In France, the king’s edict is all that is required, and even in Britain, we have only a very symbolic consent, for the number of voters is insignificant compared to the total population. Exorbitant taxes undoubtedly justify resistance, for no people will allow half of their property to be taken from them; but even if the highest standards of propriety are not observed, if there is some degree of moderation, people will not complain. No government is quite perfect, but it is better to submit to some inconveniences than to make attempts against it.

Some other writers claim that the king cannot <term>alienate</term> (transfer ownership of) any part of his dominions². This notion is based on the principle of the original contract, by which, even if a people were willing to submit to one government, they would not accept one of another’s choosing. This doctrine is, however, groundless. In France and Spain, a great part of

It is hard to determine what a monarch may or may not do. But when the *summa potestas* (supreme power) is divided as it is in Britain, if the king does anything that should be consented to by the parliament without its permission, they have a right to oppose him. The nature of a parliamentary right supposes that it may be defended by force; otherwise, it is no right at all. If the king imposes taxes or continues them after the time has expired, he is guilty of a breach of privilege. James II attempted some impositions of this kind upon imports. In the Bill of Rights² it is expressly appointed that taxes shall not continue a moment after the time determined by act of parliament.

When the parliament saw the crown going to James II, who was a Roman Catholic, they appointed two tests: namely, an abjuration of the Pope and the oath of supremacy, and that every person, within three months after accepting any office, should take the sacrament according to the form prescribed by the Church of England. The king, noticing the distaste of the people and thinking it arose from the fear of those who possessed abbey lands—fearing they would be taken from them—and from a fear of a change in the national religion, declared that he would grant liberty of conscience to all and keep everyone in possession of the Church lands. This clearly showed his intention to change the religion of the country, which is the most difficult thing in the world. It is necessary for the opinions of the people to be changed before a religion is changed, as was done by Luther, Calvin, John Knox, and others before the Reformation. Thus, we have considered man as a member of a state.

Since the clergy and the laity are the two major divisions of men in a state, under this heading we might also consider ecclesiastical law and the respective rights of these two groups of men. Here too we might consider military law, which arises from viewing the state as being divided into two bodies: civil and military. However, these are outside our current purpose.

# [DIVISION II.] DOMESTIC LAW

## [*§ 1. Husband and Wife.*]

We now come to consider man as a member of a family, and in doing this we must consider the threefold relation that exists in a family. These are, namely, the relations between husband and wife, parent and child, and master and servant <note>Hutcheson, *System of Moral Philosophy*, vol. ii. p. 149.</note>.

The first of these we shall consider is husband and wife. In every species of animal, the connection between the sexes is exactly as much as is necessary for the propagation and support of the species. Quadrupeds, whenever the female becomes pregnant, have no further desire for each other; supporting the young is no burden to the female, and there is no need for the assistance of the male. Among birds, something like marriage seems to take place; they continue to be objects of desire to each other, their connection remains for a considerable time, and they jointly support the young; but whenever the young can provide for themselves, all further inclination ceases <note>Locke, *Civil Government*, §§ 79, 80; Hume, ‘Of Polygamy and Divorces,’ *Essays*, 1748, p. 249.</note>. In the human species, women, because of their milk, are not capable of providing for their children for long. The assistance of the husband is therefore necessary for their sustenance, and this should make marriage perpetual.

<column-break/>

<note>Hutcheson, *Introduction to Moral Philosophy*, p. 257; *System of Moral Philosophy*, vol. ii. pp. 150, 161.</note>

In countries, however, where Christianity is not established, the husband possesses an

# PART I : JUSTICE

unlimited power of divorce and is not accountable for his conduct. In ancient Rome, although they had the power to do so, it was nevertheless considered contrary to good manners ¹. We may observe a usefulness in this constitution of our nature: that children have such a long dependence on their parents in order to align their passions with those of their parents, and thus eventually be trained to become useful members of society. Every child receives this part of their education, even under the most worthless parent.

On this subject, it is proposed to consider the duties of each of the two parties during their union, how this union should be begun and ended, and what the particular rights and privileges of each are.

The first duty is the fidelity of the wife to the husband; a breach of chastity is the greatest of offenses. Illegitimate children might be introduced into the family and succeed to the inheritance instead of lawful ones. This practical utility, however, is not the proper foundation for the crime. The public's indignation against the wife arises from their sympathy with the husband's jealousy, and accordingly, they are disposed to resent and punish it. The feeling of jealousy is not primarily founded—or rather, not at all—on the idea of illegitimate offspring. Jealousy does not arise from the specific act itself; rather, the husband considers her infidelity as an entire alienation of that preference over all other persons which she owes him. This is his real conception of it, as may appear from the following consideration. Our idea of a father does not arise from the sensual act that gave occasion to our existence, for that idea is partly loathsome and partly ridiculous. The real idea a son has of a father is as the director of his infancy, the supporter of his helplessness, his

# DOMESTIC LAW

guardian, role model, and protector. These are the proper filial sentiments. The father's idea of a son is of one who depends upon him and was raised in his house or at his expense, through which connection an affection toward him should grow; but a spurious offspring is disagreeable due to the resentment that arises against the mother's infidelity.

In those countries where the manners of the people are crude and uncultivated, there is no such thing as jealousy; every child that is born is considered their own. The foundation of jealousy is that sensitivity which accompanies the sentiment of love, and it exists more or less in different countries in proportion to the crudeness of their manners. In general, wherever little regard is paid to the sex, infidelity is little regarded, and there will be the greatest looseness of manners. Consistent with this, we find that Menelaus expressed his resentment against Paris, not against Helen, and this was not for seducing her, but for carrying her away. In the *Odyssey*, she talks about that action before her husband without reservation. In Sparta, it was common for them to borrow and lend their wives. When manners became more refined, jealousy began and eventually rose to such a height that wives were shut up, as they are among the Turks to this day. As mankind became more refined, the same fondness which made them shut up women also led them to allow them liberties. In the later ages of Greece, women were allowed to go anywhere. This same fondness, carried to a high degree, grants as great a license as when infidelity was disregarded. In no barbarous country is there more licentiousness than in France. Thus, we may observe the prejudice of manners, with respect to women, in the different periods of society.

Though there was little or no regard paid to women in the first state of society as objects of pleasure, yet there never was more regard paid to them as rational creatures. In North America, the women are consulted concerning the

<summary>This page discusses the historical and social evolution of jealousy, arguing that it arises from refined romantic sentiments rather than primitive instincts. It traces the status of women from ancient Sparta and Greece to modern France, noting how societal development dictates both the confinement and the liberties granted to women.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the status of women and the development of marriage laws, moving from the influence of manners to the formal legal structures and ceremonies of marriage, particularly in ancient Rome.</meta>

carrying on of war, and in every important undertaking¹.
The respect paid to women in modern times is very small; they are only spared from hardship so as not to spoil their beauty. A man will not exempt his friend from a laborious task, but he will spare his mistress. When the infidelity of the wife is considered an injury to the husband, it is necessary that women, while still unmarried, should be placed under restraints, so that when married they may be accustomed to them. Hence the origin of punishment for fornication.

We come now to consider how this union is begun. As the duty after marriage is quite different from what it was before, it is necessary that there should be some ceremony at its commencement. This differs in different countries, but in general is connected with religion, as it is supposed to make the greatest impression. In the infancy of society, though marriage seemed intended to be perpetual, the husband nevertheless had an unlimited power of divorce, though it was considered indecent to exercise it except for an enormous crime. The reason was that the government dared to interfere little with private affairs, and far less with matters in private families. For the security of government, they endeavored by all means to strengthen the power of the husband and make him as absolute as possible. In ancient Rome, the husband was sovereign lord of life and death in all matters belonging to his own family.

In Rome three kinds of marriages took place² :
First, by <term>confarreation</term>, a religious ceremony;
Secondly, by <term>coemption</term>³, when the husband bought his wife;
Thirdly, by usage. If he had lived with her a year and a day, she was his by prescription, and he could divorce her.

<keywords>marriage ceremonies, divorce, Roman law, confarreation, coemption, prescription, paternal power, social manners, Lafitau, Heineccius</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of Roman marriage laws, transitioning from the rigid forms of religious and legal possession (confarreation and coemption) to a more flexible, contract-based form of marriage that granted women greater independence and the right to divorce.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>DOMESTIC LAW</header>

The power of divorce extended to the wife after female succession took place. A woman who possessed a great fortune, who lived happily before marriage and had so much under her own control, would not be inclined to give it all to her husband. The lawyers, therefore, invented a new kind of marriage in favor of heiresses, which was called the *deductio domi*, or marriage by contract; certain terms were agreed upon between the parties, and then the husband came and took her home. To prevent prescription from occurring, she went away for three or four days every year, which, according to the form of the contract, secured her fortune <note>‘Deductio domi’ should be ‘deductio in domum.’ This was an element of all kinds of marriage. The ‘new kind of marriage’ is thus described by Heineccius: ‘If, therefore, the wife did not wish to come under the husband's hand (authority): dowry documents were indeed drawn up, and she was taken to the house, but she took care to be away from her husband for at least three nights.’ *Antiq. Rom.* book i. title x. § 14.</note>. Thus, the wife became as independent as the husband and had an equal power of divorce. As the marriage was founded on the consent of both parties, it was reasonable that the dissent of either party should dissolve it.

This form of marriage is quite similar to the present day, with this important difference, however: that it did not legitimize the children or preserve the honor of the women <note>Perhaps a rash inference</note>.

<column-break/>

from Heineccius’s statement, ‘When marriage was contracted without one of these rituals, the wife did not come under the husband's hand (authority), and thus did not become a *materfamilias* (mother of the family), but a *matrona* (married woman),’ *Antiq. Rom.* book i. title x. § 14. The Roman form caused great disorders. When the parties separated, which often happened, they married others, and very often women went through five or six husbands <note>‘Noble women do not count their years by the number of consuls, but by the number of their husbands.’ Seneca, *On Benefits*, book iii. chapter xvi, quoted in Heineccius, *Antiq. Rom.* book i. appendix § 46.</note>. This so corrupted their morals that toward the end of the monarchy, there was scarcely a great man who was not cuckolded. The disorder reached such a height

However, this flexibility led to frequent separations and social instability, which the author suggests corrupted Roman morals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal evolution of marriage and divorce, moving from the Roman context into the influence of Christianity, the customs of Western European tribes, and the rising jurisdiction of ecclesiastical courts over domestic matters.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

...that, after the establishment of Christianity, the power of divorce was restricted except for certain causes. Among the Scythian nations which settled in the West of Europe, divorce was abolished altogether. In Burgundy, however, the power of the husband was very great. According to a law there, if a man abused his wife he was liable to a fine, but if the wife misbehaved she was put to death.

As in general only flagrant crimes were recognized by the civil court, minor ones fell into the hands of the clergy, and that first gave rise to their great power. When the civil court provided no remedy for breach of contract, the clergy punished the offender for perjury; and when any dispute occurred between husband and wife, they made them perform penance for it. Afterwards, the power of divorce was taken away except in cases of adultery, or when one party feared bodily harm from the other. Even this last was not a complete divorce, for neither of the parties was allowed to marry again; it was only a separation <term>a mensa et toro</term>.

The causes for a complete divorce, after which the parties were allowed to marry again, were these three. First, if they were within the prohibited degrees of <term>consanguinity</term>, the marriage was made void unless they had a dispensation from the Pope. Secondly, a <term>precontract</term> with any other woman. Thirdly, <term>frigidity</term> in a man and incapacity in a woman. The clergy introduced other changes besides these regarding marriage. It should be observed that the laws made by men are not altogether favorable to women. They considered that the infidelity of the husband and wife should be equally punished, and he had no more power to divorce than she ¹. Adultery, <term>saevitia</term>, and <term>metus</term> were considered as causes of separation, but not of divorce.

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal history of marriage, specifically how ecclesiastical authorities adopted and adapted Roman law to govern domestic relationships and the validity of marriage contracts.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>DOMESTIC LAW</header>
<columns>2</columns>

The canon law, when it was established, was dictated by ecclesiastics who, on most occasions, copied Roman law, as they were the only people who understood Latin and the ones among whom the remains of literature were preserved. At first, even ecclesiastical law required no ceremonies for marriage. Just as the ceremonies of confarreation and coemption <gloss>1</gloss> had fallen into disuse in the later periods of Roman law, when the only requirement was the *deductio domi* <gloss>2</gloss>; so by ecclesiastical law for a long time, a contract of any kind created <insert>a</insert> marriage, whether it was a contract *in praesenti* or *in futuro*. A contract *in praesenti* occurs when I say, "I take you for a wife," or, "I take you for a husband." A contract *in futuro* occurs when they say, "I will do it." Either of these contracts could be proved either by evidence or by oath, if the parties declared themselves married persons, or stated that they intended to be. Pope Innocent III enacted that all marriages should be performed *in facie ecclesiae* [in the face of the church]; but though this was considered the only respectable form of marriage, others were often used and in some cases were valid. If a person was married *in futuro*, and afterwards *in facie ecclesiae*, and the first wife made no opposition until after the banns were announced, the first marriage was void. If it was a contract *in praesenti*, the second marriage was void <gloss>3</gloss>. This was the case in England until the recent Marriage Act <gloss>4</gloss>. If a contract *in futuro* can be proved, or if the man refuses his oath, the marriage is in some countries considered valid. The contract *in praesenti* is valid everywhere, especially if the couple cohabits afterward. All these institutions are derived from canon law, which made the breach of them liable to church censures, just as our law does.

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

Only an act of parliament can grant a divorce in England; the infidelity of the wife alone will not accomplish it. In Scotland, it is much more easily done. Protestants never took matters as far as the canon law did, because the clergy themselves married. Furthermore, love, which was formerly considered a ridiculous passion, became more serious and respectable. As proof of this, it is worth observing that no ancient tragedy focused on love, whereas now it is more respectable and influences all public entertainments. This can only be explained by the changes in mankind.

The type of marriage we have been discussing occurred only in Rome and in Christian countries, along with a few others; for in many countries, men took as many wives as they were able to maintain. This naturally leads us to consider the origin of polygamy. It should be observed that although voluntary divorce is accompanied by inconveniences, it is not entirely contrary to the principle of justice for a man to put away his wife and take another for lesser reasons than adultery, because they may be quite unhappy together, even though either of them might live very well elsewhere. The same applies to polygamy. If a woman consents to be one of five, twenty, or more wives, and the law <note>1. The manuscript reads 'laws.'</note> allows it, no injury is done to her; she receives the treatment she might naturally expect. Ancient Jewish and Eastern laws tolerated polygamy; but though it and voluntary divorce are not entirely contrary to justice, it must always be very bad policy wherever they are established or permitted.

Polygamy excites the most violent jealousy, which destroys domestic peace. The wives are all rivals and enemies; furthermore, the children are poorly cared for, and the wife complains that her children are not treated as they should be, because she measures the father’s affection by her own. There is no comparison between the two, as his <note>2. The manuscript reads 'he.'</note> affection is divided among forty or fifty children, while hers

<header>DOMESTIC LAW</header>
<columns>1</columns>

only among four or five. Where polygamy occurs, there must be both a jealousy of love and a jealousy of interest, and consequently a lack of tranquility. It may be said that in the seraglios of the Eastern monarchs there is the greatest peace, but this is due to the most imperious discipline: when rebels are subdued, their humility is remarkable. In Africa, we find the most horrific disorders, as their discipline is not severe enough. It is the greatest misery for the women that they are entirely shut up and can enjoy no company but that of the eunuchs, whom they detest.

The man who keeps a seraglio is also by no means happy, though he may appear to be. He too must be jealous, and on account of the inequality between him and them, he can have no entertainment in his own home and no opportunity for social improvement; one must never mention his wife to a Turk, and she can never be seen by men—not even by her physician, as Tournefort tells us¹. This seriousness and reserve of the husband must have a bad effect on the manners of the country. As the men have neither trust nor dependence on each other, they cannot form into political parties, and therefore the government must always be arbitrary, for which they have a model in their own houses, where there is little parental and even less conjugal affection. Besides all this, it tends to depopulate the species; the majority of men can get no wives, and many of them are castrated to tend to the seraglio. It is indeed alleged that more women are born than men. Montesquieu says that at Bantam in the East Indies, there are ten women born for every one man². Dutch authors say that on the coast of Guinea there are fifty to one. The account from Japan is better attested, where it is said there are eleven women to nine

In some of the previously mentioned places, there may indeed be more women than men. In places where the seat of religion is located, where the royal court sits, and where the wealthy consequently live, there must be more women, because only the rich have seraglios, and they purchase women from other places. Thus, there is a constant import of women from those countries where polygamy does not exist.

Polygamy occurs under despotic governments. When a country is conquered by savages, they indulge themselves in all manner of brutality, and this practice among the rest, as there is no established law to the contrary. It never existed in ancient Carthage or Rome, though it occurs in Turkey. In every country, freedom eliminates polygamy; there is nothing that free men will less submit to than a monopoly of this kind, but despotism is always favorable to polygamy.

Montesquieu observes still further in favor of polygamy that in some countries women are eligible for marriage at eight or nine, and are old and withered at twenty <note>The manuscript reads '30,' which is inconsistent with the context and *The Spirit of the Laws*, book xvi, chapter ii: 'Women are nubile in warm climates at eight, nine, and ten years old: thus childhood and marriage almost always go together there. Wherever polygamy occurs, there can be no hereditary nobility. It is difficult to establish the right of primogeniture <note>The right of the firstborn child to inherit the entire estate.</note> where there are so many wives, several of whom give birth at nearly the same time. Where there are so many children, they cannot all have the affection of the parent, and it is only through this affection that any of them can establish themselves. Where children are numerous, affection diminishes. I may care for four or five children who are connected with my friend, but if there are a hundred in that same relationship, they are little regarded.

Eastern countries, for the very reason that they lack these, offer feeble resistance against foreign invaders.)

Polygamy is exceedingly harmful to the population.

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>DOMESTIC LAW</header>

of a nation. A hundred women married to a hundred men will have more children than the same number married to two or three. It may indeed be said that in China, around the mouth of the Ganges, and in Egypt, they are populous despite polygamy. In those countries, there are regulations regarding population, and some other circumstances contribute to it, such as the remarkable fertility of the soil.

Thus we see marriage is of two kinds, *namely* polygamy or monogamy, of which the latter is of three types: first, when the husband can divorce the wife at will; second, when the power of divorce is equally in the hands of both <note>This should obviously read either ‘in her power’ or ‘in the power of each.’</note>; and third, when it is entirely in the power of the civil magistrate. Where polygamy is allowed, the wife is entirely in the power of the husband; he may divorce her or dispose of her as he pleases.

The laws concerning monogamy differ according to its specific type. The kind where the contract or agreement is indissoluble except by the civil magistrate is the most convenient. Under this system, indeed, only matters that are very harmful to society provide grounds for divorce. But it is always better for the marriage tie to be too strict than for it to be too loose. The unlimited power of divorce in the later ages of the Republic <note>Of Rome.</note> produced the most disorderly consequences, the prevention of which sufficiently justifies any hardships it may cause. When both parties have the power of divorce, they can have no mutual trust or dependence upon each other, and their interests remain quite separate.

We now come to consider what interest the husband has in the property of the wife, or the wife in the property of the husband, according to the different types of marriage. Where polygamy takes place, the wife, being in absolute

When only the husband has the power of divorce, the wife's property becomes his just as much as his own. When the power of divorce is in the hands of both, whatever portion the wife brings is secured, and the husband can do nothing more with it than manage it. When he dies, the wife has no greater share of her husband’s property than was agreed upon in the contract. In the type of monogamy where divorce is in the hands of the magistrate, the rights of the husband do not extend as far as they did previously; however, those of the wife extend further, as she is more independent of him than in any other type. If a wife has a landed estate, the husband receives the rents, which are at his absolute disposal. If the wife dies and leaves a son, the husband is the child's natural guardian and is entitled to a courtesy of the life-rent of his wife’s estate. In England, the husband can dispose of all his wife’s chattels real during his lifetime; but if he does not dispose of them during his lifetime, they go to the wife, not to the heir, at his death. He can dispose of all her personal chattels as he pleases. Debts on bonds are the same as chattels real. If the husband demands payment of the debt, he can dispose of the money as he pleases; but if he does not claim it during his lifetime, it goes to his wife after his death. If the wife dies first, all chattels real and debts on bonds go to her relatives, provided the husband has not already disposed of them. If the husband dies first, the wife has a third part of his landed estate <note>Specifically, for her life.</note>, whether there are children or not. This is considered her dowry <note>A mistake for ‘dower.’</note>. In England, she has a complete third of everything <note>Of course, with the qualification indicated in the first note regarding land, and with the <column-break/> qualification ‘if there are children’ regarding personal estate, since if there are no children, the widow takes half.</note>, but in Scotland, she has only a third of all

In Scotland, the husband can sell his wife’s land with her own consent, but she must first be examined before a court and declare that it was with her own consent, and then her executors <note>Rather, ‘heirs.’</note> cannot claim it. Both in Scotland and in England, no bond granted by the wife is binding upon the husband unless it is granted for the necessities of life. In this respect, she is considered a servant, for if a servant buys provisions in his name, he is obliged to pay for them. In Scotland, the husband may have a writ of inhibition to prevent the wife from contracting debts in his name. In England, any verbal notice that he will not be accountable for them is sufficient. If they are separated, he is not even obliged to pay for what she purchases for her <term>aliment</term>.

We come now to consider what persons are capable of contracting marriage. Between ascendants and descendants, marriage is prohibited *infinitely*. Nothing can be more shocking to nature than for a mother to marry her son. By this, the mother becomes inferior to her son, and on account of the inequality of their ages, the purposes of marriage are seldom accomplished. Therefore, it is never tolerated except where superstition takes place. In like manner, a marriage between a father and a daughter is incestuous. It is, however, to be observed that this is not so contrary to nature as the former, because the father still remains superior when he is the husband, and accordingly we find that many primitive nations tolerated this <note>The argument is the same as that of Montesquieu, *The Spirit of Laws*, book 26, chapter 14.</note>. But still, it is unnatural that the father, the guardian and instructor of the daughter, should become her lover and marry her. Besides, a mother can never look agreeably on a daughter who will probably take her place. Nothing can be more destructive to domestic happiness. For the same reasons, the uncle and

niece, or the aunt and nephew, never marry. In Rome and Carthage indeed, they used to sometimes grant a dispensation to the uncle and niece, but never to the aunt and nephew.

The marriage of collaterals, such as brother and sister, seems to have been prohibited chiefly for political reasons, because they are raised together and would be in danger of mutual corruption unless properly restrained. The same reason applied to marriage between cousins in those ages when they were brought up in the same house. In Athens, a man might marry his half-sister by the same father (*consanguinea*) but not his half-sister by the same mother (*uterine*)¹. Many eminent men married in this manner; for instance, Cimon married his father’s daughter Elpinice². Under English law, the wife of a deceased grand-uncle can marry her husband’s grand-nephew, as it is more than four degrees of separation³.

Affinity under Christian law is considered the same as consanguinity. The wife’s sister is regarded as the husband’s sister, and the wife’s aunt as the husband’s aunt. It should be observed that the rules of affinity are matters of social policy rather than of nature, for it is not contrary to nature for a man to marry his wife’s sister. In many countries of the East Indies, this kind of marriage occurs because they believe that the wife’s sister will likely make the best stepmother <note>The original uses ‘mother-in-law,’ which historically referred to a stepmother.</note> to her sister’s children. But it may be answered that this entirely hinders all social interaction between the sister and her brother-in-law’s family, and it might be expected that she would fulfill this purpose by living in his house unmarried, with no children of her own. The canon and

<meta>This page continues the discussion on relationship degrees and prohibitions of marriage, specifically addressing how civil and canon laws differ in calculating these degrees. <header>DOMESTIC LAW</header>

Civil law reckoned consanguinity <note>The original text mistakenly uses 'affinity' here; it should be 'consanguinity' or 'consanguinity and affinity.'</note> differently. The civil law counted brothers and sisters as one degree removed from the common stock, and first cousins as two. The canon law counted how far the persons were apart. Brothers were two degrees, the father being one and either of the brothers another. In the same manner, first cousins were four degrees. The canon counted both sides from the stock, and the civil law counted only one <note>Undoubtedly due to a slip by the lecturer, 'canon' and 'civil' have been swapped throughout this explanation of the two different methods of calculating degrees of relationship. The situation is exactly the opposite of what is stated in the text.</note>. When the one says the second degree was prohibited from marriage and the other the fourth, they both mean first cousins. The Pope often granted dispensations from these laws, and by that means extended his authority and promoted his interest.

Having now considered all the different types of marriage, we come to consider the effects of the lack of it. The effect of marriage is to legitimize the children. We must therefore consider the difference between legitimate and illegitimate. Legitimization gives the children inheritable blood, so that they can succeed to their father and his relatives. An illegitimate child has no inheritable blood and therefore cannot succeed his father *intestato* (intestate), because it is unknown who his father is, nor his mother, because no child inherits who is not lawfully begotten. As a bastard can inherit from nobody, so nobody can inherit from him <note>Meaning, if he dies childless.</note>, as he is not related to any human creature. If he dies intestate without children, his wife has one half of his movable property and one third of his landed estate, and the rest goes to the king; but if he has children, the wife has a third of everything <note>See above, page 86 and the accompanying notes.</note>. The king is still considered the *ultimus heres* (final heir). In Scotland, there is a further inconvenience attending this. As the king is the heir of bastards, a bastard

The king can, however, grant him letters of legitimation which make him capable of making a testament, because, as the right of succession belongs to the king, he may dispose of it as he pleases. However, this, or anything less than an act of parliament, cannot give him inheritable blood, but an act of the whole legislature can do anything.
   The canon and civil law restore inheritable blood to a person born out of wedlock in the following ways <gloss>1</gloss>:—
   First, *per subsequens matrimonium*, or marrying the woman who had the children. As concubines were numerous, it was enacted that whoever married his concubine legitimized her children. This Justinian afterwards made perpetual.
   Secondly, *per oblationem curiae*. When the children were willing to perform certain municipal offices, such as deacons <gloss>2</gloss>, etc., though this entitled them only to succeed their father, and not his relatives.
   Thirdly, *per adrogationem*. For example, one Roman could adopt the son of another, and the son accept him as a father. They had it in their power to adopt any free man. Bastards were considered as free men, and if they were willing to accept, they might be adopted as such <gloss>3</gloss>.
   Fourthly, *per [re]scriptum principis*, which was much the same as letters of legitimation.
   Fifthly, *per testamentum*, by which they probably succeeded only to their father's estate.
   The canon law introduced the *subsequens matrimonium* into all countries except England. The English clergy were

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal status of illegitimate children, specifically why the English legal system rejected the canon law's provision for legitimacy through subsequent marriage, and begins a new section on the legal relationship between parent and child.</meta>

<header>DOMESTIC LAW</header>

then <gloss>1</gloss> unpopular because they joined with the king against the barons, and therefore in England the *subsequens matrimonium* (subsequent marriage) could never grant legitimacy. For *subsequens matrimonium* to grant legitimacy, the canon law established certain restrictions that did not exist in Roman law. The illegitimate children of adulterous persons could not inherit—specifically, those born to a woman who has a living husband, or to a concubine of a man whose wife is alive, even if they should marry later. Children born of incest also could not inherit, unless they were legitimized by a dispensation from the Pope.

Thus we have seen the disabilities and incapacities of illegitimate children, which can only be effective where monogamy prevails; and indeed, these restrictions alone prevent polygamy from gaining ground in any country. This is because if illegitimate children were allowed to inherit, men would hardly subject themselves to the inconveniences of lawful marriage. It would be more convenient to have one wife entirely in their power and to take others whenever they pleased.

### [§ 2. *Parent and Child.*]

We now come to consider the history of parentage, which is the second relationship in which we consider man as a member of a family. The authority of the father over his children, regarding both their liberty and their property, was originally absolute. He was free to choose whether or not he would raise his children, and it was not considered an injustice to refuse to do so. The law prevents the infliction of injuries upon others, but there can be no fixed laws requiring acts of benevolence. The only thing the law prohibited was putting them to death immediately; however, he could abandon (expose) them if he pleased. Even in our own system, a father is not obliged to ransom a son who is taken captive, but may choose whether or not to do so

<meta>This page continues the discussion on parental authority, specifically the historical right of a father to decide whether to raise or abandon his child.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>PART I: JUSTICE</header>

as he pleases. In the same manner, in ancient times, a father might choose whether he would rescue his son from starvation, from wild beasts, and the like. Although some regulations were made in Rome concerning this, they were never well observed, and the practice was not abolished until the establishment of Christianity. In China at present, where polygamy occurs, they are often forced to abandon them¹ and generally drown them¹. Since the father had it entirely in his power to raise his son or not, he had absolute jurisdiction over him if he did raise him. In Rome, the father had the <term>ius vitae et necis et vendendi</term> (the right of life, death, and selling). Furthermore, whatever the son acquired belonged to the father, and if the son married, his children were considered members of the grandfather’s family. This power of the father over his son was very soon diminished. The son was connected with his mother’s relatives, and the uncle—whom he was to succeed on some occasions—would naturally look after the person who was to be his heir. By a law of Numa Pompilius, if a son was married, it was no longer in the father’s power to sell him². The Twelve Tables do indeed mention this privilege of the father, but it is probable it applied only to those who married without their father’s consent. In the same way, the <term>ius vitae et necis</term> fell into disuse. The father only carried out the laws of his country regarding capital crimes. He could take the power out of the hands of the magistrate and condemn his son to punishment himself, but he could not free his son if the son was accused by the laws of his country. This shows that the <term>patria potestas</term> was not altogether absolute. This power of the father weakened by degrees, and at last disappeared altogether. The father only pronounced the sentence as it was dictated to him by the civil magistrate, as the father himself might have made mistakes in legal forms and, by that means, rendered

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historical limitations placed on parental authority, specifically focusing on the transition of property rights from the father to the son in Roman law.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>DOMESTIC LAW</header>

...the whole null. It is much the same with the gentlemen in this country, who have the power to seize their tenants' goods when they are in debt without any legal process. Since they are ignorant of how it ought to be done, they are obliged, like everyone else, to apply for authority from the civil court, even though they are legally vested with the power to do it themselves.

The father’s power regarding the son's property soon declined as well. We find that very early, by a law of Marcianus, fathers were required to provide suitable wives for their sons and to bestow appropriate marriage portions upon them; if they refused, the government was to ensure it was done^1. This shows that property after marriage must have belonged to the sons. The law seems to have^2 been made because the wife brought a fortune with her, and therefore it was only reasonable that the husband should also have some property independent of his father. It must, therefore, have been only the property of unmarried children over which the parent had any power, and this is not unreasonable. A father's authority was not absolute in Rome, for we often find men accused there for failing to take proper care of their children, which could hardly have been the case if they could have legally put them to death.

Julius Caesar, and after him Augustus, were the first to grant sons property independent of their fathers. At first, they kept as their own whatever they acquired in war—the <term>*peculium castrense*</term>—and later whatever they earned through the liberal and mechanical arts. This was extended by Adrian and afterwards by Justinian to include everything except what they received from their fathers. All donations and legacies were entirely at their own disposal. We also find that the father's power to disinherit them was limited. There were only certain specific cases in which it was within his power to do so.

<meta>This page continues the legal history of domestic relations, transitioning from the rights and obligations within the parent-child relationship to the third category of family law: master and servant.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

After the fall of the Roman Empire, the power of the father over the son, as well as over the wife, was softened. The father came to have over the son, while the son continued to live in the family with him, an authority much the same as that which a father has among us—namely, that of taking care of his morals. But when the son was out of the family, the father was not as immediately concerned about him. The father has this particular privilege with respect to his son: he can become a tutor to him without providing surety, and he is not accountable, as every other tutor is, for negligence and omission. This is the natural authority the father has over the son. The father is obliged to bring up his children, and the children, in case of his old age or infirmity, are obliged to maintain the father.

->[§ 3. *Master and Servant.*]<-

We now come to consider the history of law with regard to masters and servants, which was the third relation in which we proposed to consider the family. We have found that the same principle which gave the husband authority over the wife also gave the father authority over the son. Just as the power of the husband was softened by means of his wife’s friends, with whom she was connected and to whom she could complain, so that of the father was softened by the same means. But it was not so with the servants; they had nobody to whom they could complain and no connection with any person. Having no one to take their part, they necessarily fell into a state of slavery. Accordingly, we find that the master had the power of life and death over them, quite different from the <term>*ius vitae et necis*</term> over the wife and children, which was restricted to criminal cases; the power over the servants was perfectly arbitrary. Besides, as the master had the disposal of his liberty, a slave could have no property. Whatever he has or can acquire belongs to his master. No contract of the slave could bind the master, however, unless the laws found a tacit consent...
