--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal status of slaves, specifically detailing the rights of the master over the slave's property and the various social and religious hardships endured by the enslaved.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>DOMESTIC LAW</header>

of the master implied. A slave can only acquire property for his master. If I promised a slave £10, I am obligated to pay it to the master. But besides these disadvantages, there are many others to which the ancient Greek and Roman slaves, as well as our negroes, were liable, though these are less often noted.

First, they were prevented from marrying. They may live with a woman, but cannot marry, because the union between two slaves lasts only as long as the master pleases. If the female slave does not have children, he may give her to another or sell her. Among our slaves in the West Indies, there is no such thing as a lasting union; the female slaves are all prostitutes and suffer no social degradation because of it.

Second, slavery is accompanied by even greater evils than these; for a slave who is a polytheist is effectively under the protection of no religion. He has no God, just as he has no liberty or property. Polytheistic religion consists of a great number of local deities. Every place has its own divinity. The slaves do not belong to the country, and therefore its gods are in no way concerned with them. Besides, a heathen can never approach a deity empty-handed. The slaves had nothing to offer and therefore could expect no favor from them. Those slaves who were employed in the temples were the only ones who could have any claim to the protection of the gods. The master prayed for them, but only in the same manner that he prayed for his cattle. Every person is superstitious in proportion to the uncertainty of their life, liberty, or property, and to their ignorance. Gamblers and primitive peoples are remarkably so. It is, then, a very great hardship that a slave, who is prone to superstition for both these reasons, should be deprived of that which is so well suited to soothe the natural feelings of the human heart. Therefore, a religion that revealed one God who governed all things would naturally be very acceptable to slaves. Accordingly, we find that the Jewish religion, which, though

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the religious hardships of slaves, specifically comparing the difficulty of converting to Judaism versus the rapid spread of Christianity among the enslaved, before transitioning into a broader analysis of the persistence of slavery in different political systems.</meta>

well fitted for defending itself, is, of all others, the worst adapted to the making of converts. This is because converts could never be of the lineage of Abraham, from whom the Messiah was to come; they could not be on an equal level with the Jews, but only "proselytes of the gate," and were obliged to abstain from many kinds of food. Despite all these disadvantages, it made great progress among the Roman slaves. When Christianity was introduced, which was accompanied by none of these disadvantages, it made the most rapid progress among the slaves.

We are apt to imagine that slavery is completely abolished because we know nothing of it in this part of the world; but even at present, it is almost universal. A small part of Western Europe is the only portion of the globe that is free from it, and it is nothing in comparison with the vast continents where it still prevails. We shall endeavor to show how it was abolished in this region, and for what reasons it has continued in other parts, and probably will continue to do so.

It should be observed that slavery takes place in all societies at their beginning and arises from that tyrannical disposition which may almost be said to be natural to mankind. Whatever form of government was established, it was a part of its constitution that slavery should be continued. In a free government, the members would never make a law so harmful to their interests as they might think the abolishing of slavery would be <note>'The recent resolution of the Quakers in Pennsylvania to set at liberty all their negro slaves may satisfy us that their number cannot be very great.' *Wealth of Nations*, book iii. ch. ii. vol. i. p. 391.</note>. In a monarchy, there is a better chance for its being abolished, because a single person is the lawgiver, and the law will not extend to him, nor diminish his power, though it may diminish that of his vassals. In a despotic government, slaves may be better treated than in a free government, where every law is made by their masters, who will never pass anything prejudicial to themselves—

<columns>2</columns>

selves. A monarch is more inclined to be persuaded to act humanely toward them. When Augustus was visiting Vedius Pollio, one of the slaves, who had accidentally broken a platter, threw himself down before Augustus, begging for his protection so that he might not be cut into pieces and thrown into the fish pond. Augustus was so shocked by this that he immediately freed all of Pollio's slaves, although Pollio, no doubt, did not enjoy his guest's behavior¹. In the reigns of Hadrian and Antoninus, after the monarchy had been established, several laws were made in favor of slaves, but not a single one was passed during the time of the Republic. Slavery, then, may be gradually moderated under a monarch, but not entirely abolished, because no single person can have enough authority to suddenly take away the most significant portion of the nation's property, as this would cause a general insurrection.

In a wealthy country, slaves are always treated poorly because the number of slaves exceeds the number of free people, and it requires the strictest discipline to keep them in order. If a free man was killed in a house, all the slaves

Slavery is more tolerable in a primitive society than in a civilized one. In an undeveloped country, the poverty of the people makes the number of slaves anyone can own quite small; therefore, their discipline is not as strict as when they are numerous <sup>6</sup>. # DOMESTIC LAW

town or village. In Jamaica and Barbados, where slaves are numerous and objects of suspicion, punishments even for minor offenses are very shocking; but in North America, they are treated with the greatest mildness and humanity <note>1. Kalm, *A Journey to North America*, 1753-61, vol. ii. (1756), p. 480, makes a statement to this effect, but his work was not translated into English until 1770-71. Adam Smith may how- <column-break/> ever have come across a quotation from the German version (1754-1764).</note>.

Thus, we have shown that slavery becomes more severe in proportion to the development of society. Freedom and wealth contribute to the misery of the slaves. The perfection of freedom for the master is the slave's greatest bondage; and, as slaves are the most numerous part of mankind, no humane <note>2. Humane.</note> person will wish for liberty in a country where this institution is established.

It is almost unnecessary to prove that slavery is a bad institution even for free men. A free man who works for daily wages will work far more, in proportion to the expense necessary for maintaining and raising him, than a slave will <note>3. *The Wealth of Nations*, book i. ch. viii. vol. i. p. 85.</note>. In ancient Italy, an estate managed by slaves in the most fertile country yielded to the master only one-sixth of the produce, whereas a landlord even in our barren country receives a third, and the tenants live much better. Slaves cultivate only for themselves; the surplus goes to the master, and therefore they are careless about cultivating the ground to its best advantage. A free man keeps as his own whatever exceeds his rent and, therefore, has a motive for industry. Our colonies would be much better cultivated by free men. That slavery is a disadvantage is apparent from the state of colliers and salt-workers in our own country. They indeed have privileges which slaves do not. Their property, after maintenance, is their own; they cannot be sold except along with the workplace; they enjoy marriage and religion; but they do not have their liberty entirely, and it would certainly be an advantage

sig H 2

If they were free, their prices would fall. At Newcastle, the wages do not exceed ten pence or a shilling, yet colliers often leave our coal works, where they have half a crown a day, and run there, though they have lower wages, because they have liberty.

<insert>(</insert>There is still one more disadvantage that accompanies slavery: it diminishes the number of free men even to a degree beyond imagination, for every slave takes up the place of a free man.<insert>)</insert> The inequality of wealth seemed at first a misfortune, and laws were made against it. £10 per year is reckoned as the necessary expense of one man. A landed gentleman who has £10,000 per year spends what would maintain a thousand men. At first sight, we are apt to view him as a monster who eats up the food of so many, but if we consider it, he is really useful, and he eats or wears no more than the rest. £10 serves him too, and his £10,000 maintains a thousand people who are employed in refining his £10 in an infinity of ways so as to make it worth the whole sum. This gives room for all kinds of manufactures. When slaves are employed to extract, as it were, this £10 out of the £10,000, one must be a tailor, another a weaver, a third a smith, and thus each takes up a free man’s place.

We come now to account for the abolition of slavery in this part of the world. The slaves in this and the neighboring countries were those who cultivated the ground and were what was called *adscripti glebae* <note>Bound to the soil.</note>, and could only be sold along with the land. As they had nothing but their maintenance for their labor, the ground was only poorly cultivated. To remedy this disadvantage, "steelbow" tenants were introduced¹. They had no capital themselves, and therefore the landlord gave them cattle and the implements for plowing, which they returned at the

This was the first species of free tenants, who were clearly emancipated serfs <note>The original term is "villains," referring to feudal bondsmen.</note>. After this custom had continued for a long time, the tenants saved enough to enable them to make a bargain with the landlord to give him a certain sum for a lease of a set number of years; whatever the ground produced, they would take the risk upon themselves. This is clearly an advantage to the landlord: the ground is better cultivated every year, he is at no expense, and keeping half of the product was better for the tenants than any sum they would offer in rent¹. By feudal law, the lord had absolute control over his vassals. In peace, he was the administrator of justice, and they were obliged to follow him in war. When government became a little better established, the sovereign did everything he could to lessen this influence, which on some occasions was dangerous to himself and hindered people from applying to him for justice. Therefore, as the ancient serfs were tenants at will, were obliged to perform certain duties for their master, and were entirely at his disposal, a law was made taking away all their burdens except for that of being tenants at will, and at last their privilege was extended and they became copyholders.

<insert>(</insert>Another cause of the abolition of slavery was the influence of the clergy, but by no means the spirit of Christianity, for our plantation owners are all Christians. Whatever diminished the power of the nobles over their inferiors increased the power of the clergy. As the clergy are generally more in favor with the common people than the nobility, they would do all they could to have their privileges extended, especially as they might have expectations of benefiting by it.<insert>)</insert> Accordingly we

Thus, the influence of the clergy, combining with that of the king, hastened the abolition of slavery in Western Europe. Consistent with this, we find that in countries where neither the king nor the church was very powerful, slavery still prevails. In Bohemia, Hungary, and those countries where the sovereign is elective—and consequently could never have great authority—and where the church also never had much influence, servitude still remains, because the court is by no means powerful enough to emancipate the slaves of the nobility. <margin>)</margin>

To show by what means slaves are acquired, to consider the state of domestic servants in our own country, and to mention a certain specific state of families, will be all that is to be said on this topic regarding man as a member of a family.

Slaves may be acquired in five different ways. First, in almost every country, captives in war are made slaves. If the conqueror does not kill them, he has a right to make them slaves. Secondly, as captives become slaves because they have no one to deliver them, their children also become slaves. Thirdly, persons guilty of certain crimes were made slaves, sometimes to the injured party and sometimes to the public. Fourthly, debtors in the ancient state of the Roman Republic were made slaves. If they could not pay their debt, it was thought reasonable that they should work for it. This still takes place in all countries where slavery is established. Fifthly, there is a sort of voluntary slavery where an impoverished citizen sells himself to be the slave of another person. When a person sells himself to another for any sum, by the laws of slavery this very sum becomes the property of the person who bought

But when a person was in debt and obliged to become a slave for it, he would not perhaps choose to be his creditor’s slave for fear of ill-treatment, and would therefore sell himself to another person, on the condition that they would pay his debt. The citizens of Rome were often in debt, and by that means became entirely dependent upon their superiors. Many of them had no means of subsistence except what they received from candidates for their votes, and, as this was by no means sufficient for that purpose, they often borrowed from those to whom they gave their votes, who were ready enough to lend so that they might secure them entirely to their interest. By this means, they could not give their vote to any other person unless that person paid what they owed to their creditors, which few would be willing to do, as they mostly owed more than the value of their votes.

In the middle age of the Republic, these last two methods of acquiring slaves were prohibited by express laws: the first by what was called <term>cessio bonorum</term>, and the latter by a law prohibiting any free man from selling himself.

Slavery in the West Indies took place contrary to law. When that country was conquered by Spain, Isabella and Ferdinand went to the greatest trouble to prevent the Indians from falling into a state of servitude; their intention was to make settlements, to trade with them, and to instruct them. But Columbus and Cortez were far from the law and did not obey their orders, but instead reduced them to slavery, which in a manner instituted itself among them.

We come now to consider the state of servants. A negro in this country is a free man. If you have a negro servant stolen from you, you can have no legal action for the price, but only for damages sustained by the loss of your servant. In like manner, if a negro is killed, the person who does it is guilty of murder; but though a negro servant is entitled to the privileges of a free man while

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the legal status of individuals, transitioning from the illegal practices of slavery in the Americas to the specific legal rights and obligations of domestic servants, apprentices, and the protections afforded to children under the laws of the author's contemporary society.</meta>

The most dependent people among us are domestic servants (*inter moenia* <note>literally "within the walls"</note>) who are contracted from one term to another. They have almost the same privileges as their master: liberty, wages, etc. The master has a right to discipline his servant moderately, and if the servant should die under his discipline, it is not murder unless it was done with a deadly weapon, or with premeditation and without provocation. A servant can acquire property for his master either when he acts by his express authority or when a tacit consent is implied. If a servant buys or sells goods in his master’s name, his master has grounds for a legal action in case of non-payment or non-delivery. As there is a unique connection between master and servant, they can be exonerated in many cases where any other person would be found guilty. If either master or servant kills another person in defense of the other, it is justifiable homicide. If a master dies before the term of service ends, the executors are obliged to pay the servant's full wages and to support him as well.

Apprentices are in much the same situation as servants, with only this difference: the master receives a fee with the apprentice and is obliged to teach him a trade; if he refuses to do so, he may be sued for damages and loss of time.

### [§ 4. Guardian and Ward.]

We come now to consider the particular state of families. When a father dies leaving young children, it is necessary that they should be cared for. Even in the times of <term>exposition</term> <note>the ancient practice of abandoning unwanted infants</note>, if an infant was kept for some time, it was thought cruel to put him to death; the child was

The nearest paternal relative was the person whom the law settled upon. In an early age, the maintenance of the child was all that needed to be taken care of, for there were no estates to manage, and the mother returned to her father’s family. This guardianship terminated when the child was about thirteen or fourteen years of age, at which time it was capable in that era to fend for itself. But when men came to possess estates, although a child might be supposed capable of fending for himself around that age, he still could not be capable of managing an estate. It now became necessary to retain him in <term>pupillarity</term> for more than fourteen years. By Praetorian law, at that age, he was allowed to choose his guardians or curators. A curator can do nothing without the consent of the pupil; a guardian can act without his consent, but is accountable to his pupil for whatever he does during his <term>minority</term>. At first, lunatics and idiots were almost the only persons who had guardians; and, because it was considered disgraceful to have one, the role was generally declined. Afterward, the law made invalid all acts of the pupil until he was twenty-one, unless he had the consent of his curators. As the nearest paternal relative is often the next heir, it was reckoned improper to trust the person of the son to him. English law carried this so far that if an estate was left to the son during his father’s lifetime, he was not even entrusted to his father. By our law, the care of an estate is entrusted to the next heir, as he will probably take the best care of it; and the heir himself is entrusted to a more remote relative, who will take the best care of him, as that person cannot benefit from his death.

# [DIVISION III.] PRIVATE LAW

## [§ 1. *First way of acquiring Property: Occupation.*]

We formerly explained the nature of rights and divided them into natural and acquired. The former need no explanation; the latter are divided into real and personal rights. Real rights include property, servitude, pledge, and exclusive privilege. We will first discuss property.

Property is acquired in five ways. First, by occupation, or the taking possession of what previously belonged to no one. Second, by accession, when a person has a right to one thing as a consequence of another—for example, the shoes of a horse along with the horse itself. Third, by prescription, which is a right to something belonging to another that arises from long and uninterrupted possession. Fourth, by succession to our ancestors or any other person, whether through a will or without one. Fifth, by voluntary transference, when one person delivers their right to another.

We shall first discuss occupation, the laws of which vary according to the stages of human society. The four stages of society are hunting, pasturing, farming, and commerce. If a group of people were shipwrecked on a desert island, their first sustenance would come from the fruits which the soil naturally produced and the wild animals which they could kill. As these could not always be sufficient, they eventually began to tame some of the wild animals so that they might always have them available. In the course of time, even these would

Among shepherds, the idea of property is further extended. Not only what they carry about with them, but also what they have deposited in their hovels, is their own. They consider their cattle as their own while the animals have a habit of returning to them¹. When the majority of beasts are occupied, they consider them as their own even after they have lost the habit of returning home, and they may be claimed for a certain time after they have strayed. 

But property receives its greatest extension from agriculture. <insert>(</insert>When it first became necessary to cultivate the earth, no person had any property in it, and the little plot which was tended near their hovels would be common to the whole village, and the fruits would be equally divided among the individuals. There are the remains of common land property in our own country at this day. In many places, there is a piece of ground belonging equally to several persons, and after harvest, cattle are, in many places, allowed to feed where they please. 

<insert>(</insert>Private property in land never begins until a division is made by common agreement, which is generally when cities begin to be built, as everyone would choose that his house, which is a permanent object, should be entirely his own². Movable property may be occupied in the very first beginnings of society, but lands cannot be occupied without an actual division. An Arab or a Tartar will drive his flocks over an immense country without supposing a single grain of sand in it to be his own³. By the laws of many countries, there are some things, however, that cannot be occupied by any private person. Treasure and derelict goods, by the laws of Britain, belong to the king. A sea surrounded by several nations cannot be occupied by any single one; instead, all must share the jurisdiction. However, any nation may prevent another from fishing in its bays or approaching its coasts with warships.

### [§ 2. *Second way of acquiring property: Accession.*]

The right of <term>accession</term> is founded not so much on its utility as on the impropriety of not joining it to the object on which it depends. The milk of a cow I have purchased may not be of great value, but it is very improper for another person to have a right to raise his calf on it. The most important accessions relate to land property. Land property is founded on the division or <term>assignation</term> by society to a particular person of the right to sow and plant a certain piece of ground. As a consequence of this right, he must also have a right to whatever it produces: trees, fruit, minerals, etc. <term>Alluvions</term> (land deposits) made by any river naturally belong to the owner of the adjacent territory; but when these additions are very large, as is often the case in low-lying countries, the government claims them, and the owner of the adjacent estate must purchase the new land before he possesses it<sup>2</sup>.

The principal dispute concerning <term>accession</term> is: when does the main object belong to me and the <term>accession</term> to another? Or, if they are mixed, to whom does the whole belong? It is a legal maxim that no person should be a gainer

gentium*, book iv. chapter vii. § 12.

<summary>This page explains the limitations of private ownership regarding public waters and the legal principle of accession, which dictates that the owner of a primary object also owns its natural products or additions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal discussion on property rights, specifically concluding the rules of accession before introducing the third method of acquisition, prescription.</meta>

by another's loss¹. If a person builds a house by mistake upon my ground, though the materials belong to him, it is only reasonable that I should have the house, or be compensated for my loss. In general, the <term>accession</term> (the addition) follows the <term>principal</term> (the main object), although in some cases—such as where the workmanship is of more value than the materials—*substantia cedet formae* (the substance yields to the form). The lawyers were, however, unwilling to directly contradict their general and established rule, and therefore avoided it by giving the principal to the owner of the accession when it became a "new species"—that is, when it received a new form and a new name. This, however, was subject to exceptions. A picture and the board on which it was painted were, in Latin, of the same species; each was a *tabula*, and therefore, by this rule, the picture still belonged to the owner of an insignificant board. The most general rule regarding accessions is this: when the object can be reduced to its original form without lessening its value or without any great loss to the owner of the accession, the owner of the principal may justly claim it; but when this cannot be done, the law rightly favors the owner of the accession and obliges him only to satisfy the original owner for his property.

### [§ 3. *Third way of acquiring Property: Prescription.*]

<term>Prescription</term> is founded on the supposed attachment of the possessor to what he has long possessed, and the supposed loss of affection in the former owner for what has been long out of his possession. There are four things required to establish a right by prescription. First, *bona fides* (good faith); for if a person is aware that his right to a thing is invalid, it is no injury to deprive him of it, and

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal requirements for prescription as a method of acquiring property, specifically detailing the conditions of just title, good faith, and uninterrupted possession.</meta>

# PART I : JUSTICE

the impartial spectator can easily go along with depriving him of possession. Second is *iustus titulus* <note>a just title</note>, by which is not meant a title that is just in all respects—for this is sufficient in itself without anything else—but *iustus titulus* signifies some reasonable foundation that the person has for thinking a thing is his own, such as a charter of some kind. If he claims a right without such a title, no impartial spectator can share his perspective. Third, uninterrupted possession is also necessary for prescription; for if the property has often been claimed from him, the former possessor has not abandoned his right. Fourth, the time is only counted when there was a person available to claim the property; therefore, the longest uninterrupted possession cannot grant a right if the owner was a minor, a person of unsound mind, or in exile.

A *iustus titulus* is proof of *bona fides* <note>good faith</note>, and *bona fides* is required for a *iustus titulus*. By Roman law, *bona fides* was only required when first taking possession; even if you later found a flaw in your title, prescription still occurred. Nature has fixed no specific period for prescription, and accordingly, it varies according to the stability of property in a country. In Rome, immovable property was once acquired by prescription in two years, but later more time was required <note>1</note>. In our country, a feudal lord, who continually had claims against his neighbor, could scarcely be persuaded to admit any law of this nature. He was willing to revive a claim even if it were as old as the days of Noah, and when they finally fixed on a period, they made it as long as possible—namely, forty years. Among the Romans, it should be observed that if anyone’s possession was interrupted during the time required for prescription by an enemy entering the country, he had to begin the period all over again. By

<note>1 “Therefore, immovable things in Rome were acquired by usucapion in two years, and movable things in one year. But Justinian... wanted immovable things to be acquired</note>
<column-break/>
<note>in ten years among those present, and in twenty years among those absent.” Heineccius, *Roman Antiquities*, book 2, title 6, sections 2, 9.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal requirements for prescription as a method of acquiring property, specifically detailing how English law and monarchical claims interact with the concept of uninterrupted possession, and introduces the fourth method of acquiring property: succession.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>113</page-num>
<header>PRIVATE LAW</header>

In English law, nothing can interrupt prescription except a claim by the former possessor. Kings rarely ever allow their claims to expire <note>1</note>; at least, they consider no length of uninterrupted possession sufficient to do so. However, immemorial possession will always carry this weight with it.

### [§ 4. *Fourth way of acquiring Property: Succession.*]

Succession is either legal or testamentary. By legal succession, it is meant that the law should distribute the goods of the deceased to those to whom it is presumed the person himself would have chosen to give them, according to some lawyers <note>2</note>. But this assumes that testamentary succession—the distribution of goods according to the will of the deceased—preceded legal succession, which is contrary to historical experience. In a primitive period, a man scarcely had full ownership of his goods during his lifetime, and therefore it cannot be supposed that he would then have had the power to dispose of them after his death. In all nations, the relatives of the deceased person inherited long before there was any such thing as a testament. The Twelve Tables at Rome and the laws of Solon at Athens seem to have first introduced testamentary succession <note>3</note>; but long before this, legal succession existed in both countries. The claim of the heir by blood is always considered the preferable one, but this claim is never based on the presumed will of the deceased. If we consider succession in the earliest times, we find that it is based more on the connection of goods than of persons <note>4</note>. As the father and sons lived

<note>3 Heineccius, *Roman Antiquities*, book ii. title x. § 5.</note>
<note>4 "The ancient Romans, in the succession of those who died without a will, did not aim to make those people heirs whom the deceased seemed to have held dearer than the rest..."</note>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

together, and were joint acquirers of any property they had; when the father died, the children had a joint right to the goods, not so much on account of their relation to the father as on account of the labor they had bestowed on acquiring them. The mother and the children would therefore continue in possession. Among the Romans, the wife was considered a daughter and received her share accordingly. If any of the children had left the family or were emancipated, they had no share in the inheritance <note>1</note>, because they had ceased to cooperate with the rest in acquiring the goods. It may be observed that when families lived together in this manner, it was necessary to prohibit the marriage of cousins. When a man’s <note>2</note> sons and grandsons lived in the same house, if all inherited equally, it was called <term>successio secundum capita</term> [succession by head], but if the grandson succeeded only to his father’s portion, it was <term>secundum stirpes</term> [succession by lineage]. If a man had three sons who were all deceased, but the oldest had left behind one son, the second two sons, and the third three, by the first rule, upon the death of their grandfather, each would receive a sixth; but by the latter, the son of the oldest would receive a third alone, the two sons of the second a third between them, and the three sons of the third a third among them. The grandsons were, in a sense, the representatives of their father. The right of representation is the same as <term>successio secundum stirpes</term>. Among the Romans, the right of representation was introduced in favor of the strong and to the disadvantage of the weak, but in Britain, it is the opposite <note>3</note>.

Among the Romans, a son could not inherit from his mother when she died because, as she was considered

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Roman inheritance laws, specifically focusing on the legal status of the wife as a "daughter" of her husband's family and the hierarchical rules governing who succeeds to an estate.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>PRIVATE LAW</header>
<columns>2</columns>

As a daughter of the family, everything she had belonged to her husband; if the husband died first, the wife shared the estate with her children, and then returned home to her father’s house, where she could inherit from her father again. But in more refined times under the emperors, a mother could inherit from her son, and a son from his mother <note>1 Heineccius, Roman Antiquities, book iii. title iii.</note>. In ancient times, when a son died, no person inherited from him, because he and everything he owned belonged to his father. Caesar first enacted a law that a son might possess as his own whatever he gained in war, or acquired through the liberal arts <note>2 Ibid. book ii. title ix. § 2.</note>.

Three classes of people may inherit: ancestors, descendants, and collateral relatives—meaning those in an upper line may inherit from those in a lower line, those in a lower line from those in an upper line, or those of the same line from one another. Collateral succession at first extended only to the nearest blood relative <note>3 Ibid. book iii. title ii. § 3.</note>, and if that person refused it, the goods belonged to the public <note>4 To the ‘gentiles’ or members of the clan; ibid. § 7.</note>; but afterwards the praetor extended it to the seventh degree of blood relation <note>5 Ibid. title v. § 5.</note>. When a brother died and another inherited, it was a consequence of their connection with the father, who is the common ancestor; therefore, the inheritance of ancestors must have preceded that of collateral relatives. But the right of descendants is stronger than either of these, because a son’s claim on the father is evidently stronger than that of the father on the son. The principles of succession regarding movable property are thus founded on the communal ownership of goods which existed in ancient families.

The different state of families in our country creates a significant difference between our law <note>6 That is, Scottish law.</note> and that of the Romans. Among us, a wife is a much more significant person than a daughter and, accordingly, inherits more.

<meta>This page transitions from the Roman rules of inheritance to English and feudal laws, specifically discussing the division of assets, the influence of feudal lords, and the origin of indivisible inheritance.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

When a husband dies, the goods are supposed to be divided into three equal parts: one of which is supposed to belong to the deceased husband, one to the wife, and one to the children. There is, however, this difference: the husband can dispose of his part by a will, which the wife cannot do. A <term>forisfamiliated</term> son is not in the same condition as an emancipated son among the Romans. He can inherit along with his brothers; however, if he has received a portion of the estate already, he must bring it into the common stock upon his father’s death. Grandchildren do not inherit in the place of their deceased father, as they did among the Romans. English law, however, allows for representation, and it prefers ancestors, if they are male, over collateral relatives.

We come now to treat the subject of indivisible inheritance, which was introduced by feudal law. When the nations that conquered the Roman Empire settled in Western Europe, an inequality of wealth necessarily followed. Since the great lords had no way of spending their fortunes except through hospitality, they necessarily acquired enormous influence over their <term>vassals</term> <note>1 Compare above, page 35.</note>. They gave out their lands merely as a means of support for their dependents; and it is notable that the Saxon word *farm* means food <note>2 Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, page 33.</note>.

The chieftains, because of their influence, were the sole administrators of justice within their own territories. It was in the interest of the government to authorize this jurisdiction, as it was the only method of preserving peace, and because the superior was the leader in both peace and war. As recently as the year 1745, this power remained in the Highlands of Scotland, and some gentlemen could bring several hundred men into the field <note>3 *Wealth of Nations*, book iii, chapter iv, volume i, pages 413, 414, where, however, the jurisdiction is said to be allodial, not feudal.</note>. Since these lords had no other way to dispose of their lands, they gave some of them as <term>munera</term>, which were revocable at their pleasure, and

Others they gave as <term>beneficia</term>, which continued during their life and returned to the lord after their death <note>1 Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, p. 199. See above, p. 37, n. 2.</note>.

The benefices of the clergy seem to have been based on this foundation and have retained the name. By this means, the lords secured the loyalty of their <term>vassals</term>. Since benefices were for life, ownership of them naturally came to be extended to the son of the deceased tenant, and by degrees the tenures became hereditary and were called <term>feudal</term>; thus, the tenant became more independent. When any <term>chieftain</term> died and left behind a son in his <term>minority</term>, the king appointed a leader for the vassals during that minority and took the profits and <term>emoluments</term> arising from the lands for his own use. When a woman succeeded to the estate, the lord had the power of arranging her marriage, as it was considered reasonable that he should name the husband who was to be his own vassal. Since the lord was the guardian of the male heir, it was also thought unreasonable for the heir to marry without his consent. As the feudal lord possessed the lands during a minority, before the minor could recover his estate, he was required to pay what is called a <term>relief</term>. This was introduced by the court of the king or lord, before which the minor was required to swear <term>fealty</term> before he could recover his estate. He was also required to promise <term>homage</term> to his superior before he could take possession. Thus, they held their lands from the superior in exchange for military service, homage, fealty, <term>wardship</term>, marriage, relief, etc. <term>Allodial</term> estates were free from all such services; but since it provided security for property to hold it from some powerful man who could protect the owner from violence, the majority of estates became feudal. For the same reason, men who possessed great estates paid a <term>feu</term> and swore fealty to the <term>sovereign</term> <note>2 This paragraph is a summary of pp. 36-39 above.</note>.

It appears from this that it must have been a very difficult matter to secure property, especially if it was

The consequences of dividing the kingdom of France were sufficiently experienced, and the situation would have been even worse in the case of private estates. However, because of the opposition from the other sons, it was a long time before the right of primogeniture or the indivisibility of estates could be introduced, and in Germany it did not fully take place until the last century; but as circumstances necessarily required it, estates were at last made indivisible. Since a single person had to be preferred, the eldest son would naturally be that person. This legal preference must be given based on some quality that is altogether indisputable. If it were to be given for wisdom or valor, there might be great disputes; but among brothers, there can be no contest over who is the eldest. In the early stages of society, age itself is very much respected; and to this day, among the Tartars, the king is not succeeded by his son, but by whichever member of the royal family is the oldest.

Primogeniture, once introduced, would naturally lead to succession by representation for the following reason: at first, the younger brothers would think it hard that their older brother should be preferred over them, and if he died, they would think it even harder that his son—an infant—should take precedence before them. Accordingly, in many places, this has been disputed in single combat <note>1 "It was no less debated, even by wars and single combat, whether a grandson from an older son should be preferred to a younger son." Grotius, *On the Law of War and Peace*, book ii. chapter vii. § 30.</note>. Bruce and Balliol disputed on this account. According to our modern ideas, Balliol had the better right, for he was descended from the eldest daughter, although Bruce was one step closer to the common ancestor. The difficulty of introducing this at first gave rise to a new kind of succession, by which, when a father died, his estate went to his eldest son; but if he died while his children

were minors, or if he died while his father was still alive, his brother—not his sons—succeeded him. This led to one inconvenience: upon the death of the youngest brother, his sons were preferred over the sons of the other brothers. Under Roman law, a grandson inherited only his father's portion; he might succeed in the capacity of a son, but not as an eldest son. The brothers naturally felt that they were more closely related to the father than any grandson he could have; but while this was a hardship to the brother's claim, it was also a hardship to destroy the reasonable expectation that the grandson would have had if his father had lived. This latter circumstance eventually gave rise to lineal succession. Once this difficulty is overcome, there is little dispute regarding collateral succession. In feudal lordships, a woman could not inherit, as she was incapable of performing military services; however, they could inherit lands where any other kind of service was required <note>1 Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, pp. 229-231.</note>. Of fiefs, there are two kinds: masculine and feminine. France, where no woman can inherit the crown, is an example of the former, and England is an example of the latter.

There are some rather peculiar details in Scottish law regarding the inheritance of collateral relatives. If a second brother has an estate and dies, it passes to the third brother and not to the eldest, who is assumed to have been sufficiently provided for. <language>English</language>

<header>PART I: JUSTICE</header>

We must observe that the right of primogeniture hinders agriculture <note>1 *Wealth of Nations*, book 3, chapter 2, volume 1, pages 386-389.</note>. If the entire estate were divided among the sons, each one would improve his own portion better than one person can improve the whole; besides, tenants never cultivate a farm as well as they would if it were their own property. Primogeniture is also harmful to the family; for while it provides for one son, it allows all the others to be reduced to beggary in a few generations <note>2 *Ibid.* page 388.</note>. In the succession to a monarchy, however, it has one clear advantage: it prevents all dangerous rivalries among the brothers.

There are some other types of succession that take place, or have taken place, in several countries. Thus, in some countries, the youngest son succeeds the father. Something similar exists among our tenants to this day: as the older sons grow up, they are provided for, and the youngest, remaining with the father, succeeds him.

So much for legal succession. We come next to testamentary succession. It should be observed that no extension of property rights is as great as this, and therefore it took a long time before it could be introduced; it was very natural to give a man the right to dispose of his property while he lived, but a will assumes he can dispose of a right when, strictly speaking, he can no longer have any rights himself. He cannot be said to transfer his right, for the heir has no right resulting from the will until after the testator himself no longer has any. Puffendorf whimsically explains this through the immortality of the soul <note>3 It is not Pufendorf but Leibniz, quoted by him (*On the Law of Nature and Nations*, book 4, chapter 10,</note>

<column-break/>

§ 4), who gives this explanation. Pufendorf says, "But as for what the author of the new method of jurisprudence devises on page 56, I do not know if the wise would approve of it."

In Rome, the right of making wills was introduced gradually. At first it was only allowed, and that too after the con-

<meta>This page continues the exploration of testamentary succession, specifically how the psychological concept of "piety for the dead" and the practice of adoption served as the foundations for the right to dispose of property after death.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>PRIVATE LAW 121</header>
<columns>2</columns>

...consent of the fellow citizens was asked and obtained for childless people. This was much the same as adopting children <note>1 Lord Kames, *Law Tracts*, vol. i. pp. 186-7; Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, p. 152; Heineccius, *Antiq. Rom.* book ii. title x. § 2.</note>. When a person died and wanted to leave his estate to a son in exile, he would naturally request his neighbors not to take it from him after his own death. This request would be respected, not so much because it was his will, but out of a kind of reverence for the dead. We naturally find pleasure in remembering the last words of a friend and in carrying out his final instructions; the solemnity of the occasion deeply impresses the mind. Besides, we enter, as it were, into his dead body and imagine what our living souls would feel if they were joined with his body, and how much we would be distressed to see our last instructions not performed <note>2 Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, p. 154.</note>. Such sentiments naturally inclined people to extend property rights a little further than a person's lifetime.

This seems to have been the foundation of testamentary succession. It was considered a sort of impiety not to comply with the father’s desire, even though it was no injury to deprive the heir of the estate, since no law had been established in his favor and his being in exile cut off all reasonable expectation of succeeding. The injury is perceived as being done to the deceased person, as we imagine what his feelings would be were he to live again. It should be observed that this practice is a significant refinement in human civilization and was never practiced in a primitive nation. Before the Twelve Tables, no Roman had a right to make a will <note>3 See above, p. 113.</note>. Our Saxon ancestors had no right to dispose of their lands by a will, and in the history of the Old Testament, we hear of no such practice. Reverence for the dead could take

<column-break/>

<note>4 This overly absolute assertion is likely a hasty inference from Dalrymple’s history of the transfer of land property by will in *Feudal Property*, chapter iii. section 3, pp. 149-162.</note>

<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

...placed only with regard to the immediate successor; therefore, at first, the right of making testaments extended no further, unless the person in whose favor it was made refused to succeed, in which case another might be appointed. This was a further extension of the right. Again, if a man died and left his sister's¹ son as his heir, so that the estate might not go to distant relations, the testator was allowed to state that if the ward (pupil) died before a certain age, the estate would go to another person. This was called <term>pupillar substitution</term>². In this way, property rights were still further extended.

<note>Handwritten bracket in left margin alongside the start of the following paragraph.</note>

The greatest of all extensions of property is that through <term>entails</term>. To give a man power over his property after his death is very significant, but it is nothing compared to an extension of this power to the end of time. In the early stages of society, the condition of families was very different from what it is at present. As the wife was subject to the husband and, at best, only on the level of a daughter, she seldom added anything to the husband’s estate except through her own industry. However, once female succession was established and women came to possess fortunes, they would not marry without a prior agreement by which they ensured they were treated well and stipulated that some part of their fortune should go to their own relatives after their death. From this, a new type of marriage based on agreement arose, which made the parties equally independent³. This major change in domestic affairs would naturally be complained about at first, and since the ultimate cause of it was the succession of females, people would try to prevent them from becoming wealthy. For this reason, a law was made at Rome to return matters to their ancient status, called the <term>Voconian law</term>. To evade this law, a <term>*fidei commissum*</term> was invented, by which, when

<meta>This page continues the history of testamentary succession, moving from the concept of substitution to the development of entails, referencing both Roman law and the influence of the clergy in medieval Europe.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>PRIVATE LAW</header>
<columns>2</columns>

If a man wanted to leave his estate to a person whom the law would not allow, [he left it to someone else] and took his solemn promise that he would transfer it to the person for whom he intended it. Augustus made a law obliging the trustee always to restore it, and appointed a [fidei-]commissary praetor for that purpose¹. The person to² whom the estate was left was called *heres fiduciarius*, and the person to whom it was to be restored was called *fide[i] commissarius*. Thus, property rights were extended beyond the first successor, and once this step was gained, they easily advanced further and introduced entails.

Entails were first introduced into modern law by the clergy, whose education made them familiar with Roman customs³. As they were the preachers of this doctrine, they naturally became the interpreters and executors of wills until Theodosius and Valentinian⁴ took that power from them. In England, William the Conqueror restored it to the clergy⁵.

By the customs of our country⁶, if a man leaves a wife and children, he can dispose of only a third of his estate by will; and if he leaves a wife without children, only a half. After the introduction of the feudal system, lands could only be disposed of by will in the same manner as military services: with the consent of the superior. Originally in England, there were no entails by will, but by

⁴ <note>The manuscript reads "Theodosius Valentinus." It is difficult to account for the statement in the text, which enormously exaggerates the powers of the clergy. Justinian considered it clearly absurd that they should attempt

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>124</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>
<columns>2</columns>

Tenure. A man held an estate for himself and his heirs, but if he had no heirs he could not <term>alienate</term> <note>transfer or sell</note> it; it returned to the <term>superior</term>. But if he had heirs, he could alienate it, and thus the lord was deprived of his right of <term>reversion</term>. A law <note>Statute of Westminster II, 13 Edward I, chapter 1. *Regarding conditional gifts*.</note> was afterward made to secure this right.

On the whole, nothing can be more absurd than perpetual <term>entails</term>. In them, the principle of testamentary succession can by no means take place. Piety to the dead can only take place when their memory is fresh in the minds of men: (a power to dispose of estates forever is manifestly absurd.) The earth and the fullness of it belong to every generation, and the preceding one can have no right to bind it up from <term>posterity</term> <note>“In the present state of Europe . . . nothing can be more completely absurd [than entails]. They are founded upon the most absurd of all suppositions: the supposition that every successive generation of men does not have an equal right 

<column-break/>

to the earth and to all that it possesses; but that the property of the present generation should be restrained and regulated according to the fancy of those who died perhaps 500 years ago.” *Wealth of Nations*, book 3, chapter 2, volume 1, page 388. “Absurd” occurs twice, as in the text above.</note>; such an extension of property is quite unnatural. The gradual progress of entails was due to people not knowing how far the right of the dead might extend, if they had any right at all. The utmost extent of entails should be limited to those who are alive at the person’s death, for he can have no affection for those who are unborn. Entails are disadvantageous to the improvement of the country, and those lands where they have never occurred are always best cultivated: heirs of entailed estates do not have it as their goal to cultivate lands, and often they are not able to do it. A man who buys land has this entirely in mind, and in general, the new purchasers are the best cultivators.

<summary>This page argues against the practice of perpetual entails, stating that each generation should have an equal right to the earth and that tying up land for the deceased's wishes hinders agricultural improvement.</summary>

### [§ 5. *Fifth way of acquiring Property: Voluntary Transference.*]

In voluntary transference, two things are required: first, a declaration of the intention of both the person who transfers and the person to whom it is transferred; second, the actual delivery of the thing. In most cases, the first of these is not binding without the latter, because there is no right without possession. If a man indeed has borrowed a thing and afterwards purchases it, there is no need for delivery, for it is already in his possession. Before possession, you can have no right to the thing, though you may have a right to make the man keep his promise or contract. If I buy a horse from a man, and before delivery he sells it to a third person, I cannot demand the horse from the possessor, but only from the person who sold it. But if it has been delivered, I can claim it from any person. Property, therefore, cannot be transferred without <term>tradition</term> or delivery. Grotius indeed justly observes that in the transference of a pledge, there is no need for delivery, because in this case the thing is already in the man's possession <note>*De iure belli et pacis*, book ii. chapter viii. § 25.</note>. In France, if a man declares his purpose to make a donation and dies before the delivery, the donation goes to the heir. This was also a custom among the Visigoths. In transferring the property of lands and other large objects, what constitutes possession is not so easy to determine. As there cannot be an actual delivery, in our country a symbolical delivery is used: an ear or sheaf of grain signifies the whole field; a stone and turf signify the estate to the center of the earth; and the keys of the door signify the house. By Scottish law, if there is a transference of several estates, the purchaser must be <term>infeft</term> in each. By English law, <term>infeftment</term> in one

<summary>This section explains that voluntary transfer of property requires both a declaration of intent and physical delivery (tradition). It also describes the use of symbolical delivery for large properties like land and houses in Scottish and English law.</summary>

serves for all <note>That is, in the same county.</note> when done in the presence of the county court <note>This should probably read "as the feoffment and giving of livery was anciently done in the presence of the county court." See Bacon, *Abridgement*, under "Feoffment," volume 2, page 492; Coke, *Littleton*, page 253 a.</note>. In Scotland, it must be done on the land; in England, it is enough if it is done in view of it. In addition to delivery, a charter or writing, showing the terms on which the transfer was made, is also necessary for security. Until the custom was abolished by a recent statute <note>In Scotland. The "recent statute" is 20 George II, chapter 50.</note>, no vassal or possessor had the right to alienate his estate without the consent of the superior. Since he held it for military service, it was required that the estate be resigned to the superior, who then transferred it to the purchaser, as it was proper that the superior's vassal should be of his own choosing. Afterwards, however, it became necessary to accept creditors, and this was often used as a means to evade the law. The seller would give a bond for a sum of borrowed money without mentioning that it was a sale; by this means, the lands were legally awarded to the creditor, and the lord was obliged to accept him as his vassal. In like manner, as the tenant was liable to oppression from a new superior, the lord could not dispose of his estate without the consent of his vassal. If, therefore, either of them alienated any part of their estates without the other’s consent, his right was forfeited.

The duty of vassals to their lords lasted longer in Scotland than in England, which may be explained by the difference in their governments; for the government of England always favored democracy, while that of Scotland favored aristocracy. Once society was fully established, there was no need for mutual consent, because the tenant was protected by the law, regardless of who the lord was.

In the time of the civil wars <note>The Wars of the Roses between the Lancastrians and Yorkists.</note>, a new type of delivery took place. When a person transferred his estate to another

### [§ 6. Of Servitudes.]

The second category of real rights is servitudes or burdens that one person has on the property of another. These rights were originally personal, as they were established by a contract between the individuals. If it is necessary for me to have a road to the market town, and someone's estate lies between me and it, I must negotiate with him for the privilege of a road through it. This contract produces only a personal right, even if I should bind him not to sell this estate without that burden. However, this caused an inconvenience: if the land was sold and the new owner refused the road, I could not sue him based on a personal right against the previous owner. Before I can reach the new purchaser, I must sue the person from whom I obtained the right, who then must sue the person to whom he sold it. If the land has passed through several hands, this process is very tedious and inconvenient. To remedy this, the law made servitudes real rights, enforceable *<term>a quocumque possessore</term>*.

Servitudes were *<term>rusticae</term>*, such as the right of a road to the town or the river and the right of grazing a certain number of cows on another person's pasture, or *<term>urbanae</term>*, such as the right to lean the beams of my house on your gable, the right to require the owner of the lower story to make his wall strong enough to support mine, and so on. These are all naturally personal rights and are only made "real" by lawyers. Life rents on estates and many other things are also servitudes, and are properly personal. Feudal burdens were only personal rights; therefore, every new vassal had to renew his homage and his promise of fealty.

<summary>This page explains the legal transition of servitudes from personal contractual rights to "real rights" that remain with the property regardless of ownership. It distinguishes between rural and urban servitudes and notes that feudal duties were originally personal obligations rather than property burdens.</summary>
<keywords>servitudes, real rights, personal rights, property law, rusticae, urbanae, feudal burdens, vassal, homage, fealty</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on feudal obligations and the transition of property rights from personal agreements to real rights, moving into the specifics of forfeitures and securities.</meta>

In the beginning of feudal law, if the proprietor did not perform his duty in every respect, he forfeited his feu; in the same way, if the tenant encroached on his lord's grounds, the land he had feued returned to the superior. The right of the vassal is based on the charter of the superior, and every part of it must be fulfilled; furthermore, every new possessor must renew the obligation. When tenants became independent and held real property, they were said to have the *dominium directum*, not the *dominium utile*<note>Due to a mistake by the lecturer or the reporter, 'directum' and 'utile' have been swapped. The text should read 'the dominium utile, not the dominium <column-break/> directum.' Dalrymple, *Feudal Property*, pp. 200, 238.</note>.

### [§ 7. Of Pledges and Mortgages.]

Pledges and mortgages are specific securities for the payment of debts. At first, they could not be claimed as real rights, though later the law considered them as such. Pledges strictly concern movable subjects, and mortgages concern immovable ones; if a pledge is not redeemed by a certain time, it is forfeited. As people in difficult circumstances are naturally inclined to be negligent, the negligence of debtors among the Romans led to the *lex commissaria*, by which the creditor was empowered to seize the pledge and return the surplus if there was any. According to English law, if no day is specified, the pledge falls to the pawntaker upon the death of the pawner<note>Bacon, *Abridgement*, s. v. Bailment, vol. i. p. 239.</note>. Regarding immovable property, lands are mortgaged but not delivered, and in case of failure to pay, they are forfeited. Roman law and our own are very similar on this point. If payment is not made within a few months after being demanded, the creditor claims the land for the total sum and the penalty incurred; however, his property is not secure without long possession, for the proprietor has the power to redeem it within a reasonable time. But, as upon redemption much trouble must be

<term>Hypothecs</term> are another kind of pledges really arising from contract, but made real rights by the civil law. By them, in ancient times, the landlord was empowered to detain the furniture and whole stock of the tenant if he became bankrupt, and could claim them <term>a quocumque possessore</term>. This arose from the practice of keeping tenants by <term>steel-bow</term>, by which the whole stock on the farm belonged to the landlord. At present, the landlord has only a right of preference, and we do not have as many hypothecs as the Romans had.

All pledges are naturally personal rights, and are only made real by the civil law.

->[§ 8. *Of Exclusive Privileges.*]<-

Exclusive privileges are the last division of real rights. Among these is the right of inheritance, which is not a creature of the civil law, but arises from nature. The heir, prior to any other person, has a privilege of demanding what belonged to the deceased, and after he is admitted as heir, it becomes his real property. Again, if a person starts a wild beast, he has an exclusive privilege of pursuing it, and any person who interferes with the chase is liable to punishment<note>The manuscript erroneously reads "not" here.</note> because he breaks in upon his exclusive privilege. In the year 1701, an English man-of-war engaged with a French merchant fleet under convoy, which was just about to fall into their hands, when a Scottish privateer came and carried off the prize. A lawsuit commenced, and the Scottish privateer was declared guilty of a breach of property, but upon strict inquiry, we shall find that it was only a breach of privilege<note>No such case appears to have occurred in 1701. <meta>This page continues the discussion of exclusive privileges, moving from those that arise from nature to those created by civil law, such as monopolies and corporate rights. It then introduces the next major topic: personal rights, beginning with the foundation of contracts.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
# PART I: JUSTICE

privileges arise from nature; they are generally the creations of the civil law. Such are monopolies and all privileges of corporations, which, although they might once have been beneficial to the interest of the country, are now harmful to it. The wealth of a country consists in the abundance and cheapness of provisions, but the effect of these privileges is to make everything expensive. When a group of butchers has the sole privilege of selling meat, they may agree to set the price at whatever they please, and we must buy from them whether the meat is good or bad. Even this privilege is not an advantage to the butchers themselves, because the other trades are also formed into corporations; if they sell beef at a high price, they must buy bread at a high price. But the great loss is to the public, for whom everything is made less accessible, and all types of work are more poorly done; towns are not well populated, and the suburbs have grown. The privilege, however, of selling a new book or a new machine for fourteen years does not have such a negative tendency; it is a proper and adequate reward for merit. It should be observed that a right to servitudes and exclusive privileges may be acquired by prescription.

<margin>Thus far for the different kinds of real rights: we proceed now to personal rights, which arise either from contract, quasi-contract, or delinquency.</margin>

### [§ 9.] *Of Contract.*

The obligation of performance which arises from contract is founded on the reasonable expectation pro—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of personal rights, specifically focusing on the foundations and historical development of contract law as a subset of justice.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>PRIVATE LAW</header>

duced by a promise, which differs considerably from a mere declaration of intention. Though I say I have a mind to do such a thing for you, yet if on account of some occurrences I do not do it, I am not guilty of a breach of promise. A promise is a declaration of your desire that the person to whom you promise should depend on you for the performance of it. Consequently, the promise produces an obligation, and the breach of it is an injury.

Breach of contract is naturally the slightest of all injuries, because we naturally depend more on what we possess than what is in the hands of others. A man robbed of five pounds thinks himself much more injured than if he had lost five pounds by a contract. Accordingly, in primitive ages, crimes of all kinds—except those that disturb the public peace—are punished lightly, and society is far advanced before a contract can support a legal action or the breach of it be redressed. The causes of this were the little importance of contracts in those times and the uncertainty of language.

The first contracts that supported legal action would be those where the damage done was very great, and where there could be no doubt that the person once intended to perform. Accordingly, among the ancients, promises entered into with great solemnity first supported legal action. Among them, no <term>stipulation</term> could be made unless the contractors were personally present, and no promissory note in writing was binding. Just as no promises by the <term>Roman law</term> supported legal action without a stipulation, so by English law a <term>consideration</term> or cause for the promise was at first necessary to make it obligatory. It was thought contrary to good manners to insist on a promise: if a man promised a certain sum with his daughter, there is a consideration, and therefore he was obliged to perform it; but if he promised it with any other man's daughter, it was *sine causa*, and, unless she was a relative, it could not support legal action. If I made you a promise, it did not support legal action, but

If I promised again not to forget my previous promise, the second promise was binding, and the first promise was the consideration that made it so <note>This seems to be an exaggerated or misreported account of the old rule: "wherever a defendant is under a moral obligation, or is liable in conscience and equity to pay, that is a sufficient consideration." Cowper, *Reports*, p. 294. Thus, a man may make himself liable for otherwise irrecoverable debts contracted when he was a minor, and a liability barred by the statute of limitations may be similarly revived.</note>.

Under civil law, the first promises that supported a legal action were those entered into in the presence of a court where there could be no doubt of the intention; accordingly, a <term>recognizance</term> of every promise was recorded before some court. A recognizance occurs when a debtor comes before a court with the creditor and acknowledges that he owes him a specific sum. A copy of this acknowledgment was given to the creditor and another was kept by the clerk; whenever the creditor produced this, if it was found to match the other, he could sue for his money. Later, a recognizance before the magistrate of a staple town served the same purpose <note>Bacon, *Abridgement*, s. v. Execution, vol. ii. pp. 330-332.</note>.

The next contracts that supported legal action were the <term>*contractus reales*</term> (real contracts), or those which were entered into by the delivery of an item to be returned in its original form, in kind, or in value. These are of four types: the <term>*mutuum*</term>, <term>*commodatum*</term>, <term>*depositum*</term>, and <term>*pignus*</term> <note>*Instit.* lib. iii. tit. xiv.</note>.

The <term>*mutuum*</term> occurs when I lend something to be returned in equivalent value, such as money. This soon supported legal action.

<term>*Commodatum*</term> occurs when the item itself is to be restored, such as a borrowed horse.

<term>*Depositum*</term> occurs when an item is entrusted to another person’s care but not for their use.

<term>*Pignus*</term> is a security for debt.

All of these supported legal action before the consensual contracts, which are also four: namely, buying and selling, letting

and hiring, partnership, and commission. In buying, if the contract is not fulfilled, you lose your earnest money. Letting and hiring once included leases, daily wages, building, and almost everything regarding society. If the contract of commission was performed for free, it could not at first support any legal action; but if a reward was given, it was nearly the same as the *commodatum*. If a small price is paid for the loan of an item, it becomes letting and hiring. The *mutuum* does not imply interest, and in a bond, unless the interest is specified, it will carry none.

Besides these, there was in Roman law what was called a *pactum nudum*, which was a bare promise without any consideration; this provided an exception or defense against the legal action of the pursuer <note>‘A bare agreement does not produce an obligation, but it does produce an exception.’ *Digest*, *book ii. title xiv. § 7.*</note>. As contracts deprive men of that liberty which every man wishes to enjoy, a very small defense could set them free. Originally, no contracts were sued before any court but the ecclesiastical court, but they gradually moved to civil courts <note>‘No contracts were’ should be ‘some cases of breach of faith were not.’</note>. The canon <note>The manuscript reads ‘common.’</note> law, which judged based on principles of honor and virtue, obliged men to perform even those promises that were made for free. This was imitated by civil law; and by our law, if a promise is clearly proven, the person who promises must perform it. In general, the law only provided actions for damages until the Court of Chancery was introduced. It is indeed the natural purpose of a court to redress injuries, and accordingly, if a person refused to perform his contract, he was only obliged to pay for the loss which the other had sustained; but the Court of Chancery forced the person to perform the agreement.

Nothing can be more different than the present and ancient state of contracts. Execrations and the most

solemn ceremonies were scarcely thought sufficient to secure the performance of a contract; drinking blood and water mixed, bleeding one another, promising before the altar, breaking a straw, and a number of other ceremonies to impress the mind were invented. At present, almost anything will make a contract obligatory.

There are some questions concerning contracts frequently debated by lawyers, especially one in the case where the coin happens to be debased <note>Pufendorf, *De iure naturae et gentium*, book v. chapter vii. § 6.</note>. If I borrow £100 when the coin is 4 oz. to the pound, and it is afterwards debased to 2 oz., should I pay £100 of the new coin or £200? When the government makes any alteration in the coin, it is to answer some urgent necessity. In 1705, the crown of France had a demand for ten million and could raise only five <note>The date in the manuscript is not clearly written and might be 1703, 1705, or 1706. It is probable that the reference is to the recoinage of 1708–9 mentioned in Melon, *Essai politique* <column-break/> *sur le commerce*, 1734 (in Daire’s *Économistes financiers*, p. 721), and Dutot, *Réflexions politiques sur les finances et le commerce*, 1738 (in the same collection, p. 797), though on that occasion the increase was only 25 percent.</note>. They revalued the coin and paid the ten with five. As the government allows private persons to pay with the new coin, the injury is not great. The debasement of the coin temporarily cheapens all commodities and provisions, as all are paid in the new coin, and therefore the purposes of money may be served by the new as well as the old coin <note>See below, Part ii. Division ii. § 8.</note>.

### [§ 10. Of *Quasi-Contract*.]

*Quasi-contract* is founded on the duty of restitution. If you find a watch on the road, you are obligated to restore it by the right of property, because a man does not lose property with possession. But if you and I settle accounts, and you pay me a sum which we both think is due, but you afterwards find you did not owe that sum, how

<summary>This page explores the transition from ceremonial contracts to modern obligations, the legal implications of currency debasement on debt repayment, and the definition of quasi-contracts based on restitution.</summary>

<keywords>contract law, currency debasement, coinage, quasi-contract, restitution, property rights, Pufendorf, legal history</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the principles of restitution and quasi-contracts, specifically addressing how obligations arise without a formal contract when one party benefits from another's loss or necessary actions.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>PRIVATE LAW</header>

How will you claim it? You cannot ask for it as your property, for you transferred that sum; nor can you claim it by contract, for there was never one made between us <note>‘For he who gives money with the intention of paying appears to give it so that he may dissolve a business relationship rather than enter into one.’ *Institutes*, book iii, title xxvii, § 6.</note>. Yet it is evident that I am a gainer by your loss, and therefore restitution is due.

In the same manner, if a man were called away by a sudden order of the state without leaving an attorney to manage a lawsuit he had ongoing, and a friend undertakes this office without a commission—as the defense is necessary and the undertaking is prudent—restitution of his expenses is due. The *actiones contrariae* of Roman law were founded on the same principle. If you lent me a horse that cost me extraordinary expenses, under the contract of *commodate* (a loan for use), you could demand your horse back in the same condition in which you lent it, but I could claim my extraordinary expenses through an *actio contraria*.

The same principle applies in many other cases. If a person borrows money and obtains three of his acquaintances as sureties for him, jointly and severally, and if he becomes bankrupt, the creditor pursues the most solvent surety. That surety then has a claim, based on the duty of restitution, against the other two for their respective thirds. Scots law carries this even further. If a bankrupt had two estates and two creditors, *A* and *B*: *A* has security on both estates, while *B* has security only on the best one. *A* has the liberty of drawing his money from whichever estate he pleases, and he draws from that on which *B* has his security. As *B* is cut out in this case, the law obliges *A* to give up his security on the other estate to *B*. The same was the case in Roman law with regard to guardianship (tutory).

### [§ 11. *Of Delinquency.*]

We come now to the third kind of personal rights, namely, those *ex delicto* (arising from a wrong).

Delicts are of two kinds, as they arise *ex dolo* (from deceit) when
