<meta>This page continues the discussion on the principles of justice and the foundations of punishment, transitioning from the concept of restitution to the moral psychology of resentment and its role in legal penalties.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

There is a blameworthy intention, or *ex culpa*, when they are done through a culpable negligence.

Injury naturally excites the resentment of the spectator, and the punishment of the offender is reasonable as far as the impartial spectator can go along with it. This is the natural measure of punishment. It is to be observed that our initial approval of punishment is not founded upon a regard for public utility, which is commonly assumed to be the foundation of it. It is our sympathy with the resentment of the sufferer which is the real principle. That it cannot be utility is evident from the following example. Wool in England was conceived to be the source of public wealth, and it was made a capital crime to export that commodity¹. Yet though wool was exported as before and men were convinced that the practice was harmful, no jury and no evidence could be obtained against the offenders. The exportation of wool is naturally no crime, and men could not be brought to consider it punishable by death². In the same manner, if a sentinel is put to death for leaving his post—even though the punishment is just and the injury that might have followed could have been very great—mankind can never sympathize with this punishment in the same way as if he had been a thief or a robber.

Resentment not only prompts punishment, but points out the manner of it. Our resentment is not gratified unless the offender is punished for the particular offense done to ourselves, and unless he is made aware that it is for that action. A crime is always the violation of some right, natural or acquired, real or personal. The non-performance of a contract indeed

---

² The preamble of 7 and 8 William III, chapter 28, quoted in *Wealth of Nations*, book IV, chapter VIII, volume II, page 232, says that owing to the severity of the penalty under 13 and 14 Charles II, chapter 18, ‘the prosecution of offenders has not been so effectively carried out.’

<summary>This page argues that the true basis of punishment is the impartial spectator's sympathy with a sufferer's resentment, rather than public utility. This is illustrated by the historical failure to enforce the death penalty for wool exportation and the different moral reactions to military desertion versus common theft.</summary>

is not a crime, unless it is through some fraudulent intention.

The greatest crime that can be committed against any person is murder, for which the natural punishment is death—not as a compensation, but as a reasonable retaliation. In every civilized nation, death has been the punishment for the murderer; but in barbarous nations, a monetary compensation was accepted because the government was then weak and did not dare to interfere in the quarrels of individuals, except by way of mediation. Particularly in the age of hunters, there was little more than the name of authority, and a man of superior influence could do no more than persuade the parties to reach an agreement. When one man killed another, the whole society met and advised one party to give, and the other to accept, compensation. In America <note>The author refers to the Indigenous peoples of North America as described in 18th-century travelogues.</note>, when one member of a family kills another, society does not interfere with them, as this cannot harm the peace of the society; they only take notice of it when one family attacks another. It was a long time before the government could summon a man before them and tell him what he must do, because it was a long time before people would submit to such absolute authority.<insert>)</insert>

In the laws of all nations, we see the remains of this ancient state of weakness. When government became more powerful, the murderer was obliged to provide compensation not only to the relatives of the deceased but also to the public, who were put to the trouble of granting him their protection on that occasion against the revenge of those who were involved. This was the state of criminal law among the Germans during the decline of the Roman Empire. The Germans were much more advanced than the Americans are today. Although they seldom punished with death, they still seemed to make the punishment somewhat proportional to the crime. A price was set

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical development of criminal law and punishment, specifically examining how fines for murder were determined by social status and how the sovereign's authority over justice grew.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

on every person according to his social standing. There was one price paid for killing the king, and another for killing a slave. The compensation was proportional to the dignity of the person and of his relatives. The amount paid to the prince for his intervention was increased or decreased in the same proportion. The fine for killing a man belonging to a lord was higher than for killing someone belonging to a minor baron. Disturbing the king's peace resulted in a greater fine than disturbing the peace of a baron or lord. If the offender refused to pay the compensation, he was left to the resentment of the injured party; if he was unable to pay, he was forced to implore the assistance of his friends. Since the compensation was not adequate for the offense, the government, once it gained strength, took this additional compensation for itself as the price of the offender's freedom. From this, the sovereign acquired the right to pardon criminals, for naturally he has no more right to pardon a crime than to cancel an unpaid debt.

In ancient times, a crime was considered in two ways: as being committed against the injured family and against the peace. The government had the exclusive right to punish those who had disturbed the peace and killed any of the king's vassals. The compensation paid to the government was later changed into capital punishment. After the king's pardon, the offender was free, and the relative had no right to pursue him. In England, the offender can be punished on behalf of the relative as well as for the king. When an appeal¹ is made to the king, he cannot grant a pardon; however, appeals are rarely or never used, as they are difficult to initiate. If a man was murdered, no one but the wife could pursue an appeal, or, if she was an accomplice, the legal

<meta>This page continues the discussion of legal appeals, specifically noting their technical difficulty and strict procedural requirements, before transitioning into a classification of different types of homicide under English law.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>PRIVATE LAW</header>

heir¹. Any mistake in the process, such as a misspelled word, stopped the procedure ², for the statute of amendment—which permitted courts to overlook errors—did not extend to appeals. In former times, appeals were often made in cases of maiming, hurting, etc.³

There are several kinds of murder according to English law. The word originally signified stealth, as the crime was usually committed in private. Afterwards, felonious⁴ killing of every kind was called murder, and compensation was made for it accordingly. Murder arises either from malice prepense, or from sudden provocation, or from chance *per infortunium*. Of these, the first alone is properly called murder; the second is manslaughter; and the last is chance medley, which is often excusable and often justifiable. Murder committed *se defendendo* occurs when two persons quarrel, and one is obliged to kill the other for his own safety. This is excusable, not justifiable homicide. Justifiable homicide is of two kinds. First, in defense of one’s person, goods, or house. It differs from homicide *se defendendo* in this regard: that there is no quarrel, but rather an attack on the highway or in a man’s house. Second, homicide is justifiable when committed in support of a constable or officer of justice.

These are the different species of murder and homicide; we shall next show the nature of each. When a person lies in wait for another and kills him, it is plainly murder. It is the same when a man kills another without provocation. Under English law, there is no provocation without a physical blow; no words or threats are sufficient. However, if a man gives you a blow, and you return it and kill him, it is not murder, but manslaughter. If a man is shooting at tame fowl, or performing any other criminal action,

and without intending it, kill a man, it is murder. Wherever there is any appearance of malice or forethought, it is murder. If a person kills another in the afternoon for some provocation received in the morning, it is murder; but if he has only retreated a few steps and returned to do it immediately, it is not murder, but manslaughter. Homicide *se defendendo* <note>in self-defense</note> is not punishable if there was no possibility of escape, but if a man had time to retreat and draw his sword, it is punishable, because he might have escaped¹.

The Scottish law makes no distinction between manslaughter and murder². In England, manslaughter was introduced by what is called "benefit of clergy." When civil government increased in authority, the punishments of crimes were made more severe so that the peace might be less disturbed. The clergy pleaded that this was not agreeable to the word of God, and as they derived their authority from Jesus Christ and the Pope, they would answer before no civil court. They claimed that the scripture did not consider any crime where there was no malice or forethought as murder, and this they proved from Deuteronomy 19. When any <insert>clerical</insert> person, therefore, had committed a crime, the bishop had the power to claim him and take him out of the hands of the secular power. If a person could get twelve people to swear for him, he was acquitted. If not, the bishop judged whether he was corrigible <note>capable of being corrected</note> or not. If he was incorrigible, he was degraded. The bishop could claim in this manner all clergy and beadles, wardens, or other persons who had

Our resentment naturally falls upon inanimate as well as animate objects, and in many places the sword or instrument that had killed any person was considered as execrable, and accordingly was destroyed, particularly among the Athenians. Deodand signifies to be given to the devil, by the same sort of metaphor that the scripture uses where it is said he blessed God in his heart, that is, he cursed him. Afterwards the clergy applied deodands to charitable uses. If a man was killed by an object at rest, only the part by which he was killed was forfeited.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of legal forfeitures and types of homicide, specifically focusing on the concept of "deodand" and the history of compensation for physical injuries across various legal traditions.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>PART I : JUSTICE</header>

If he was killed [by falling] from the wheel of a wagon standing still, only that wheel was <term>deodand</term>, but if the wagon was in motion, the whole team was forfeited. It was long questioned whether a ship was forfeited if a man was killed in it, but as sailors are so frequently exposed to danger, it was thought harsh that it should be ¹.

A person may also be injured in his body by dismemberment, mutilation, assault and battery, or restraint of his liberty. Maiming and mutilation originally, under <term>Roman law</term>, were compensated for in the same way as murder; and if the person was unable to pay the compensation, even with the assistance of his friends, he was handed over to the person he had maimed, to be maimed in the same manner, as we know from the <term>Salic law</term>, which gives us the form of their procedure. In the same manner, all injuries among many nations—particularly among the <term>Lombards</term>—were settled through compensation; they paid a certain amount for a tooth, significantly more if it was a front tooth, and a certain amount for two teeth. However, what is very remarkable is that even if twenty teeth were knocked out, the injured person could claim no more than the price of three. They had a precise sum for every member of the body. Among the Romans, if a man could not pay his <term>composition</term>, he was obliged to provide satisfaction by the <term>law of retaliation</term>; he received as many blows as he gave. An eye went for an eye, and a tooth for a tooth. This custom continued for a long time and is generally reasonable, but in some cases, it is not appropriate. If a man had his arm broken while wrestling, it was harsh that another person's arm should be broken for it in cold blood. In some cases, it was impractical, such as when a man causes an abortion in a woman; he could not be punished in the same manner. This custom gradually faded out, and <term>pecuniary fines</term>, based on the circumstances of the person who was to pay them, were introduced; the <term>praetor</term> at Rome ensured they were collected. However, in some countries, the old custom continued longer, and there are remains of it—

By the Coventry Act, maiming in the face out of malice or forethought was punished with death². The reason for this was that Sir John Coventry had spoken impertinently against the king in parliament. The Prince of Wales, along with some others—probably not without the king’s permission—lay in wait for him and cut his ears and his face³. Parliament immediately enacted that maiming in the face with forethought should be punishable by death. However, no one was ever executed under this law except for a man named Cook, who lay in wait to murder his brother but did not carry it out, only maiming him in the face. He was therefore found guilty of deliberate malice under the Coventry Act. He pleaded that his intention was to murder, not to maim, but the court, based on the instrument he used, found that he intended to maim as well as murder⁴.

A person may also be injured by assault and battery. When a person is put in fear of bodily harm, it is assault; and when he is actually struck, it is battery. Originally, no verbal assault resulted in punishment unless there was also

<meta>This page continues the discussion on personal injuries, transitioning from the definition of assault and battery to the legal protections for personal liberty, bail procedures, and the specific applications of the Habeas Corpus Act in England versus Scotland.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>

a shaking of the fist, drawing a weapon, or something of this kind. A <term>composition</term> was the first punishment for these crimes, but now it is a fine and imprisonment.

A person may further be injured in their body by the restraint of their liberty; therefore, the laws of every country are particularly careful to secure it. No magistrate in this country has an arbitrary power of imprisonment. It is indeed reasonable that he should have the power to imprison when there are grounds for suspicion, though an innocent person may sometimes suffer slightly because of it. Nothing is more difficult than perfectly securing liberty. If the person can present circumstances to alleviate the suspicion, he may be released on bail, unless it is a capital crime. If the bail is not sufficient, it is unjust for the magistrate to accept it; but if it is, he is punishable if he does not. If a person is wrongfully kept in prison beyond the time when he ought to have been tried, he receives a certain amount of money per day according to his social status.

In England, if a person is confined the day after the <term>assizes</term>, forty¹ days later he may have the benefit of the <term>Habeas Corpus Act</term>; that is, he may be taken to London at his own expense, but if he cannot afford this, he must wait until the next <term>assizes</term>. In Scotland, there is no need for the <term>Habeas Corpus Act</term>. A person may be tried by the <term>sheriff</term> if he chooses, and in any case, can be taken to Edinburgh to the king's court. All this is for the security of liberty in free governments, but in despotic governments, the will of the magistrate is law.

It is to be observed regarding what is done through fear that a bond given under this principle is not binding; no obligation is valid unless the person acted voluntarily. However, if a person is threatened with prosecution², and gives a bond to avoid it, the bond is valid, and the fear is not considered as a *metus iniustus*.

<note>² That is, of course, prosecuted.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on personal injuries, moving from physical assault and the protection of liberty to crimes involving the violation of women and injuries to reputation through affronts, slander, and libel.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>PRIVATE LAW</header>
<page-num>145</page-num>

A rape or forcible marriage is a capital crime, because the woman is so dishonored that no other punishment can be a sufficient retaliation. Although forcible marriage is forbidden by law, if the woman afterwards consents, her relatives can have no appeal; yet the king may still pursue the case.

A person may be injured in their reputation by affronts, by words, and by writings. An affront in company is a real injury; if the affront is offered in words, it is a verbal injury; if in writing, it is a written injury. In all these, the law gives redress. Affronts under the old law were punished in the same manner as assault and battery. Affronts in company are most atrocious crimes; the trifling fine of five or ten pounds is by no means an adequate compensation for them. Where the law denies justice, we are naturally led to take it ourselves. This introduced duelling in Europe, which brings with it an additional injury: I must not only receive a box on the ear, but I am also obliged to expose my life or become altogether odious. It is to be observed that in Socrates’ time, the affront of calling someone a liar was little thought of; he does it himself without any ceremony.

Verbal injuries are redressed by both ancient and modern laws. When a person is accused by words, it warrants a trial before a court of justice. If he is accused of forgery, theft, or any crime—since he may be subjected to great damages—he is entitled to sufficient redress. In the same manner, if a person’s right or title is slandered, he suffers an injury. If I say you have no more right to your own house than I have, it is an injury, as it may incite those who have false claims to it. Even if it is true, this is only a mitigation and will not protect me from a prosecution. There are some offenses that are only prosecuted in spiritual courts, such as if a person calls a woman a whore.

Written injuries are subjected to severer punishments than verbal ones, as they involve more deliberate malice.

<page-num>146</page-num>

# PART I: JUSTICE

Abusive words in a libel provide grounds for legal action, even though the same words would not if they were only spoken. Libels and satires are punished according to the nature of the government. In aristocratic governments, they are punished severely. Minor princes may be completely ruined by abusive libels, whereas kings and ministers of state in a free country, being far beyond their reach, cannot be harmed by them. In <note>some, possibly the Greek,</note> governments, and in Rome for a long time, they were not punished. Augustus finally revived the law subjecting the authors to capital punishment <note>Tacitus, *Annals*, book 1, chapter 72.</note>. In general, people of status take no notice of such libels unless it is absolutely necessary to clear themselves of some crime.

A person may be injured in their estate, whether real or personal property. Regarding real estate, they may be injured in either their movable or immovable property. In their immovable property, they may be injured by arson or forcible entry. Arson is intentional <term>fire-raising</term> <note>The term "intentional fire-raising" is used for arson in Scottish law books. Bayne, *Institutions of the Criminal Law of Scotland*, p. 26.</note> either in the house of another person or in one’s own house in a way that affects someone else's property. By Roman, English, and Scottish law, this is punished as a capital offense. If the fire is caused by negligence, no punishment is inflicted. Forcible entry is the violent removal of a person from their estate. The laws are so strict on this matter that the person who was ousted may retake their property by force <note>It seems that under common law, a person <term>disseised</term> (deprived) of any lands or tenements—if they could not succeed by peaceful means—might lawfully regain possession by force.</note>. This was caused by feudal customs, under which it was very common for barons and their vassals to deprive one another of their possessions; this was the only method that could then be found to have the property restored. Afterward, it was enacted that if any person could prove

A person may be injured in their movable property in three ways: by theft, robbery, and piracy. Theft is the secret taking away of the property of another. This crime does not naturally provoke that degree of resentment which leads to capital punishment; and for a long time, it was not punished by death. Under the old Roman law, the thief was obliged to restore what he had taken, and to add to it as much more. If he stole a sheep, he restored two. There was, however, a peculiar distinction between the <term>fur manifestus</term> <note>a thief caught in the act</note> and <term>fur nec manifestus</term> <note>a thief not caught in the act</note>. The former, as he was taken with the goods on him, paid four times the value, and the latter only double the value of the things stolen. This they borrowed, it is said, from the Lacedaemonians <note>3 But Heineccius, *Roman Antiquities*, book iv. title i. § 12, says they borrowed it ‘*from Attic law*.’</note>, who taught their youth to steal and hide well, as they thought it improved them in that cunning which is necessary in war. However, the Lacedaemonians never encouraged the stealing of another's property. In their feasts, nothing was prepared for their young men, and it was expected that they should steal from the tables of their fathers what was sufficient for themselves; to steal such trifles as a crust of bread was allowed, but nothing else. The real reason for their punishing the <term>fur manifestus</term> more severely than another was that primitive nations punish crimes according to the degree of resentment they excite, and when the thief was caught in the act, their resentment was very high, and consequently disposed them to punish him severely. Since

Since the thirteenth century, this crime has been punished by death. The vassals of great lords were continually making raids into neighboring territories and carrying off plunder. When government was established, it naturally punished most severely those crimes to which men had the greatest tendency, and consequently endeavored to restrain this practice. The Emperor Barbarossa first made this crime a capital offense, and he was followed by all civilized nations, though undoubtedly the punishment is too severe; for a thief is but a petty, mean creature and does not excite a very high degree of resentment; he seems to be, in some degree, beneath this passion. According to old Scottish law, theft by a landed gentleman was considered treason <note>One circumstance regarding the offender—specifically, being a landed man—formerly made the punishment for this crime [theft] that of high treason. *Bayne, Institutions of the Criminal Law of Scotland, p. 46.*</note>, because the gentry were seen as the abettors and assistants of thieves and vagrants; and, as they made war on one another—which appeared to be an usurpation of sovereignty—they were considered guilty of treason. Under English law, any theft of less than a shilling was punished with the pillory, and anything above that with death. In Scotland, it requires a much greater sum <note>No sum was specified, as many circumstances besides value were taken into account.</note>. With us, nothing is considered theft unless it belongs to specific individuals. A person who stole deer in a forest or pigeons at a distance from a pigeon-house could not be punished until a recent statute <note>2 George III, chapter 29, or more probably an error for "recent statutes." *MacDouall, Institute, volume i, page 594; Bacon, Abridgement, under "Game."*</note>. Indeed, housebreaking was punished by death, even if not even a shilling’s value was carried off. Such punishments, however necessary they once were, are certainly now too severe. Government was at first weak and could not punish crimes, but was obliged to intervene in those cases in which the interest of society was concerned; but

<meta>This page continues the legal history of punishments, shifting from the specific penalties for theft to the historical evolution of capital crimes, including treason, robbery, piracy, and fraud.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>PRIVATE LAW</header>

When the law acquired more strength, it made punishments severe so that it might restrict the licentiousness of behavior which lax discipline had introduced. Accordingly, we find that the Laws of the Twelve Tables made almost every crime capital. In Europe, after the custom of compensation ended, they punished many crimes as treason; theft by a landed man, a servant killing his master, a curate killing his bishop, or a husband killing his wife were all <term>petty treason</term>. Afterward, only crimes concerning the state were considered treason, and this crime gradually reached its proper scope.

Robbery, as it subjects a man to the greatest bodily fear, is subject to the greatest punishment: no circumstance can save the robber, even if he should try to hide the injury by pretending to buy a man’s goods after he has forced him to sell them.

Piracy is punished even more severely.

A man may be injured in his personal estate by fraud or forgery. The natural punishment for <term>dolus malus</term> <note>deceit with evil intent</note> is not death, but some sort of public shame such as the pillory. Some frauds, however, because of the ease and security with which they may be committed and the loss which they cause, are justly subject to capital punishment. When an insured ship, for instance, is wrecked, it is difficult to prove that it was done by fraud; but if she is insured to her full value, there is a great temptation to wreck her, and therefore the law, in order to intimidate the merchant, made death the punishment. It was a question whether a ship should be insured for her value at the port from which she sets out, or at the port to which she is—

If a Glasgow merchant sends out a ship with £3000 worth of goods for Virginia, they are worth more than £4000 when they arrive there; and if the merchant were allowed to insure for this latter sum, he would have a great temptation to cause her shipwreck. He can expect no more profit even after he has undergone the expense of taking his goods to Virginia; he may encounter bad debtors, and yet he would lose nothing if paid by the insurers. In the same way, it was anciently a capital offense to steal anything from a plow, because it was so exposed¹.

In England, a bankrupt may receive a discharge upon surrendering himself and all his assets; however, since he has the power to defraud his creditors, if he does not give up everything he has, he is punishable by death². Forgery is also punished by death, and nobody complains that this punishment is too severe, because when contracts support legal action, property can never be secure unless the forging of false ones is restrained. However, the forgery of every deed is not a capital offense³, but only the forgery of deeds payable to the bearer on demand; this is because any forgery of a deed regarding the transfer of land may easily be discovered before any damage is done.

Perjury is not punished by death⁴.

As there are several ways of acquiring personal rights, so there are several ways in which they expire. First, by the payment of what is due by contract or quasi-contract, because the fulfillment of the obligation satisfies the other party. Secondly, by discharge or release, even though the debt is not paid. This also takes place with regard

# PRIVATE LAW

to crimes, for when the king or the injured person chooses to drop prosecution or to give a pardon, the person is free. Thirdly, by prescription. If a debt is not claimed within a certain time, the debtor is free. This is very reasonable, for if a debt is not claimed for a long time, the negligence of the debtor is encouraged. By Scotch law, if a person calls for neither the principal nor the interest of a bond for forty years, it very justly prescribes. No one of common prudence would neglect any part of his affairs for forty years if he ever intended to deal with them again. According to strict law, if the interest is demanded in the thirty-ninth year, the capital does not prescribe. Crimes likewise prescribe, and it is reasonable that they should, whether they are punished out of sympathy for the resentment of the sufferer, for public utility, or to satisfy the public. Resentment wears out in a few years, and a person who has behaved well for twenty years—the time fixed by our law—cannot be very dangerous to the public. Appeals under English law prescribe in one year¹; but an indictment does not prescribe so soon², because the king prosecutes for public security and not to gratify private resentment, and therefore the law favors his claim. At any rate, it would be unreasonable to prosecute a man for a crime committed forty years ago, because he may now be a completely different man from who he was then. Besides, the matter is quite forgotten, and the purpose of punishment and public example is entirely lost. Treason itself prescribes in a few years³. Due to a resentment in law, however, if a sentence has actually passed upon a person and he has made his escape, he may be executed on his former sentence; the escape is considered a new crime. In every country, if a person returns after twenty years, he is not troubled; it would be thought malicious for the officers of justice to interfere with him.

A few general observations on criminal law are all that remain on this subject.

Resentment seems to best account for the punishment of crimes. If a person fires a pistol down a street, even if he does no harm, public utility requires that he should be punished; but such crimes are, by the laws of every country, punished more lightly than if some mischief had followed. The reason is plain. Resentment never rises to any great height unless some injury is actually done; some things that are in themselves criminal are not punished unless some bad consequence follows. A man meets with little resentment for riding an unruly horse in the market-place, but if he kills anyone, resentment is very high. For the same reason, <term>deodands</term>, though they are inanimate objects, are considered detestable. In many cases, the resentment falls upon the very member of the body which perpetrated the

# PART II: OF POLICE

---

## ->[DIVISION I. CLEANLINESS AND SECURITY.]<-

POLICE is the second general division of jurisprudence. The name is French, and is originally derived from the Greek *πολιτεία*, which properly signified the policy of civil government, but now it only means the regulation of the subordinate parts of government, namely:—cleanliness, security and cheapness or plenty <note>Johnson (*Dictionary*, 1755) describes "police" as being of French origin, and mentions *πολιτεία* as the original of "policy." He defines "police" as "the regulation and government of a city or country, so far as it regards the inhabitants;" and "policy" as "the art of government, chiefly with respect to foreign powers."</note>. The former two—specifically, the proper method of carrying dirt from the streets, and the execution of justice as far as it concerns regulations for preventing crimes or the method of keeping a city guard—though useful, are too minor to be considered in a general discourse of this kind. An observation or two before we proceed to the third particular is all that is necessary.

We observe, then, that in cities where there is the most police and the greatest number of regulations concerning it, there is not always the greatest security. In Paris the regulations concerning police are so numerous as not to be contained in several volumes; in London there are
<column-break/>

<columns>2</columns>

only two or three simple regulations. Yet in Paris, scarcely a night passes without someone being killed, while in London, which is a larger city, there are scarcely three or four in a year. On this account, one would be inclined to think that the more police there is, the less security there is; but this is not the cause. In England as well as in France, during the time of the feudal government, and as late as Queen Elizabeth’s reign, great numbers of retainers were kept idle about the noblemen’s houses <note>Hume, *History of England under the House of Tudor*, vol. ii. p. 735. Compare above, p. 35.</note>, to keep the tenants in awe. These retainers, when turned out, had no other way of obtaining their subsistence but by committing robberies and living on plunder, which caused the greatest disorder. A remnant of feudal manners, still preserved in France, gives occasion to the difference. The nobility in Paris keep far more menial servants than ours do; these servants are often dismissed for their own conduct or through the caprice of their masters, and, being in the most destitute circumstances, are forced to commit the most dreadful crimes. In Glasgow, where almost nobody has more than one servant, there are fewer capital crimes than in Edinburgh. In Glasgow, there is not one in several years; but not a year passes in Edinburgh without some such disorders. Upon this principle, therefore, it is not so much the police that prevents the commission of crimes as having as few persons as possible living off of others. Nothing tends so much to corrupt mankind as dependency, while independence consistently increases the honesty of the people.

The establishment of commerce and manufactures, which brings about this independence, is the best police for preventing crimes <note>In *Wealth of Nations* bk. ii. ch. iii. vol. i. pp. 338-340, the want of

The nobility of Paris and London are no doubt much on the same level; but the common people of the former, being much more dependent, are not to be compared with those of the latter. For the same reason, the common people in Scotland differ from those in England, though the nobility too <note>That is, the nobility of England and Scotland as well as the nobility of Paris and London.</note> are much on a level.

#[DIVISION II. CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY.]

##[§ 1. *Of the Natural Wants of Mankind*.]

In the following part of this discourse, we are to confine ourselves to the consideration of cheapness or plenty, or—which is the same thing—the most proper way of procuring wealth and abundance. Cheapness is, in fact, the same thing as plenty. It is only because of the plenty of water that it is so cheap as to be obtained simply by lifting it; and it is on account of the scarcity of diamonds (for their real use seems not yet to be discovered) that they are so expensive ¹). To determine the most proper method of obtaining these conveniences, it will be necessary to show first what opulence consists of; and even before this, we must consider what the natural wants of mankind are that must be supplied. If we differ from common opinions, we shall at least give the reasons for our non-conformity.

Nature produces for every animal everything sufficient to support it without having to resort to improving the original production. Food, clothes, and lodging are all the wants of any animal whatever², and most

---

¹ The cheapness of water on account of its abundance is an ancient commonplace. Barbeyrac on Pufendorf, *On the Law of Nature and Nations*, book v, chapter i, § 4, quotes Plato, *Euthydemus* 304 B: "For that which is rare, Euthydemus, is precious; but water is the cheapest, even though it is the best, as Pindar said." The low value

<column-break/>

of useful water arising from its plentifulness is contrasted with the high value of useless diamonds arising from their scarcity by Law, *Money and Trade Considered*, 1705, chapter i, and by Joseph Harris, *Essay on Money and Coins*, 1757, part i, § 3.
² "The things necessary for the"

of the animal creation are sufficiently provided for by nature in all those wants to which their condition is subject. Such is the delicacy of man alone, however, that no object is produced exactly to his liking. He finds that in everything there is a need for improvement. Although the practice of savages shows that his food needs no preparation, yet being acquainted with fire, he finds that it can be rendered more wholesome and easily digested, and thereby may preserve him from many diseases which are very violent among them. But it is not only his food that requires this improvement; his delicate constitution is also hurt by the intemperature of the air he breathes, which, though not very capable of improvement, must be brought to a proper temperature for his body, and an artificial atmosphere prepared for this purpose. The human skin cannot endure the inclemency of the weather, and even in those countries where the air is warmer than the natural warmth of the constitution, and where they have no need of clothes, the skin must be stained and painted to be able to endure the hardships of the sun and rain. In general, however, the necessities of man are not so great but that they can be supplied by the unassisted labor of the individual. Everyone can provide all the above necessities for himself, such as animals and fruits for his food, and skins for his clothing.

As the delicacy of a man's body requires much greater provision than that of any other animal, the same—or rather the much greater—delicacy of his mind requires a still greater provision, to which all the different arts <note>are</note> subservient. Man is the only animal who possesses such a refinement that the very color of an object hurts him. Among different objects, a different division or arrangement of them pleases. The taste for beauty, which consists

<summary>Adam Smith explains that while nature satisfies the needs of animals, human beings require "improvement" through fire, clothing, and shelter to satisfy their physical and mental refinements. These unique human demands for beauty and comfort form the basis for the various arts and industries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the refinement of the human mind, explaining how aesthetic principles like variety, connection, and order drive human desire and the valuation of goods beyond basic survival needs.</meta>

->CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY<-

chiefly in the three following particulars: proper variety, easy connection, and simple order, is the cause of all this refinement. Nothing without variety pleases us; a long, uniform wall is a disagreeable object. Too much variety, such as the crowded objects of a parterre, is also disagreeable. Uniformity tires the mind; too much variety, increased too far, causes an excessive distraction of it. Easy connection also renders objects agreeable; when we see no reason for the contiguity of the parts, when they are without any natural connection, and when they have neither a proper resemblance nor contrast, they never fail to be disagreeable. If simplicity of order is not observed, so that the whole may be easily understood, it hurts the delicacy of our taste. Again, imitation and painting render objects more agreeable. To see trees, forests, and other such representations upon a plain is an agreeable surprise to the mind <note>1 ‘Of the Imitative Arts,’ *Essays*, p. 137.</note>. A variety of objects also renders them agreeable. What we are accustomed to every day affects us only very indifferently. Gems and diamonds are, on this account, much esteemed by us. In like manner, our pinchbeck and many of our toys <note>2 Johnson (*Dictionary*, 1755) gives as the first meaning of toy, ‘a petty commodity; a trifle; a <column-break/> thing of no value,’ and quotes from Abbot, ‘They exchange for knives, glasses, and such toys, a great abundance of gold and pearls.’</note> were so much valued by the Indians that, in bartering their jewels and diamonds for them, they thought they had made by far the better bargain.

### [§ 2. *That all the Arts are subservient to the Natural Wants of Mankind.*]

Those qualities, which are the ground of preference and which give occasion to pleasure and pain, are the cause of many insignificant demands, which we by no means stand

in need of. The entire industry of human life is employed not just in providing for our three humble necessities—food, clothing, and lodging—but in providing for the conveniences of life according to the refinement and delicacy of our taste. Improving and multiplying the materials which are the primary objects of our necessities gives rise to all the various arts.

Agriculture, whose primary object is the supply of food, introduces not only the tilling of the ground, but also the planting of trees, the production of flax, hemp, and countless other things of a similar kind. From these, in turn, various manufactures are introduced, which are highly capable of improvement. The metals dug from the depths of the earth furnish materials for tools, by which many of these arts are practiced. Commerce and navigation also serve the same purposes by collecting the products of these various arts. From these, other subsidiary arts are created: writing, to record the multitude of transactions, and geometry, which serves many useful purposes. Law and government, too, seem to have no other objective than this: they secure the individual who has increased his property so that he may peaceably enjoy its fruits. Through law and government, all the various arts flourish, and that inequality of fortune to which they give rise is sufficiently preserved. Through law and government, domestic peace is enjoyed, as is security from foreign invaders. Wisdom and virtue also derive their prestige from supplying these necessities. For as the establishment of law and government is the highest effort of human prudence and wisdom, the causes cannot have a different influence from that which the effects have. Besides, it is through the wisdom and integrity of those with whom we live that proper conduct is demonstrated to us, as well as the proper means of attaining it. Their valor defends us; their benevolence provides for us; the hungry are fed and the naked are clothed by the exercise of these divine qualities. Thus,

### [§ 3. *That Opulence arises from the Division of Labor.*]

In an uncivilized nation, and where labor is undivided ^1, everything is provided for that the natural needs of mankind require; yet, when the nation is cultivated and labor divided, a more generous provision is granted to them; and it is on this account that a common day laborer in Britain has more luxury in his way of living than an Indian ^2 sovereign. The wool coat he wears requires very considerable preparations—the wool-gatherer, the dresser, the spinner, the dyer, the weaver, the tailor, and many more, must all be employed before the laborer is clothed. The tools by which all this is accomplished employ a still greater number of skilled workers—the loom-maker, millwright, rope-maker, not to mention the bricklayer, the woodcutter, the miner, the smelter, the forger, the smith, and so on. Besides his dress, consider all his household furniture, his coarse linens, his shoes, his coals dug out of the earth or brought by sea, his kitchen utensils and different dishes, those who are employed in providing his bread and beer, the sower, the brewer, the reaper, the baker, his glass windows and the art required in preparing [them], without which our northern climate could hardly be inhabited. When we examine the conveniences of the day laborer, we find

It is the division of labor which increases the wealth of a country.

### [§ 4. *How the Division of Labor multiplies the Product.*]

We shall next show how this division of labor causes an increase in production, or—which is the same thing—how wealth arises from it. To demonstrate this, let us observe the effect of the division of labor in certain manufacturing processes. If all the parts of a pin were made by one man—if the same person dug the ore, smelted it, and cut the wire—it would take him a whole year to make one pin, and this pin must therefore be sold for the cost of his maintenance for that time, which, by a moderate calculation, would be at least six pounds for one pin. If the labor is divided so that the wire is already made, he will not make more than twenty per day, which, allowing ten pence for wages, makes the pin cost a half-penny<note>4 Manuscript reads ‘two pence,’ the copyist probably having misread ‘½d.’</note>. The pin-maker, therefore, divides the labor among a great number of different persons; the cutting, pointing, heading, and gilding are all separate professions. Two or three people are employed in making the head, one or two in putting it on, and so on, to the putting

When labor is divided in this way, and each person produces so much more in proportion, the surplus beyond what they need for their own maintenance is considerable. Each person can exchange this surplus for a fourth <note>5 ‘A fourth of’ is probably a copyist’s mistake for ‘4 times.’ The calculation is perhaps from Cantillon, who says that the labor of 25 adults is sufficient to provide all the necessities of life for 100 others. *Essai*, pp. 113, 114.</note> of what they could have produced if they had finished the work alone.

# CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY

By this means, the commodity becomes far cheaper and labor more expensive. It should be observed that the price of labor by no means determines the wealth of a society; it is only when a small amount of labor can procure abundance. For this reason, a wealthy nation, when its manufacturing is greatly improved, may have an advantage over a poor one by underselling it. Cotton and other goods from China would undersell any made here, were it not for the long transport distance and other taxes that are placed upon them <note>1 According to the fashion of the time, China is regarded here as a wealthy nation. See Cannan, *History of the Theories of Production and Distribution*, 1893, p. 12, note.</note>. We must not judge the dearness of labor by the money or coin that is paid for it <note>2 According to Defoe, *Plan of the English Commerce*, 1728, the manufactured goods of China, India, and other Eastern countries "force themselves upon the world by the mere stress of their cheapness" (p. 65). A page or two further on, he says that in China <column-break/> men get 2d. a day for towing boats from the canal banks, while in the American colonies five shillings a day and in Jamaica six or seven shillings a day are regular wages (pp. 66, 67).</note>. One penny in some places will purchase as much as eighteen pence in others. In the country of the Mughal, where the daily wages are only two pence, labor is better rewarded than in some of our sugar islands, where men are almost starving on four or five shillings a day. Coin, therefore, cannot be an accurate measure. Furthermore, although human labor is employed both in the increase of goods and of money, the chance of success is not equal. A farmer, through the proper cultivation of an acre, is certain of an increase; but the miner may work again and again without success. Goods must, therefore, multiply in greater proportion than gold and silver <note>3 And therefore will become cheaper, so that the same income in coin will indicate a larger amount of real wealth as time goes on.</note>.

But to return <note>4 That is, "to return to our main subject again."</note>, the quantity of work which is performed by the division of labor is much increased by the three following factors:

## PART II : POLICE

articles: first, increase of dexterity; secondly, the saving of time lost in passing from one species of labor to another; and thirdly, the invention of machinery. Of these in order:

First, when any kind of labor is reduced to a simple operation, a frequency of action naturally prepares men for dexterity in accomplishing it. A country blacksmith not accustomed to making nails will work very hard to produce three or four hundred a day, and those will be of very poor quality; but a boy used to the task will easily make two thousand, and those of incomparably better quality. Yet the improvement of dexterity in this relatively complex manufacture can never be equal to that in others. A nail-maker changes postures, blows the bellows, changes tools, etc., and therefore the quantity produced cannot be as great as in the manufacture of pins and buttons, where the work is reduced to simple operations <note>1 *Wealth of Nations*, book i, chapter i, volume i, page 9. The *Encyclopédie*, volume i, 1751, under 'Art', says, 'When a manufacture is large, each operation employs a different man. One worker does, and will do for his whole life, only one single and <column-break/> unique thing; another does something else. From this it follows that each task is performed well and quickly, and the best-made work is also the one obtained at the lowest price.'</note>.

Secondly, there is always some time lost in passing from one species of labor to another, even when they are closely connected. When a person has been reading, he must rest a little before he begins to write. This is even more the case with the country weaver who owns a small farm; he must dawdle a little when he moves from one to the other. Generally, this is the case with country laborers; they are always the greatest dawdlers. Because the country employments of sowing, reaping, and threshing are so different, they naturally acquire a habit of laziness and are seldom very skilled. By fixing every man to his own operation,

and by preventing the shifting from one piece of labor to another, the quantity of work must be greatly increased ^1.

Thirdly, the quantity of work is greatly increased by the invention of machines. Two men and three horses will do more in a day with the plow than twenty men without it. The miller and his servant will do more with the water mill than a dozen with the hand mill, though it, too, is a machine ^2. The division of labor no doubt first gave rise to the invention of machines. If a man's business in life is the performance of two or three things, the inclination of his mind will be to find out the cleverest way of doing it; but when the power of his mind is divided, it cannot be expected that he should be so successful ^3. We do not have, nor can we have, any complete history of the invention of machines, because most of them are at first imperfect and receive gradual improvements and increased power from those who use them. It was probably a farmer who made the original plow, though the improvements might be due to someone else. Some miserable slave who had perhaps been employed for a long time in grinding corn between two stones probably first discovered the method of supporting the upper stone with a spindle. A millwright perhaps found out the way of turning the spindle by hand, but the person who contrived that the outer wheel should be moved by water was a philosopher, whose business it is to do nothing but observe every—

Fire engines, wind and water mills were the invention of philosophers, whose skill is also increased by a division of labor¹. ### [§ 5. *What gives Occasion to the Division of Labor.*]

We have already shown that the division of labor is the immediate cause of opulence; we shall next consider what gives occasion to the division of labor², or from what principles in our nature it can best be accounted for. We cannot imagine this to be an effect of human prudence. It was indeed made a law by Sesostris that every man should follow the employment of his father³, but this is by no means suitable to the inclinations of human nature, and can never long take place; everyone is fond of being a gentleman, regardless of what his father was. Those who are strongest and, in the bustle of society⁴, have risen above the weak, must have as many people under them as are needed to defend them in their station. From necessary causes, therefore, there must be as many in the lower stations as there is occasion for,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the division of labor, arguing that it arises from a natural human propensity to barter and exchange, rather than from human wisdom or benevolence.</meta>

there must be as many up as down, and no division can be overstretched¹. But it is not this which gives occasion to the division of labor; it flows from a direct propensity in human nature for one man to barter with another, which is common to all men, and known to no other animal. No one ever saw a dog, the most sagacious animal, exchange a bone with his companion for another. Two greyhounds, indeed, in running down a hare, seem to have something like a compact or agreement between them, but this is nothing else but a concurrence of the same passions. If an animal intends to "truck," as it were, or gain anything from man, it is by its fondness and kindness. Man, in the same manner, works on the self-love of his fellows by setting before them a sufficient temptation to get what he wants. The language of this disposition is, "Give me what I want, and you shall have what you want." It is not from benevolence, like the dogs, but from self-love that man expects anything. The brewer and the baker serve us not from benevolence, but from self-love. No man but a beggar depends on benevolence, and even they would die in a week were their entire dependence upon it².

By this disposition to barter and exchange the surplus of one’s labor for that of other people, in a nation of hunters, if anyone has a talent for making bows and arrows better than his neighbors, he will at first make presents of them, and in return get presents of their game. By continuing this practice he will live better than before,

<note>solicitude in life of every individual person. To expect that others should serve us for nothing is unreasonable; therefore all commerce that men can have together must be a continual bartering of one thing for another." *Fable of the Bees*, part ii, Dialogue vi, page 421.</note>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the division of labor arises from the human tendency to trade, rather than from innate differences in talent.</meta>

and will have no reason to provide for himself, as the surplus of his own labor does it more effectively ¹.

This disposition to barter is by no means based on different genius and talents ². It is doubtful if there is any such difference at all; at least, it is far less than we realize. Genius is more the effect of the division of labor than the division of labor is the effect of genius. The difference between a porter and a philosopher in the first four or five years of their life is, properly speaking, none at all. When they come to be employed in different occupations, their views expand and differ by degrees. Since everyone has this natural disposition to <term>truck</term> and barter, by which they provide for themselves, there is no need for such different endowments; accordingly, among savages, there is always the greatest uniformity of character. In other animals of the same species, we find a much greater difference than between the philosopher and porter prior to the influence of custom. The mastiff and spaniel have quite different powers, but though these animals possess talents, they cannot, as it were, bring them into a common stock and exchange their productions. Therefore, their different talents are

² <note>This contradicts a passage in Harris’s *Essay on Money and Coins*, part 1, section 11. <column-break/>

with ease and comfort. This creates a dependence of one man upon another, and naturally unites men into societies. In the same way, as all countries differ more or less, either in the kinds or quality of their products—whether natural or artificial—individual men find their advantages (which extend to communities in general) by trading with the most remote nations." Hume, in "Of the Original Contract," requires his readers to "consider how nearly equal all men are in their physical strength, and even in their mental powers and faculties, before they are cultivated by education." *Essays*, 1748, page 291.</note>

<summary>The author argues that human differences in talent are the result of the division of labor rather than its cause, noting that unlike humans, animals cannot pool their diverse abilities through trade. He cites Mandeville, Harris, and Hume to discuss how mutual dependence and trade naturally unite individuals and nations into societies.</summary>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that humans possess a unique capacity for exchange, which is the true driver of the division of labor, unlike animals who cannot pool their diverse talents.</meta>

# CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY

of no use to them¹. It is quite different among mankind; they can exchange their various productions according to their quantity or quality; the philosopher and the porter are both of advantage to each other. The porter is useful for carrying burdens for the philosopher, and in turn, he burns his coals more cheaply because of the philosopher's invention of the fire machine².

Thus we have shown that a difference in genius is not the foundation of this disposition to barter, which is the cause of the division of labor. The real foundation of it is that principle of persuasion which so prevails in human nature. When any arguments are offered to persuade, it is always expected that they should have their intended effect. If a person asserts anything about the moon, even if it is not true, he will feel a kind of uneasiness when contradicted, and would be very glad if the person he is endeavoring to persuade were of the same way of thinking as himself. We ought then mainly to cultivate the power of persuasion, and indeed we do so without intending it. Since an entire life is spent in the exercise of it, a ready method of bargaining with each other must undoubtedly be attained. As was observed before, no animal can do this except by gaining the favor of those whom they would persuade. Sometimes, indeed, animals seem to act in concert, but there is never anything like a bargain among them. Monkeys, when they rob a garden, throw the fruit from one to another until they deposit it in the hoard, but there is always a scramble over the division of the booty, and usually some of them are killed.

<summary>This page argues that the division of labor originates from the uniquely human drive to persuade and bargain rather than innate differences in talent. While animals may cooperate, they lack the capacity for exchange, leading to conflict over resources where humans would instead trade.</summary>

PART II : POLICE

### [*§ 6. That the Division of Labor must be proportional to the Extent of Commerce.*]

From all that has been said, we may observe that the division of labor must always be proportional to the extent of commerce <note>¹ ‘Limited by the extent of the market.’ *Wealth of Nations*, book 1, chapter 3, heading.</note>. If only ten people want a certain commodity, its manufacture will never be as divided as if a thousand wanted it. Again, the division of labor, for the sake of prosperity, always becomes more perfect through easy methods of transportation within a country. If the roads are infested with robbers, or if they are deep with mud and transportation is not easy, the progress of commerce must be stopped. Since the repair of roads in England forty or fifty years ago, its prosperity has increased extremely. Water transport is another convenience, as 300 tons can be carried by it at the expense of the wear and tear of the vessel and the wages of five or six men; this is also done in a shorter time than by a hundred wagons, which would require six horses and one man each <note>² In *Wealth of Nations*, book 1, chapter 3, volume 1, page 19, the ship only carries 200 tons and has six or eight men; the wagons have eight horses <column-break/> and two men, and carry four tons each.</note>. Thus, the division of labor is the great cause of the increase of public prosperity, which is always proportional to the productivity of the people <note>³ ‘The more labor there is in a State, the more the State is naturally considered rich.’ Cantillon, *Essay*, page 113.</note>, and not to the quantity of gold and silver, as is foolishly imagined; and the productivity of the people is always proportional to the division of labor.

Having thus shown what gives rise to public prosperity, in further considering this subject we propose to examine:

First, what circumstances regulate the price of commodities:

<summary>This page explains that the division of labor is limited by the extent of the market and improved by efficient transportation, such as well-maintained roads and water carriage. It argues that a nation's wealth is derived from the productivity of its people rather than its reserves of gold and silver.</summary>

<keywords>division of labor, commerce, transportation, public prosperity, wealth of nations, water carriage, market extent, productivity</keywords>

Secondly, money in two different views: first as the measure of value, and then as the instrument of commerce.

Thirdly, the history of commerce, in which notice shall be taken of the causes of the slow progress of prosperity, both in ancient and modern times; these causes shall be shown to affect either agriculture or arts and manufactures.

Lastly, the effects of a commercial spirit on the government, temper, and manners of a people, whether good or bad, and the proper remedies. We will address these in order.

### [§ 7. *What Circumstances regulate the Price of Commodities.*]

Of every commodity there are two different prices, which, though apparently independent, will be found to have a necessary connection: namely, the natural price and the market price <note>¹ *Wealth of Nations* book 1, chapter 7, heading. Pufendorf distinguishes 'common or natural price' from 'legal price' ('pretium legitimum') fixed by the magistrate (*On the Law of Nature and Nations*, book 5, chapter 1, § 8), thus including both Adam Smith's kinds of price in 'natural price.' But he also says: 'In regulating this natural price, regard is to be had to the labor and expense of the merchant in importing and taking care of his goods; . . . his time, his study, his care in getting, preserving, and sorting his commodities, as also his servant's wages, may fairly be rated by him. . . . But what sudden and frequent <column-break/> alterations the markets admit of, by reason of the plenty or scarcity of buyers, money, or commodities, is well known.' *Ibid.* § 10, Kennet's translation, 3rd edition, 1717.</note>. Both of these are regulated by certain circumstances. When men are induced to pursue a certain species of industry rather than any other <note>² 'People who have children to educate who must earn their livelihood are always consulting and deliberating what trade or calling they are to bring them up to, until they are settled; and thousands think on this who hardly think at all on anything else. The fees of lawyers are so far from being extravagant, as they are generally thought to be, that they are actually quite low in proportion. In the same way, we will find that the price of gold and silver is not excessive if we consider it from this perspective; for in a gold or silver mine, there is a high probability of finding nothing at all. If we assume an equal number of men are employed in growing grain and digging for silver, the former will earn more than the latter; because, perhaps out of forty or fifty employed in a mine, only twenty earn anything at all. Some of the rest may indeed make fortunes, but every farmer succeeds in his ventures, so that, on the whole, more is earned this way than the other. It is the hope of a great fortune that is the main attraction of a mine.

A man, then, receives the natural price of his labor when it is enough to maintain him during his working hours, to cover the cost of his education, and to compensate for the risk of not living long enough or not succeeding in the business. When a man receives this, there is sufficient incentive for the worker, and the commodity will be produced in proportion to the demand ¹.

<margin><image-desc>manicule</image-desc></margin> The market price of goods is determined by entirely different circumstances. When a buyer comes to the market, he never asks the seller what expenses he incurred in producing the goods. The determination of the market price of goods depends on the three following factors:—

First, the demand, or need for the commodity. There is no demand for a thing of little use; it is not a rational object of desire.

Secondly, the abundance or scarcity of the commodity in proportion to the need for it. If the commodity is scarce, the price is raised, but if the quantity is more than enough to supply the demand, the price falls. Thus it is that diamonds and other precious stones

...are expensive, while iron, which is much more useful, is many times cheaper, although this¹ depends mainly on the final cause, namely:—

Thirdly, the wealth or poverty of those who demand the goods. When not enough is produced to serve everyone, the wealth of the bidders is the only regulator of the price. The story told about the merchant and the carrier in the deserts of Arabia is evidence of this. The merchant gave 10,000 ducats for a certain amount of water. In this case, his wealth determined the price; for if he had not possessed that sum, he could not have paid it, and if his wealth had been less, the water would have been cheaper. When a commodity is scarce, the seller must be satisfied with the level of wealth held by those who buy it. The situation is much like an auction. If two people have an equal desire for a book, the one with the larger fortune will win it. Consequently, things that are very rare always go to wealthy countries. Only the King of France could afford to purchase that large diamond worth many thousands of pounds². According to this principle, everything is more expensive or cheaper depending on whether it is purchased by a higher or lower class of people. Utensils made of gold are obtainable only by people in certain financial circumstances. Those made of silver fall to another group of people, and their prices are regulated by what the majority can afford to pay. The prices of grain and beer are regulated by what everyone can afford, and for this reason, the wages of the day laborer have a great influence on the price of grain. When the price of grain rises, wages also rise, and *vice versa*³; when the supply of grain falls short, as in

Thus we have considered the two types of prices—the natural price and the market price—which every commodity is supposed to have. We observed before that however seemingly independent they appear to be, they are necessarily connected. This will become clear from the following considerations. If the market price of any commodity is very high, and labor is very highly rewarded, the market becomes immensely crowded with that commodity; greater quantities of it are produced, and it can then be sold to the lower ranks of people. If there were ten thousand diamonds for every ten that currently exist, they would become affordable to everyone, because they would become very cheap and would drop to their natural price. Conversely, when the market is overstocked and there is not enough earned to pay for the labor of manufacturing, nobody will apprentice themselves to that trade; they cannot earn a living by it, because the market price then falls below the natural price. It is claimed that as the price of grain sinks, the wages of the laborer should also sink, as he is then better rewarded. It is true that if provisions were cheap for a long time, more people would flock to this labor where the wages are high; through this competition for labor, the wages would come down. However, we find that when the price of grain is doubled, wages continue the same as before, because the laborers have no other options for employment. The same is the case with domestic servants <note>¹ *Wealth of Nations*, book i, chapter viii, volume i, pages 90, 91. The reporter has evidently condensed the text excessively at this point.</note>.

From the above, we may observe that whatever policy tends to raise the market price above the natural price also tends to diminish public wealth. High prices and scarcity are, in effect, the same thing. When commodities are in abundance, they can be sold to the lower classes of people, who...

First, all taxes on industry: on leather and on shoes, which people resent most; on salt, beer, or whatever the strong drink of the country is, for no country lacks <note>1. That is, of course, "lacks" or "is without."</note> some kind of it. Man is an anxious animal and must have his cares swept away by something that can exhilarate his spirits. It is claimed that this tax on beer is an artificial safeguard against drunkenness, but if we pay attention to it, we will find that it by no means prevents it. In countries where strong liquors are cheap, such as France and Spain, the people are generally sober; but in northern countries, where they are expensive, they do not get drunk on beer, but on spirituous liquors. No one presses his friend to take a glass of beer unless he chooses to do so <note>2. *Wealth of Nations*, book 4, chapter 3, part 2, volume 2, pages 66–68.</note>.

Secondly, monopolies also destroy public wealth. The price of monopolized goods is raised above what is sufficient to encourage labor. When only a certain person or persons have the liberty of importing a commodity, less of it is imported than would otherwise be; the price of it is therefore higher, and fewer people are supported by it. It is the competition of different laborers that always brings down the price. In monopolies, such as the Hudson’s Bay and East India companies, the people engaged in them set the price at whatever they please.

Thirdly, the exclusive privileges of corporations have the same effect. Butchers and bakers raise the price of their goods as they please, because none but their

Again, after the ages of hunting and fishing, in which provisions were the immediate produce of their labor, when manufactures were introduced, nothing could be produced without a great deal of time. It was a long time before the weaver could carry to the market the cloth which he bought as flax. Suppose then, as is really the case in every country, that there is in store a stock of food, clothes, and lodging; the number of people that are employed must be in proportion to it <note>3. *Wealth of Nations*, Introduction, volume 1, pages 2, 3.</note>. If the price of one commodity is sunk below its natural price, while another is above it, there is a smaller quantity of the stored stock left to support the whole. On account of the natural connection of all trades in the stock, by allowing bounties to one <note>4. <summary>The author argues that government bounties distort the natural balance of industry by drawing labor away from un-subsidized trades and depleting the national stock of resources. Specifically, the corn bounty intended to support landlords eventually led to lower rents despite its initial goal of stabilizing prices during the introduction of the land tax.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument against government intervention in the grain market, specifically addressing how lowering the price of corn through bounties inadvertently raises the cost of meat and transportation. It then transitions into a new section regarding the nature of money.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>PART II : POLICE</header>
<columns>2</columns>

Improving agriculture brought down the price of corn, yet it raised the value of grass farms, for the more corn that is grown, the less grass there is. Because the price of grass was raised, butchers’ meat—due to its dependence upon it—must be raised also; so that if the price of corn is diminished, the price of other commodities is necessarily raised. The price of corn has indeed fallen from forty-two to thirty-five shillings <note>1. Red wheat is quoted at 33s.—35s. in the *London Chronicle* on Jan. 28, 1763, and at 33s.—36s. on Oct. 20 and 27, Nov. 3 and 10, 1763. The other weekly prices during the academic session of 1762-1763, and October to December, 1763, are somewhat lower.</note>, but the price of hay has risen from twenty-five to nearly fifty shillings. As the price of hay has risen, horses are not as easily maintained, and therefore the cost of transportation has risen also. But whatever increases the cost of transportation diminishes abundance in the market <note>2. In *Wealth of Nations* book 1, chapter 11, volume 1, pages 208-210, and book 4, chapter 5, volume 2, pages 81-84, Adam Smith rejects the theory that the bounty had lowered the home price, and <column-break/> shows that by stimulating the foreign demand it must have instead tended to raise it. He even speaks of the ‘very moderate supposition’ that the bounty may have kept the price 4s. per quarter higher than it would otherwise have been. Having done this, he proceeds to argue that every alteration in the price of corn must be nominal and not real. He has therefore no need for the argument in the text above regarding hay.</note>. Upon the whole, therefore, it is by far the best policy to leave things to their natural course, and allow no bounties, nor impose taxes on commodities.

Thus we have shown what circumstances regulate the price of commodities, which was the first thing proposed.

### [§ 8. *Of Money as the Measure of Value and Medium of Exchange.*]

We come now to the second particular, to consider money, first as the measure of value and then as the medium of permutation or exchange. When people deal

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the prices of different commodities, transitioning into an explanation of how a single commodity eventually becomes the universal measure of value and the standard for trade.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>
<header>CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY</header>
<columns>2</columns>

Among many types of goods, one of them must be considered as the measure of value. Suppose there were only three commodities: sheep, corn, and oxen; we can easily remember their comparative values. However, if we have a hundred different commodities, there are ninety-nine values for each, arising from a comparison with every other item. Since these cannot be easily remembered, people naturally settle on one of them as a common standard with which they compare all the rest. Initially, this will naturally be the commodity with which they are most familiar. Accordingly, we find that black cattle and sheep were the standard in Homer’s time. The armor of one of his heroes was worth nine oxen, and that of another was worth a hundred ¹. Black cattle were the common standard in ancient Greece. In Italy, and particularly in Tuscany, everything was compared with sheep, as this was their primary commodity. This is what may be called the natural measure of value. In a similar way, there were natural measures of quantity, such as fathoms, cubits, and inches—taken from the proportions of the human body—which were once used by every nation. However, through observation, they found that one man’s arm was longer or shorter than another’s, and that they could not be compared with one another. Therefore, wise men who studied these matters endeavored to establish a more accurate measure, so that equal quantities might have equal values. This method became absolutely necessary when people began to trade in many commodities and in large quantities. Although an inch was completely insignificant when their dealings were limited to a few yards, more accuracy was required

...when they began to trade in the thousands. We find the remains of this inaccuracy in countries where their dealings are small. The slight tilt of the scale is not considered at all when dealing with their coarse commodities.

Since, then, there must necessarily be a common standard in which equal quantities represent equal values, metals in general seemed to best serve this purpose; and among these, the value of gold and silver could most easily be determined. The hardness of steel cannot be precisely known, but the degree of alloy in gold and silver can be exactly discovered. Gold and silver were therefore chosen as the most precise standard for comparing goods and were consequently considered the most appropriate measure of value.

As a result of gold and silver becoming the measure of value, they also became the instrument of commerce. It soon became necessary for goods to be taken to market, and they could never be effectively<sup>1</sup> exchanged unless the measure of value was also the instrument of commerce. In the age of shepherds, it might not have been a great inconvenience for cattle to be the medium of exchange, as the expense of maintaining them was nothing, since the whole country was considered one large common pasture. However, when lands were divided and the division of labor was introduced, this custom resulted in significant inconveniences. The butcher and the shoemaker might sometimes have no use for each other’s goods. A farmer often cannot maintain even one more cow on his land than he already has. It would be a very great hard-
