ship for a Glasgow merchant to give him a cow for one of his commodities. To remedy this, those materials which were previously considered the measure of value also became the instrument of exchange. Gold and silver had every advantage. They can be kept without expense, they do not decay, and they are very portable. Gold and silver, however, do not derive their entire utility from being the medium of exchange; even if they had never been used as money, they would be more valuable than any other metals. They possess a superior beauty, are capable of a finer polish, and are more suitable for making any instrument, except those with a sharp edge. For all these reasons, gold and silver became the proper measure of value and the instrument of exchange; but to make them more suitable for these purposes, it was necessary that both their weight and their fineness should be determined. At first, their scales were not very accurate, and therefore frauds were easily committed. However, this was gradually corrected; but ordinary business would not allow for the experiments necessary to determine the precise degree of fineness. Even with a large amount of alloy, they appear to be of good quality. Therefore, to facilitate exchange, it was necessary to find some method to accurately determine both weight and fineness. Coinage most effectively secures both of these. The public authorities, realizing how much it would help facilitate commerce, placed a stamp upon certain pieces so that anyone seeing them could have the public faith that they were of a certain weight and fineness; and this weight and fineness were likely the first things marked upon the coin, as they were of the greatest importance <note>Aristotle, *Politics*, 1257 a 38-41, quoted in Pufendorf, *On the Law of Nature and Nations*, book 5, chapter 1, § 12. On the properties which have been observed in gold</note>.

Accordingly, the coins of every country appear to have carried the names of the weights corresponding to them, and they contained the weight they expressed. The British pound sterling seems originally to have been a pound weight of pure silver.

Since gold could be easily exchanged for silver, the latter always became the standard or measure of value. Since there cannot be two standards, and silver is necessary for most purchases, we never say a person is worth a certain amount of guineas, but always pounds.

It should be observed that the measure of quantity has always increased, while the measure of value has decreased. The British pound has now decreased to less than a third of its original value, which was sixty-three shillings, while the measure of quantity has significantly increased. The reason is that it serves the interests of the government. It is in the interest of the baker and the brewer to make the measure of quantity as small as possible; therefore, inspectors are appointed who, whenever the measure is reduced, always set it a little higher. All our measures, which were derived from the Roman foot, fathom, and inch,

are now significantly larger. Similarly, what was called Troy weight—named after Troyes, a town in Champagne $^1$ where the greatest trade was then conducted—gave rise to a heavier weight. This happened because the "cast of the balance" (the extra amount given to tip the scales) was usually included with it. As this made transactions imprecise, it became necessary to standardize this cast of the balance. Accordingly, avoirdupois (*avoir du poise* $^2$), or heavy weight, was established at thirteen ounces; but because thirteen is a number not easily divided, it was later set at sixteen ounces, with the weight of the ounces adjusted to fit this scale $^3$.

Thus, while the measure of quantity has been increasing, we shall next show how the value of the coin decreased. When the government takes control of minting coins, the expenses naturally fall upon it. If any private individual were to mint coins, he would have to reduce their value or receive nothing but his labor for his efforts. Furthermore, since no individual's authority is great enough to make his own coins acceptable for general payments, he would be forced to forge the government’s stamp. Once the government took this task upon themselves, they sought to prevent fraud by stopping the counterfeiting of the king’s coin and protecting his royal prerogative. Moreover, since public trust was involved, it was necessary to prevent all forms of deceit. It was also required that people be obliged to accept the coin according to its denomination; if anyone refused it after a legal tender of payment was offered, the debtor would be released from the debt, and the creditor would be considered guilty of a felony $^4$. <meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the government's historical role in regulating and debasing currency, transitioning from the legal obligations of tender to the motivations and consequences of devaluing the coin.</meta>

<header>PART II : POLICE</header>

barbarous periods, the government was subjected to many temptations to debase the currency—or, in mint terminology, to "raise" it. For instance, when on any important occasion, such as paying off debts or paying soldiers, it needed two million but had no more than one, it would call in the country's coin. By mixing it with a larger quantity of alloy, the government reissued it as two million, making it look as much like the original as possible. Many operations of this kind have been performed in every country; but England, due to the freedom it has almost uninterruptedly enjoyed, has been less troubled by this than any other nation. There, the value has only fallen to one-third, but in many other countries, it is not even a fiftieth of its original value.

The disadvantages of such practices are very significant. The debasement of the coin hinders commerce, or at least greatly complicates it. A new calculation must be made to determine how much of the new coin must be given for a certain amount of the old. People are inclined to keep their goods away from the market, as they do not know what they will receive for them. Thus, a stagnation of commerce is caused; furthermore, the debasement of the coin destroys public trust. No one will lend any sum to the government or enter into a contract with it, as they might be repaid with only half of the original value. Since a fraud has been committed by the government, every subject must be permitted to do the same and pay his debts with the new money, which is worth less than what he owed. This scheme, however, serves its purpose for a short time for the following reason. The use of money is twofold: for the payment of debts and for the purchasing of commodities. When the coin is debased, a debt of twenty shillings is then paid with ten; but if the new coin is taken to a foreign market, it will yield only its original intrinsic value. All day laborers are paid in the new coin. The necessities of life must be sold at what the majority of people can afford to pay, and consequently their price will

To prevent this loss, the French—and indeed all other nations on similar occasions—when they double the value of money by decree without re-minting it, make the augmentation after the money is collected and before it is issued, and a diminution is made before the next payment deadline <note>The French understood an augmentation without re-minting to be an increase in the number of livres (a unit of account, not actual coins) that correspond to a coin of a specific weight, and a diminution to be a decrease in this number. A diminution always has a worse effect than an augmentation. An augmentation harms the creditor, while a diminution harms the debtor, who should always be favored <note>“Reductions favor the creditor, and increases favor the debtor; and all else being equal in matters of state, it is the debtor who should be favored.” *Ibid.* ch. xii. at the end. Dutot criticizes this proposition and the historical examples brought forward in support of it quite severely. *Political Reflections on Finance and Commerce*, 1738, ch. i. ### [§ 9. *That National Opulence does not consist in Money.*]

We have shown what rendered money the measure of value, but it is to be observed that labor, not money, is the true measure of value⁴. National opulence consists, therefore, in the quantity of goods and the facility of barter. This shall be considered next.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on national wealth, shifting from the specific valuation of gold and silver coins to the functional role of money as a tool for circulation rather than a source of wealth itself.</meta>

<page-num>191</page-num>
# CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY

The more money that is necessary to circulate a country's goods, the more the quantity of goods is diminished. Suppose that the whole stock of Scotland in grain, cattle, money, etc., amounts to twenty million, and if one million in cash is necessary to carry on circulation, there will be in the country only nineteen million worth of food, clothes, and lodging; the people have one million less than they would have if there were no need for this expedient of money. It is therefore evident that the poverty of any country increases as its money increases, money being a "dead stock" ¹ in itself, providing no convenience of life. In this respect, money may be compared to the highways of a country, which produce neither grain nor grass themselves but circulate all the grain and grass in the country. If we could find any way to save the land taken up by highways, we would considerably increase the quantity of commodities and have more to take to market ².

In the same manner as the worth of a piece of ground does not lie in the number of highways that run through it, so the riches of a country do not consist in the quantity of money employed to circulate commerce, but in the great abundance of the necessities of life. If we could, therefore, find a method to send half of our money abroad to be converted into goods, and at the same time supply the channel of circulation at home, we would greatly increase the wealth of the country.

Hence the beneficial effects of the establishment of banks and paper credit. It is easy to show that the establishment of banks is of advantage to the commerce of a country. Suppose, as above, that the whole stock of Scotland amounted to twenty million, and that two million are employed in the

² <note>*W. of N.* bk. ii. ch. ii. vol. i. p. 322.</note>

<summary>This page argues that money is a "dead stock" that facilitates trade like a highway but does not produce value itself; therefore, a country's wealth consists of its goods rather than its currency. It introduces the idea that banks and paper credit increase national wealth by allowing gold and silver to be sent abroad for goods while maintaining domestic circulation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between money and national wealth, specifically explaining how paper credit and banking allow a nation to export its "dead stock" of gold and silver in exchange for productive commodities.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>
<header>PART II : POLICE</header>

...circulation of it, and the other eighteen million are in commodities. If, then, the banks in Scotland issued notes to the value of two million pounds, and kept £300,000 among them to meet immediate demands, there would be one million seven hundred thousand pounds circulating in cash, besides two million pounds in paper money. The natural circulation, however, is two million, and the channel will receive no more. Whatever is extra will be sent abroad to bring home materials for food, clothes, and lodging. That this tends to enrich a nation can be seen at first sight; for whatever commodities are imported, just that much is added to the opulence of the country. The only objection against paper money is that it drains the country of gold and silver, that banknotes will not circulate in a foreign market, and that foreign commodities must be paid for in <term>specie</term> <note>Coin or precious metal.</note>. This is undoubtedly the case; but if we consider it carefully, we will find that this is no real harm to a country. The opulence of a nation does not consist in the quantity of coin, but in the abundance of commodities which are necessary for life; and whatever tends to increase these also tends to increase the riches of a country.

Money is suited for none of the necessities of life. It cannot by itself provide food, clothes, or lodging, but must be exchanged for commodities fit for these purposes. If all the coin of the nation were exported and our commodities were increased proportionally, the coin could be recalled during any sudden emergency sooner than anyone could imagine. Goods will always bring in money, and as long as the stock of commodities in any nation increases, the people have it in their power to increase the quantity of coin—if thought necessary—by exporting their stock to foreign countries. This reasoning is confirmed by facts. We find that the commerce of every nation in Europe has been vastly increased by the establishment of banks. In this country, everyone is aware of their beneficial effects,

<sig>o</sig>

and our American colonies, where most commerce is conducted through paper circulation, are in a highly flourishing condition ¹.

What first led to the establishment of banks was the desire to facilitate the transfer of money. To this day, this is the sole purpose of the bank at Amsterdam. When commerce reaches a high level, the delivery of gold and silver consumes a great deal of time. When a major merchant had ten or twenty thousand pounds to pay out, it would take him almost a week to count it out in guineas and shillings. A bank bill prevents all this trouble. Before the establishment of the bank ² at Amsterdam, the method merchants used to reduce the trouble of counting out large quantities of cash was to keep certain sums stored in bags to meet immediate demands. In this case, you must either trust the honesty of the merchant, or you must take the trouble of counting it yourself. If you trusted his integrity, frequent frauds were committed; if not, your labor was not reduced. The inconveniences arising from this led to the establishment of that bank, whose entire operation is this: you deposit a certain sum of money there, and the bank gives you a bill for that amount. This money is secure, and you never ask for it because the bill will generally sell above par <note>Above its face value.</note>, and it is therefore to your advantage to let it remain; the bank has no office for payment because payment is seldom demanded. In this manner, the bank of Amsterdam has a positive effect in facilitating commerce, and its notes circulate only there; the credit of that city is not in the least endangered by the bank. In 1701 ³, when the French army was at Utrecht, a sudden demand was made upon it, and all Holland was alarmed by the expected fatal

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the banking systems of Europe, specifically addressing the transition from full-reserve systems like Amsterdam's to the fractional-reserve and lending practices found in Scotland and England.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>
<header>CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY</header>

on the same footing as the Amsterdam bank, but because the directors took the liberty of lending out money, they gradually came to their current situation. The ruin of a bank would not be as dangerous as is commonly imagined. Suppose all the money in Scotland was issued by one bank, and that it became bankrupt; a very few individuals would be ruined by it, but not many, because the amount of cash or paper that people have in their hands bears no proportion to their total wealth. Neither would the wealth of the whole country be much hurt by it, because not even a hundredth part of the riches of a country consists of money. The only method to prevent the bad consequences arising from the ruin of banks is to give monopolies to none, but to encourage the establishment of as many as possible. When several are established in a country, a mutual jealousy prevails, and they are continually making unexpected runs on one another. This puts them on their guard and obliges them to prepare themselves against such demands. If there were only one bank in Scotland, it would perhaps be a little more enterprising, as it would have no rival, and through mismanagement it might become bankrupt; but having many banks puts this beyond all danger: even if one did fail, every individual would have very few of its notes. From all these considerations it is manifest that banks are beneficial to the commerce of a country, and that it is bad <term>police</term> to restrain them.

Several political writers have published treatises to show the harmful nature of banks and paper money. Mun, a London merchant, published one with this intention, in response to a book that had been written on the opposite side before. He affirms that as England is drained of its money, it must go to ruin. The circulation of paper banishes gold and silver from the country; all other goods which we have in our possession, being spent upon our subsistence, gradually diminish and must at last come to an end. Money never decays; a stock of it will

Mr. Hume published some essays <note>‘Of Money,’ ‘Of the Balance of Trade,’ in *Political Discourses*, 1752, and ‘Of the Jealousy of Trade’ in *Essays and Treatises*, 1758.</note> demonstrating the absurdity of these and other such doctrines. He proves very cleverly that money must always maintain a certain proportion to the quantity of commodities in every country; that whenever <note>The manuscript reads ‘wherever.’</note> money is accumulated beyond the proportion of commodities in any country, the price of goods will necessarily rise; that this country will be undersold in the foreign market, and consequently the money must flow to other nations. On the contrary, whenever the quantity of money falls below the proportion of goods, the price of goods diminishes, the country undersells others in foreign markets, and consequently money returns in great plenty. Thus, money and goods will maintain a roughly stable level in every country <note>‘Of the Balance of Trade.’ *Political Discourses*, 1752, p. 82 ff.</note>. Mr. Hume’s reasoning is exceedingly clever. He seems, however, to have partly accepted the notion that public <term>opulence</term> <note>wealth or prosperity</note> consists of money <note>Perhaps where he argues against paper money. ‘Of Money’ and ‘Of the Balance of Trade.’ *Ibid.* pp. 43–45, 89–91.</note>, a concept which was considered above.

We may observe regarding this that human industry always multiplies goods and money together, though not always in the same proportion. The labor of men will always be employed in producing whatever is the object of human desire, and things will increase in proportion to the power of man to cultivate them. <page-num>198</page-num>

<columns>2</columns>

because they are more within the reach of human industry. Almost any part of the surface of the earth may, by proper cultivation, be made capable of producing grain, but gold is not to be found everywhere; and even where it is to be found, it lies concealed in the depths of the earth, and to produce even a small quantity of it, a long time and much labor are required$^1$. For these reasons, money never increases in proportion to the increase of goods, and consequently money will be sold at a lower rate in proportion as a country becomes <term>opulent</term> <note>wealthy</note>. In savage nations, money commands a vast price because savages have no money except what they acquire by plunder, for they do not have that knowledge which is necessary for producing money in their own country. But when a nation arrives at a certain degree of improvement in the arts, its value diminishes; then they begin to search for mines and manufacture it themselves. From the fall of the Roman Empire to the discovery of the West Indies, the value of money was very high and continually increasing. Since that later period, its value has decreased considerably$^2$.

Mr. Locke, too, published a treatise to show the harmful consequences of allowing the nation to be drained of money. His ideas were likewise founded upon the notion that public opulence consists of money, though he treats the matter in a more philosophical light than the rest. He affirms, with Mr. Mun, that if there is no money in a nation it must soon come to ruin, and that all commodities are soon consumed, but money lasts forever$^3$.

<columns>2</columns>

On the whole, we may observe on this subject that the reason our riches do not consist in money but in commodities is that money cannot be used for any of the purposes of life, whereas commodities are suited for our subsistence. The "consumability"—if we may use the word—of goods is the primary cause of human industry$^1$, and an industrious people will always produce more than they consume. It is easy to show how small a proportion the cash in every country bears to the public wealth. It is generally supposed that there are thirty million pounds of money circulating in Britain$^2$, but the annual consumption amounts to much more than a hundred million; for, estimating the inhabitants of the island at ten million, and allowing ten pounds per year for the subsistence of each person—which is far too little—the total annual consumption amounts to that sum. So it appears that the circulating cash represents only a small proportion of the total wealth of the country. It is probable, however, that there are not thirty million in Britain, and in that case, the proportion will be even smaller.

It is said by some who support the notion that the

wealth of a country consists in money, so that when a person retires from trade he immediately turns his stock into cash. It is clear, however, that the reason for this is that because money is the instrument of commerce, a person can exchange it for the necessities and luxuries of life more easily than anything else. Even the miser who locks up his gold in his chest has this goal in view. No man in his right mind hoards up money for its own sake; rather, he considers that by keeping money always with him, he has the power to supply all the needs of himself and his family at once.

This opinion that wealth consists in money, while absurd in theory, has given rise to many harmful errors in practice, some of which include the following.

### [§ 10. *Of Prohibiting the Exportation of Coin*.]

It was due to these beliefs that the government prohibited the exportation of coin <note>The manuscript reads ‘corn.’</note>, a prohibition that has been extremely harmful to the commerce of the country. This is because whatever amount of money there is in any country beyond what is sufficient for circulation is merely idle capital.

In King William’s time there were two types of coin: milled and unmilled. The unmilled coin was frequently clipped by various people during its circulation. This caused frequent disorders among the people, and therefore the parliament ordered all the clipped money to be brought into the mint. The government took on the expense of recoining it, an operation that cost them about two million pounds. Since they had incurred this expense, they thought it just and proper to prohibit the exportation of money for the future <note>There is evidently an omission at this point which may perhaps <column-break/> be supplied by the words, ‘Until the Restoration, indeed, it had been unlawful to export any gold...</note>. Any regulation of this kind is very absurd, for there is no fear—if things are left to their natural course—that any nation will lack enough money for the circulation of its commodities. Furthermore, every prohibition on exportation is always ineffective and very often causes the exportation of more money than would otherwise occur. For instance, suppose the Portuguese were prohibited from exporting their money under threat of capital punishment. ...must set such a price on his goods as will be enough to reward him for the risk he runs of being caught; and the Portuguese merchant, being forced to buy his goods at too high a price, must be a loser. Generally, every prohibition of this kind harms the commerce of a country. Every unnecessary accumulation of money is idle capital that could be used to enrich the nation through foreign trade. It also increases the price of goods and causes the country to be undersold in foreign markets.

It should be observed that prohibiting the export of money is actually one major cause of the poverty of Spain and Portugal. When they gained possession of the mines of Mexico and Peru, they thought they could dominate all of Europe with the constant supplies they received from there, provided they could keep the money among themselves; therefore, they banned its export. But this had the exact opposite effect, for when money is, so to speak, dammed up to an unnatural level—and there is more than the circulation requires—the consequences are very unfavorable for the country. For it is impossible to completely stop the export of gold and silver, as the balance of trade must be against them—that is, they must buy more than they sell—and it is absolutely necessary that this balance be paid in money. The price of every commodity rises to an extreme level. The Portuguese pay for English cloth, in addition to its natural price, the cost and risk of transporting it there, for no one has ever seen a Spanish or Portuguese ship in a British harbor. All the goods sent to those countries are carried by ourselves and consigned to British agents to be sold by them. But besides the transport and insurance, the British merchant must be compensated for the risk of having his money seized in Portugal as a result of the prohibition. All risk of forfeiture or penalty must be factored into the price of the goods¹. This has a miserable

effect upon the domestic industry of those countries, and has put a stop to their manufactures. No one ever saw a piece of Spanish cloth in any other country, yet they have the best materials in the world; with the same skill that we possess, they might monopolize the trade of Europe. It caught the attention of the nations that trade with them in these commodities when a general, on a certain occasion, presented to his majesty the regiment under his command clothed in the manufactures of Spain. In general, they export no manufactured goods—swords and armor excepted, as they admittedly have the best steel in the world—but only the natural products of the country, such as fruits and wines.

Regulations of a similar nature were made in Britain during King William’s time<sup>1</sup>. Money was thought to constitute wealth, and therefore the accumulation of it commanded the full attention of the public. They coined all money brought in for free, and the costs of coinage, which amounted to about £140,000<sup>2</sup>, were entirely thrown away; and, besides, great encouragement was given to exportation because, as gold and silver were coined at no charge, coined money could never be more expensive than bullion. As the exportation of bullion was free, they melted down the coins and sent them abroad. At present, there is a great temptation to such practices, for an ounce of pure silver at the mint price is valued exactly at 5s. 2d., but bullion is often bought at 5s. 6d. Since nothing is lost in the melting process, there is a profit of 4d. per ounce. It is for this reason that we seldom or never see a new shilling, and it is one of the causes for silver being so scarce in proportion to gold<sup>3</sup>.

### [§ 11. *Of the Balance of Trade.*]

<margin>1 *Wealth of Nations*, book iv, chapter ii, vol. i, p. 29.</margin>
The idea that public wealth consists of money has produced other negative effects. Based on this principle, very harmful regulations have been established. Those types of trade which drain us of our money are thought to be disadvantageous, and those which increase it beneficial; therefore, the former are prohibited and the latter encouraged. As France is thought to produce more of the luxuries of life than this country, and as we take much from them while they need little from us, the balance of trade is considered to be against us. Therefore, almost all our trade with France is prohibited by high taxes and duties on importation. On the other hand, as Spain and Portugal take more of our goods than we take of theirs, the balance is in our favor, and this trade is not only allowed but encouraged. The absurdity of these regulations will appear upon the slightest reflection. All commerce that is carried on between any two countries must necessarily be advantageous to both. The very purpose of commerce is to exchange your own goods for others which you think will be more convenient for you. When two men trade between themselves, it is undoubtedly for the advantage of both. One person perhaps has more of one type of good than he has need for; he therefore exchanges a certain quantity of it with the other for another good that will be more useful to him. The other person agrees to the bargain for the same reason, and in this manner, their mutual trade is advantageous to both. The case is exactly the same between any two nations<sup>1</sup>. The goods which the English merchants want to import from France are certainly more valuable to them than what they give in exchange for them. Our very desire to purchase them shows that we have more use for them than either

<meta>This page continues the argument against mercantilist trade restrictions, specifically addressing the misconception that hoarding money is preferable to consuming goods and explaining how export bans stifle domestic productivity.</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>
<header>CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY</header>

the money or the goods which we give for them. It may be said indeed that money lasts forever, but that claret <note>a dry red wine</note> and cambrics <note>a fine, thin white linen fabric</note> are soon consumed. This is true: but what is the purpose of industry if it is not to produce those things which are capable of being used, and are conducive to the convenience and comfort of human life? Unless we use the products of our industry, unless we can support more people in a better way, what good is it? Besides, if we have money to spend upon foreign goods, what purpose does it serve to keep it in the country? If the circulation of goods requires it, there will be none to spare; and if the channel of circulation is full, no more is necessary. And if only a certain sum is necessary for that purpose, why throw more into it?

Again, by prohibiting the export of goods to foreign markets, the industry of the country is greatly discouraged. It is a very great incentive to industry that people have it in their power to exchange the products of their labor for whatever they please, and wherever there is any restraint on people in this respect, they will not be as energetic in improving manufactures. If we are prohibited from sending grain and cloth to France, that industry is stopped which grows grain and prepares cloth for the French market. It may be said indeed that if we were allowed to trade with France we would not exchange our goods for theirs, but our money, and thus human industry is by no means discouraged; but if we pay attention to it, we shall find that it comes to the same thing in the end. (By hindering people from disposing of their money as they think proper, you discourage those manufactures by which this money is gained.) All jealousies therefore between different nations, and prejudices of this kind, are extremely harmful to commerce, and limit public opulence¹. This is always the case between France and us in the time of war.

---

¹ Hume, "Of the Jealousy of Trade," in *Essays and Treatises*, 1758, throughout.

<summary>The author argues that the ultimate goal of industry is consumption rather than the accumulation of money, and that trade restrictions based on national jealousy stifle domestic manufacturing and wealth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that trade restrictions, driven by national jealousy and the desire to hoard money, are counterproductive to a nation's wealth. <page-num>206</page-num>
<header>PART II: POLICE</header>

In general, we may observe that these jealousies and prohibitions are most harmful to the richest nations, and that they are harmful in proportion to how advantageous a free commerce would be. When a rich man and a poor man trade with one another, both of them will increase their riches if they trade prudently, but the rich man’s stock will increase in a greater proportion than the poor man’s. In the same way, when a rich and a poor nation engage in trade, the rich nation will have the greatest advantage; therefore, the prohibition of this commerce is the most harmful to it of the two. All our trade with France is prohibited by the high duties imposed on every imported French commodity. It would, however, have been better <term>police</term> <note>In the 18th century, "police" referred to public policy, administration, or the regulation of a community</note> to encourage our trade with France. If any foreign commerce is to be prohibited, it ought to be that with Spain and Portugal. This would have been most advantageous to England. France is much more populous, a more extensive country, and further advanced in arts and manufactures of every kind; the industry which commerce with that country would have stimulated <note>The manuscript reads ‘exerted.’</note> at home would have been much greater. Perhaps twenty million people in a great society, working, as it were, into each other’s hands due to the nature of the division of labor previously explained, would produce a thousand times more goods than another society consisting only of two or three million. It would be fortunate, therefore, both for this country and for France, if all national prejudices were rooted out and a free and uninterrupted commerce established.

It may be observed in general that we have never heard of any nation ruined by this balance of trade. When Gee <note>Joshua Gee, author of 'Trade and Navigation of Great Britain Considered' (1729)</note> published his book, the balance with all nations was against us, except for Spain and Portugal <note>And Holland, according to the Custom House accounts, which, however, Gee finds it convenient to reject as untrustworthy in this case. See *Trade and Navigation*, chapters 12 and 34.</note>. It was then

<column-break/>

convenient to reject as untrustworthy in this case. *Trade and Navigation*, chs. xii, xxxiv.

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding national wealth, refuting the persistent fears of economic decline by demonstrating that national wealth has increased despite high government spending and trade.</meta>

<page-num>207</page-num>
<header>CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY</header>

[It was] thought that in a few years we would be reduced to an absolute state of poverty. This indeed has been the cry of all political writers since the time of Charles II; despite all this, we find ourselves far richer than before, and, when there is occasion for it, we can raise much more money than ever has been done. A late minister of state levied in one year twenty-three million pounds <note>For the service of the year 1760, £19,616,119, and for that of 1761, £18,299,153, were voted, and these amounts far exceeded the votes for any other year <column-break/> before Adam Smith left Glasgow (Sinclair, *History of the Public Revenue*, pt. iii. 1790, p. 69). Pitt resigned Oct. 5, 1761, but Newcastle continued in office until May 1762.</note> with greater ease than Lord Godolphin could levy six million in Queen Anne's time. The French and Dutch writers, embracing the same principle, frequently alarmed their countries with the same groundless terror, but they still continue to flourish. It is to be observed that the poverty of a nation can never result from foreign trade if it is carried on with wisdom and prudence. The poverty of a nation proceeds from much the same causes as those which make an individual poor. When a man consumes more than he earns by his industry, he must impoverish himself unless he has some other means of subsistence. In the same manner, if a nation consumes more than it produces, poverty is inevitable; if its annual produce is ninety million and its annual consumption a hundred million, then it spends, eats, drinks, and wears out ten million more than it produces, and its stock of wealth must gradually dwindle to nothing.

### [§ 12. *Of the Opinion that no Expense at Home can be harmful.*]

There is still another bad effect proceeding from that absurd notion that national wealth consists in money. It is commonly imagined that whatever people

<meta>This page continues the discussion on national wealth, specifically addressing the fallacy that domestic spending cannot decrease public wealth.</meta>

<page-num>208</page-num>
<header>PART II : POLICE</header>

[...spending] in their own country cannot diminish public wealth, if you take care of exports and imports. This is the foundation of Dr. Mandeville’s system—that private vices are public benefits—arguing that what is spent at home is all spent among ourselves, and none of it leaves the country. <note>In *The Fable of the Bees*, part 1, Remark (L), Mandeville actually argues against this doctrine.</note> But it is evident that when any man wears out, consumes, and spends his capital without engaging in any kind of industry, the nation is that much poorer for it by the end of the year. If he spends only the interest on the money, he does no harm, as the capital still remains and is used to promote industry; but if he spends the capital itself, the whole is gone. To illustrate this, let us suppose that my father at his death, instead of a thousand pounds in cash, leaves me the necessities and conveniences of life to the same value. This is exactly the same as if he had left it in money, because I would otherwise purchase them with money. I gather a number of idle people around me, and we eat, drink, wear out, and consume everything until the whole amount is exhausted. By doing this, I not only reduce myself to poverty, but certainly rob the public wealth of a thousand pounds, as it is spent and nothing is produced in return. As a further illustration of the harm the public receives from such practices, let us suppose that this island were invaded by a numerous band of Tartars—a people who are still in the state of shepherds, who lead a nomadic life, and have little or no idea of industry. Here they would find all commodities for the taking; they would put on fine clothes, and eat, drink, wear out, and consume everything they laid their hands upon. The consequence would be that from the highest degree of wealth, the whole country would be reduced to the

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding public wealth, specifically concluding the illustration of how a nation can be reduced to poverty through consumption without production, as exemplified by the hypothetical invasion of Tartars.</meta>

lowest level of misery, and brought back to its ancient state. The thirty million in money would probably remain for some time, but all the necessities of life would be consumed. This shows the absurdity of the opinion that no home consumption can hurt the wealth of a country.

Upon this principle that no public expense used at home can be harmful, a war in Germany is thought to be a dreadful calamity because it drains the country of money, and a land war is always thought to be more harmful than a sea war for the same reason; but upon reflection, we will find that it is the same thing for the nation, regardless of how or where its stock is spent. If I purchase a thousand pounds’ worth of French wines and drink them all when they arrive home, the country is two thousand pounds poorer, because both the goods and the money are gone; if I spend a thousand pounds’ worth of goods at home on myself, the country is only deprived of one thousand pounds, as the money still remains; but in maintaining an army in a distant war, it is the same thing whether we pay them in goods or money, because the consumption is the same in either case. Perhaps it is the better policy to pay them in money, as goods are better suited for the purposes of life at home¹. For the same reason, there is no difference between land and sea wars, as is commonly imagined.

From the above considerations, it appears that Britain should by all means be made a free port, that there should be no interruptions of any kind made to foreign trade, and that if it were possible to pay for the expenses of government by any other method, all duties, customs, and excise taxes should be abolished, and that free commerce and liberty of exchange should be allowed with all nations, and for all things.

<page-num>210</page-num>
<header>PART II : POLICE</header>

But further still, and on the same principles as above, a justification is offered for the public debt. They say that although we currently owe more than a hundred million$^1$, we owe it to ourselves, or at least very little of it to foreigners. It is simply the right hand owing the left, and on the whole, it can be of little or no disadvantage. But it must be considered that the interest on this hundred million is paid by industrious people and given to support idle people who are employed in collecting it. Thus, industry is taxed to support idleness. If the debt had not been contracted, through prudence and economy, the nation would have been much richer than it is at present. Their industry would not be harmed by the oppression of those idle people who live off of it. Instead of the brewer paying taxes which are often inappropriate, the <term>stock</term> might have been lent out to such industrious people as would have earned six or seven percent with it and given better interest than the government does; this <term>stock</term> would then have been employed for the country's welfare. When there are such heavy taxes to pay, every merchant must carry on less trade than he otherwise would; he has to pay his taxes before he sells any of his goods. This narrows, as it were, his <term>stock</term>, and prevents his trade from being as extensive as it would otherwise be$^2$. To silence this outcry, Sir Robert Walpole attempted to show that the public debt was no inconvenience$^3$, though it is to

### [§ 13. *Regarding the Scheme of Mr. Law.*]

The last bad effect that will be noted is the idea of Mr. Law, a Scottish merchant. He believed that national wealth consists of money, and that the value of gold and silver is arbitrary and depends on laws and agreement. He imagined that the concept of value could be transferred to paper, and that paper would be preferred over money. If this could be done, he thought it would be very convenient, as the government could then do whatever it pleased: raise armies, pay soldiers, and incur any expense whatsoever. Mr. Law proposed his scheme to the Scottish Parliament in 1701¹. It was rejected, and he went over to France, where his project was favored by the Duke of Orleans. In this book² he agrees with the previously mentioned writers that, if the balance of trade is against a nation, it must soon be drained of its money. To turn the balance of trade in our favor, he proposed the following scheme to the Scottish Parliament:

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>PART II : POLICE</header>

As there was little gold or silver in this country, he thought they might adopt some other method of creating money independent of it—namely, through paper. On this account, he proposed the establishment of a land bank in Edinburgh, in which, it should be noted, he makes many mistakes concerning tenures and the nature of property. At this bank, they were to keep on hand only twenty or thirty thousand pounds to meet small demands, and to issue notes backed by land. For every two acres of arable land, they were to issue a note of equal value; and if any extraordinary demand was made upon them, they would pay part of it in money and part in land. By this means, in a very short time, all the land of Scotland would pass from hand to hand, just as a twenty-shilling note does¹.

As this project was never executed, it is difficult to say what the consequence might have been; it is, however, obviously subject to the following problems. Assuming the land rent of Scotland is five million pounds per year—though it is actually much more—at a <term>twenty years' purchase</term> it amounts to one hundred million pounds. There would then be exactly that much currency in the country; and if only one million was then necessary for circulation, there would be ninety-nine million serving no purpose, as none of it could be sent abroad. They would not have been able to support a single person more than before, as their food, clothing, and housing would not have increased, and every commodity would have risen to ninety-nine times its current value.

Mr. Law, not meeting with the encouragement he expected, went over to France in the year² 1714 and, as was previously mentioned, found favor with the Duke of Orleans, who was then³ Regent, and obtained permission to establish a bank

there, which at first was only to the extent of six million livres¹ or £320,000 sterling. From this beginning, he carried it to a very great height, issued many notes, and in a short time took control of the entire circulation of France. As Mr. Law’s notes were accepted as payment for the government revenue², this contributed to the success of the scheme. This also had a greater effect in France than it could have had here, considering the number of taxes and the way in which they are collected. Because of this and other circumstances, his notes were always equal in value to gold and silver, especially since they were making frequent changes to their coinage. Around that time, twenty-eight livres, which were equal to eight ounces of pure silver, were increased in value to sixty³, and as a devaluation of coinage is always the consequence of a sudden rise in nominal value⁴, this was expected daily. Mr. Law made his notes payable in what was called "the money of the day"⁵. Instead of promising to pay his notes in pounds sterling, as we would say, he did so in crowns and half-crowns, which was a very effective method to make them equal in value to gold and silver. Suppose that our coinage were doubled in nominal value; a half-crown would become a crown, and in this manner, the bank notes and money would rise and fall together⁶.

As Law wanted to make his notes worth more than their face value, he came up with the following scheme. He issued his bank notes payable in *livres tournois*, so that when the value of the coin was reduced, he would not be obliged to pay more than half¹. The coin was not accepted in the market or elsewhere because a devaluation was still expected and did not occur for some time. This favored his plan and kept the notes above face value, which established the credit of his bank.

The next step Mr. Law took was the relief of the public debts, which amounted to 2,000 million². As he saw that the devaluation was inevitable, he used another method to support his notes. He obtained a grant of the exclusive privilege of trading to Canada and established the Mississippi Company. To this, he joined the African, the Turkey, and the East India companies. He also farmed the tobacco monopoly and all the public revenues of France for 52 million³, for in France the whole

Mr. Law took on this responsibility, and, having monopolized the entire trade of the country, it was difficult to say how much profit he would make. He intended to lend the government 80 or 90 million [sterling], which he could easily do by issuing bank notes of that value, but then he realized that they would soon be returned to him for payment. To prevent this, he set his ingenuity to work, and we shall see how successful he was. As the company he established seemed to be in a very flourishing condition, shares in it were purchased at a very high price. He opened a subscription for it at 500 livres, allowing a navy ticket or *billet d’état* to purchase a share in it. This raised the value of the tickets to par, as they had been worth far less for a long time. The government of France was never in such a wretched condition as it was then. The interest on the money that should have paid the *billets d’état* was seized for other purposes. Never was a monarch more humiliated than Louis XIV. After the Treaty of Utrecht, he needed to borrow 8 million livres from Holland; he not only had to give them his bond for 32 million but also had to find merchants to act as security for him<sup>2</sup>. Since that was the case, we should not be surprised that the *billets d’état* sold at a great discount, as they earned no interest, and it was quite uncertain when they would be paid. Law published a declaration that one of these tickets, granted for 500 livres, could purchase a share in the company, and thus they returned to par value. As the people continued to have great expectations of profit, he opened a new subscription at 5,000 livres in just a few days,

Had he stopped here, it is probable that he would have met all his obligations, but his future actions ruined everything. It was impossible for the value of shares to remain at such a high rate for long. He thought, however, that it was necessary to do everything he could to keep them up, as the entire fortunes of many people were tied up in the bank. He had issued notes that doubled the circulation of the country, which raised the price of everything to an enormous level, and consequently, the exchange rate was against France in all foreign trade. This was primarily caused by his opening an office to purchase 500-livre shares at 9,000 livres <note>2. *Ibid*., pages 280, 281.</note>, which forced him to issue many notes. Prudent people who were involved opposed this scheme, and indeed, it was the first thing that caused his bank to lose credit and led to its dissolution. Since he was not required to pay back the capital sums, only the annual dividend of 200 livres arising from the profits <note>3. The original fixed interest was only 4 percent, or 20 livres on the shares of 500 livres; but a general meeting of the com-

<column-break/>

pany, held on Dec. 30, 1719, resolved that the dividend should be 40 percent, or 200 livres per share. *Ibid*., pages 215, 267, 268, 317.</note>, he might have let the shares fall to their original 500 without any great loss except for his reputation; but his buying up the shares caused him to issue so many notes that they inevitably had to be returned to him for payment. This occurred to such an extent that he was forced to open offices in different parts of Paris for the payment of them. When he was overwhelmed in this manner, he made continual changes to the coinage in order to dissuade people from returning to the bank and to make them lose interest in gold and silver <note>4. “To make the people lose their taste for gold and silver coins.” *Ibid*., page 316.</note>. cannot be kept much above the value of the metal; when it was so significantly depreciated, it was not accepted. If a person had 20,000 guineas, fearing that the coin would not maintain its value, he went to the bank and exchanged it for notes. The same concern prevented people from returning notes to the bank, as they would there be paid in coin. By this means, he not only prevented his notes from being returned for payment, but also filled his coffers with almost all the gold in the country. In order to accomplish this part of his scheme more perfectly, he most arbitrarily issued an edict prohibiting any persons from keeping more than a certain sum of gold or silver by them <note>1. Namely, 500 livres. Duverney, *Examen*, volume 1, page 335.</note>. He also removed the severe penalties that were in force against the exportation of coin, and every person was allowed to export money duty-free <note>2. *Ibid.*, pages 320, 321.</note>. By this means, much of it went to Holland. He reasoned with himself that some instrument of exchange is necessary; paper, gold, and silver are currently the medium. If gold and silver are entirely exported, only paper remains and can be made the sole instrument of commerce. This he thought he had done effectively when, by an edict, he had swept a portion into his coffers and cleared the country of the remainder. People would therefore be forced to use paper. At last, however, after many different strategies, he found it was impractical. By paying out large sums, he delayed ruin for some months, but finally issued an edict stating that all bank notes were to be paid at only half their value. Indeed, if he had stuck to this, as some imagined he might have done, it would have been far better than suffering the subsequent consequences. Following this edict, the credit of the bank was entirely destroyed, and the value of bank notes suddenly sank to nothing <note>3. *Ibid.*, volume 2, pages 6–8.</note>. This ruined an immense number of people. Britain can never be much hurt by

The South Sea scheme in our own country was nothing compared to this. Nobody was under any obligation to participate in it, the government had no share in it, and the loss was but a trifle in comparison. This amazing scheme was founded on these two principles: that public wealth consists in money, and that the value of money is arbitrary, founded upon the common consent of mankind. Consistent with these principles, he thought he might easily increase public wealth if he could attach the idea of money to paper, and the government could never be at any loss to produce any effect that money could achieve. This scheme of Mr. Law’s was by no means contemptible; he really believed in it and was a victim of it himself. It was thought he had provided well for himself, but it was found to be otherwise. It was established in the latter end of Queen Anne’s reign, and its purpose was to carry on a trade to the South Seas. For this purpose, they bought up the greater part of the national debt. Their stock, however, was not large, and the profits which could be expected from it were very small; the people's expectations were never greatly raised, and its collapse was not very harmful to the nation.

### [§ 14. *Of Interest.*]

We have only two more things to mention relating to the price of commodities, namely, interest and exchange.

It is commonly supposed that the rate of interest depends upon the value of gold and silver <note>2. In *Wealth of Nations* book ii. chapter iv. volume i. page 357, the common opinion is attributed to Locke, Law, Montesquieu, and ‘many other writers,’ and it is remarked that ‘this notion, which at first sight seems so plausible, has been so fully <column-break/> exposed by Mr. Hume, that it is perhaps unnecessary to say anything more about it.’ See Locke, *Some Considerations of the Consequences of the Lowering of Interest and Raising the Value of Money*, 2nd ed., 1696, pp. 6, 10, 11; Law, *Money and Trade Considered*, 2nd ed., p. 17, and *Memoirs on Banks* in Daire’s *Financial Economists*, p. 518; Montesquieu, *The Spirit of the Laws*, book xxii. chapter vi; Hume, ‘Of Interest,’ *Political Discourses*, 1752, pp. 61-78.</note>. The value of these is regulated by their quantity, for as the quantity increases, the value diminishes, and as the quantity de-

<summary>This page concludes the analysis of John Law’s influence by comparing his scheme to England's South Sea Company and introduces a new section on the economic principles of interest and exchange.</summary>
<keywords>John Law, South Sea Company, Queen Anne, national debt, interest rates, exchange, gold and silver value, Adam Smith, David Hume, economic history</keywords>

<page-num>220</page-num>
<header>PART II : POLICE</header>

...increases, the value rises. If we pay attention to it, however, we shall find that the premium of interest is regulated by the quantity of stock. Around the time of the discovery of the West Indies, it is to be observed that common interest was at 10 or 12 percent, and since that time it has gradually diminished. The plain reason is this: Under the feudal constitution, there could be very little accumulation of stock, which will appear from considering the situation of those three orders of men who made up the whole body of the people: the peasants, the landlords, and the merchants. The peasants had leases which depended upon the caprice of their masters; they could never increase in wealth because the landlord was ready to squeeze it all from them, and therefore they had no motive to acquire it. As little could the landlords increase their wealth, as they lived such an indolent life and were involved in perpetual wars <note>1. Above, pp. 36, 37; *Wealth of Nations* book iii. chapter ii. volume i. pages 389, 390.</note>. The merchants, again, were oppressed by all ranks and were not able to secure the produce of their industry from robbery and violence. Thus, there could be little accumulation of wealth at all; but after the fall of the feudal government, these obstacles to industry were removed, and the stock of commodities began gradually to increase.

We may further observe that what one trade lends to another is not so much to be considered as money, as commodities <note>2. *Ibid.* book ii. chapter iv. volume i. page 354.</note>. No doubt it is generally money which one man delivers to another in a loan, but then it is immediately turned into stock, and thus the quantity of stock enables you to make a greater number of loans. The price of interest is entirely regulated by this circumstance. If there are few who have it in their power to lend money, and a great number of people who want to borrow it, the price of interest must be high; but if the quantity of stock on hand is so great as to enable a great number to lend, it must fall proportionately.

<summary>This page argues that interest rates are determined by the availability of stock rather than the value of gold and silver, citing historical feudal restrictions on wealth accumulation as evidence. It explains that loans between trades are essentially transfers of commodities, and the interest rate reflects the balance between lenders and borrowers.</summary>

### [§ 15. *Of Exchange.*]

Exchange is a method invented by merchants to facilitate the payment of money at a distance. Suppose I owe £100 to a merchant in London; I apply to a banker in Glasgow for a bill drawn on another merchant in London, payable to my creditor. For this, I must not only give the banker £100, but I must also compensate him for his trouble. This reward is called the price, or premium, of exchange. Between Glasgow and London, it is sometimes at 2 percent, sometimes more, sometimes less. Between London and Glasgow, again, it is sometimes 4 or 5 percent below par; and between Glasgow and the West India colonies, it is often at 50 percent below par. The value of exchange is always regulated by the risk of sending money between two places. It is often, however, higher than the risk might be expected to be, and this is due to paper circulation. Between Glasgow and London, one can easily have £100 transported for fifteen or sixteen shillings; but as paper money in Scotland makes up a large part of the currency, and as there is an inconvenience in getting bank notes exchanged for gold and silver, a merchant chooses to pay 2 percent rather than take the trouble of exchanging the notes for cash and sending the physical money <note>1. *Wealth of Nations*, book ii, chapter ii, volume i, pages 327, 328.</note>. This, too, is the cause of the high price of exchange between Virginia and Glasgow. In the American colonies, the currency is paper, and their notes are 40 or 50 percent below par because the backing funds are insufficient. In every exchange, one must pay the cost, the risk, some profit to the banker, and an amount reflecting the degradation of money in notes. This is the cause of the rise of exchange. Whenever it rises beyond the cost of insurance, it is because the money of one country is of lower value than that of another. This was the cause of the high price of

<summary>This page defines exchange as a merchant's tool for long-distance payments and explains that its cost is determined by risk, banking fees, and the depreciation of paper currency relative to specie.</summary>

All the money had been driven out of France by the scheme of Mr. Law, and the entire circulation consisted of paper, and the credit of the bank had fallen. All these reasons combined to raise the exchange rate to such an enormous height.

### [§ 16. *Of the Causes of the Slow Progress of Opulence.*]

We come now to the next topic proposed: to examine the causes of the slow progress of opulence. When one considers the effects of the <term>division of labour</term>, and what an immediate tendency it has to improve the arts and industries, it appears somewhat surprising that every nation should continue for so long in the poor and destitute state in which we find them. The causes of this may be considered under these two headings: first, natural impediments; and secondly, the oppression of civil government.

An uncivilized and barbarous people are ignorant of the effects of the <term>division of labour</term>, and it is a long time before one person, by continually working at different things, can produce any more than is necessary for his daily subsistence. Before labor can be divided, some accumulation of <term>stock</term> is necessary; a poor man with no stock can never begin a manufacturing business. Before a man can begin as a farmer, he must at least have laid in a year’s provisions, because he does not receive the fruits of his labor until the end of the season. Consistent with this, in a nation of hunters or shepherds, no person can quit the common trade in which he is employed—and which provides him with daily subsistence—until he has some <term>stock</term> to maintain himself while he begins the new trade. Everyone knows how difficult it is, even in a refined society, to raise oneself to moderate circumstances. It is still more difficult to raise oneself by those trades which require no skill nor

<summary>This page discusses how John Law's Mississippi Scheme caused extreme exchange rates in France and introduces the primary obstacles to economic growth: natural impediments and government oppression. It emphasizes that the division of labor cannot occur without the prior accumulation of stock or capital.</summary>

<margin>^1^ *Wealth of Nations, book ii. Introduction, vol. i. pp. 273-275.*</margin>
ingenuity. A porter or day-laborer must remain poor forever. In the beginnings of society, this is even more difficult. Bare subsistence is almost all that a savage can procure, and having no stock to begin with—having nothing to maintain him but what is produced by the exertion of his own strength—it is no wonder he remains for a long time in a state of poverty. The humblest laborer in a refined society has in many respects an advantage over a savage: he has more assistance in his labor; he has only one particular thing to do, which, through diligence, he becomes skilled at performing; he also has machines and instruments which greatly assist him. An Indian has not so much as a pickaxe, a spade, or a shovel, nor anything else but his own labor. This is one great cause of the slow progress of <term>opulence</term> in every country; until some stock is produced, there can be no <term>division of labor</term>, and before a division of labor takes place, there can be very little <term>accumulation of stock</term> ^1^.

The other cause that was assigned was the nature of <term>civil government</term>. In the infancy of society, as has often been observed, government must be weak and feeble, and it is a long time before its authority can protect the industry of individuals from the greed of their neighbors. When people find themselves every moment in danger of being robbed of all they possess, they have no motive to be industrious. There could be little accumulation of stock because the lazy, who would be the majority, would live off the industrious and spend whatever they produced. When the power of government becomes so great as to defend the produce of industry, another obstacle arises from a different direction. Among neighboring nations in a primitive state, there are perpetual wars, with one continually invading and plundering the other; and though private property may be secured from the violence of neighbors, it remains in danger from hostile invasions. In this

manner, it is almost impossible for any accumulation of <term>stock</term> to be made. It is observable that among savage nations there are always more violent upheavals than among those further advanced in refinement. Among the Tartars and Arabs, great bands of barbarians are always roaming from one place to another in search of plunder, and they pillage every country as they go along. Thus, large tracts of country are often laid waste, and all possessions are carried away. Germany, too, was in the same condition around the fall of the Roman Empire; nothing can be a greater obstacle to the progress of <term>opulence</term>.

We shall next consider the effect of oppressive measures, first with regard to <term>agriculture</term>, and then with regard to commerce.

Agriculture is, of all arts, the most beneficial to society, and whatever tends to delay its improvement is extremely harmful to the public interest. The produce of agriculture is much greater than that of any other <term>manufacture</term>. The rents of all the lands in England amount to about 24 million <note>Refers to footnote 1</note>, and as the rent is generally about a third of the produce, the total annual produce of the lands must be about 72 million. This is much more than the produce of either the linen or wool industries; for, as annual consumption is calculated to be about 100 million, if you subtract from this the 72 million from the produce of agriculture, there will remain only 28 million for all the other industries of the nation. Therefore, whatever measures discourage the improvement of this art are extremely harmful to the progress of opulence.

One great hindrance to the progress of agriculture is the placing of vast tracts of land into the hands of single individuals. If any man’s estate is larger than he is able to cultivate, a part of it is, in a sense, lost. When a nation

of savages takes possession of a country, the great and powerful divide the whole lands among them, and leave none for the lower ranks of people. In this manner the Celts, and afterwards the Saxons, took possession of our own island ¹.

When land is divided in large portions among the powerful, it is cultivated by slaves, which is a very unprofitable method of cultivation. The labor of a slave proceeds from no other motive but the dread of punishment, and if he could escape this, he would not work at all. Should he exert himself in the most extraordinary manner, he cannot have the least expectation of any reward; and as all the produce of his labor goes to his master, he has no encouragement to industry. A young slave may perhaps exert himself a little at first, in order to attain his master's favor; but he soon finds that it is all in vain, and that, whatever his behavior may be, he will always meet with the same severe treatment.

When lands, therefore, are cultivated by slaves, they cannot be greatly improved, as the workers have no motive for industry. A cultivation of the same kind is that by <term>villains</term> <note>A feudal term for a class of semi-free peasants or serfs</note>. The landlord gave a man a piece of ground to cultivate; allowing him to maintain himself by it, and obliging him to restore whatever was over his own maintenance. This was equally unfavorable to the progress of agriculture, because the villains, who were a kind of slave, had no motive for industry beyond their own maintenance. This objection lies equally against all cultivation by slaves. Some of the West India islands have indeed been cultivated by slaves, and have been greatly improved, but they might have been cultivated by freemen at less expense; and had the profits of sugar not been very great, the planters could not have supported the expense of slaves, but their profits have been so enormous, that all the extraordinary expense of slave cultivation has vanished before it ².

In the colonies, they employ few slaves, and although they are in a very flourishing condition in those colonies, the lands are generally cultivated by the proprietors themselves, which is the most favorable method for the progress of agriculture. A tenant of the best kind always has a rent to pay, and therefore has much less to spend on improvements $^1$. When a country sends out a colony, it may prevent a large tract of land from being occupied by a single person $^2$; but when savages take possession of a country, they are subject to no laws; the strongest man takes possession of the most ground, and therefore agriculture cannot be quickly promoted among them.

After <term>villains</term> <note>Feudal serfs</note> were phased out, as was explained before $^3$, tenants by "steel bow" succeeded them. The landlord gave a farm with capital <note>The livestock and equipment</note> to a villain, which were restored to the landlord at the end of the year along with half of the produce; but as the tenant had no capital—nor, even if he had possessed any $^4$, any encouragement to spend it on improvements—this method was always unfavorable to agriculture. For the same reason that <term>tithes</term>, by depriving the farmer of a tenth of his produce, hinder improvement, this system was a hindrance to an even higher degree because the tenant was deprived of one-half of the produce. A great part of France is still cultivated by steel bow tenants, and it is said that it still remains in some parts of the Highlands of Scotland $^5$.

The next species of cultivation was that by tenants, such as we have at present. Some of the steel bow tenants, through extreme frugality and cleverness, saved up a small amount of capital and offered their masters a fixed rent for the ground. Thus, in the course of time, the present method of cultivation was introduced, though it was long

...subject to inconveniences. If the landlord sold his land, the new owner was not bound by the terms of the agreement, and the tenant was often evicted from his farm. The landlord also invented a method to get rid of the tenant whenever he pleased by selling the estate to another person, from whom he held a "back bond" <note>A legal counter-agreement</note> to make him return the estate once the tenants were evicted. As the tenants were constantly in danger of being evicted, they had no incentive to improve the ground. This remains the case to this day in every country of Europe, except Britain. In Scotland, contracts of this type were established as "real rights" <note>Legal rights enforceable against any owner</note> during the reign of James III $^1$, and in England during the reign of Henry VII $^2$.

Besides these, there were several other obstacles to the progress of agriculture. At first, all rents were paid "in kind" <note>Paid in produce rather than money</note>, by which, in a year of high prices, the tenants were in danger of being ruined. A reduction in produce seldom hurts the tenant who pays his rent in money, because the price of grain rises in proportion to its scarcity $^3$. Society, however, is significantly advanced before money becomes the sole instrument of commerce.

Another difficulty was that the feudal lords sometimes allowed the king to levy subsidies from their tenants, which greatly discouraged their hard work $^4$. Above all, under the tyranny of the feudal aristocracy, the landlords had nothing to stop them from exploiting their tenants and raising the rents of their lands as high as they pleased. Several circumstances combined to continue the engrossment of lands. The right of primogeniture was established quite early and prevented estates from being divided. The institution of entails is to this day attended with the same bad consequences ². The complications, too, of feudal law in transferring property delayed the progress of agriculture. Any quantity of any other commodity may be bought or sold in an instant, but in purchasing four or five acres of land, a great deal of time must be spent in examining the progress of writs ³, and getting your right legally established. This tends greatly to the engrossment of lands and, consequently, stops their improvement. If all the formalities in buying lands were abolished, almost every person who had earned a little money would be ready to invest it in land, and the land, by passing through different hands, would be much better improved. There is no natural reason why a thousand acres should not be as easily purchased as a thousand yards of cloth. Keeping land out of the market always hinders its improvement. A merchant who buys a small piece of land intends to improve it and make the most of it he can. Great and ancient families seldom have either the capital or the inclination to improve their estates, except for a small piece of pleasure-ground around their house.

There are many errors in the administration <note>police</note> of almost every country which have contributed greatly to stopping the progress of agriculture. Our ancestors, finding themselves once every two or three years subject to the most grievous

dearth; to escape that calamity, they prohibited the exportation of grain <note>corn</note>. This is still the policy <note>police</note> of the greater part of Europe, and it is the cause of all that scarcity <note>dearth</note> it is intended to prevent. In a plentiful year, the grain of Spain, though it is the most fertile country in the world, is not worth harvesting; they allow it to lie rotting on the ground because they would earn nothing for it. The cause of this is commonly imagined to be the indolence of the people. The fact is, the farmer, finding he cannot sell his grain this year, will not risk a crop next year but turns his land into pasture. The next year a famine follows, and then he sows more than can be sold for the following season. It should be observed that this was one major cause of the depopulation of ancient Italy. The exportation of grain was prohibited by severe penalties, and its importation was encouraged by high subsidies <note>premiums</note>, so that Italian farmers had no incentive for industry, as they were not guaranteed a market. In the later times of the Republic, the Emperors tried several methods of promoting the cultivation of the country; however, being ignorant that the real cause of their shortage was the immense quantity of grain daily imported from Egypt and other parts of Africa, all their efforts were ineffective. Caligula and Claudius gave their soldiers land for free, on the condition that they would cultivate it; but since the soldiers had no other motivation, very minor improvements were made. Virgil, too, published his *Georgics* to bring the cultivation of land into fashion, but it was all in vain. Foreign grain was always sold cheaper than their own could be grown¹. In accordance with this, we find Cato, in the third book of Cicero’s *Offices*, preferring pasturage of any kind to farming². The Kings of Spain have also done everything in their

Philip IV went to the plow himself in order to set the fashion. <insert>(</insert>He did everything for the farmers except providing them a good market; he conferred titles of nobility upon several farmers; he very absurdly endeavored to oppress manufacturers with heavy taxes in order to force them to the country; he thought that as the number of inhabitants in towns increased, those in the country decreased. This notion was highly ridiculous; for the population of a town is the very cause of the population of the country, because it gives greater encouragement to industry. Every man in a town must be fed by another in the country, and it is always a sign that the country is improving when men go to town. There are no parts of the country so well inhabited nor so well cultivated as those which lie in the neighborhood of populous cities.

All these causes have hindered, and still hinder, the improvement of agriculture, the most important branch of industry. We may observe that the greater the number of manufacturers there are in any country, the more agriculture is improved, and the causes which prevent the progress of manufacturing react, as it were, upon agriculture. It is easy to show that the free export and import of grain <note>corn</note> is favorable to agriculture. In England, the country has been better stored with grain <note>corn</note>, and the price of it has gradually fallen, since its exportation was permitted. The bounty on exportation does harm in other respects, but it increases the quantity of grain <note>corn</note> ¹. In Holland grain <note>corn</note> is cheaper and more plentiful than anywhere else, and a famine <note>dearth</note> is unknown there. That country is, as it were, the storehouse <note>magazine</note> of grain <note>corn</note> for a great part of Europe: this is entirely owing to the free export and import they enjoy. place, any country in Europe could do more than sustain itself with all sorts of grain.

The slow progress of arts and commerce is due to causes of a similar kind. In all places where slavery existed, manufacturing was carried out by slaves. It is impossible for it to be as well managed by slaves as by free men, because slaves can have no motivation to labor except the fear of punishment, and can never invent any machine to facilitate their business. Free men who have a stock <note>capital</note> of their own can get anything accomplished which they think may be useful for carrying out their labor. If a carpenter thinks that a plane will serve his purpose better than a knife, he may go to a smith and have it made; but if a slave makes any such proposal, he is called a lazy rascal, and no experiments are made to give him ease. Currently, the Turks and Hungarians work mines of the same kind, situated upon opposite sides of the same range of mountains, but the Hungarians make much more of them than the Turks, because they employ free men, while the Turks employ slaves. When the Hungarians encounter any obstacle, every invention is put to work to find an easy way to overcome it; but the Turks think of no other solution but to put a greater number of slaves to work ¹. In the ancient world, as the arts were all carried out by slaves, no machinery could be invented because they had no stock; after the fall of the Roman Empire, this was also the case all over Europe.

In a primitive society, nothing is considered honorable except war. In
