<header>PART II : POLICE</header>
<columns>2</columns>

the *Odyssey*, Ulysses is sometimes asked, as an insult, whether he is a pirate or a merchant ¹. At that time, a merchant was considered hateful and despicable; but a pirate or robber, as he was a man of military bravery, was treated with honor. We may observe that those principles of the human mind which are most beneficial to society are by no means marked by nature as the most honorable. Hunger, thirst, and sexual passion are the great supports of the human species, yet almost every expression of these excites contempt. In the same manner, that principle in the mind which prompts one to truck <note>trade</note>, barter, and exchange—though it is the great foundation of arts, commerce, and the division of labor—is nonetheless not marked with anything particularly pleasing. To perform anything, or to give anything without a reward, is always generous and noble, but to barter one thing for another is considered petty. The plain reason for this is that these principles are so strongly implanted by nature that they have no need for that additional social encouragement which the weaker principles require. In primitive ages, this contempt reaches its highest pitch, and even in a refined society, it is not utterly extinguished. In this country, even today, a small retailer is to some degree looked down upon. When the trade of a merchant or mechanic was thus devalued in the beginnings of society, it is no wonder that it was confined to the lowest ranks of people. Even when emancipated slaves began to practice these trades, it was impossible for much stock <note>capital</note> to accumulate in their hands, for the government oppressed them severely, and they were obliged to pay for licenses for their freedom to trade. In the Domesday Book, we have an account of all the different traders in every

This lowly and contemptible view of merchants significantly hindered the progress of commerce. The merchant is, as it were, the middleman between the manufacturer and the consumer; the weaver must not go to the market himself, there must be someone to do this for him. This person must possess a considerable amount of stock <note>capital</note> to buy up the goods and support the manufacturer; but when merchants were so despised and subjected to such heavy taxes for the freedom to trade, they could never accumulate the amount of capital necessary for the division of labor and the improvement of manufacturing. The only people in those days who made any money through trade were the <term>Jews</term>, who, because they were viewed as vagabonds, had no right to purchase land and had no other way to support themselves except by becoming craftsmen or merchants; their reputations could not be ruined by trading, because they could not be more hated than their religion already made them. Even they were severely oppressed, and consequently, the growth of wealth was greatly delayed.

Another factor that significantly slowed commerce was the imperfection of the law regarding contracts, which were the last category of rights to be legally enforceable; for originally, the law provided no remedy for any except those concluded on the spot². At present, all significant commerce is carried out through commissions, and unless these were legally enforceable, little could be accomplished. The first legal actions on contracts applied only to the movable property of the contractor; neither his land nor his person could be seized; his goods were often very incon-

considerable, and honesty is not among the most prevalent virtues among an uncivilized people. It is commerce that introduces honesty and punctuality.

Another obstacle to the improvement of commerce was the difficulty of transport from one place to another. The country was then filled with <term>retainers</term>, a type of idle people who depended on the lords, whose violence and disorder made traveling from one place to another very difficult. Besides, there were no good highways then. The lack of navigable rivers in many places was¹ also an inconvenience. This is still the case in Asia and other Eastern countries: all inland commerce is carried out by large caravans, consisting of several thousands for mutual defense, with wagons, etc. In our own country, a man made his will before he set out from Edinburgh to Aberdeen, and it was even more dangerous to go to foreign countries. The laws of every country toward aliens and strangers are far from being favorable. It is difficult, or rather impossible, for them to obtain legal satisfaction. Even after this was slightly improved, transport by sea remained difficult. Piracy was an honorable occupation. Men were ignorant of navigation and exposed to dangers for this reason. The cost of all these risks was added to the price of the goods, and in this way, they were raised so far above their natural price that the growth of commerce was greatly delayed.

Another piece of <term>police</term> <note>regulations or public policy</note> which was thought to be a wise institution by our ancestors had the same effect. This was the fairs and markets held all over Europe. Until the sixteenth century, all commerce was carried out through fairs. The fairs of Bartholomew², Leipzig, Troyes in *Champagne*, and even Glasgow, are frequently mentioned in history. These

All linen and black cattle were brought in from the country to these appointments or gatherings, and, to ensure the purchaser would not be disappointed, they were all brought on a certain day and were not allowed to be sold on any other day. **Forestallers**, who traveled throughout the country buying up goods, were severely punished, as this acted as a temptation not to bring them to the market. This might have been necessary when it was not safe to travel anywhere alone, but even if you establish no fairs, buyers and sellers will find a way to reach each other. Easy transport and other conveniences of trade will be of more advantage than bringing them to a fixed market and thereby restricting buying and selling to a certain season. All fairs, however necessary they were then, are now actual nuisances. It is absurd to preserve in people a regard for their old customs when the causes for them have been removed.

(Another obstacle to commerce was **staple towns**, which had the exclusive privilege of selling a certain commodity within that district.) Calais, when it belonged to the English, was for a long time the staple for wool¹. Since men were forced to carry their wool such a distance, its price was very high. It was, however, a very great advantage to any town to hold the staple, and therefore the king gave it to the town with which he was best pleased and took it away whenever it displeased him ². Staple towns had all the disadvantages of fairs and markets with this additional one: that the staple commodity could be sold at no fair or market except one. By this, the liberty of

<margin>{</margin> All taxes on the export and import of goods also hinder commerce. At first, merchants were in such a lowly state that the law essentially abandoned them, and it did not matter what they were forced to pay. They must, however, pass the tax on to their goods; consequently, their prices are raised, fewer of them are bought, manufacturing is discouraged, and the division of labor is hindered.

All monopolies and exclusive privileges of corporations have the same negative effect, regardless of the good intentions for which they were first established. Similarly, the statute of apprenticeship, which was originally an imposition on government, has a harmful tendency. It was thought that the reason for so much poor-quality cloth was that the weaver had not been properly trained; therefore, they created a statute requiring that he serve a seven-year apprenticeship before he attempted to make any. However, this is by no means a sufficient guarantee against poor-quality cloth. One cannot personally inspect a large piece of cloth; this must be left to the stampmaster, whose reputation must be relied upon ¹. Above all other causes, the granting of bounties for one commodity and the discouraging of another diminishes the growth of opulence and harms the natural state of commerce.

Before we discuss the effects of police upon the manners of a people, we propose to consider taxes or revenue, which is actually one of the reasons why the progress of opulence has been so slow.

## [PART III: OF REVENUE]

---

### [Introduction.]

In the beginnings of society, all public offices were performed by the magistrate without any reward, and he was fully satisfied with the eminence of his station. This is the case among the Tartars, Arabs, and Hottentots even to this day. Only voluntary presents are accepted, which always have a bad effect but cannot be prevented as long as one person is willing to give and another is willing to receive. It was in this manner, too, that the governors of the Roman provinces obtained their revenues. When government becomes so complex that it takes up the entire attention of the public magistrate, he must undoubtedly have some reward; if this is not given to him by the public, he will resort to some more dangerous method of obtaining it; few will be so generous as to demand nothing <gloss>1</gloss>. When applications are made, everyone must bring a gift, and the person who pays the most will be heard most favorably.

When government is a little more advanced, magazines must be provided, ships built, and palaces and other public buildings erected and maintained; consequently, a public revenue must be levied. At first, indeed, among the Romans there

<note>1 Above, p. 16.</note>

<vocab>revenue, magistrate, society, government, Roman provinces, Tartars, Arabs, Hottentots, magazines</vocab>

<summary>This introduction to public revenue explains that while early magistrates served without pay, the growing complexity of government necessitated a formal revenue system to prevent corruption and fund public infrastructure.</summary>
<keywords>public revenue, magistrate, government evolution, corruption, infrastructure, Roman provinces, Tartars, Arabs, Hottentots, magazines</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the discussion of public revenue and government complexity into the historical methods of funding warfare and the early use of state-owned land to support the sovereign.</meta>

there was no revenue levied for carrying on war, because the soldiers required no pay. In savage nations this is always the case; every one of the Athenians went out to war at his own expense. The same was the case with our feudal lords; the burden of going to war was connected with the duty of the tenant or vassal. Such a practice cannot be of long duration, and accordingly we find that it ceased at Rome, and was the great cause of the dissolution of that republic. The governors of provinces made such grievous exactions from the people that they alienated their affections, so that they gave no assistance in defending the state when it stood in need of assistance.

### [*§ 1. Of Taxes on Possessions.*]

After the appropriation of land property, a portion of lands was commonly assigned for the maintenance of government. The free states of Greece had land set apart for this purpose, and we find Aristotle giving his opinion that private property should surround the royal lands, because those who were near a city were always for war, as they were sure of defense, and because the enemy would first come upon those lands which were near the boundaries <note>He recommends that half the private property should be near the city, and the other half on the border, and that each citizen
<column-break/>
should have one lot in each portion. *Politics*, 1330 a 14-23.</note>. In all [barbarous] countries we find lands appropriated to the purposes of sovereignty, and therefore little occasion for taxes and customs. We shall show that this is a bad policy, and one cause of the slow progress of opulence.

Let us conceive what an immense tract of land would be required to support the British government. The annual expense of it in times of peace amounts to 3 million, while the whole land rents amount <note>The manuscript reads ‘amounts.’</note> to 24 million <note>Above, page 224.</note>. Therefore

<summary>This text explores the shift from self-funded military service to state-funded warfare and argues that relying on dedicated state lands for revenue is a poor policy that hinders economic growth. It contrasts historical practices in Greece and Rome with the contemporary financial requirements of the British government.</summary>

<keywords>revenue, military service, Athenians, feudal lords, Rome, Aristotle, land property, British government, opulence, taxation policy</keywords>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the inefficiency of using state-owned land to fund a modern government, transitioning into a classification of different types of taxes.</meta>

the government would need to have an eighth part of the land in its own hands. If we further consider how such a tract of land would be cultivated, the quantity required would be enormous. Even if we assume it were only half as well cultivated as the rest—which, for many reasons, would not be the case—the government would have to hold a fourth of the entire country. Because of this, the nation's total stock would be greatly diminished, and fewer people could be supported. Once a government becomes expensive, supporting it through land rent is the worst possible method. We can observe that the government in a civilized country is much more expensive than in a primitive one; and when we say that one government is more expensive than another, it is the same as saying that one country is further advanced in its development than another. To say that a government is expensive and yet the people are not oppressed is to say that the people are wealthy. 

There are many expenses necessary in a civilized country that are unnecessary in a primitive one. Armies, fleets, fortifications, and public buildings, as well as judges and tax officials, must be supported, and if they are neglected, disorder will follow. A land rent, intended to serve all these purposes, would be the most unsuitable thing in the world.

All taxes may be considered under two divisions: namely, taxes upon possessions and taxes upon consumption. These are the two ways of making citizens contribute to the support of the government. The land tax is of the former kind, and all taxes upon goods are of the latter.

Possessions are of three kinds: namely, land, <term>stock</term>, and money. It is easy to levy a tax upon land because it is obvious how much land everyone possesses, but it is very difficult to impose a tax upon stock or money without very arbitrary proceedings <note>*Wealth of Nations*, book v. chapter ii. part ii. article 2, volume ii. page 442.</note>. # TAXES ON POSSESSIONS

The taxes on customs and excise, which produce such immense sums, are almost consumed by the legions of officers employed in collecting them. These officers must have supervisors over them to examine their proceedings. The supervisors have collectors over them, who are under the commissioners, who must account to the exchequer; to support these officers, much more must be levied than the government requires, which is a manifest disadvantage.

<note>A handwritten bracket in ink marks the following two sentences.</note>
Another advantage of a land tax is that it does not tend to raise the price of commodities, as it is not paid in proportion to the corn and cattle, but in proportion to the rent. If the tenant pays the tax, he pays just that much less rent. Excise raises the price of commodities and makes fewer people able to conduct business. If a man purchases £1,000 worth of tobacco, he has a hundred pounds of tax to pay and therefore cannot trade to such an extent as he would otherwise do; thus, as it requires greater stock to carry on trade, there must be fewer dealers, and the rich have, as it were, a monopoly against the poor. It was observed before that in England, out of a kind of delicacy regarding the examination of the circumstances of particular persons—which is apparently an infringement upon liberty—no tax is laid upon stock or money, but all upon consumption. Whatever advantages this method may have, there is evidently an inequality in it. The landlord who pays his annual land tax also pays a great part of the taxes on consumption. On this account, the landed interest is the first to complain of a war, thinking the burden of it falls upon them, while on the other hand, the moneyed men are gainers and therefore oppose them. This perhaps causes the continuation of what is called the Tory interest.

PART III : REVENUE

## [§ 2. Of Taxes on Consumptions.]

( Taxes upon possessions are naturally equal, but those upon consumption are naturally unequal, as they are sometimes paid by the merchant, sometimes by the consumer, and sometimes by the importer, who must be reimbursed by the consumer. ) In Holland, all goods are deposited in a public warehouse, one key of which is kept by the commissioner of the customs and another by the owner of the goods. If the goods are exported, no tax is paid in advance; but if they go into the country, the consumer pays the price to the merchant and the custom to the commissioner. This method is much the same as the famous excise scheme of Sir Robert Walpole, which was ultimately his ruin. Its intent was that a general excise should be established, and all imported goods deposited in a public warehouse; the tax would only be paid upon the inland sale of them ¹. Although this scheme is liable to inconveniences—such as subjecting the owner to anxiety from not having his goods entirely in his own power—yet it is plainly this which gives the Dutch so great an advantage over all the other nations of Europe. The Dutch are, in a sense, the carriers for other Europeans; they bring corn from the Baltic and those places where it is cheap, and wines from those places where there has been a good vintage, and keep them until they hear of a scarcity, and then export them to the places where there is a shortage. But in England, the moment you bring the commodities into the country, you must pay the tax and sell them where you please. Thus, the merchant may lose the interest on his money for a long time, and therefore must sell his commodities at a higher price. The Dutch, having no tax to pay except upon inland sales, are enabled to sell more cheaply than the English or any other nation.
(Taxes on consumption have, however, some advantage over those on possessions. They are not felt, being paid

Consumption taxes are not complained about as much because they are placed on the merchant, who adds them to the price of the goods; thus, they are paid by the people without them realizing it. <insert>)</insert> When we buy a pound of tea, we do not consider that most of the price is a duty paid to the government, and therefore we pay it willingly, as if it were only the natural price of the commodity. In the same manner, when an additional tax is placed on beer, its price must be raised, but the common people do not directly vent their anger against the government—who are the proper objects of it—but against the brewers, as they confuse the tax price with the natural one. Consumption taxes, therefore, which are paid by the merchant, seem most to favor liberty and will always be favored by this government. In Holland, they buy a hogshead of wine and first pay the price to the merchant, and then a certain amount to the excise officers, as if to get permission to drink it. We actually do the exact same thing, but as we do not feel it immediately, we imagine it is all one price and never reflect that we might drink port wine for less than sixpence a bottle, <insert>/</insert> were it not for the duty.

The collector must be paid at a specific time of year, and few people have enough self-discipline to set money aside to be ready. Consequently, he has to borrow £20 to meet his current obligations. When the next payment comes, he has not only the tax to pay but also the interest on the money borrowed the previous year. <insert>/</insert> He begins to burden his estate with debt; and thus, upon examination, it will be found that many landowners have been ruined. <margin>{</margin>
It should be observed that taxes on both consumption and possessions are more or less beneficial to industry depending on how they are collected. The land tax in England is permanent and uniform and does not increase with the rent, which is determined by the improvement of the land²; despite modern improvements, it remains the same as it was formerly. In France, the tax increases proportionally to the rent, which is a great discouragement to the landowner. It has much the same effect as tithes in England. When we know that the produce is to be shared with those who contribute nothing, it prevents us from spending what we otherwise would on the improvement of our lands. We are better financiers than the French³, as we also have an advantage over them in the following details.

In the method of collecting our customs, we have an advantage over the French. Our customs are all paid at once by the merchants, and goods—after being recorded in the custom house books—may be transported by a permit through any part of the country without harassment or expense, except for some small amounts for tolls, etc. In France, a duty is paid at the entrance of almost every town they pass through, equal to, if not greater than, what we pay at the start; inland

   We have another advantage in collecting our taxes through a commission, while theirs are collected by farming, by which means less than half of what they raise actually reaches the government. In England, the total expense of collecting over seven million pounds is less than £300,000. In France, twenty-four million is collected every year, and no more than twelve million goes toward government expenses; the rest is used to cover the costs of collection and for the profit of the tax farmer¹. In England, no excise officers are required except at the seaports, along with a few scattered throughout the country. The profits made by the tax farmers in France would pay the expenses of all of them. In the collection of our excise, there is a regular hierarchy of officers who receive fixed salaries and nothing more; but in France, the highest bidder obtains the position, and since the man who undertakes it must advance the sum at a specific time and runs the risk of not recovering it, he deserves a very high profit. Additionally, in an auction of this kind, there are few bidders, as no one is capable of undertaking the office except those raised in business who possess both significant capital and credit, and can provide reliable security. When there are few bidders, they can easily form an association among themselves and obtain the contract at a very low rate². On the whole, we may observe that the English are the best financiers in Europe, and their taxes are collected with more propriety than those of any other country³.

When the inhabitants of a country are essentially prohibited by high taxes from exporting the products of their industry, they are restricted to domestic consumption, and their motivations for industry are diminished. Taxes upon importation, on the contrary, encourage the manufacturing of those specific commodities. The tax upon Hamburg linen, for example, prevents the importation of great quantities of it and causes more linen to be manufactured at home. In general, however, all taxes upon importation are harmful in this respect: they divert the industry of the country into an unnatural channel. The more capital there is employed in one way, the less there is to be employed in another; but the effects of taxes upon exportation are even more destructive. This is one major cause of the poverty of Spain; they have imposed a high tax on the export of every commodity and think that by this means the taxes are paid by foreigners, whereas if they were to impose a tax on importation, it would be paid by their own subjects<note>'I have found ministers and others, both in their conversation and writings, maintain the erroneous maxim that high duties are to be laid upon commodities exported because foreigners pay them; and, on the contrary, very moderate ones on such as are imported because his majesty’s <column-break/> subjects are at the charge of them.' *Uztariz, Theory and Practice of Commerce and Maritime Affairs*, translated by John Kippax, 1751, vol. ii. p. 52; compare vol. i. p. xiii.</note>, not reflecting that by placing a burden on the export of commodities, they limit the consumption of them and diminish industry<note>*Wealth of Nations*, book v. chapter ii. part ii. article 4, vol. ii. pp. 495, 496.</note>.

To Russia, for example, we send fine linen and other manufactured goods, and for a small quantity of these receive, in return, great quantities of unmanufactured goods. This kind of trade is very advantageous because goods in an unmanufactured and raw state provide employment and maintenance for a great number of people. It is merely from the absurd notion that wealth consists of money that the British encourage most of those branches of foreign trade where the balance is paid in money.

There are still some other types of taxes, but as their nature is much the same, it is unnecessary to mention them.

Having thus given a general view of taxes, it will not be improper here—on account of their connection—to consider the nature of stocks and the causes of their rising and falling.

### [§ 3. *Of Stocks.*]

Soon after the Revolution, on account of the necessities of government, it was necessary to borrow money from subjects—generally at a higher rate than common interest—to be repaid in a few years. The funds allotted for the payment of this interest were taxes on certain commodities. These taxes were at first imposed for a certain number of years, according to the term for which the money was borrowed; but when, by various strategies of government, these loans became perpetual, the taxes naturally became perpetual, and thus the funds were mortgaged. Though they <note>That is, the mortgaged taxes or funds. "The funds" originally meant nothing but the total produce of certain taxes.</note> were made perpetual when money could no longer be borrowed upon them, yet they were still redeemable

When these taxes were first imposed, nothing would have shocked people more than the thought that they were to be perpetual; but their progress was so gradual that it was never complained about. What is shocking at first will soon become acceptable through custom, which makes everything seem right. Thus the taxes were first imposed, and thus they reached the situation they are in at present. When a sum of money is lent to a private individual, the creditor can demand both the principal and interest from the debtor whenever he wishes; but the government does not borrow money on these terms. They give you a right to a perpetual annuity of three or four percent, but not the right to demand back your principal. It seems very strange at first glance that the creditor would agree to such an inconvenience as never having his money paid back <note>² Mortimer discusses "the inconveniences that might arise to contributors from being forced to keep their money constantly in the hands of the government." *Every Man His Own Broker*, 5th edition, 1762, page 12.</note>, but this is actually to his advantage. If you lend the government a thousand pounds during a time of war, as they have an immediate use for it, they will perhaps be forced to give you five percent interest; and when peace comes, they continue your annuity. You have the power to sell your annuity, and since your money is perfectly secure—and no private individual pays interest with as much punctuality as the government—you may very often sell the annuity for your £1000 for £1100 or more. The government, finding that these annuities sold above their face value, and for the same reason that people were very willing to subscribe to government funds, decided—as the funds were still redeemable—to take advantage of this by paying back the sums borrowed at five percent, and

This caused the contractors with the government to be on their guard, and, as they saw their plan, they would not lend them any more money unless at least some part of the interest were irredeemable, perhaps two percent of the four they were to receive. In every fund, therefore, there was an irredeemable part², which caused them to continue selling above par.

In the reigns of King William, Queen Anne, and at the beginning of King George I’s reign, the funds rose and fell according to the credit of the government, as there was still some risk of a revolution. Lately, though there is no danger of a revolution, even in times of peace, stocks are sometimes ten, twenty, or even fifty percent below par, and sometimes as much above it³. Nobody can suspect any risk of losing that money by a change of government. How then does it happen that stocks fluctuate every day without any visible cause? How is it that good or bad news has such an influence on the rising and falling of stocks? The real cause is as follows:

Every misfortune in war puts peace at a greater distance, and every fortunate occurrence seems to favor its approach. When war continues, the necessities of government must be supplied, more money levied, and new subscriptions opened for these purposes. As the interest rate must necessarily rise during war, everyone is eager to join the new subscription, and those who have annuities find that it will be to their advantage to sell out of the old stocks in anticipation of a higher interest. The number of sellers, therefore, increases with the prospect of a war, and

In wartime, everyone who has any capital rushes to place it in the hands of the government, as it cannot be as profitably employed anywhere else; they receive interest at perhaps seven or eight percent, of which two or three percent is perhaps irredeemable, and frequently a lottery ticket is included in the bargain. A person who has an annuity at only three percent will do everything he can to sell it so that he may employ his capital to greater advantage, and for this reason, he will often sell it below par; consequently, stocks must necessarily fall. But in wartime, for the following reasons, even new subscriptions come to be sold below par.

As there are many stockholders who are merchants, and who keep their capital in the hands of the government so that they may be ready to sell out upon any sudden demand and take advantage of a good bargain when it arises<gloss>¹</gloss>, and as these opportunities occur most frequently in wartime, they often have reason to sell out. Thus, more stock enters the market, and the new subscriptions fall<gloss>²</gloss> below par. Furthermore, in wartime, as was observed before, capital cannot be as profitably employed elsewhere, and everyone is tempted to subscribe. Even those whose financial circumstances are very modest subscribe for large sums in the hope that stocks will rise and that they may sell out at a great profit before the time of delivery; but when things do not meet their expectations and they are forced to sell one way or another to maintain their credit, they are often obliged to sell below par. In this manner, new subscriptions may fall.

### [§ 4. *Of Stock-jobbing.*]

The practice of stock-jobbing, or buying stocks on time, also has a very considerable influence on the rise and fall of stocks on all occasions. The method by which this practice is carried out is as follows. A man who perhaps does not have £1,000 in the world subscribes for £100,000, which is to be delivered at several fixed times and in certain portions. He therefore hopes to sell these various portions at a great profit as stocks rise before they become due; but since everything he owns would be lost if the stocks were to fall, he uses every means to make them rise. He spreads reports at 'Change Alley that victories have been won, that peace is to be concluded, and so on.<note>¹ Mortimer, *Every Man his own Broker*, 5th ed., pp. 31, 37-40.</note> On the other hand, those who want to purchase a stock and want it to fall spread reports that will sink the stocks as low as possible, such as that war will continue, that new subscriptions are being considered, and so on. It is because of this that, in time of war, our newspapers are so filled with invasions and schemes that were never even considered. In the language of 'Change Alley, the buyer is called the bull², and the seller is called the bear³, and as the bulls or bears predominate,

This practice of buying stocks on time is prohibited by the government, and accordingly, even if they do not deliver the stocks they have contracted for, the law gives no redress $^1$. There is no natural reason why £1,000 in stocks should not be delivered, or the delivery of it enforced, as well as £1,000 worth of goods; but after the South Sea Scheme this was thought of as an expedient to prevent such practices, though it proved ineffective. In the same way, all laws against gambling never hinder it, and though there is no legal remedy for a sum above £5 $^2$, yet all the large sums that are lost are punctually paid. People who gamble must maintain their credit, otherwise nobody will deal with them. It is exactly the same in stock-jobbing; those who do not maintain their credit will soon be turned out and, in the language of *'Change Alley*, be called a *lame duck* $^3$. It is unnecessary here to give any account of particular funds, as they are all of the same nature and the security is equal. If the interest on any sum of money is not paid by the funds allotted for that purpose, it is paid out of the sinking fund, which is the surplus of all the rest. There is perhaps some little difference in the ease of payment, but this is by no means considerable and does not deserve our attention.

# [PART II: OF POLICE]

### [§ 17. *Of the Influence of Commerce on Manners.*]

It <note>The manuscript reads 'In.'</note> now remains for us to consider the last division of policy, and to show the influence of commerce on the manners of a people. Whenever commerce is introduced into any country, integrity and punctuality always accompany it. These virtues are almost unknown in a primitive and barbarous country. Of all the nations in Europe, the Dutch—being the most commercial—are the most faithful to their word. The English are more so than the Scots, but they are much inferior to the Dutch; and in the remote parts of this country, they are far less so than in the commercial parts of it. This should not be attributed to national character at all, as some claim; there is no natural reason why an Englishman or a Scotsman should not be as punctual in performing agreements as a Dutchman. It is far more easily traced to self-interest—that general principle which regulates the actions of every man, and which leads men to act in a certain way based on prospects of advantage—and it is as deeply ingrained in an Englishman as a Dutchman <note>'They make use of their skill and their wit to take advantage <column-break/> of the ignorance and folly of the other men they deal with; they are strict in their demands where the law is in their [favor]...'</note>. When a person makes perhaps twenty contracts in a day, he cannot gain as much by trying to deceive his neighbors as the mere appearance of a cheat would cause him to lose. <margin>* "…own hands: in other respects, where they deal with men who understand as they do, and are within the reach of justice and laws, they are the most straightforward and best dealers in the world; which seems to arise as much from a principle of conscience or morality as from a custom or habit introduced by the necessity of trade among them. Those whom we call politicians are not the most remarkable men in the world for integrity and reliability ¹. <margin>¹ *Wealth of Nations*, book 4, chapter 2, volume 2, page 41, contrasts a legislator governed by general principles with "that insidious and crafty animal, commonly called a statesman or politician, whose councils are directed by the momentary fluctuations of affairs."</margin> Ambassadors from different nations are even less so; they are praised for any small advantage they can take, and pride themselves a great deal on this degree of shrewdness. France has had this reputation with us ever since the reign of Louis XIV, yet it has never in the least hurt either its interest or splendor. But if states were obliged to deal once or twice a day, as merchants do, it would be necessary to be more precise in order to preserve their reputation. Wherever dealings are frequent, a man does not expect to gain as much by any single contract as he does by integrity and reliability in his business as a whole, and

# CHEAPNESS OR PLENTY

A prudent dealer, who is aware of his true interests, would rather choose to lose what he has a right to than give any ground for suspicion. Everything of this kind is as offensive as it is rare. <insert>When the majority of people are merchants, they always bring integrity and punctuality into fashion; these, therefore, are the primary virtues of a commercial nation.</insert>
<insert>There are some inconveniences, however, arising from a commercial spirit. The first we shall mention is that it narrows the perspectives of men.</insert> Where the division of labor is brought to perfection, every man has only a simple task to perform; his entire attention is confined to this, and few ideas pass through his mind except those that have an immediate connection with it. When the mind is occupied with a variety of objects, it is somehow expanded and enlarged; for this reason, a country craftsman is generally acknowledged to have a range of thoughts much broader than a city craftsman $^1$. The former is perhaps a joiner, a house carpenter, and a cabinet-maker all in one, and his attention must, of course, be employed on a number of objects of very different kinds. The latter is perhaps only a cabinet-maker; that specific kind of work occupies all his thoughts, and as he has not had the opportunity of comparing a variety of objects, his views on things beyond his own trade are by no means as extensive as those of the former. This must be even more the case when a person’s entire attention is devoted to the seventeenth part of a pin $^2$ or the eightieth part of a button, so far divided are

<margin>(</margin>Another bad effect of commerce is that it lowers the courage of mankind and tends to extinguish the martial spirit. In all commercial countries, the division of labor is infinite, and everyone’s thoughts are employed on one particular thing. This is confirmed by universal experience.

In the year 1745, four or five thousand naked, unarmed Highlanders took possession of the developed parts of this country without any opposition from the non-military inhabitants. They penetrated into England and alarmed the whole nation, and had they not been opposed by a standing army, they would have seized the throne with little difficulty. Two hundred years ago, such an attempt would have roused the spirit of the nation. Our ancestors were brave and warlike; their minds were not weakened by cultivating arts and commerce, and they were all ready with spirit and vigor to resist the most formidable foe. It is for the same reason, too, that an army of four or five hundred Europeans has often penetrated into the Mughal's country, and that the most numerous armies of the Chinese have always been overthrown by the Tartars. In those countries, the division of labor and luxury have arrived at a very high level; they have no standing army, and the people are all intent on the arts of peace. Holland, were its barriers ¹ removed, would be an easy prey. In the beginning of this century, the standing army of the Dutch was beaten in the field, and the rest of the inhabitants, instead of rising in arms to defend themselves, planned to desert their country and settle in the East Indies ². # [PART IV:] OF ARMS

---

### [§ 1. *Of Militias.*]

In the beginning of society, the defense of the state required no police, nor any particular provision for it. The whole body of the people rose up to oppose any attempt that was made against them, and he who was chief in time of peace naturally preserved his influence in time of war. But after the division of labor took place, it became necessary that some should stay at home to be employed in agriculture and other arts, while the rest went out to war. After the appropriation of lands and the distinction of ranks were in some measure introduced, the cultivation of the ground would naturally fall to the lowest rank. The less laborious, but more honorable employment of military service, would be claimed by the highest order. Accordingly, we find that this was the practice of all nations in their primitive state. The Roman *equites* or knights were originally horsemen in the army, and no slaves or those who did not pay taxes ever went out to war. In like manner among our ancestors, only those who held land by what was called knight's service were employed in the defense of the state, and the ancient villains <note>feudal tenants or serfs of low status</note> were never considered as a part of the national force.

When the state was thus defended by men of honor

<summary>This section traces the evolution of military service from early societies, where all citizens fought, to more complex societies where the division of labor and social hierarchy restricted defense to the higher ranks and landholders.</summary>

<keywords>military history, division of labor, social ranks, Roman equites, knight's service, militias, feudalism</keywords>

--- Page 307 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of military systems, specifically focusing on how the rise of commerce and industry shifted the responsibility of national defense from the elite to the lower classes.</meta>

MILITIAS

who would do their duty from this principle, there was no need for discipline. <insert>(</insert> But when arts and manufactures increased and were considered worthy of attention, and men found that they could increase their status by applying themselves to them, it became inconvenient for the rich to go out to war because of a desire for profit. These arts, which were at first despised by the <insert>active and</insert> ambitious, soon came to claim their full attention. The merchant who can make two or three thousand pounds at home will not be inclined to go out to war, whereas it was a diversion for an ancient knight who had nothing else to do. When the improvement of arts and manufactures was thought to be an object deserving the attention of the higher ranks, the defense of the state naturally became the responsibility of the lower ranks, because the rich can never be forced to do anything except what they please. <insert>)</insert> In Rome, after the knights stopped serving in the army, the lowest of the people went in their place; and in our own country, after the feudal militia ended, another group from the lowest ranks succeeded them. This, therefore, is the progression of military service in every country. Among a nation of hunters and shepherds, and even when a nation has advanced to agriculture, the entire population goes out together to wage war. When arts and manufactures begin to advance, the whole population cannot go out; and as these arts are laborious and not very lucrative, for the reasons previously mentioned, the highest ranks go out. After that, when arts and commerce are even further advanced and begin to be very lucrative, it falls to the lowest classes to defend the state¹. This is our present condition in Great Britain.

## [§ 2. *Of Discipline*.]

When the entire population went to war together, there was no need for military discipline. Since everyone was on essentially the same level and their shared cause was so clearly understood, discipline was quite unnecessary. When the highest social classes went out to fight, a principle of honor would take the place of discipline. However, when this duty fell upon the lowest class, the most severe and rigid discipline became necessary, and accordingly, we find that it has been introduced into all standing armies. In general, it is necessary that soldiers should be kept under such authority that they are more afraid of their general and officers than of the enemy. It is the fear of their officers and of the rigid penalties of martial law which is the chief cause of their good behavior, and it is to this principle that we owe their valiant actions. In the recent war, eight hundred Prussians defended a pass for an entire day against several thousand Austrians, and at night, during their retreat, they deserted almost to the last man. What could be the foundation of this courage? It was not a principle of honor, nor love for their country, nor a regard for their officers, for these motivations would still have kept them together; it was nothing but the dread of their officers, who were hanging, as it were, over their heads, and whom they dared not disobey. This, by the way, shows the governableness of our nature, and may also show how much that manly courage we boast of so much depends upon external circumstances. We may further observe how far this principle of fear may be carried. If a bold, fierce, and tyrannical adjutant is succeeded by one of a mild and gentle disposition, the ideas of terror are conveyed with the uniform coat, and it is some time before it is perceived that he is not as terrible as the other.

<vocab>military discipline, honor, standing armies, martial law, Prussians, Austrians, courage, adjutant</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that modern standing armies, composed of the lower classes, rely on strict discipline and fear of officers rather than honor or patriotism. Using the example of Prussian soldiers, the text suggests that military courage is often an external product of rigid authority and martial law.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the necessity of military discipline to the historical introduction and different organizational models of standing armies, noting their risks and benefits to national liberty.</meta>

->[§ 3. Of Standing Armies.]<-

In this manner, standing armies came to be introduced, and where there are none, the country is an easy prey to its enemies. The only thing to be observed concerning them is that they should be raised in the most convenient way, and with as little harm as possible to the country. However much standing armies may be criticized, in a certain period of society they must be introduced. A militia commanded by landed gentlemen in possession of the public offices of the nation can never have any likelihood of sacrificing the liberties of the country for any person whatsoever. Such a militia would no doubt be the best security against the standing army of another nation.

Standing armies are of two kinds: the first is when the government gives offices to particular persons, and a certain amount for every man they recruit. From such a standing army as this, which is the model of our own, there is less danger than from the second kind, when the government makes a lump-sum bargain with a general to lead out a certain number of troops for their assistance, which is the model of the standing armies in some small states of Italy. They make a bargain with some chieftain in those parts where the arts of civilization have not yet reached, and as the officers are all dependent on him, and he is independent of the state, his employers are at his mercy. But a standing army like ours is not so likely to turn its arms against the government, because the officers are men of honor and have great connections in the country. Yet on some occasions, a standing army has proved dangerous to the liberties of the people when the question concerning the power of the sovereign came to be disputed, as has been the case in our own country, because the standing army generally takes the side of the king <note>*Wealth of Nations*, book 5, chapter 1, part 1, volume 2, page 290; and above, pages 29, 30.</note>. The principle

# PART V:
## OF THE LAWS OF NATIONS

---

### Introduction.

It should be observed that the rules which nations ought to observe, or do observe toward one another, cannot be treated as accurately as private or public law. We find that the rules of property are quite precisely established in every nation. The extent of the sovereign's power, as well as the duty of the subject, so far as justice is concerned, are fairly uniform everywhere. But regarding the laws of nations, we can hardly mention a single regulation that is established with the common consent of all nations and observed as such at all times <note>"Hardly any law is found outside of natural law . . . common to all nations." Grotius, *On the Law of War and Peace*, book 1, chapter 1, § 14.</note>. This must necessarily be the case, for where there is no supreme legislative power or judge to settle disputes, we may always expect uncertainty and irregularity.

The laws of nations are those that apply in either peace or war. Those that apply in times of peace have been explained previously, where it was shown regarding aliens that they are entitled to security for their persons and effects, but that they have no power to

---

<summary>This page introduces the law of nations, noting that it is less precise than domestic law because there is no international supreme judge or legislature. It begins to categorize these laws into those governing peace and those governing war.</summary>
<keywords>laws of nations, international law, private law, public law, sovereign power, Hugo Grotius, natural law, aliens' rights</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the rights of aliens to the laws of nations in times of war, specifically defining the criteria for a just cause of war.</meta>

make a will, but all goes to the sovereign at their death¹.
The laws or rules observed in time of war shall be considered in the following order:
First, what is a just cause of war, or according to the Latin phrase, *quando liceat bellare?*
Secondly, what it is lawful for one nation to do to another in time of war, or *quantum liceat in bello*, and upon this head we shall consider the differences between the ancient and modern governments, and the great modifications of the latter.
Thirdly, what is due to neutral nations from the belligerent powers.
Fourthly, the rights of ambassadors between different nations.

## [§ 1. When is War Lawful?]

First, *quando liceat bellare?* <insert>(</insert>In general, whatever is the foundation of a proper lawsuit before a court of justice may be a just occasion of war². The foundation of a lawsuit is the violation of some perfect right whose performance may be extorted by force, and is so extorted in a primitive society, but in modern times is decided by the magistrate, lest the society should be disturbed by everyone taking justice into his own hands. When one nation encroaches on the property of another, or puts to death the subjects of another, imprisons them, or refuses them justice when injured, the sovereign is bound to demand satisfaction for the offense, as it is the intention of the government to protect its several members from foreign enemies, and if redress be refused, there is a foundation for war.<insert>)</insert> In the same manner, a breach of contract, such as when a debt is due by one nation to another and payment is refused, is a very just occasion of war. If, for example, the king of Prussia should

---

¹ <note>Above, p. 65; see note 2 there.</note>
² <note>“And clearly, as many sources as there are for legal actions, there are just as many for 
<column-break/>
war; for where trials fail, war begins.” Grotius, *On the Law of War and Peace*, book ii, chapter i, § 2.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the structure for discussing the laws of war and defines a just cause for war as being equivalent to any violation of a perfect right that would warrant a lawsuit in domestic law. It notes that sovereigns must seek redress for injuries to their subjects' property or persons, as well as for breaches of contract between nations.</summary>

<keywords>just cause of war, laws of nations, Hugo Grotius, sovereignty, international debt, redress of grievances, primitive society, legal action</keywords>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the just causes of war, completing an example regarding debt and the King of Prussia before examining exceptions and transitioning to the laws of conduct during war.</meta>

refuse to pay the money advanced for him by the British nation during the last war, a declaration of war against him would be just and reasonable. Every offense of the sovereign of one country against the sovereign of another, or of the sovereign against the subject, or of the subject of one country against the subject of another, without giving reasonable satisfaction, may be the cause of a war.

<There seems to be only one exception to the general rule, that everything that is the subject of a lawsuit may be a cause of war, and that is with respect to quasi-contracts.> In this case, indeed, it is difficult to determine whether a war would be reasonable or not, and we find no instance of a war declared upon the violation of this right. It must be allowed that the introduction of <term>quasi-contract</term> was the highest stretch of <term>equity</term>, and except in <term>Roman law</term> it was never perfected nor introduced. In England, if you repair a man’s house in his absence, you must trust him for the payment of it, for you have no action by law. In the same manner, if a Russian performs a service for an English merchant, which, if he had not done, the merchant would have suffered extremely, and afterwards demands satisfaction for his trouble, if he is refused it and applies to the courts of justice, they will tell him that he must depend on the honor of the merchant for payment. Excepting this, everything which is the foundation of a proper lawsuit will also make war just and reasonable.

### [§ 2. *What is Lawful in War?*]

[Second,] *quantum liceat [in] bello?* How far a nation may push the resentment of an injury against the nation which has injured them is not easy to determine. The practice of ancient and modern nations differs extremely. In general, when an injury is clearly and distinctly done, or when it is plainly intended and satisfaction

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limits of resentment and the lawful actions a nation may take in response to an injury, specifically addressing when satisfaction is refused or when a threat is imminent.</meta>

<page-num>268</page-num>

<header>PART V : LAWS OF NATIONS</header>

refused, resentment is necessary and just. There are a few cases in which it is lawful even without demanding satisfaction. If a robber clearly intended to kill you, it would be perfectly lawful for you to do all you could to prevent him. The injury is obvious. In the same way, when one nation seems to be conspiring against another, even though it may have done no real injury yet, it is necessary that it should be required to declare its intentions and to provide security, provided this demand does not subject it to significant inconvenience. Even if this satisfaction is not demanded, when the King of Prussia saw his dominions about to be overwhelmed by the Elector of Saxony and the Queen of Hungary <note>In 1756.</note>, it was quite right for him to preempt them and to take possession of their territories; nothing would have been more absurd than for him to have told them that he was going to attack them. On the other hand, if it is only a debt that is due, it would be unreasonable to go to war without demanding satisfaction, and it is only because of a dilatory and evasive manner of providing satisfaction that a war becomes lawful in such a case.

To consider more specifically what is lawful in war: suppose a subject of any government is injured. Those who have injured him become natural objects of resentment, as does the government that protects them if it refuses satisfaction; however, the greater part of the nation is perfectly innocent and knows nothing about the affair. In the recent war with France, not one in twenty people, whether among the French or ourselves, knew anything of the offenses committed. On what principle or foundation of justice, therefore, do we seize their goods and distress them in every possible way? This can by no means be founded upon justice and equity, properly so-called; it must be based on necessity, which, indeed, in this case, is a part of justice.

<meta>This page continues the critique of why entire nations are held accountable for the actions of their governments during wartime, specifically examining and refuting Francis Hutcheson's arguments based on Roman law.</meta>

<page-num>269</page-num>
<header>WHAT IS LAWFUL IN WAR?</header>

Mr. Hutcheson¹ indeed accounts for this very ingeniously, but if we examine his opinion thoroughly, we shall find that he has not built his reasoning on a proper foundation. Every nation, he says, maintains and supports the government for its own good. If the government commits any offense against a neighboring sovereign or subject, and its own people continue to support and protect it in that action, they thereby become an accessory and liable to punishment along with it. According to Roman law, if any of those slaves whom every private person kept for his own advantage had caused any damage to another person, one of two things had to be done: the owner must either no longer keep the slave, or pay for the damage. In the same way, a nation must either allow itself to be liable for the damages or give up the government altogether². It should be observed that in this reasoning, though it is extremely ingenious, the cases are not parallel in the slightest degree. A man can do with his slave as he pleases; he can either get rid of him or pay whatever damages he has caused. But a nation, in most cases, can do neither. A government is often maintained not for the nation’s preservation, but for its own sake. It was never the doctrine of any public law that subjects had a right to dispose of the sovereign—not even in England, where the sovereign’s right has been so heavily contested. How then does it happen that a nation should be guilty of an injury that was not within its power to prevent?

The real reason why the whole nation is considered a reasonable object of resentment is that we do not feel for those at a distance as we do for those near us. If we have been injured by France, our resentment rises against the whole nation instead of just the government; and they, through a blind, indiscriminating faculty natural to man—

<column-break/>
336. Compare Grotius, *De iure belli et pacis*, Book 2, chapter 21, §§ 2, 7.</note>

<summary>The author challenges Francis Hutcheson’s view that a nation is collectively liable for its government's actions, arguing that citizens often have no power to control or remove their rulers. --- Page 316 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of collective responsibility in war, explaining why innocent citizens are often targets of resentment and how the practices of war have evolved from the harsh standards of the Barbarians and Romans.</meta>

<page-num>270</page-num>
<header>PART V : LAWS OF NATIONS</header>

mankind, become the objects of an unreasonable resentment. In a war between France and us, a Dane would naturally share the same sentiments that we do, and would group together without distinction both the guilty and the innocent ¹. This is, however, quite contrary to the rules of justice observed with regard to our own subjects. We would rather choose that ten guilty persons should escape than that one innocent person should suffer. Another cause is that it is often very difficult to obtain satisfaction from a subject or from a sovereign who may have offended. They are generally in the heart of the country and perfectly well-secured. If we could reach them, no doubt they would be the first objects of our resentment, but as this is impossible, we must make reprisals in some other way. We have suffered unjustly on account of our connections; let them also suffer unjustly on account of theirs. In war there must always be the greatest injustice, but it is inevitable.

The practice of ancient and modern nations differs widely with regard to the extent to which the outrages of war may be carried. Barbarians, if they do not kill those taken in war, may dispose of them as they please. As all who made war were considered robbers and violators of the peace of society, such punishments were by no means thought inadequate. Even among the Romans, if the battering ram had once struck the walls, no agreement nor capitulation was allowed, but everything fell into the hands of the conquerors, and they were at liberty to use it as they pleased. This was so much the case in Cicero’s time that he represents it as the greatest act of humanity that a capitulation was allowed after the ram had once struck the walls ².

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the laws of war, specifically contrasting ancient brutality with modern refinements in the treatment of prisoners and the use of exchange treaties.</meta>

-> WHAT IS LAWFUL IN WAR? <-

In former periods, the great virtues of war were different, but modern manners have reached a greater degree of refinement, both with respect to persons and property. Captives in war are now by no means made slaves or liable to oppression: an officer is set free upon his parole or word of honor; and in the war between France and us, they generally treated our wounded prisoners better than their own wounded soldiers <gloss>1</gloss>. Indeed, there is no nation that pushes this point of gallantry further than we do. When the sixpence a day which was allowed to the French prisoners at Edinburgh and elsewhere was thought insufficient to maintain them—on account of the deductions it sustained before it reached their hands through subcontracts, etc.—a collection of £10,000 was generously made for them. In general, prisoners of war are now as well treated as other people.

In the same manner, cartel treaties—by which soldiers and sailors are valued at a certain rate and exchanged at the end of every campaign, with the nation that has lost more prisoners paying the balance—are evidence of our refinement in humanity <gloss>2</gloss>. In the late war indeed, we refused to enter into any such treaty with France for sailors, and by this wise regulation soon unmanned their navy, as we took a great many more prisoners than they did <gloss>3</gloss>. It was the want of humanity

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of the laws of war, specifically explaining why modern sieges are less desperate than ancient ones and how the influence of the Church and the Crusades fostered greater humanity among Christian nations.</meta>

<header>PART V : LAWS OF NATIONS</header>

There is no doubt that this is what rendered ancient towns so obstinate, for it was better to endure the most terrible hardships than to surrender; but now the besieged know very well how they will be treated before they capitulate, and will run no great risk before they do so <note>1</note>.

This superior degree of humanity was introduced during the era of the Papacy. We never find it among the Greeks and Romans, notwithstanding all their attainments. The Pope was considered the common father of Christendom, the clergy were under his subjection, and he had intercourse by his legates with all the courts of Europe. By this, they were more closely connected, and he obliged them to treat one another with more humanity. The Holy War too, which at that time was undertaken by most of the princes in Europe, made them turn their arms against all those of a different religion, whom they thought deserved to be treated in the most cruel manner; but when they came to be engaged in a war among themselves—as they had all been on one side in that common cause, and as they thought that Christians should not be treated in the same manner as infidels—a greater degree of humanity was introduced. From these causes, moderns behave differently from the ancients with regard to the persons of prisoners.

It is more from motives of policy than humanity that the possessions of enemies are protected. When a French army invades Germany, the general makes a law that all the people who will live quietly, and do not rise against him, shall be secure in their persons and possessions, and he will punish a soldier as severely for injuring the peasants of his enemy's country as those of his own. But this is not the case in a sea war. An admiral seizes and plunders

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the treatment of property and individuals in war, contrasting the protection of peasants for logistical reasons with the seizure of merchant ships, and noting the introduction of gallantry in modern warfare.</meta>

<columns>2</columns>

...all the merchant ships he can take. Many of the merchants have done as little harm as the peasants; why then is there this distinction? It is in the interest of the general not to rob the peasants, because it would be difficult to march an army while carrying all its provisions through an enemy's country. But by encouraging them to stay, he is supplied without any other means. By this means, war is so far from being a disadvantage in a well-cultivated country that many grow rich from it. When the Netherlands is the seat of war, all the peasants grow rich, for they pay no rent when the enemy is in the country, and provisions sell at a high price. This is indeed at the expense of the landlords and the upper classes, who are generally ruined on such occasions. This is so much the case that all the poor people who are abroad, whenever they hear of a war, will not stay away from their native country. It is quite different in a sea war. Every ship carries its own provisions and has no dependence on them from the ships that it meets.

Another cause of modern refinement is that courtesy, or rather gallantry, which exists between hostile nations, by which even ambassadors are kept at their respective courts. In ancient times, it was considered the greatest act of bravery to kill the general of an army <note>1</note>, but nothing could make a person more infamous at present than such a practice. When the King of France in person besieged a certain castle, the governor sent word to ask in what part of the camp the King was staying, so that he might not cannonade it <note>2</note>. The King of Prussia, indeed, did not grant the princes of Saxony this request, when they

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of warfare, moving from the personal targeting of leaders to the treatment of conquered territories and the role of neutral nations.</meta>

informed him where the royal tent stood, but this was because he was assured that the chief magazine was there. Now, if there are any in a nation who have caused more injury than others, they are the king and the generals. How does it happen, then, that it is no longer considered lawful to kill them, as it was in the past? The simple reason is that monarchies, whose interest it always is to show respect to those in authority, set the example at present; however, republics, whose interest lies in adopting the opposite principle, formerly set the trend <note>1</note>.

The same policy that makes us less likely to go to war also makes us more lenient than before after a complete conquest. In ancient times, an enemy forfeited all his possessions and was disposed of at the will of the conquerors. It was for this reason that the Romans often had to repopulate a country and sent out colonies <note>2</note>. It is not so now; a conquered country essentially only changes masters. They may be subjected to new taxes and other regulations, but they require no new population. The conqueror generally allows them to keep their religion and laws, which is a much better practice than the ancient one. Modern armies, too, are less enraged with one another because firearms keep them at a greater distance <note>3</note>. When they always fought sword in hand, their rage and fury were raised to the highest level, and as they were mixed together, the slaughter was much greater.

### [§ 3. Of the Rights of Neutral Nations.]

Third, we are next to show what is owed to neutral nations by the warring powers.

The rule of justice regarding neutral nations is,

--- Page 321 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the rights of neutral nations, specifically addressing trade freedoms, the recovery of captured property (ius postliminii), and the differences in treatment of neutral powers between land and sea warfare.</meta>

<page-num>275</page-num>
<header>RIGHTS OF NEUTRALS</header>
<sig>T 2</sig>

...that as they have offended no party, they should suffer no injury. In a war between France and England, the Dutch should have the freedom of trading with both countries, as in times of peace, since they have injured neither party. Unless they carry contraband goods, or are going to a town that is besieged, they can trade to any part of the country without interference. A <term>neutral bottom</term> will not, however, protect the goods of the enemy, nor does the hostility of the vessel, so to speak, forfeit the goods of the neutral power. There is some difference between the practice of ancient and modern nations with respect to the *<term>ius postliminii</term>*, or the recovery of what was lost <note>1</note>. The maxim in time of war anciently was: we are always in the right, and our enemies are always in the wrong; whatever is taken from the enemy is justly taken, and whatever is taken from us is unjustly taken. On this account, if a Carthaginian had sold a Roman ship taken in war to a Roman, the former owner, whenever he had an opportunity, took it back, as—based on the principle above—it was unjustly taken from him. Now it is quite different; we consider everything done in war as just and equitable, and neither demand nor would take back any captures made in it. If an English ship is taken by the French and sold to the Dutch, and then comes to a British harbor, the former owner makes no claim to her, for he had lost all hope of recovery once it had passed into the possession of the enemy.

It should be observed that there is a very great difference in the conduct of warring nations toward a neutral power in a land war compared to a sea war, which is more the result of policy than of humanity. When an army retreats and the conqueror pursues it into a neutral nation, unless that nation has the power to keep both out, it becomes the seat of war—as is often the case—and little or no compensation is given for damages; but in a sea war, a ship taken from even the most insignificant neutral power is always restored.

---

<note>1</note> Grotius, *On the Law of War and Peace*, Book 3, Chapter 6, § 3; Chapter 9, § 15.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the rights of neutral nations, specifically addressing why small nations can more easily maintain neutrality at sea than on land, and begins a new section on the history and rights of ambassadors.</meta>

<page-num>276</page-num>
<header>PART V : LAWS OF NATIONS</header>

The reason commonly assigned—that it harms their commerce more to capture their ships than anything else—is unsatisfactory, for a land war damages commerce even more than a sea war does. The real reason is that a small country does not have the power to assert its neutrality in a land war, but even the smallest is able to do so in a sea war. A small fort can compel the greatest nation to respect the neutrality of its harbor.

### [§ 4. Of the Rights of Ambassadors.]

Fourth, we are finally to consider the rights of ambassadors between different nations.

When nations began to have a great deal of business with one another, it was found necessary to send messengers between them; these were the first ambassadors. In ancient times, as there was little commerce carried on between different nations, ambassadors were only sent on particular occasions; they were what we now call ambassadors extraordinary, who returned home after their business was finished. We find nothing like resident ambassadors in Rome or Greece; their entire office was to conclude peace, form alliances, and so on, on specific occasions. The first time that resident ambassadors were employed was at the beginning of the seventeenth century by Ferdinand, King of Spain. Even the word "ambassador" comes from the Spanish verb *ambassare*, meaning "to send" <note>1</note>. The Pope, indeed, had residents—or legates—at all the courts of Europe from the earliest times. The very same reason that makes embassies so frequent now induced the Pope to adopt this method in the past. He had business in all the countries of Europe, and a great part of his revenue was collected from them; and as they were continually attempt-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the history of ambassadors, explaining how the Pope and later Ferdinand of Spain established the practice of resident ambassadors to protect economic and political interests.</meta>

<page-num>277</page-num>
<header>RIGHTS OF AMBASSADORS</header>

...ing to infringe the right he claimed, he found it necessary to have a person constantly residing at their courts to see that his privileges were preserved. The Pope derived several advantages from this custom.

When commerce was introduced into Europe, and the privileges of every country—along with the duties payable on goods in another—were settled, the merchants of one country had constant claims against those of another. They themselves were strangers in those countries and could very easily be injured, and more often would think themselves so. It became necessary, therefore, to have one of their countrymen constantly residing at the courts of different nations to protect the rights of his fellow subjects. In ancient times, as was observed, there was little intercourse between different nations, and therefore no occasion for resident ambassadors; but now, as there is something to adjust between dealers almost every day, it is necessary that there should be some person of weight and authority who has access to the court to prevent any cause for quarrel between them. We have already observed that it was Ferdinand of Spain who established this practice. At first, it caused great jealousy among neighboring nations to have ambassadors residing at their courts. He, indeed, pretended to have no right to do this, but by sending an ambassador on a certain occasion and raising various questions, he found ways to keep him there. This practice was soon imitated, and it immediately became the universal custom of European princes; it was so far from being taken poorly that it was considered a great affront not to send one. Grotius, whose opinions are founded on the practice of ancient nations, speaks out against resident ambassadors and calls them resident spies <note>1 Grotius, *De iure belli et pacis*, book ii. chapter xviii. § 3, says "permanent embassies, which are now in use, may be rejected with the best of rights; ancient custom, to which they were unknown, shows how little they are needed," but does not call permanent ambassadors "resident spies."</note>, but if he had lived in the present

age, he would have found that extensive commerce makes it impossible to preserve peace for even a month unless grievances are redressed by an official of authority who knows the customs of the country and is capable of explaining what injuries have actually occurred. The custom of sending ambassadors preserves peace and, by providing intelligence, prevents one country from being invaded by another without timely notice. When any kind of dispute occurs and the ambassador is recalled, you can still gain intelligence through your communication with other courts where your ambassadors are informed, as ambassadors in general are familiar with all the business in Europe.

Although one country might achieve some kind of pre-eminence through the influence and diligence of its ambassador, no attention was paid to this for a long time, and that balance of power which has been so much discussed lately was never heard of back then. Every sovereign had enough to do within his own dominions and could pay little attention to foreign powers. Before the establishment of resident ambassadors, they could gather little intelligence; however, since the beginning of the sixteenth century, the nations of Europe have been divided into two great alliances. On the one hand were England, Holland, Hungary, Muscovy, etc.; on the other were France, Spain, Prussia, Denmark, Sweden, etc. In this manner, a kind of alliance was maintained, with one nation occasionally leaving one side and another joining it, just as Prussia is currently allied with England, while Hungary is on the opposing side. A system of this kind was established in Italy around the fifteenth century among the great families there. The resident ambassadors of these nations prevent any one country from dominating another, whether by sea or land, and they form a kind of council not unlike that
