of the Amphictyons in ancient Greece. They have the power to advise and consult concerning matters, but not to determine any; and by combining together, they can threaten any one country that claims superiority or makes an unreasonable demand. Post offices, too, are of great importance for obtaining intelligence, as communication is open through all these countries in both peace and war, which facilitates commerce and provides notice of every movement.

An ambassador’s person must be sacred and not subject to any of the courts of justice in the country where he resides. If he contracts debts or causes any injury, a complaint must be made to his own country. When the Dutch arrested the Russian ambassador in the year 1718, it was complained of as a violation of the laws of nations <note>1 It is probable that the reporter has confused two, or possibly three, cases here. The Dutch arrested Goertz, the Swedish minister, in 1717; the English arrested the Russian ambassador for debt in 1708; the French</note>. The goods which an ambassador buys are not subject to any customs duties. Just as a sovereign would be exempted from taxes, so must his ambassador who represents him <note>2 Eighteenth-century London local rating acts commonly provided that taxes on houses leased to ambassadors should be paid by the landlords.</note>. When an ambassador makes any attempt to disturb the peace by entering into conspiracies or the like, he may be imprisoned. As a matter of courtesy, and to maintain the dignity of an ambassador, his house is considered a sanctuary for offenders. He must be cautious, however, with this privilege, and extend his authority only to the protection of debtors and minor offenders, for the right will be violated if he shelters those guilty of capital crimes. The servants of ambassadors are also entitled to significant privileges; if indeed they have contracted debts, they may be arrested, but this is never done willingly.

<column-break/>

<vocab>Amphictyons, ambassadors, diplomatic immunity, Goertz, laws of nations, asylum, Russian ambassador, Spanish ambassador</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the legal protections and privileges afforded to ambassadors, including immunity from local courts, tax exemptions, and the status of their residence as a sanctuary. It also notes that these rights can be forfeited if an ambassador engages in conspiracies or shelters major criminals.</summary>
<keywords>diplomatic immunity, law of nations, Amphictyonic League, international relations, sanctuary, ambassadorial privileges, taxation</keywords>

--- Page 326 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of international relations and the rights of ambassadors, transitioning into the terminology and specific ranks of diplomatic officials.</meta>

<page-num>280</page-num>
<header>PART V : LAWS OF NATIONS</header>

All the words that signify those persons employed by one court at another are derived from the Spanish language. The Spanish court was then the most ceremonious in the world, and Spanish dress was adopted everywhere. As <term>ambassadors</term> were obliged to maintain much ceremony, they were hindered in the conduct of their business. A man who has to negotiate matters of the highest importance could not allow so much time to be spent in the endless ceremony of paying and returning visits. <term>Envoys</term> were therefore sent, to whom less ceremony was due, and who could be addressed on any occasion; however, their dignity soon increased, which eventually hindered them from transacting business. As they stayed for some time, they were called <term>resident ambassadors ordinary</term>, being of a lower rank than the <term>ambassadors extraordinary</term>. Below this rank is the <term>minister</term>, who resides in the country for the sake of his own business, and has the power to transact any minor business for the country to which he belongs.

A <term>consul</term> is a specific magistrate who is a judge of all matters relating to the merchants of his own country, and takes care to ensure they receive justice in those places where it may not be very accurately administered.

These are the names and offices of the various persons employed in the foreign affairs of the nation, brought about by the introduction of commerce, and which have now become absolutely necessary.

Thus we have considered both the laws of nature and the laws of nations.

->FINIS<-

<summary>This page explains the historical Spanish influence on diplomatic terminology and defines the various ranks of foreign representatives, including ambassadors, envoys, ministers, and consuls. It concludes the legal treatise by summarizing the study of the laws of nature and nations.</summary>

<keywords>diplomacy, Spanish influence, ambassadors, envoys, ministers, consuls, international law, commerce, law of nations</keywords>
